## ZULUETA, NO. 28

1890

Translator’s note. Each English page faces, and ends where, its page of the 1890 Havana edition. Evident misprints are corrected silently (e.g. Royer-Collard for “Roger Collard,” Hutcheson for “Hukcheson,” Ritter and Burdach for “Reiter” and “Berdath,” 1833 for “1433”; Latin tags such as “sed tu me coegisti,” “placuerit,” “sententiæ”), and the book’s own errata slip is applied (p. 8: “member,” not “President”). Proper names take their usual English forms; Luz’s Elenco is rendered Syllabus. Phrases left in Latin, French or Italian keep their original with a bracketed gloss. Some wordplay does not survive in English: sociedad / suciedad (Aphorism CCXLII, “society… sootiety”), aspira / respira (CCXVII), the Ripalda epigraph on p. 17 (“Salvador” also names Luz’s school, El Salvador), and the bills-of-exchange puns of the second Rejoinder to the Dómine (pp. 199–200). On p. 224 a fold in the scan hides part of eight lines; the missing words are supplied from the sense.

## Don José de la Luz Caballero

## BIOGRAPHICAL NOTICE

He was born in Havana on the 11th of July of the year 1800, his parents being Lieutenant Colonel of Militia and perpetual Alderman of the Havana Town Council, Don Antonio de la Luz, and the pious and respectable lady Doña Manuela Teresa Caballero.

His education was directed by his maternal uncle, the Presbyter Don José Agustín Caballero, notable on many counts. In the convent of San Francisco, in the Royal and Pontifical University and in the San Carlos Seminary, he studied Latin, philosophy, law and theology.

(1) *In 1817 he publicly delivered a dissertation on a juridical question. (2) In that same year he

(1) The biographical data preceded by the sign * are previously unpublished.

(2) See appendix no. 1.

[p. 6]

graduated as Bachelor of Philosophy, and in 1820 as Bachelor of Laws.

In 1824 he was appointed Professor of Philosophy at the San Carlos Seminary, in which chair he had been preceded by Varela and Saco. (1)

Four years later, still convalescent from a grave illness, he set out for the United States and from there for Europe, returning in 1831, after having come to know and converse with numerous notable figures of the countries he traveled through. While in Paris, he published a translation of Volney’s “Viaje por Egipto y Siria,” with a prologue and notes by the translator.

*In the said year of 1831 he was admitted to the number of members of the Patriotic Society of Havana, and named corresponding member of the “Imperiale é Reale Accademia Económica-Agraria dei Georgofili de Firenze.”

At about that time he made a minute study of the state of instruction in the schools, and, with the object of introducing the explanatory method into them, he took charge of two classes in a school in order to demonstrate its advantages, and gave to the public a “Libro de lectura graduada.”

*In 1832 he applied for authorization to establish a secondary school, which he did not carry out, taking over in that same year (2) the direction of the school of San

(1) See appendix no. 2.

(2) Don J. I. Rodríguez says that in 1834.

[p. 7]

Cristóbal, better known as Carraguao , and in the following year requesting permission to inaugurate a chair of chemistry.

In that same year 1833 he married Doña Mariana Romay, (1) by whom he had a daughter who died in 1850.

In 1834 he opened a course of philosophy at the College of San Cristóbal, and published his well-known report on the Cuban Institute .

*He was elected Vice-Director of the Economic Society in that same year,

In 1835 he issued a Syllabus of philosophical topics.

In 1836 he obtained from the Audiencia of Puerto Príncipe the title of Lawyer . (3)

In 1838 he was named Director of the Economic Society, being re-elected in 1840.

General Ezpeleta granted him, in the said year 1838, provisional license for a chair of philosophy at San Francisco, which he taught until 1843. (4)

*Provisionally also he was authorized in 1839 to open a school, both provisional permits being confirmed at the end of that year by two Royal Orders. (5)

(2) and re-elected to that office at the end of the two-year term.

(1) Rodríguez places this event in 1834. See appendix no. 3.

(2) See appendix no. 4.

(3) Rodríguez is mistaken in saying that in 1837.

(4) See appendix no. 5.

(5) See appendix no. 6.

[p. 8]

At this time began the celebrated polemic on the philosophy of Cousin, which continued through almost the whole of the year 1840. In that year Luz published two installments of an “Impugnación al examen de Cousin sobre el Ensayo del entendimiento de Locke,” and a pamphlet containing several articles of the said polemic. He likewise published another S yllabus of philosophical questions.

*In 1841 he received the appointments of alternate Member of the Board of Population, (1) of corresponding Member of the Academy of Belles-Lettres of Barcelona, and of member of a Commission charged with carrying out the project of a Cuban Institute. (2)

While he was still Director of the Royal Economic Society, in 1842, an incident took place that brought into relief the integrity of Luz’s character and his noble devotion to the cause of justice. Mr. David Turnbull, an English writer, was a corresponding member of the said Society, and consul of his nation in Havana: suspected of inciting the Blacks to rebellion and of propagating doctrines baneful to the slaveholders, these sought and obtained his removal from that post; and at the same time, at the suggestion of the Governor General, the Society resolved to withdraw from Turnbull the title of member, considering unworthy of being called “friend of the

(1) See appendix no. 7.

(2) See appendix no. 8.

[p. 9]

country” one who harbored such perfidious intentions.—On learning of this resolution, Luz, kept at home by his illnesses, at once drew up an energetic and manly protest, convoked a new meeting, and the said resolution was revoked. (1)

His health much broken, he left a second time for Europe in 1843, and, implicated in the famous C onspiracy of the B lacks , he hastened the following year to answer the summons of the terrible Military Commission, being later fully acquitted. (2)

In 1848 he established the College “El Salvador,” directed by him until his death, and continued by Don José M. Zayas until 1869. (3)

*In 1859 he was designated Member of the Inspectorate of Studies, which office he resigned, (4) and, elected Alderman of the Town Council in 1860, he likewise declined to occupy the post. (5)

He contributed chiefly to the “Revista Bimestre,” “Diario de la Habana,” “Memorias de la Sociedad Económica” and “Gaceta de Puerto Príncipe.” He was a member of merit of the Economic Society and of the Academy of Sciences.

He died in Havana on the 22nd of June of 1862 at

(1) See appendix no. 9.

(2) See appendix no. 10.

(3) See appendix no. 11.

(4) See appendix no. 12.

(5) See appendix no. 13.

[p. 10]

a quarter past seven in the morning, (1) and was buried on the following day in the afternoon, with a cortege never before seen in Cuba, and amid general consternation and public mourning. The Government joined in the popular feeling, decreeing the closing of the educational Institutes for three days as a sign of homage, sending a representative to the burial and inviting all the official Corporations to attend it. (2) The ecclesiastical Authority showed itself displeased at these honors to the deceased, on the false ground that he was a Freemason , and this attitude has given rise to the much-debated question of Luz’s confession. (3)

Over his body speeches were delivered by Don José Francisco Ruz and Don Francisco de Zayas. Today his remains rest in the Colón Cemetery of this Capital, beneath a simple monument due to the patriotic efforts of Don Raimundo Cabrera, and raised by popular subscription.

A. Z. Havana 1890.

(1) See appendix no. 14.

(2) See appendix no. 15.

(3) See appendix no. 16.

## VARIOUS OPINIONS

## concerning

Don José de la Luz Caballero

The master of all the sciences.

(Popular designation.)

With which of the learned men of Europe have I the honor of speaking?

(Words of Walter Scott, when Luz visited him, in 1829.)

A man of talent, much instructed in the sciences.... of conversation at once animated and instructive.

(Humboldt.—1829) (1)

Illustrious patrician.... honor and glory of our soil, and one to whom his country owes so much on more than one count.. He has given us countless proofs of the generous temper of his soul,

(1) Letters to Emilio Aragó and to Luz, the originals of which I keep.

[p. 12]

as of his profound and varied instruction in the branches of true knowledge, eminently turned to account by his unalloyed love of country.

(Fr. Francisco Ruiz. 1839) (1)

Don José de la Luz Caballero is the man of letters of greatest prestige in Havana; but I believe the name of sage suits him better than that of man of letters. His writings are usually profound, but too scholastic. Through his vast knowledge, especially in philosophy, there shows a bad taste in diction...... Some articles on philosophy inserted in the “Diario de la Habana” reveal a profound learning, but the controversy is of the lecture hall...... It seems to us that Mr. de la Luz is too good a teacher to be a great writer.

(J. Salas y Quiroga) (2)

It depends only, perhaps, on favorable circumstances whether the names of Don José Antonio Saco, Don José de la Luz and Don Domingo del Monte come to be surrounded by a European luster.

Don José de la Luz is a delicate, penetrating intelligence, a chemist of the first order, a notable philologist, a writer of talent.

(The Countess of Merlin) (3)

In philosophy Luz attains the highest place.

(R. R. Madden) (4)

You have never ceased to be regarded by me with the greatest feelings of affection and the most sincere ones of respect and veneration, for in my long dealings with men

(1) Diario de la Habana.

(2) Madrid—1840.

(3) La Havane—Brussels—1844. Vol. IV, p. 13.

(4) The Island of Cuba its resources, etc.—London, 1853.

[p. 13]

of different countries, more or less notable for their intelligence or their moral excellences, I have never found one whom I have grown accustomed to contemplate with so much glory, for the devotion of his noble energies to the good of the rising generation of his compatriots, to the cause of education, to the progress of letters and of the sound principles of the social and political sciences, as I have done and do with respect to you.

.... You have entirely sacrificed your own interests to those of your country. All the rich treasures of your well-endowed brain, your private resources, a whole life of intellectual labor, have been given over to the cause of education in Cuba.

(Ibidem) (1)

Don Pepe, the angelic man, the sage, the amiable gentleman, the undaunted man who knows no fear, and the one whom all love and exalt......

He is a liberal man, and is endowed with a great heart and with great reason at the same time.... Like all wise and prudent philanthropists, he sees in education the principal source of good for men, and he consecrated his life and his fortune to this object.

(Julia Ward Howe) (2)

Don José de la Luz has unfortunately not condensed his philosophical teaching in any work. He had occasion to expound it, though partially, on the occasion of the polemics in which he combated Cousinian eclecticism around the year 1839: he has gone on developing it in his classes, inimitably conducted, for Mr. Luz has no rival in teaching: he has been formulating it in various interesting syllabuses: he has explained it each time that all Havana

(1) Letter to Luz—Málaga, January 30, 1862.—I keep the original in English.

(2) A trip to Cuba—Boston, 1860.—(Cited by Rodríguez.)

[p. 14]

has gathered around him, eager for his most eloquent word: he has made it practical in his example: he has gone on writing it, in short, in the understanding and in the heart of his disciples. But in this manner, it is easy to see how difficult it must be to give an exact account of the doctrines of the distinguished master, and all the more so considering that his immense erudition embraces everything and turns it to account for the ends of teaching.

....Its substance and its essence can be expressed by this single word: Harmony.

(J. M. Mestre) (1)

A certain Luz.... disaffected toward Spain.

(El Reino—Madrid, 1862)

A teacher of primary instruction, who owed his celebrity in the Island to his annexationist and independentist opinions

(El Contemporáneo—Madrid, 1862)

Idol of the enemies of Spain in Cuba.

His literary merits came down to the publication of a little teaching treatise under the name of Texto de lectura graduada, and of the Informe sobre el Instituto cubano; and his public and private virtues consisted in having perverted the hearts of children with his antipatriotic and subversive maxims where the peninsular family was concerned, and in having fomented hatred against Spain in the College of El Salvador, which he directed.

....The skillful master preferred spoken propaganda to written, because it penetrated further into opinion, whose dominion he sought; and if in his unimportant publications he appears timid or equivocal, not so in the lectures he gave to his co-religionists and disciples.......

(1) De la filosofía en la Habana, 1862.

[p. 15]

....The indeterminacy of Luz Caballero’s philosophical school, and the confusion in his doctrines.

(J. Zaragoza) (1)

Don José de la Luz loved his country, the Island of Cuba, with that purest love of which only spirits as finely tempered as his are capable; and, dreaming always of the aggrandizement, the happiness and the progress of his country, in the Christian and philosophical sense of these words, it seemed to him, with good reason, that there was no surer or more legitimate means of reaching that noble end than to prepare souls, forming the people for whom he so longed for those benefits. The end, then, of Mr. Luz was eminently patriotic; and as he carried it into effect with true consecration, with complete self-denial, and at the same time with resolute zeal and acknowledged success, the influence of his word and of his example came to be powerful, and his name was immortalized in the country.

....It was the same in Mathematics as in Physics or in Natural History, the same in the classes of sciences as in those of languages, classical as well as living; wherever his word was heard, it was heard with pleasure and with profit....

....By saying that he was a patriot in the best and broadest acceptation of the term, that he decidedly loved the beautiful land in which he saw the light, and that his patriotism, never lukewarm or drowsy, but energetic and vigilant, was always in exercise, though constantly in harmony with the loftiest and purest sentiments of religion and morality, one would have........ an exact expression of the political ideas of Mr. Luz.

....If the reputation that Don José de la Luz enjoyed in the Island of Cuba as a public man, as a patriot,

(1) Las insurrecciones de Cuba—Vol. II—Madrid, 1873.

[p. 16]

as an educator, as a teacher, as a learned man profoundly versed in Literature, Philology and the natural sciences, was in reality extraordinary, that which he enjoyed as a philosopher certainly did not lag behind it. On the contrary, it might be said without inexactitude that in this aspect he was perhaps better known and more popular among his contemporaries.

The scholar as well as the ignorant, the sage as well as the layman, respected in him the philosopher, the careful observer of the human soul, of its functions and of its relations, the profound thinker. And in truth they were not mistaken. Even from this exclusive point of view, the illustrious name of Mr. Luz constitutes for Cuba a title of glory.

....Mr. Luz was a profound physicist and also a physiologist...... He knew Natural History, Anatomy, Chemistry and the other natural sciences well enough... But these sciences were in Mr. Luz nothing but elements and preparation for a greater work. Everything in him had come to converge upon another, more sublime study: and like tributary streams that unite in one bed to form a great river, the knowledge accumulated in the understanding of Mr. Luz came to flow together and unite in a given object, serving as data for the better study of man and of his relations with the Creator and the created, and to tend toward their greater and more complete knowledge.

.......A profound observer, thoroughly acquainted with history, familiar with the study of the natural sciences, possessing with most remarkable perfection the greater part of the languages spoken in the civilized world, Mr. Luz had all the conditions required to be an eminent philosopher. He was a worthy imitator of the Platos, Aristotles and Leibnitzes. The unlettered people called him “the master of all the sciences”; and as he was endowed with a prodigious memory and with an untiring activity,

[p. 17]

the store of his knowledge kept constantly increasing and confirmed his essentially harmonious and comprehensive doctrine.

## (José Ignacio Rodríguez.) (1)

## —What were his chief offices?

—Those of savior and master.

Ripalda.

....That man was the glory of Cuba.... That man was more than a scholar, he was a treasure of evangelical meekness, an ocean of infinite love, in his heart there was room for the whole of humanity; he was an unshakable rock in the fulfillment of duty; an energetic defender of the rights of justice; an invincible obstacle to the corrupters of the individual and of society; he was a sage, a philosopher, a savior, a master; he was one of those few benefactors of men whom history recalls from time to time, and toward whom humanity turns its eyes in confidence, to strengthen itself in their love, to temper itself in their word; he was a Confucius, a Socrates, a Christ; he was, in short, Don José de la Luz Caballero, he was Don Pepe.

....Master not only of all the sciences, but, what is more, of all the virtues.... a finished model of patriotism, of affability, of universal benevolence, a man without gall, severe to excess with himself, indulgent to weakness toward all men; preacher of peace and of work; enemy of tyranny and of perversity; opposed to every violent measure, ever the slave of the law.

.... Where are the works of Don Pepe? Blind are they who do not see them, most ignorant they who do not understand them. Where are the works of Socrates? In the history of Greece, in one formula: “know thyself.” Where

(1) Vida de D. José de la Luz Caballero.—New York, 1874.

[p. 18]

are the works of Jesus Christ? In the history of modern civilization, in one precept: “love thy neighbor as thyself.” Where are the works of José de la Luz Caballero? In the history of Cuba, in one sentence: “truth alone will invest us with the toga of manhood.” He is the author of this social condition that we have attained: he is the spiritual father of all who have stood out here for their virtues, for their learning, or for their aspirations....

.... The eloquent posthumous eulogies of our notable men, his polemics on subjects of pedagogy and of philosophy, the numerous labors of the Royal Patriotic Corporation, particularly in its Section of education, his brilliant report on the “Cuban Institute,” his various syllabuses of philosophy, are the plain proofs that there existed in Havana a profound thinker, a true sage, a model educator, a pure and classic writer, nourished on Jovellanos and on Cervantes, on Quevedo and on Luis de León, his favorite authors. They are the proofs, we add, that Cuba held in her bosom one of the men who have most honored the Spanish family.

(J. M. Zayas) (1)

He was simply a separatist in disguise.

(Diario de Barcelona—1881)

By the perspicacity of his genius, sharpened in the constant study of the loftiest works of human knowledge, and by the powerful flight of his reasoning.... Luz Caballero was, in this remote corner of the civilized world, a true precursor of doctrines that are today preached with applause in the centers of human culture.

...... Endowed with a prodigious faculty of systematization, he was clearly aware of the course that philosophical inquiry

(1) Revista de Cuba.—1879.

[p. 19]

was taking, and pointed out in advance many of its most important conclusions.

(E. J. Varona) (1)

José de la Luz Caballero was an eminent man whom his contemporaries sincerely respected and loved, and whose memory is venerated in the island of Cuba.

...... Rarely are so many excellences united and harmonized as made up his individuality: varied and extensive knowledge, noble meekness, boundless generosity, inflexible rectitude of character, a pious heart, loftiness of aims, inexhaustible love, faith, philanthropy, patriotism....

He was, in effect, a superior man; for his countrymen a model of men— at least in many respects—for some of them, the most finished model of patriotism “that could be found among things human.”

...... He was a pure man and he was, also, a precursor. He never dreamed, assuredly, of unsettling consciences, preparing them for immediate and devastating action: he longed, on the contrary, to enlighten them in truth and to calm them in virtue; but, in the end, he unsettled them all the same.

His discourses.... are a faithful and complete expression of great sensibility, of excited sensibility; they breathe the candor of his soul, the least restrained tenderness, in beautiful, rounded, solemn periods, like periods of Jovellanos. But his form turns out inferior when he writes, for lack of charm and grace, of ease and of that ineffable something which, in the productions of one who uses the pen as an instrument...... makes works of art.

With the qualities proper to him and the exceptional faculties of his individuality, it is easy to understand that he will be an ardent patriot without ever being a revolutionary; that no one will equal him as a teacher, nor will anyone surpass him as a man.

(1) Conferencias filosóficas—Lógica.—Havana, 1888.

[p. 20]

...... He was.... a thinker of brilliant and surprising penetration, approaching at times the most advanced frontiers of philosophy, to the point of seeming a modern, a colleague and contemporary of Spencer or of Wundt; but commonly amalgamated with the primitive religious man; something like a man of the first half of the XIXth century cast in the mold of a man of the last days of the Middle Ages....

I cannot bring myself to believe that the work he wrote at that time, with the title “Texto de lectura graduada para ejercitar el método explicativo,” was in reality useful and suited to its object.... That essay must, nevertheless, be judged more by its intention than by its real value; perhaps, with its illustrious and most learned author as the teacher using it, the results were bound to be very gratifying .....

The greatest praise of Luz Caballero, as well as the demonstration of his clear-sighted patriotism and of the depth of his aims, follows from the very fact of his having fought in the island of Cuba against the pernicious tendencies of that Cousinian doctrine, in the order of ideas as well as in the social and political order.

...... During that speculative agitation, it is evident that Luz was a contender greatly superior to his adversaries, and that in the fundamentals of his ideas he was in full accord with those of Locke.....

José de la Luz Caballero—to judge by what his publications known up to the present reveal—corresponds, as a philosopher, to what we might call the transitional type. Viewed in one aspect of his spirit, he seems a man of the past: viewed in another, he seems a contemporary of ours marching in the vanguard, on the road to the future.

The foregoing propositions (on Eclecticism) prove him, above all if his country and his time are taken into account, a thinker of genius and depth.... An illustrious member, though unknown outside Cuba, of the family of European sages who, setting out from Bacon and from Locke, and passing

[p. 21]

through Hume and Kant, have ended up, under diverse forms, in a more or less idealist or phenomenalist positivism.

.... Religiosity was the deepest sentiment of his soul, and an exuberant sentiment. The simplest object set in commotion that organism so sensitive, so ready to respond to its own suggestion while believing it was responding to the suggestion of the world.

Reading his writings, one comes to be persuaded that very early he grew accustomed to intercourse and commerce with the great philosophers, and most especially with those who determined and constituted the great modern scientific movement....

He left to his country the example of his life; a life without stain, the prodigy of having lived always amid temptations, amid baseness and miseries, without ever being contaminated. In his modest sphere and from the corner of his school he realized an admirable type of man. He lived perpetually immaculate, and dreamed constantly of the happiness and the glory of his country.

(Manuel Sanguily). (1)

(1) José de la Luz Caballero.—Estudio crítico.—Havana, 1890.

[p. i]

## APHORISMS

[PDF 23]

## P reliminary N otes (1)

N. B.—In Havana I began to collect some aphorisms in this style, which came to some 50 or 60. It was in June of 1842, at the Cerro, at the side of my idol, of my Mariana and of my Luisa: “When God so willed.”—When will He will it?

(Paris, January 2, 1844.)

N. B.—I refrain as yet from speaking of the very matter in which feeling and observation have given me the richest store; because in the frail state of my health, the sole cause for which I parted from the two halves of my heart, it would excite me beyond measure: to wit, “love, marriage, friendship and illness.” And the mine of disillusion? [although for that I have two inexhaustible sources—religion and philosophy]—though I believe (with closed fist) in virtue and friendship, more than anyone.

(January 5, 1844)

N. B.—There are some aphorisms that are only motives for thinking.

(1) The first two notes that follow appear in a small notebook in which only 15 thoughts or propositions are found, of a date earlier than the said notes. In another notebook of nine sheets of common paper, in which Luz began to copy his aphorisms in 1847, is the third note, and the two preceding it are copied there, with the statement that they may serve as a prologue or preliminary notice to those aphorisms, and with no other variant than the addition, after “June of 1842,” of a parenthesis that says: “(and on reproducing it now ‘mi viene il ribrezzo.’ [a shudder comes over me]—(A. Z.)

[p. 308]

## R eligion

(T he aphorisms not preceded by a * are previously unpublished .)

I

(Undated).—Ah! Christianity! Christianity! Christianity!

“Tantum religio potuit suadere bonorum!” [so much good could religion prompt] (instead of malorum [evil], as Lucretius sang).

This belief honors and dishonors humanity. It honors it, because it is founded on the love and esteem of the good engraved in our hearts; it dishonors it, because it betrays a league off the weakness of its reason, or the predominance of the passions.

II

(Idem).—Religion is a harmonizing power; the consolation of the unfortunate, and the curb of those favored by fortune—“sperate miseri, cavete felices.” [hope, you wretched; beware, you fortunate]

III

(Idem).—Who would have believed that by way of unbelief we should have come back to Christianity?

* Though the good who are good are few, the wicked are not so many as is commonly believed.

[p. 28]

“Potens est Deus de lapidibus istis suscitare etc.” [God is able of these stones to raise up etc.] One might say: “potens est Deus de fulminibus istis suscitare filios Abrahæ. (Jesu?)” [God is able of these thunderbolts to raise up children unto Abraham. (Jesus?)]

Who can doubt that the philanthropism of the age is the legitimate child of the eighteenth century? For one must be impartial; besides which, God is thus the more glorified.

IV

(June 6, 1842).—Man cannot be the term between God and creation.

V

(June 7).—The cross, a ladder between heaven and earth.

VI

(Paris.—December 28, 1843).—* Religion is the soul of the soul.

VII

(January 15, 1844.—* Admirable providence!—That God does not send great trials to small souls!

## VIII

(January 24, 1845).—* 1° Religion is the soul of the soul. (1).

2° Great need of the age: Religion!

3° The most admirable phenomenon in history: The Church.

4° A religion is needed at all costs.

5° Religion, the true philosopher’s stone, which turns even dross into gold, misfortune into rejoicing.

6° Without it there is no love, and without love the earth is a frightful wilderness, not even a vale of tears, which is a thousand times

(1) Luz’s manuscript says next: “I also had it in Paris in 1843.”—(A. Z.)

[p. 29]

preferable, for tears are dried, and it is good that they be shed.

7° Evils, physical as well as moral, are true blessings that Providence dispenses to us: without them man would not unfold all his latent strength, and for that very reason would be less virtuous; thus evils are special favors, privileges to be coveted, but which ought not to be coveted, because then the craving degenerates into vanity.

8° Evils, the true germ of science, and of the most profound, that which teaches us to be ignorant.

9° Are religion and philosophy one and the same thing? For some, and according to some.

10° Religion, daughter and mother of feeling: philosophy, the sure path to religion.

11° Religion, the goal of philosophy.

12° The latter the doctrine, the former love. The one, the knowledge, the confession of the Maker; the other, intercourse and communion with Him.

IX

(May 12).—Blessed be evils, and more blessed sacrosanct religion—you who tear us from this earthly mire and raise us up to heaven.

Thus I have come to the point of blessing evils more than goods, and of relishing the latter the better when they present themselves on the wing. Blessed be their fleetingness!

Were it not for the two halves of my heart, I should even be glad not to recover; but their lot is in the hands of providence, which will not fail them. Nevertheless, they, and always they, wife and daughter, in my mind and in my heart, to console and to sadden them. By this I am bound to humanity, and by love of my neighbor; but never by love of myself, which is null.

[p. 30]

X

(May 15).—What is life without faith? It is not life: it is hell in life.

To live, one must believe without seeing.

XI

(Idem).—The exercise of thought, the most acceptable worship of the Divinity.

XII

(May 18).—What is the universal asylum against tyranny, against the world, against misfortune, or for all these causes? Religion.

Woman without religion may be said to be more unhappy, and man without religion less happy.

## XIII

(May 21).—I find myself hanging, like Mahomet’s coffin, between the lodestone of stoicism and that of Christianity.

For myself stoicism, for my neighbor Christianity: although all that was good in stoicism passed over into Christianity.

XIV

(December 19).—What is the most beautiful spectacle in the Universe? —The just man in misfortune.

The greater this is, the more necessary it becomes for man to take refuge in a higher region.

The earthly atmosphere stifles him if he does not fly up and soar to the heavenly one.

But as that is not his element either, he comes down again at every step, or remains suspended between the two regions.

A decree of Providence, which thus deifies man without

[p. 31]

his ceasing for all that to be human—the bonds that knot him to his brothers, to his fellow-sufferers.

Otherwise he would attain happiness in this world, if impassibility can be called happiness.

The struggle would cease, and consequently the life of the just man.

Not even the most seasoned veteran ought to rest upon his laurels.

XV

(Jesús del Monte—April 6, 1846).—1. I should like to write a commentary on the Our Father.

2. Each word, each conception, each sentiment, and the whole! Ah! divine! and most exquisitely calculated for human beings as separate members, and as members of one same body.

3. How should souls eminently enthusiastic and religious not hold all the essential words of Christianity to have been materially uttered by God himself, when even the most frozen and unbelieving are warmed and kindled at the fire and the sweetness of so much sun and so much love!

What precepts, what lessons, what hymns, what ejaculatory prayers in the world contain what the Our Father contains in its profound simplicity!

Dig, dig, philosophers—miners, I say, and not underminers—of the heart, and each time you will find the vein richer!

XVI

(Jesús del Monte.—April 21).—* Yesterday.—Look, madam, that goat is going to break its neck!

—“It is not mine,” the woman answered me. I might have replied: “nor mine either, and yet I have warned you.”—But that would have been to throw in her face that I had some religion, and she none. Meanwhile the little animal got itself free, and I was left thinking over my theme: the lack

[p. 32]

of religion in our age, even in the lowest class, and in the female sex, which is the most lamentable thing of all! At every step one stumbles upon the lack of love among men: gathered together, not associated; men, not brothers! How long, O Lord! “Adveniat regnum tuum!” [Thy kingdom come!]

There is no thinker who does not acknowledge the power of religion and its necessity.

## XVII

(May 8).—Even for the unkindness of those from whom we ought to expect love, religion consoles—or philosophy, for me united or merged into one.

It is folly for men to believe that they will find any other panacea in this vale of tears.

The positivists (and the positivist women more so) laugh at all these spiritual remedies; and yet indirectly they feel their benign influence, in the way in which they are treated and cared for by those who believe in them amplectis ulnis. [with open arms]

Such is the world: it falls to some to hoard up virtues, in order to distribute consolations. Here is the reward of the just man, if indeed he needs any other than the testimony of his conscience, of God.

## XVIII

(Madruga.—June 2).—What a lesson, and from the mouth of the venerable octogenarian!—“Every day I am more persuaded,” he said, “that faults are punished in the long run in this world, and at times with great severity.... Often neither foresight nor precaution avails..... So one must resign oneself and conform to the will of God,” and forgive, I add.

And when the virtuous man is the target of the blows of fortune? This fact of the moral world, though not so frequent as is believed, drives to despair and demoralizes weak souls (which are wont to consider themselves very strong), whereas for me it is the most irrefragable proof of the intervention of Providence in this world.

[p. 33]

I have said on other occasions that the just man in misfortune is the most beautiful spectacle in the world. Misfortune, the only crown worthy of virtue. “Quia manus Domini tetigit me.” [For the hand of the Lord hath touched me.] (Job).

## XIX

(August 30).—This determination of Providence to make our duties known to us, as much by the light of the intelligence as by the warnings of the heart, says much, or says everything, in favor of the excellence of our being; or at least in support of human responsibility.

Before these facts all else falls silent.

XX

(September 5).—Hymns without end to the Almighty for all that is good, and all that seems evil.

I was always a profound applauder of the works of God, in the physical world as in the moral, and a most loving friend of humanity.

Can I say more energetically that I am profoundly religious? Yes: that what they call misfortune has raised and refined the carats of my religiousness.

I say what they call, because there can be no misfortune for the religious soul.

Nevertheless, there are griefs, and great griefs, because they are blows that reach through to those who touch us most nearly.

For the rest, “si Deus est nobiscum, quis contra nos?” [if God be with us, who can be against us?]—What a true truth!

XXI

(September 6).—One comes to the point of despising and hating so much the greatest enemy of the soul—the flesh—that one has to set about seeking reasons to justify its influence in the designs of the great Maker.

[p. 34]

## XXII

(September 16).—Ah, my God, my God, how I find Thee even where I least expected Thee! How plainly one sees that Thou art everywhere!

Even in the work of the wicked (and it is no blasphemy) I see Thee by reflection, by contrast; because then I feel the good more keenly.

Lessons in everything, for those who long to be instructed with an unquenchable thirst, as the hart longs for the water brooks.

## XXIII

(February 20, 1847).—The dogma of the resurrection of the flesh, profound under three aspects.

1° Most exquisitely calculated to satisfy the hopes, the longings of humanity as it is, that is to say, corporeal as well, and with a semblance of everlasting joys.

2° As a representation of the transformations in the physical world— metempsychosis.

3° As the replacement of some races by others.

## XXIV

(March 4).—Neither worship nor religion can be too simple; or rather, they must be well composed, must form a true system; at least for certain peoples and certain individuals (truly very numerous); for since religion addresses itself to all the faculties, how else is it to nourish them?

There are peoples, on the contrary, that have a marked tendency to simplicity or simplification of dogmas and of worship, for whom concentration within themselves suffices, so to speak, who spend their lives as in a perpetual examination of conscience, with no need of exterior stimulus (e. g. the friends). (1) That is why these questions can never be resolved

(1) A Protestant sect: the Quakers. (A. Z.)

[p. 35]

in an absolute way; the human soul has needs that are the same and needs that are diverse, and diverse ways of satisfying the same moral needs.

Thus in progress, and in the last analysis, we must come to rest in liberty, so that every good germ may thrive, and be applied and extended as far as it ought: “Unica tellus quæ fert omnia” [the one land that bears all things]— but beginning is another story.

XXV

(Idem).—How we are constituted.

There is in us the germ of the ideal, of the marvelous (it is not one and the same thing), of mystery.

Which germs it is fitting to develop. Whether this can be aspired to by means of philosophy alone. Whether there are not other means according to the ages.

If the tree is to be known by its fruits, those which Christianity has produced—and why.—Whether it owes them in great part to the circumstance of its being given out as revelation. Christianity as Catholicism and Protestantism. (1)

## XXVI

(April 2).—When I feel cold and slack, lacking inspiration, I have only to read a verse of the Gospel for feelings and thoughts to flow in torrents, warming the heart and illuminating the head; the holy spirit is communicated to me (in the full force of the term)—the gift of tongues.

Behold there the divinity of Christianity. A humanizing divinity, and a divinized humanity.—“Oh altitudo!” [O the depth!]

## XXVII

(San Antonio.—August 15).—1° The last Gospel of today’s Sunday mass. The divine parable of Jesus (what a name

(1) It is well not to forget that some aphorisms are only motives for thinking, as Luz points out. (A. Z.)

[p. 36]

so sweet!) on the understanding of the precept “to love one’s neighbor as oneself.” The priest and the Levite and the Samaritan. How small all books are beside the Gospel!

2° And what a scandalous dissonance with that precept was the compliment which, with a jocular and fawning air, a San Antonio man paid to J., about his giving a face-down flogging (it is a stain to set it down in print!) to the Black man who was with him when he fell from his horse; besides which, there was no fault in the Black man. And by all accounts he was a man of sweet and amiable aspect; but it did me harm, and all of Christian love is needed to pity these vermin that slave soils produce.

3° The only inoculation for our leprosy, for everyone, for ministers and all.

## XXVIII

(San Antonio de los Baños.—August 22).—The religious principle is dead among us, and even the few fops who proclaim it do not practice it. Some neither dream of it, nor so much as glimpse its influence.—And the women? I would rather not even touch that string. If they were made to feel what they would gain—who knows? Not even so. Our atmosphere is deleterious. The reorganizing element, the breath of life, is indispensable.

## XXIX

(June 26, 1850).—There is no respect because there is no religion.

Behold there modern society.

XXX

(October 11).—How should I not turn all to tongues of gratitude in praise of the Divinity, who, sending me the greatest of tribulations, (1) accompanies it for me with the greatest of consolations!

(1) He alludes to the death of his only daughter, which had occurred three months before. —(A. Z.)

[p. 37]

The rehabilitation, after ten years, in mental labor, the only instrument (for it would be blasphemy to call it the only refuge, which is found nowhere but in the sancta-sanctorum of religion, my soul, my life and my all!) which, innocently captivating the spirit, lulls it meanwhile against sorrow,—“adversus perfugium de solatium prebent” [they afford a refuge and a solace in adversity]—and I more than anyone could add “pernoctant nobiscum,” [they spend the night with us] since I do it all in the deep silence of the night, when everything sleeps.

## XXXI

(February 24, 1851).—There is no religious dogma that is not founded on some philosophy more or less profound. Even the ceremonies.

The Catholic liturgy, above all in the rites of Holy Week, reveals countless doctrines of the most remote antiquity.

## XXXII

(May 20).—After I feel the blow I bless God for the effect it produces in my soul. Then I savor the tribulation, or its purifying result, if indeed there is anything to purify in an innocent soul (and there always is), and in the end I am left as it were more consoled; and it restores me to find my consolation by isolating myself from everything human, with nowhere to rest but in God, and I exclaim: “Bonum mihi quia humiliasti me ut discam justificationes tuas” [It is good for me that thou hast humbled me, that I may learn thy justifications]—so as not to fall asleep on my laurels, or, to put it better, on my thorns.

## XXXIII

(January 5, 1852).—Mysticism is the refuge of pure souls against this rottenness that we call the world.

## XXXIV

(June 22, 1853).—What a great thing is this testimony

[p. 38]

of conscience, which in the midst of the storm raises its voice, saying: thou hast done well!

Ecce, ecce Deus. [Behold, behold God.]

## XXXV

(May 28, 1855).—To feel, to think, to pray—wings and compass that bear us to the haven of God.

## XXXVI

(Idem).—O divine Religion! O natural aspiration of the soul, which like its incense rises from the ground to heaven! O Philosophy, ripe daughter of the divinity, who like another stream joins that of religion, and mingles in the same torrent, and bears us to the bosom of God: Religion and Philosophy, the two eyes with which we gaze upon Him, and the arms with which we clasp Him.

Even if Religion produced no other fruit than the consolation of man in adversity, we ought to hail it as the greatest of blessings for humanity. Truly well-tempered souls experience not only consolation, but enjoyment in tribulations. There are souls, however, whom the blows of destiny make more stubborn and rebellious. But what? does there not exist a spark of poetry and of love in those breasts? How, being human, can this blood of the divine spirit be lacking in them? I know well that this language is foreign and unintelligible to those who pride themselves on being positive and enlightened; but let them know too that there are none the less souls that understand it and speak it, by virtue of feeling it and thinking it. It is a reality as effective as that of the sun that gives us light: it is the very sun of the human soul.

[PDF 36]

❖     ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖       ❖   ❖   ❖   ❖   ❖    ❖     ❖

P hilosophy .

## XXXVII

(Undated).—Terrible would be the I without the not-I.

## XXXVIII

(Idem).—The spiritualist doctrine does not prove but supposes the existence of God. We shall see which of the two systems (spiritualist or sensationalist) is the more atheistic.

## XXXIX

(Idem).—The eclectic school has gone to seek Philosophy in Greece, Scotland and Germany, leaving it in its own house.

It is good to know how others think, but it is better to think for oneself.

The philosophers have feared that their science might be reduced to a chapter, though an important one, of physiology, and for that reason they have taken refuge in the field of erudition.

An incentive for youth.

XL

(Idem).—I too wish to spiritualize man, but for that one must not deceive him, nor deceive ourselves.

[p. 40]

Man is convinced of the great advantages of spiritualizing him.

XLI

(Idem).—Philosophy in the heart more than on the lips.

## XLII

(Idem).—Consciousness is the field in which the I meets the not-I: here the difference and the identification.

## XLIII

(Idem).—Like the bee, let the philosopher gather, choose and assimilate—sweet honey and light-giving wax.

## XLIV

(Idem).—The mind comes to be hermaphrodite in the production of ideas; for it needs only to think upon what it has already conceived in order to beget and bring forth new offspring.

The world serves it only as occasion, as motive; but it is no longer the fecundator nor the brooder.

XLV

(Idem).—* The idea lives before and lives after the reality.

## XLVI

(May, 1842).—Philosophy owes more to the intolerant than to the conciliators.

## XLVII

(Idem).—* The philosopher is (and ought to be) like the candle; it burns and is consumed to give light to others.

[p. 41]

## XLVIII

(January 2, 1844).—It would always be more exact for man to say “there is in me,” rather than I have, such or such a faculty.

## XLIX

(Idem).—* The vainest creature, and the one that ought least to be vain in this world: man.

Whenever he does something great, an extraordinary force that is in him is revealed, and at the very first turn the conviction (or, to put it better, the experiment) of his nothingness and his frailty.

And in the midst of all the whirlwind of the passions, of greatness or of weakness, the voice of reason rises up to make him feel it and know it.

L

(January 5).—* Does man by any chance owe his littleness or his greatness to himself? Is he not perhaps a piece of the great whole, destined to perform a special or partial movement, but nevertheless, or for that very reason, a harmonizing one?

It is not a question, however, of impairing the merit or demerit of human actions. Nothing secures it better than the existence of the knowledge of evil, which reveals itself in man at every instant from the moment the light of reason dawns.

LI

(January 6).—* Knowledge of good and of evil: therefore responsibility. Struggle: therefore responsibility. Experience of evil: therefore increase of responsibility.

Widening of knowledge: therefore responsibility. That is why J. C. said “each shall be judged according to what has been given him.” Thus more or less merit in the same action, according to the circumstances of the person and of the things.

[p. 42]

## LII

(April 17, 1845].—Ah nature! nature! or, to put it better: O God! O God! who even in the shades of scents offer us the infinite, and the idea, the preconceived plan par excellence. Sublime Plato, thou wast one of the first interpreters of the Divinity.

## LIII

[Idem].—Reality exceeds the imagination, but yields to the idea, which loses itself in the mind of the Most High, where we lose ourselves, poor rivulets of the immense ocean.

LIV

(May 9).—The obscurity that envelops the question of human destiny, the germ of all philosophy, and even of a very great deal of religion.

The same is true of the deep night of the origin of the world.

Blessed obscurity, which lights our understanding and tempers our heart!

There are data, however, in both questions, which establish certain truths in so irresistible a manner that man cannot evade his assent, nor flee from his responsibility. These data are: the providential plan of the universe, and the science and conscience of human reason, as universal laws engraved in all understandings and in all hearts.

LV

(May 10).—Everything in nature is animate. Always spirit in matter, even in the last pebble.

Is not attraction in all bodies? And forces even in the most inert?

Where in nature does life cease to be? And what

[p. 43]

is life but the result of the soul, or of the spirit, or force, or cause that comes in the end to be substance?

This way of seeing is somewhat Leibnizian. The historian of the human mind must not only relate everything there is in it, but in the order in which it is found. Thus everything is a question of origin.

The eighteenth century did well to seek, even if it could not find, the origin of all things.

That was not where the eighteenth century blundered, but in the superficiality characteristic of every beginner.

Nevertheless, Montesquieu and J. J. Rousseau set the tone for the eighteenth century.

LVI

[May 13].—* Why does man complain of life? Illusions, disillusions, sorrows, consolations, which are pleasures sweeter than those at first hand.

Is not this the law of nature? Roses and thorns, light and shade. Of what does he complain, then? If one door closes, two are wont to open.

But one wants pleasure, pleasure, and pleasure always, and everywhere, without interruption, and physical pleasure. To what end? To reach satiety, and sickness, and physical or moral slackness. Without exercise there is no strength.

## LVII

[December 15].—Can it be possible that man may gain on one side without losing on another?

Which are the true losses and which the true gains?

To answer duly, it is necessary that all dogmas appear before the tribunal of reason, with humanity granted a hearing.

Ought humanity to be represented exclusively by the philosophers?

[p. 44]

In the mass, utique [certainly], but not nationally, which is individually with respect to the human race.

Would it suit humanity, or will it be necessary for its progress, that is, for its improvement, that two doctrines be preached, one for the masses, and another for the cream?

One, and only one, for all, I answer after the most mature examination. But one that comprehends the moral needs of the whole community: in sum, a religion.

The Christian moralists are those who have most trusted in and distrusted human nature; true conciliators.

## LVIII

[December 19].—All philosophical systems “strip one saint to clothe another.”

The fine thing would be to clothe them all, without stripping anyone.

He who came nearest to this type, among the ancients, was Aristotle; among the moderns, none perhaps could have done it better than Leibniz.

But in the ideal, that is, the aspirations, Plato carried off the palm.

A baneful divorce among the sciences. Here is where the great conciliation must be summed up.

LIX

(January 31, 1846).—We are struck by the coincidence of the great thinkers across times and space; a proof of the identity of the human mind. (And this in the physical sciences as in the moral).

2° It is noticed, moreover, that if the human mind is hard pressed, and as it were in labor, sometimes a subaltern talent, or one not so superior, is wont to give birth to a child that seems a production of genius.

3° This coincidence proves two things more to me: 1° A new argument for plan in the Universe; God everywhere, where we least expect Him. 2° His providence,

[p. 45]

which comes opportunely to the needs of humanity, even making use of the lowliest instruments, or converting into instruments those who hold themselves, and are held, to be causes.

Here is where that saying fits in: “Potens est Deus de lapidibus istis suscitare filios Abrahæ....” [God is able of these stones to raise up children unto Abraham] (Saint Matthew).

LX

(February).—The absolute is the height of the relative.

LXI

(February 26).—The method of experimentation is eminently rational, for it does nothing but yield to the promptings of reason in carrying out trials in order to know it.

## LXII

(Idem).—There is a mode of discovering truths that is neither intuition, nor induction, nor deduction, and which I would call apparition by its effect, and virtual and unconscious elaboration by its cause and procedure. It happens after one has been meditating at length upon a subject, when the understanding has been strongly excited, or, as we might say, in erection, that after the powers have rested, without our being occupied any longer with the matter, a great thought appears like a flash of lightning, and one that is in reality deduced from the antecedents; but we have not deduced it. We are ignorant of our own virtuality.

How is it that the partisans of innate ideas have not taken advantage of this fact, which is stronger than everything that has been heaped up concerning them?

Nevertheless, well examined, it is neither for nor against, but what it proves is the virtuality of the understanding, its nature of producing thoughts.

The contrary opinion might rather make capital of it: because the phenomenon aforesaid is always the child of its antecedents,

[p. 46]

as is the case even with spontaneity or intuition itself.

“Prolem cum patre et matre creatam.” [offspring created together with father and mother]

## LXIII

(Idem).—Strictly speaking, there is no spontaneity in the exercise of our intellectual faculties: some motive always precedes that determines the thought. Which is not inconsistent with there being notions that the understanding attains at once, and others that it obtains only through reflection.

The I is nothing but a phenomenon of the intelligence, and it has place in sensations just as in volitions: it is neither more nor less than what it is generally held to be, the expression of personality; which does not prevent its being considered as subject and as cause.

## LXIV

(Idem).—Experimentation affords the senses an aid with which they did not count before; it isolates and arrests the facts, so to speak, so that they let themselves be observed.

Nature pronounces at once very complicated judgments, judgments of many subjects and many predicates: that is why we cannot determine with certainty which is the predicate and to which subject it belongs.

It is necessary, then, that we separate the cases for her, that we afford her more opportunity of expressing herself in judgments of one subject and one predicate.

The moment she does so, we understand her at once; then we shall know the essence of a determinate force, and shall be able to distinguish it afterwards, even when it disappears together with others.

This separation becomes all the more necessary as it is the means of keeping error from our comprehension, and as only thus can we establish judgments and observations.

[p. 47]

And here are explained the great results of the method founded on experimentation.

Trials serve merely to remove the difficulties, and to present the phenomena to the intelligence in so simple a comparison that it can judge rightly, with full knowledge and without illusions.

Since trials perform an office somewhat like that rendered by mathematics, they are also in a certain way set against them....... (1)

LXV

(Jesús del Monte, March 26.)—I do not find it strange that many great thinkers have believed in innate ideas: 1°, because of the productive force they felt in themselves; 2°, because of the ease or spontaneity with which they conceived and brought forth; 3°, because of the splendor of their deductions, as happened to Descartes in Mathematics, which, being the learned sciences par excellence, as their baptismal name profoundly says, seemed to him divined. All these causes hid from them the cause in the effects, the virtuality in the phenomena.

My formula: the faculties innate, but not the ideas.

## LXVI

(April).—An argument on innateness drawn from wonder, the sure sign of novelty.

Virtualite [virtually], everything is in the understanding, that is to say, all the notions are there. How men have striven to obscure so clear a thing!

## LXVII

(May 8).—Philosophy must be the exact reflection of God! (That is understood, as far as man can reach).

(1) The original breaks off. (A. Z.)

[p. 48]

To unfold that proposition is the legitimate prolegomenon of philosophy.

Next follows the means.

Last of all the end. So, three parts.

The means is the idea, the axis on which the system must turn.

Synthesis, of course.—And perhaps I am suited to sketch one, having found myself at different periods of my life, and even simultaneously, in circumstances the most opposed and even contradictory that can be imagined.

Spirit of examination—then communion with God. Religion from my earliest years. Misfortune and religion. The latter makes one look upon the former even with enthusiasm, because, besides what it is worth in itself for bettering us, it is the first of inspirers. One comes to regret that the scourge of misfortune does not crack.

## LXVIII

(May 9)—* Those who, professing other sciences, or engaging in industry, deny the influence of philosophy upon them, are like the atheists who, living, enjoying and thinking, deny the existence of God.

What is philosophy? The last (and the first) expression of human reason. The why of the past, the how of the present, and the when of the future; for without adveniat regnum tuum [Thy kingdom come] there is no philosophy.

An ideal, a type, a soul and a target are needed in everything. Whence? Who? Whither? In the verb lies the complex expression of thought, and consequently the embryo of philosophy.

## LXIX

(Madruga, May 27.)—Philosophy is a sponge that cleans, but that can also dry up the heart; one must keep the heart’s fertility with the dew of religion.

[p. 49]

Mind, he who most adores philosophy, for whom it is a religion, cannot seek to belittle it: rather, what has been said is meant to forestall a drawback to which it may lead, though slowly.

LXX

(June 22.)—The system of compensations is eminently consoling; but more consoling is Christianity.

In the political order it may have baneful consequences, by inspiring a resignation that does not sort well with the progress of peoples.

It must be confessed that it was a philosophy that came out of the school of misfortune, and as such the daughter of a good mother.

It can also inspire good sentiments, by showing the sorrows with which pleasures are compensated, and the pains that embitter passion or crime.

## LXXI

(July 7.)—A proof of the human soul is that injuries by word are felt more (even by the most abject and material) than injuries by deed.

## LXXII

(August 22).—The prodigies that take place in the world of the intelligence!

The motives or series of ideas by which we arrive at those that seem least connected! This phenomenon is not that of the apparition of which I make mention in another aphorism. (1)

And if such things happen in a middling mind, what must it be in souls inspired by genius, or by grace! In each genius, in each saint.

Only by what happens in us can we conjecture, infer, what may be in others.

(1) The aphorism referred to is number LXII (A. Z.)

[p. 50]

## LXXIII

(1847.)—Time is the space of ideas.

There is no doubt that there exists a great relation and correlation between time and space, but not that quasi-identity in the idea that some philosophers find in it. I say quasi because nobody claims, nor can claim, that they are identical. Thus, while granting Kant that time, or the idea of it, is “a form of intuition,” I do not grant it to him with respect to space, which materially exists outside us, in bodies themselves—and also as an abstraction.—For let it be borne in mind that time is not motion, but that motion contributes to the idea of time.

## LXXIV

(January 21.)—Let life come, from wherever it may, for without life there is no philosophy.

Those diverse systems that contradict one another, or do not contradict one another as much as it seems, are a conditio sine qua non for intellectual development, and for the great synthesis. This is how it is brought about that each does what he can and what he ought.

Parallelism of synthesis in philosophy with the harmonizing tendency of civilization.

## LXXV

(February 22.)—According to Plato, nothing is desired without previous knowledge; but this must be understood thus: “at least not without the knowledge of another, of the initial Cause, of the Creator of the world”; for in truth the first, instinctive needs, which we seek to satisfy without knowing their object, and whose conformity with it we notice afterwards, show plainly the depth of the plan.

## LXXVI

(March 4.)—Being more or less fertile, more or less

[p. 51]

inclined to thought, depends very principally on the state of the organs, and consequently on the physical or moral causes that alter them.

## LXXVII

(March 5.)—* Aristotle was the substantial form of philosophy. Who more of a creator, and above all an organizer? Who gave more form to matter?

Sometimes to give form is to create; thus he created in two ways.

## LXXVIII

(March 6.)—* Philosophical genius presupposes the artistic germ. And art flashes forth the deepest philosophy, sometimes without consciousness of its work.

## LXXIX

(April 5.)—Four causes to explain a philosophical system. 1° The century.—2°   The    nation.—3°      Individual        antecedents     (education).—4° Individuality (the mental form.) This last is commonly passed over.

Otherwise, how is one to explain the different bent of two contemporaries?—Plato and Aristotle—(almost contemporaries).

So it is in the nature of man. Some poetical, others mathematical; these analytical, those synthetical, etc.

## LXXX

(May 28, 1848.)—The philosopher may grow impatient, but must not despair, in the cause of humanity.

## LXXXI

(October 15, 1850.)—On the providential order in the discoveries of the physical world, and acquisitions in the moral and intellectual: their harmony and parallelism. Profound philosophy is needed to penetrate the bearing of the facts.

[p. 52]

How Christianity escaped Tacitus, no less—and Christianity, no less! —Will not the same thing happen to Balmes with Luther’s Reformation? (1)

## LXXXII

(November 4.)—Christianity, (like every powerful synthesis, inherited from all the doctrines that preceded it; but many of the benefits it has rendered to humanity have been new and immense. New, and so new, was its moral doctrine, that it excited the most unbridled persecution in some and the most ardent enthusiasm in others.

One must be very shallow in history to fail to recognize all this, and not to take account of what it cost the human race to make the idea of the unity of God its own. But one must be shallower still in the study of the human heart to fail to recognize that even in the midst of the greatest absurdities, when there is a great vital principle in a doctrine, poor humanity is quickened and bettered, and even raised from the dead.

## LXXXIII

(February 25, 1851.)—The same doctrines do not produce the same result in different peoples. This phenomenon has not escaped good observers. Madame de Staël already notes it with respect to Germany, Tocqueville with reference to the United States, and lately Lamartine (and earlier, too, Madame de Staël) with regard to England.

I, without having them in mind, have had occasion to make the same observation in connection with the pantheism with which the modern philosophy of Germany is reproached.

The northern race never completely strips itself of its religious, reverent, metaphysical, and essentially grave and contemplative character.

From this point of view, and in the midst of the indifferentism

(1) See the note on page 35.—(A. Z.)

[p. 53]

of modern times, of religious death, to say it once and for all, which morally finished off the society that was heir to the eighteenth century, German philosophy, though Protestant in its essence and emancipating in its tendencies, has been the redeemer, the restorer of religious ideas, the one that has given them back their importance, and has rehabilitated Christendom on the rebound.

We must be just and impartial. Without this no philosophy is possible: there is not that superior judgment and tranquil mind which soars over, and surveys, the whole of things; there is no synthesis.

## LXXXIV

(February 10, 1853).—Space is for sense, time for memory.

We do not posit them by the understanding; rather they are in nature for the understanding; otherwise everything would be subjective.

## LXXXV

(August 18, 1855).—Philosophy, the morning star and compass by which to find our bearings among the works of God and of man.

Society must mold itself to Philosophy, and not Philosophy to society.

Rationalism is an idle but necessary development of Philosophy.

The atheism of Feuerbach [1] is not that of the encyclopedists of the eighteenth century.

(1) Ludwig Feuerbach, celebrated German philosopher. He was studying theology when he went to Berlin to attend Hegel’s lectures, whose ideas he embraced with enthusiasm, devoting himself ardently to propagating them. In 1830 he published his “Ideas on Death and the Immortality of the Soul,” in which he argues against the latter. His other most notable works are History of Philosophy (1833), Philosophy and Christianity (1839), The Essence of Christianity (1841), The Philosophy of the Future (1843), The Essence of Religion (1845), and a few more. In several of his works Feuerbach defends himself against the charge of being an atheist, which has been leveled both at him and at his master Hegel. (A. Z.)

[p. 54]

Science can never be formed out of the negative.

Uses of the negative, as in algebraic elimination and in Baconian exclusion.

## LXXXVI

(March 31, 1856).—Philosophy is the mysticism of strong souls.

❦

[PDF 52]

❖      ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖   ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖       ❖

T eaching and E ducation .

## LXXXVII

(Undated.) *—Let us not dwell on how one teaches, but on the spirit with which one teaches.—Seek ye first the kingdom of God, and all the rest shall be added unto you.

## LXXXVIII

(Idem.)—We propose to found a philosophical school in our country, a nursery of ideas and sentiments, and of method. A school of virtues, of thoughts and actions; not of spectators or of erudite men, but of men of action and of thinkers.

## LXXXIX

(Idem.) *—To educate is not to give a career for making a living, but to temper the soul for life.

XC

(Idem.) *—Woe to youth if it does not feel study as a religion!

XCI

(Idem.) *—The spirit of our teaching is to make ignorance felt.

[p. 56]

## XCII

(Idem.) *—Anyone can instruct; only he who is a living gospel can educate.

## XCIII

(Idem.) *—Teaching is a thorny apostolate: for there is no apostle without feeling the force of truth, and the impulse to propagate it.

## XCIV

(May 7, 1845.)—Youth is the age for travel. Neither in childhood nor in old age should one leave one’s homeland: at both these periods the warmth of the mother is needed.

The child ought not to be educated outside the country where he is to live as a man.

How many irreparable losses education on foreign soil brings! The native tongue is lost, filial love cools, every family tie is loosened, and even the holy love of country suffers most grievous detriment in the continual comparison of the habits acquired with those that must perforce be acquired.

XCV

(May 16.)—Education begins in the cradle and ends in the grave.

## XCVI

(May 21.)—Wherever we dig deep, we come in the end to the necessity of a strong religious education, in order to form men who are men.

## XCVII

(December 15.)—Man is a child who must be taught without his perceiving it;—at least without his perceiving where he is being led.

[p. 57]

## XCVIII

(September 27, 1848.)—Almost all the professions can get along without enthusiasm; that of the teacher absolutely cannot. He has need of it to inculcate doctrine and to overcome obstacles.

He must be all inspiration, priesthood, meekness, character, temperance, flexibility.

## XCIX

(June 23, 1852.)—Sacred is this ministry of teaching, and tremendous for the duties it imposes, still more on him who teaches than on him who is taught; but in any case neither can do anything without the other; they are closely bound together before God and men.

C

(August 22.)—Teaching is a contract with God, not with men.

An art par excellence, since it is all inspiration, even though it rests on experience.

❦

[PDF 55]

❖      ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖

S aint P aul and S aint A ugustine .

CI

(Madruga, May 23, 1846).—The first philosopher of Christendom, Saint Paul. The FF. the first moralists in the world, .... and the last.

CII

(October 23)—There is not a line of Saint Augustine that does not breathe penitence and praise.—Even in the matters seemingly most remote from it,

A great soul, a very great one: greater still in his humiliation than in his worldly greatness.

Nothing makes one greater than humiliation before God. For it makes us stronger against the exterior and the interior world (the passions, the leo rugiens [roaring lion] of Saint Paul).

Saint Augustine must be considered, not merely as a writer of the first order, but as one of the greatest men who have ever lived.

As a writer, he is distinguished by concision, and is without equal in

[p. 60]

the felicity of expression. He writes with the heart, like Saint Paul; that is why he makes so strong an impression.

One of the greatest marvels of Christianity is the conversion of such athletes.

❦

[PDF 57]

❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖   ❖   ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖

M usic and P oetry .

## CIII

(Undated).—The poet and the philosopher differ in this: that the latter discovers things, and the former expression.

CIV

(June 8, 1842).—He who does not feel the charms of poetry and of music may be, if you will, pure spirit or pure animal, but not that compound of soul, life and heart which we call man.

CV

(April 18, 1845).—Misfortune, the food and even the seed of genius.

All the immortal poets were as celebrated for their misfortunes as for their works: great witness the king of them all, Dante; and Camoens, Milton, Cervantes—the most original since there have been men—, Tasso, Shakespeare, Byron, etc.

CVI

(April 20).—Music is a language that goes straight to the heart, without paying the customs duty of the brain, as befalls poetry.

[p. 62]

Music disposes of us at its pleasure, inasmuch as it addresses itself essentially to feeling.

That is why it is an exception to be insensible to its influence. It forms part of our very selves; it enters into the harmony, into the play of our organs and their functions.

For that same reason it is less intellectual, but more religious, than poetry.

Both, together with all the arts, ought to join as sisters and be harmonized in order to improve man with one accord—the sole end of religion and of philosophy.

I cannot see philosophy except in the whole and in harmony, the great synthesis, the eternal da capo of analysis.

Hence, to be a philosopher it is as necessary to be a poet as a mathematician, and religious as an analyst; as much a lover of nature as of books, and above all, to “love God above all things and thy neighbor more than thyself.”

## CVII

(May 16).—Music alone has the power to make us wish to prolong melancholy sensations. How tyrannically it rules us!

## CVIII

(March 8, 1846).—At times we sing so spontaneously, unconscious of what we are doing, that it is like a physical necessity.

At other times it is a moral one, as when we are impelled by joy.

CIX

(June 29).—Inspiration is not sought: it is born within ourselves. It is a God who sweeps us along and sets us aflame, and from whom we cannot free ourselves except by marching on or burning ourselves, bringing forth what has been conceived.

[p. 00]

❖     ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖

S leep .

CX

(Undated).—Meditation partakes of sleep and of waking. It is evidently the wakefulness of the soul.

Sleep satisfies the organic functions and restores those of relation. It is the winding of the clock.

It is, for that very reason, essentially the life of the body.

That is why, while it lasts, there is no government even for the spirit, which then operates even organically or mechanically. Only thus can its phenomena be understood and explained.

“We will consult the pillow about it” means not only the recollection of reflection, but also the elaboration of ideas, which is wont to take place during sleep; for it often happens that an idea which was in embryo before it, and even without our being aware of it, unfolds in sleep, and we find ourselves with it on waking without knowing how.

CXI

(May 9, 1845).—Sleep, the image of death in appearance, is the fountain of life in reality.

Sleep is a true function, a state of activity for the organism, and not a negative state of mere repair, as it is generally considered.

[p. 64]

I incline to believe that it even has its own organ specially assigned to it.—Neither the psychologists nor the physiologists have seen all that there is in sleep.

I do not mean by the foregoing proposition that the organs do not all fall asleep gradually, but that there may be one specially destined to determine the phenomena of sleep.

## CXII

(February 11, 1847).—Sleep: the life of relation functioning after the manner of organic life.

The dependence between the senses and the spirit is very close indeed, and not separate and belated, as the psychologists feign it to be.

❦

[PDF 61]

❖      ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖    ❖      ❖   ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖

S lavery .

## CXIII

(Undated).—In the question of the Blacks, the least black thing is the Black.

## CXIV

(May, 1845).—Seeing the blindness with which men still seek to persist in the slave trade, one would say that destiny wishes to adopt the cause of the Africans, in order to avenge them with usury.

Interest is the life of modern society, and the poison that eats it away.

With regard to the slave trade, even interest is against it—that is, interest rightly understood. But men must always be superficial! Such is the lot of those they call positive: the visionaries par excellence.

CXV

(Idem).—The introduction of Blacks into Cuba is our true original sin, all the more so since the just shall pay for the sinners.

But it is just, too, that the members of society should be jointly and severally bound in that debt, since none of them is exempt from complicity.

[p. 66]

## CXVI

(Idem.)—To perceive the impurity of the air it is necessary to change one’s atmosphere.

Habit hides from us the magnitude and gravity of the evil. A providential compensation or a destructive narcotic.... which of the two?— We would answer in one way, they in another.

## CXVII

(April 5, 1847).—How slavery contaminates slaves and masters alike! In what an atmosphere we live submerged!

They understand neither my energy nor my gentleness. Such is the empire of custom that they prefer habitual ill-treatment to unwonted good treatment.—There cannot exist a man more out of harmony with this society (from the summit to the foundation), and yet I live loving them all, and even for that reason I love them the more, for there is no other remedy in human things than love. May God increase it in me, that I may exercise it, for at times it is exceedingly hard.

❦

[PDF 63]

❖     ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖      ❖   ❖   ❖   ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖

## L iberty and T yranny .

## CXVIII

(Undated.)—Tyranny is an atmosphere that does not let the heart breathe, and stifles its impulses.

Peoples who wait for the initiative of the Government in everything cannot deny their slavery. I refer the matter to Europe.

Here in America they find everything done, because they do everything themselves; that is, in America par excellence (the U. S.), for there are spots worse than in Asia and Africa. The free man is the soul of the slave, and liberty is the universal soul.

## CXIX

(April 24, 1845.)—It is the captive who appreciates liberty. Without feeling there is no motive for thought or for action.

Humanity must pass over certain steps (of thistles and thorns) to arrive at certain heights.—It was a slave who said: “homo sum, humani nihil á me alienum puto.” [I am a man; I count nothing human foreign to me]

[p. 68]

## CXX

(May 21, 1847.)—Liberty, the soul of the social body.

Liberty, the fiat of the moral world.

The only panacea for staunching and healing the wounds which it itself (its abuse, license) or other causes inflict upon society.

Absolute it must be, and this is the tendency of humanity. I want no other curb than religion and reason; including in the latter that of authority.

❦

[PDF 65]

❖    ❖     ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖     ❖     ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖

## L anguages .

## CXXI

(Undated.)—Nothing is comparable to the dignity of the Latin tongue. It was spoken by the king-people, which stamped upon it that character of grandeur, unique in the history of human speech, which not even the most perfect languages have ever been able to attain.

Born to command, this language still commands in the books of those who spoke it. It is the language of the Roman conquerors, and of the missionaries (conquerors) of the Roman Church. Trajan, the last effort of the power of Rome, carried it as far as the Euphrates: the Roman Pontiff has made it understood in the Indies, in China, in Japan.

The language of civilization, mingled with that of the Barbarians, our fathers, it was able to soften, refine and, so to speak, spiritualize their coarse idioms.

Cast your eye over a map of the world, and draw the line where this universal tongue fell silent: there lie the limits of civilization and of fraternity; beyond it you will find nothing but human kinship.

[p. 70]

## CXXII

(June 23, 1842.)—* To show off one’s language, or to let oneself be governed by language instead of governing it, is the mark of run-of-the-mill writers.

## CXXIII

(April 26, 1845.)—* Languages—the only faithful and complete history.

The clearest spring from which flows the theory of thought (of ideas.)

How many marks of ignominy would peoples wish to erase from them, if these were pointed out to them!—And mark that the favorable and the adverse are engraved upon them indelibly.

## CXXIV

(March 8, 1846.)—There are words that have died physically and live on morally or metaphorically: e.g. esplicar [to explain], which signifies desplegar [to unfold], and is applied only to the unfolding of ideas. In Italian, on the contrary, it preserves its physical life, having lost the moral one, and so spiegare is the equivalent of our esplicar.

## CXXV

(September 17.)—Consonants are the skeleton of languages, and therefore the best basis for their comparative anatomy, for their classification.

## CXXVI

(February 10, 1851.)—There is in languages, as in peoples, something organic and something adventitious, peculiar, according to their diverse character.

## CXXVII

(February 12.)—The human spirit proceeds according to its

[p. 71]

needs.
