When it had to explain more objects, or new ones, and greater clearness and consequently greater order were required for this, it took the grammatical forms of some kindred language, or of the one with which it was most in contact; or, if the need pressed hard and it did not soon find the form ready-made, it invented it, in the same way that, when it had to rise higher in the science of quantity and analyze more complicated relations, it invented the infinitesimal calculus. Thus grammatical analogy between languages is often no sign of kinship, but a proof of progress in civilization.

In like manner, without being in contact, some peoples, of the same race or of a different one, may hit upon an analogous mode of grammatical structure, even though their languages do not resemble one another in vocabulary (as happens in certain particulars with Basque and the Indo-American languages), just as necessity may have caused geometry (e.g.) to be invented in different places.

❦

[PDF 68]

❖      ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖       ❖   ❖   ❖   ❖      ❖   ❖   ❖

M orality .

## CXXVIII

(Undated.)—Even though men very often act following the voice of their interest, well or ill understood, this is not the only motive of their actions, nor the rule of their morality.

Men never measure the merit or demerit of actions by the utility they produce. Were it so, there would be a morality for each case, and the means, whatever they might be, would stand justified provided the end were attained. This is the morality of tyranny.

## CXXIX

(April 10, 1845.)—The ideal is the air that souls breathe.

Moral needs are as compelling as bodily ones.

The moralist-lawgiver must not content himself with satisfying them; he must create them.

## CXXX

(May 3.)—Evil is the atmosphere of the moral world: that is why not even the most robust constitutions escape its influence.

[p. 74]

Men repeat everything readily, and above all calumny: often without ill intention, and merely out of vulgarity.

## CXXXI

(January 31, 1846).—On what can it depend, the material, organic repugnance that I feel, as though I were being goaded, on seeing the moral part of man materialized, be it in the slightest expression?

I am so scrupulous in matters of morality, above all where children and the fair sex are concerned, that where others neither fear nor see danger, I see death or incurable disease. This is a feeling, almost an instinct; but I believe that the widest experience and the deepest science will justify and corroborate that most profound instinct.

## CXXXII

(March 8).—There are things that ought not to be spoken, nor written, nor thought, not even with a good end in view.

One runs the risk of putting out the lamp of morality if one blows too hard, or of corrupting it if one feeds it with mephitic gases.

Ah! if influential men had perpetually before them and within them the image of morality, pursuing them as it pursues me, how much humanity would gain!

## CXXXIII

(Güines—June 4, 1847).—The people tend to generalize by moralizing, and if the maxim does not occur to them, they resort to the universal treasury of proverbs. Not everything can be bread and silver.

❦

[PDF 70]

❖     ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖     ❖     ❖   ❖     ❖      ❖   ❖   ❖   ❖

R eligious R eform .

## CXXXIV

(Undated).—Protestantism has been the salvation of Catholicism, and it still goes on supplying it with a principle of life.

I hold the Reformation to have been a true retrempment [re-tempering] for Catholicism, which was very sick indeed.

## CXXXV

(February 4, 1847.)—It is great partiality to deny the influence of the Reformation on civilization.

On this occasion I wish to call thinkers to a sincere study of the influences of the various causes that have contributed to the progress of Christian Europe. In all great phenomena, whether physical or moral, there are concurrent causes that so modify the principal cause that in reality they make it another. I will call race alone as my witness. Why is the same doctrine more efficacious in

[p. 76]

the European than in the Jew?—“Quidquid recipitur ad modum recipientis recipitur” [whatever is received is received according to the mode of the receiver]—one is bound to say, in medicine as in morals.

## CXXXVI

(March 4.)—The turn (I speak of the good one) that social development has taken is not even conceivable without the Reformation: e.g. the United States.

## CXXXVII

(March 5.)—What a source of civilization, merely to have popularized the Bible, as Luther did, raising at the same time a monument to the honor of the German language!

❦

[PDF 72]

❖     ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖   ❖     ❖     ❖   ❖   ❖   ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖

## L ove , W oman

and M arriage .

## CXXXVIII

(Undated.)—Who fears not, loves not.

For love everything is surmountable and everything insurmountable.

## CXXXIX

(Idem.)—Woman seeks not fame but consideration.

She knows, she feels, being weak, that she cannot stand without a support.

Man desires to love, and woman to be loved.

In the relations of man to woman, sex is sometimes forgotten; in those of woman to man, never.

CXL

(Idem.)—Woman, be the sun of your house and the moon of the world.

## CXLI

(Idem.)—Dew from heaven upon the troubled soul—a discreet woman.

[p. 78]

A philosopher’s stone that turns all the dross of life into gold—a loving woman.

## CXLII

(Idem.)—* A difficult problem, the one that must engage woman!— Being the soul of domestic life, to appear no more than its instrument.

## CXLIII

(Idem.)—* In a young lady’s album.

Keep in the depths of your breast, as in a precious phial, the talisman with which you are to conjure all the tempests of life: your innocence.

## CXLIV

(Idem.)—* In another album.

I would have heaven itself for the surrounding air of our fair ones; but since the air they breathe is so deleterious, let us at least teach them to cleanse and purify it.

The world is a book that is read in pieces, but it must be bound, first burning some of its leaves and making fair copies of others.

## CXLV

[June 9, 1842].—* Love is a despot essentially unjust; and so it ought to be.

## CXLVI

(April 25, 1845.)—Woman, feeling; man, analysis.—Woman, passion; man, action.

Which is the superior? Neither: they are different in order to fulfil their end.

Feeling is the origin of all that is great, and of all that is petty, in woman.

[p. 79]

In one sense woman is vas infirmius, the weaker vessel, because she requires more consideration so as not to be broken.

Woman needs both to be and to seem.

To her the ministry of the interior, but with a voice (always beneficial and profitable) in that of foreign relations, which by right belongs to the man.

Is society too rigorous in demanding more of the wife than of the husband in the matter of morality? Not at all; and so it is in the order of nature, and in the interest of woman, and in the spirit of Christianity, to which she owes the rank she holds in modern civilization.

## CXLVII

(April 26).—Woman, with all the warmth of her feeling, is nevertheless more positive than man in regard to business; that is why I give her a voice in it.

Woman, who is all disinterestedness and lack of calculation, once she comes to love, is eminently calculating in one single business, which unhappily is wont to turn out a failure for her, even when crowned with success.

Man is more capable than woman of loving twice.

Man is apt to lose his head in love, even at a well-advanced age. Woman, on the contrary, is apt to be senseless by dint of calculation. All of them necessary consequences—physiological, so to speak—of the fundamental difference in their constitution.

## CXLVIII

(April 30).—* True love desires more perfections for the beloved object than for the lover.

Hence arises its partiality toward everything it observes in that object.

[p. 80]

So that legitimate love (like the spirit of party) is essentially unjust.

## CXLIX

(May 2).—* Without several sons and several daughters the family is not well constituted.

A marriage without children is not only a tree without fruit, but a soil where certain plants cannot spring up, nor those that live in it thrive in any way.

But there are never wastelands or barrenness for religious souls, in whom evil is the seed of ineffable and countless blessings.

CL

(May 15).—To esteem one another, it is necessary to be apart.

The heart, like the mind, needs isolation in order to feel and to judge.

Sweet are the tears that friendship and love cause us to shed in solitude.

CLI

(May 23.)—Love, the first pledge of purity, being the last degree of faith.

## CLII

(June 16, 1846).—A strong passion devours itself or is drowned in another.

Actions the passions ought to be called. But the name may well stand, for it is hard to correct the copybook of the universal human spirit, and they are alternately effects and causes. We, their target or their plaything.

To combat one’s own, always go at them head-on; for those of others, sometimes head-on, at other times obliquely, and most often by roundabout ways.

With the young the first means rarely proves effective;

[p. 81]

for the female sex the best is the last, curing them with that in which they are most masterly.

## CLIII

(September 6).—Woman gains for herself by being governed.

Man too needs to be governed, but in another sense. She by love, he by reason.

Woman, if she does not feel, does not suffer: if she does not feel she is nothing, she is not a woman; and if she feels she is apt to be more than a woman.

But woe to her if her feeling is not well directed! Only religion—which is reason under the form of feeling—can bring her to reason, because her reason is feeling.

## CLIV

(February 17, 1847).—Woman is so weak a being that even the most energetic in temper does not always show her intention straightforwardly, but gives to understand that she has some motive other than the end she is making for, above all if it serves as a cloak for her vanity (and here, in the cause, is another weakness). Not even in the proverbial abandon of love does she abandon herself in this respect. She moves along curves, or, like light, along straight lines that undergo reflection, refraction, diffraction, and even polarization.

There are men who proceed thus, who are weak; but these are forced to dissemble by external causes, whereas woman is so by her very nature.

Woman never dispenses with seeming; man is apt to content himself with being.

## CLV

(June 15). *—The two antipodes, love and lovelessness (that is, the complete absence of womanly love), the motives of

[p. 82]

the most energetic resolutions; although I would call the former, rather than energetic, dragged along.

Man is apt to treat love as the madman of Cervantes treated every dog he came upon:

—“This one is a hound.”

## CLVI

(December 27).—Lovelessness and resignation cannot produce; nothing negative can take the place of the positive. To produce is to progress.

Each virtue has its application. I am a worshiper of resignation; but the soul that has nothing more has died to its neighbor and even to God.

A motive of action is needed. It is necessary to love.

To love and to be resigned, there you have the life of the saints; always to love, not always to be resigned, there you have the history of heroes.

But lovelessness not only hinders producing, it gradually extinguishes even what already existed; so that it is not even the statu quo, but the vade retro [get thee behind]; it is eminently retrograde and enfeebling.

## CLVII

(May 30, 1848). *—Nothing is more contrary to impartiality than indifference.

That is why love and hatred lead us to form some exact judgments.

## CLVIII

(December 21). *—To take what she can, so as to do afterwards what she wants: there you have marriage for many women.

Society is largely to blame.

[p. 83]

## CLIX

(July 13, 1850).—Woman, even the most virtuous and rational, is never properly within reason. That is not the road by which to seek her out in order to do her good. In feeling her camp is pitched; on it she levers herself to defend herself and to offend. If one would attack her, one must needs penetrate into that field.

Whence it is inferred that a woman without beliefs is not even a woman: she is an entity midway between man and woman, because she has not the reason of the former (the essence even of the most unbelieving), and she lacks the faith so essential in woman.

CLX

(September 21).—Woman is blind in her trust as she is in her love.

## CLXI

(July 7, 1856). *—In an album.

Woman is a little bark sailing between the reefs of frivolity and exaggeration, and only the true idea of her being is the star that can bring her safe to harbor.

## CLXII

(February 15, 1857). *—Love is the raising of all our powers to the highest power.

Where there is no love, all is pain.

## CLXIII

(January 5, 1860)—In the album of B. A.

Woman!.... You are the flower of creation; but, as such, ephemeral: strive, then, to deserve rather than to demand, and you will be a flower eternally.

[PDF 79]

❖      ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖     ❖     ❖   ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖

N apoleon and W ashington .

## CLXIV

(Undated).—Great, and very great, superior to himself was Napoleon when he yielded to the arguments of Dessoles and adopted Moreau’s plan in the campaign of the Rhine, setting aside his own sublime ideas, and this despite the rivalry that reigned between them. Genius, naturally tenacious of its conceptions, renouncing them!

## CLXV

(Idem).—Napoleon: result, form, instrument and cause.

The reaper of what so many had so painfully sown, but not a mere reaper for himself, as many impassioned or superficial persons judge, but very particularly for society.

Nevertheless, his failing, his divorce from liberty, is lamentable.

[p. 86]

## CLXVI

(April 15, 1845).—Washington is one of the few saints in my calendar.

Who was greater, Washington or Napoleon?

They were different, and each answered relatively and admirably to his end, and, we might add, instrumentally.

❦

[PDF 81]

❖     ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖   ❖      ❖

P olitic s .

## CLXVII

(Undated).—To José Mª Zayas.—We are at sea; faith, and onward!....

## CLXVIII

(Idem).—In a country at war one cannot, even if one would, remain neutral.

## CLXIX

(Idem).—One must fight, destroy, even while trying to build.

## CLXX

(Idem).—Fraternity!—Synonym of love, equality and justice among men. What a trinity!

## CLXXI

[Idem]. *—We are such spoiled children that, not

[p. 88]

satisfied with having the rim of the cup sweetened, we want to come upon the lump of sugar at the bottom, forgetting that bitters are the only restoratives of the ruined tropical stomach.

## CLXXII

(Idem). *—Is progress only a universal fact, or is it perhaps a legitimate theory? Both one and the other; for the human spirit is not slow to raise to that sphere a fact of such dimensions.

Will the progress of today be the retrogression of tomorrow?

Progress is the sun around which the stock of Japheth inevitably gravitates, drawing all the others along in its orbit, and thus fulfilling the law of its polarity.

## CLXXIII

(June 7, 1842).—The difference of races is constantly forgotten in the explanation of political phenomena: that is why we have penetrated so little into the springs of human physics.

## CLXXIV

(Idem).—Why are politics and medicine, or why do they pass for, the most backward of the sciences?

—Because they complain, and the others cannot do so. (This needs developing).

## CLXXV

(April 22, 1845).—We do not live associated, but heaped together.

Men, citizens if you will, but not brothers.

Our age is not the golden age, but the age of gold.

I live only in the past and the future: could it be

[p. 89]

said with more force that “my kingdom is not of this world”?

And must we therefore despair of humanity? No, for “magnus ab íntegro seclorum nascitur ordo,” [the great order of the ages is born anew] and that dawn is already beginning to break.

One must be impatient and not impatient: the first, to ripen the fruit; the second, because it is bound to ripen.

Certain questions must be viewed in history and as matters of history.

The father burns to see his newborn child already a man, or at least a youth; but how many steps must both of them climb to reach the mark! Nature, said the shrewd old men, does nothing by leaps.

There are moments, however, when it is fitting to hasten ripeness.

## CLXXVI

(April 23).—Divine Providence! who offer us a kind of compensation by causing to spring up in the most degraded countries plants of a purity and freshness rarely seen in soils fertilized by the dew of liberty. Witnesses: Italy and Cuba: Manzoni, Pellico, Guerrazzi,—Varela, Saco, Escobedo.

## CLXXVII

(April 28).—Peoples are wont to need men, more than institutions, in order to have institutions.

And when they are needed, Providence sends them into the world.

## CLXXVIII

(Idem). *—We must proceed not only on grounds of expediency and according to the spirit of the age, but principally

[p. 90]

according to reason and conscience, even against the spirit of the age and against expediency.

So long as nations and governments do not proceed thus, they will not have gathered the fruit of true civilization.

In other words, let the distinction between the moral and the political principle be banished forever.

## CLXXIX

(April 30).—In founding a new family, to animate and foster it, we must concentrate upon it all our vital warmth.

Why have the mother countries been an exception to this law?

To say that it is because they have been stepmothers rather than mothers is a begging of the question, as the Schoolmen would say.

The true reason is that the colonies had their origin not in love but in interest. The metropolises, mistresses and not mothers.

## CLXXX

(May 5).—Man in society tends, and ought to tend, to harmonize; but he is obliged to do so only up to a certain point, for with duty, the supreme law of humanity, there is no compromise.

In our manner of life we must strive to be of the people, even when swimming in comforts. Otherwise we work our own misfortune. People in restricting our wants; people in physical labor and in sparing the moral; people in eating, walking, sleeping....; and people in always striving to excel.

When we cannot or ought not to harmonize with a society, we must withdraw from it. But even though we do not harmonize completely, we must promote the cause of truth by every means, and, if need be, sacrifice ourselves.

[p. 91]

The doctrine of sacrifice is the mother of the little that we are. Let Golgotha bear witness.

## CLXXXI

(1846).—Man as such, at once moral and corrupt, just in his intelligence and perverse in his will, must necessarily be governed; otherwise he would be at one and the same time sociable and unsociable, and society both necessary and impossible.

## CLXXXII

(Idem).—“Sempre á gli uomini sani è buoni fú men grave udíre ì mali della patria loro, che vederli; è cosa piú gloriosa reputano essere uno onorevole ribello, che uno schiavo citadino.” [To sound and good men it was always less grievous to hear of the ills of their country than to see them; and they deem it a more glorious thing to be an honorable rebel than an enslaved citizen.]—Machiavelli.

In this there is a more and a less; no doubt it is more unpleasant to see than to hear of the ills, even when distance magnifies them, and above all if they cannot be remedied; but it is not as heroic as seeing them and striving to remedy them, though it be by struggling, and as far as one’s strength will reach. Thus the case may arise in which remaining in one’s country is the more meritorious and the more profitable to it.

We speak here apart from all personal consideration, for there are circumstances in which it is necessary (and it ought to be done at once) to live outside one’s country in order to be more useful to it, without its advantage ever being set against our honor, which is bound up in it.

## CLXXXIII

[May 27].—What is most needed for the country? what for humanity?

At times the interest of the latter will be better served by beginning with satisfying that of one’s country, without egotism reigning therein, but rather universal love.

[p. 92]

## CLXXXIV

(September 7).—A carriage-driver asked a calesa-driver:—“How goes it on the Isle of Pines, the Siberia of the island of Cuba?”

Answer.—“Those they don’t kill here, they finish killing there.” (1)

## CLXXXV

[February 7, 1847]—“Pace, pace, pace!” [Peace, peace, peace!].... fra noi. [among ourselves]

## CLXXXVI

[February 20].—There are declarations that ought to be made by those who govern, even though they promise themselves no positive result, out of deference to public opinion.

These moral facts have, for politics, as much reality as physical ones.

## CLXXXVII

[November 15].—Not all wars that seem to be waged for interest, under pretext of religion, have ceased to be essentially religious and only very secondarily interested.

Our age, self-interested and unbelieving, does not know how to gauge the strength of the religious spring.

I know of a lawyer, and of a well-known physician, who were prosecuted as vagrants a few years before the one in which Luz Caballero was writing. [A. Z.]

(1) By the names carriage-driver and calesa-driver allusion is perhaps made to a stranger and to a native of the country, respectively. It should be noted that to the Isle of Pines were sent the “vagrants and idlers,” with or without previous administrative proceedings, and that this power of the Governors was often the servant of private passions, and generally a political weapon against those suspected of disaffection toward the colonial regime then existing.

[PDF 87]

❖     ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖   ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖

M anzoni .

## CLXXXVIII

(October 8, 1850).—Manzoni’s ode on the death of Napoleon was dictated by God. This may be affirmed without blasphemy. Only God and Christianity can inspire such a composition, in which all inspirations were vanquished and surpassed.

So I feel it after twenty-five years, and after having repeated, not without interruption, with astonishment and profound delight, times without number, that prodigy of composition.

This is the sure sign of genius: far from time doing it any detriment, each time the work is recalled it produces new pleasure, and inspires new ideas, I will almost say new impressions.

## CLXXXIX

(Idem).—Manzoni was the purest soul of all that have breathed the air of letters in the XIXth century, and one of the

[p. 94]

most eminently religious that ever were in the world, and the most full of love of country.

It was fitting that he through whom the divinity was to communicate its oracles should be a chosen vessel; and with so worthy an interpreter and representative of the Universal Father, that death which left humanity mute became the more edifying.

CXC

(October 12).—I share with no less than Goethe my admiration for Manzoni.

It has been said that reflection stands out in him. It is true; he has that superiority which makes him rise above the present, and on the wings of genius fly to the future. He is a burning-glass that concentrates light and heat.

His imagination is gigantic, as much for representing as for inventing.

It is a singular thing that, being so gentle and affable, he should be distinguished precisely by the boldness of his brush, and that his energy should be such that, no parallel to him being found among poets ancient and modern, it has been necessary to unearth a Roman, Tacitus.

❦

[PDF 89]

❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖

M azzini .

## CXCI

(1848) *—Mazzini: an Italian head, a Vesuvian heart, a European figure, a historic character; more republican than Cato, a Christian democrat, he comes upon the stage as the tribune worthy to lead his country, and as the apostle of present-day society.

“To preach, to struggle, to act”—there is his banner. He is the Luther of the new age: he is so in his heart and in his tongue of fire; in his faith and hope, infinite as the future. “Let democracy,” he says, “be like the Nile, which overflows upon the very lands it is destined to fertilize.” But he is not only the Luther, for he is head, heart and arm.

The introduction to “Republique et Royauté en Italie” is excellent, temperate and judicious. Mazzini would be very well fitted to write history: perspicacity, truth, dignity, philosophy, and happy formulas that represent it.

The preface of the translator (George Sand), though well written like everything of hers, is too plaintive; which does not sit well beside the manly voice of the great Italian.

[PDF 90]

❖     ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖

H istory .

## CXCII

(April 10, 1845).—The doctrine of probabilism, introduced into morality by the Jesuits, has wrought great havoc in history at large.

## CXCIII

(April 22).—There are many beautiful histories, few good ones.

Besides impartiality, which is its foundation, the most varied and even opposed gifts are required in the historian. He must be a profound statesman, a still better moralist, a most thoroughly learned scholar, a most severe logician and a keen-sighted discriminator, a knower not merely of the heart but of all hearts. He needs science and conscience in more than one sense. He must be as ardent in feeling as he is dramatic in exposition, but with his ardors tempered and his outbursts held in check by the ice and the bridle of the supreme empress: reason. More of a poet must he be than the epic poet himself; he must fuse into his narrative philosophy, criticism and poetry, without degenerating into the abstract, or the pedantic, or the fanciful; rising, finally, above all humanity.

History is the last and most transcendent expression of the literature of a people.

[PDF 91]

❖     ❖     ❖      ❖     ❖      ❖     ❖     ❖      ❖     ❖     ❖      ❖     ❖     ❖      ❖

B almes .

## CXCIV

(Undated).—An analytical talent, Balmes’s, such as there are few: and yet how unhappy he usually is in applying an example to a principle, or vice-versa. It seems a contradiction that a talent discriminating by its very essence should fall precisely into that fault. Such are the peculiarities of the human mind: and Balmes himself is scandalized, and even grows incandescent, on hearing from the lips of Schelling that there exist prejudices which are the natural offspring of the understanding; a truth that becomes trivial through being obvious, and that perhaps would not even have shocked him in the mouth of another.

Balmes, notwithstanding the superiority of his brain, cannot be impartial towards Protestant philosophy: for that the heart is needed, and his always stood prow-on against everything German. It grieves me to the soul to be just, because I love and venerate Jaime Balmes, but he could not be independent. The same thing, and for an identical reason, happens to Gioberti (1) with respect to Descartes. To be consistent, Balmes ought to attack the latter as much as Luther, for

(1) Vincenzo Gioberti, an eminently Catholic philosopher. He was born in Piedmont, but, compelled by political events, he left Italy and settled in Brussels, where from 1833 he brought out various works of philosophy: “Teoría de lo sobrenatural,” “Introducción al estudio de la filosofía,” “Crítica de las ideas de Cousin,” etc. (A. Z.)

[p. 100]

Descartes is the Luther of Philosophy, and Luther the Descartes of Religion. (1)

## CXCV

(April 27, 1846).—I should like more scientific rigor in the exposition, so that Balmes might not suffer from the same ailment that he attributes to the pseudo-philosophy.

A certain Frenchism is noticeable in the generalizations, now inexact, now excessive.

Diction at times most beautiful, at times Catalanishly defective.

He lavishes maxims too freely, and although I am no miser of them, since in philosophical matters one ought at times to aspire to the aphoristic style, nevertheless they should not be heaped up but sown.

Ingenuous and of good mettle Balmes does seem to me. (2)

## CXCVI

(April 30).—Father Balmes is not what I had been told, but, after all, he speaks European, a rare thing in Spain.

One can see that he is a disciple of the French. And who in Spain is not?—Only the redundancy is Spanish, although the French have more than enough of it. (3)

## CXCVII

(Idem)—Nor should the figures of the Reformation be belittled as much as Balmes does. Luther at the Diet of Worms is not small, and still less in the course of his life.

(1) These observations refer to Balmes’s “El Criterio.” (A. Z.)

(2) On the occasion of the work “Bienes del Clero.” (A. Z.)

(3) In 1847, in the notebook to which I refer in the note on page 25, Luz copied this aphorism, removing its last sentence and adding to it: “Nevertheless he has improved greatly, as may be seen in his ‘Filosofía fundamental y elemental.’ I have grieved with all my soul at his death; he is far superior to the French crowd of today.” (A. Z.)

[p. 101]

German (Protestant) philosophy has contributed much to reviving the importance of religious questions.

I do not say the Reformation—even the French Revolution, the bloodiest of protests, has been a germ of life for Religion.

## CXCVIII

(February 25, 1851).—From the description that Balmes gives of German Philosophy, it is not possible for the young to form an exact idea of it, nor even a remote one, and they may form one contrary to reality.

Where there are Protestants in the case, there is no counting on Balmes.

❦

[PDF 94]

❖     ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖    ❖   ❖    ❖      ❖   ❖   ❖   ❖    ❖    ❖     ❖

M iscellany .

## CXCIX

(Undated).—For everything one needs science and conscience.

CC

(Idem).—Whoever is not master of himself will be master of nothing.

CCI

(Idem).—Passion always overshoots the goal. Even reason usually stops short of reality or goes beyond it.

## CCII

(Idem).—Good and bad fortune, the two sculptors of nature for the polishing of the human material.

## CCIII

(Idem).—A natural human malady, to seek a cause for everything.

## CCIV

(Idem).—Wretched the age in which hearts dare not come out of their skins.

[p. 104]

## CCV

(Idem).—It is to deny and disown the descent of the XVIII century and that of the XVI, to attack with virulence the origin of the very fact of attacking.

If we had not passed through certain antecedents, through certain stages (ordéals)—where would we be even now?—In the XV century. One must be consistent; a most difficult thing, not to say an impossible one.

Children forget their parents. For we are enjoying the goods that they conquered, without knowing how they were won, and breathing this atmosphere as our natural air, as one enjoys health, without feeling it, or knowing what it is worth so long as it is not lost.

## CCVI

(Idem.)—Analogy is the soul of discoveries.

The investigator who is not capable of finding analogies is not capable of making inductions.

## CCVII

(Idem.)—To see the rule in the exceptions, and the exceptions (better said, applications, modifications) in the rule: there lies science.

When one would forecast the fate of modern societies from the ancient ones, one must needs remember this principle.

## CCVIII

(Idem.)—At times talent and discretion are not enough to conduct oneself well: charity and a good heart are needed, guided by sound judgment.

## CCIX

(Idem.)—Men without merit must praise themselves; who else would praise them?

[p. 105]

Fame is not begged for, but enters by the door, and usually goes out.... by the window.

## CCX

(Idem)—I marvel that others should marvel, above all the old. Happy those who have remained young!

## CCXI

(Idem.)—There is nothing more like men than men; especially those of the Yankee species: there indeed one may say “ab uno disce omnes.” [from one, learn all]

There neither is, nor can there be, a more perfect republic, I mean, a more equal one.

Even equality of intelligences exists, and very few that stand out.

Is this good or bad?—I am not now trying to judge it, but to set down the fact.

## CCXII

(Idem)*—Truth alone will invest us with the toga of manhood.

## CCXIII

(Idem.)*—Blessed are they who know the signs of the times, and follow them!

## CCXIV

(Idem.)*—Nature abhors rest: movement is as natural to souls as to bodies.

## CCXV

(Idem.)*—Is there ideality?—Then we have horizon, approximation, perfectibility, transmutation of form into substance and of substance into form, infinite heaven.... Let us bless the creator of so many marvels!

[p. 106]

## CCXVI

(Idem.)*—Present-day society, like a subterranean fire, harbors in its bowels latent forces whose manifestation is bound to leave astounded the century of steam, of electricity, and of universal suffrage.—“Res vestra, aut ego fallor, res nostra agitur.” [it is your cause, or I am mistaken, it is ours that is at stake]

## CCXVII

(Idem.)*—He who does not aspire does not respire. (1)

## CCXVIII

(Idem.)*—Reason is the man, the rest is the animal.

## CCXIX

—(Idem.)*—Once reason has been made to turn into feeling, and predominant feeling, as a rule of conduct, to the point of our rejoicing in tribulations, to use the expression of the Apostle, still and always cold reason has to judge warm reason. But so many are the parts that reason plays, notwithstanding its frailty, that it must be judge, witness, prosecutor and court, all in one piece.

## CCXX

(Idem.)*—By the mere fact of accepting a discussion, the supremacy of the tribunal of reason is already acknowledged.

(1) The present aphorism has been published many times in this form, and indeed the suspicious have chosen to see in it the revolutionary motto of Luz Caballero. The circumstance of its being very well known obliges me to reproduce it, but I must point out that I have not found it in the manuscripts I possess, and that, on the other hand, I have before me one that bears the date of September 21, 1851, and which, under the word “Ideal” by way of title, says: “If humanity does not aspire, it does not respire;” and in 1856 the “Floresta Cubana” (p. 217) published among other thoughts the following: “Humanity, if it does not aspire, does not respire, and there you see the necessity of the Ideal.”—(A. Z.)

[p. 107]

## CCXXI

(Idem.)—It is neither lawful nor needful to run one man down in order to exalt another.

## CCXXII

(Idem.)—Contrast is the soul of nature.

## CCXXIII

(Idem.)—The history of the deaf-mutes and that of the youth without childhood, Kaspar Hauser, (1) are closed books for the spiritualists and pseudo-eclectics.

## CCXIV

(Idem.)—One cannot say non plus ultra [no further] to justice. It is absolute in its essence and unlimited in its application.

Kaspar Hauser, until the age of 17, was shut up in an underground vault or dungeon, without seeing any other living being than the person who had him in his care up to that age. On an afternoon in May of the year 1828 he appeared in the town of Nuremberg, and soon became the object of the liveliest curiosity, giving rise to inquisitorial proceedings on the part of the police, without satisfactory results. From the observations made it seems established that the place where Kaspar Hauser lived for so long had a low ceiling, so that he was generally to be found seated with his legs stretched out before him, which explains how painful it was for him to stand on his feet, and how difficult it was for him to learn to walk, which at first he did by little hops. From lack of use the skin of the soles of his feet was very delicate, as was that of his hands, which he used rather clumsily. He said nothing but a few phrases learned by heart, which he repeated when questioned, and the word “ross” [horse], which he would joyfully pronounce on seeing a horse; from which, and from other indications, it was deduced that for his amusement he must have had one of wood or some other material in his prison.

Curious and interesting are the incidents of the initiation of this Adam (as ignorant as the one in Espronceda’s Diablo Mundo) into social life, whose habits, nevertheless, he was not slow to acquire, having come within a hair of dying by assassination when his civilization was already quite notable.

To concern ourselves with the subject in more detail would lengthen this note too much.— (A. Z.)

(1) The history of Kaspar Hauser was first published in German by Anselm Feuerbach, President of one of the Courts of Appeal of Bavaria, and was translated into various languages. In Boston an English version was printed in 1832, of which a second edition was made in 1833: of this I possess a copy, preceded by a portrait.

[p. 108]

## CCXXV

(Idem.)*—There is a motive force more powerful than steam and electricity: the will.

## CCXXVI

(Idem.)*—To expect the waters of interest to cease following their natural channel is usually the illusion of the good and of patriots. But to better the world those illusions are needed.

## CCXXVII

(Idem.)—There is no doubt that there was philosophy in Voltaire.....

How the world has changed!.... It takes an effort today to be impartial towards Voltaire. But one must be so even towards the Devil, on pain of damning ourselves; and even if no penalty followed, by virtue of that saying (in the style of Saint Teresa)

“I am not moved, truth, to love thee, By the heaven thou hast promised me,” out of honesty, out of the purest pleasure of conscience, out of the indispensable duty of everyone who aspires to call himself a man.

## CCXXVIII

(Idem.)—Writings are sepulchres that preserve the name and memory of each one.

## CCXXIX

(May, 1842.)—Coldness, the raw material of wickedness.

## CCXXX

(Idem.)*—Logic is the queen of the world, and yet the world neither can nor should be governed by logic.

[p. 109]

## CCXXXI

(Idem.)*—Envy does not get up on stilts (that it leaves to its kinswoman vanity) to reach or overtop the tree of merit; instead it lowers it or cuts it down: everything in it is negative.

## CCXXXII

(Idem.)—A rare fruit, wit; much scarcer, honesty.

## CCXXXIII

(Cerro, May 28.)—The exercise of thought, even though it does not turn on useful things, the best counterweight to the passions.— This is proved physiologically.

## CCXXXIV

(Idem.)—Meditation kills; the soul bleeds, stumbling against the daggers of reality, the points of comparison.

## CCXXXV

(June 2.)—More respect is owed to children than to the old.

## CCXXXVI

(June 3.)*—Just as species are born against species in the physical world, e. g. cats against mice; so social species are born against social species, e. g. the truthful against the hypocrites, and even individuals against individuals. Who lacks his natural antagonist?

## CCXXXVII

(June 4.)*—Just as nature needed time and cataclysms to develop and perfect itself, so humanity (hard it is to note it down!) must pass through the same stages for its elaboration and improvement.

[p. 110]

## CCXXXVIII

(June 5.)*—Life: to row, to row, and on the shore to drown. (1)

## CCXXXIX

(Idem.)*—An inhabitant of the earth will sooner understand a dweller in the stars than the healthy man the nervous one. Ah, profound Virgil, with your: “non ignara mali, miseris succurrere disco!” [no stranger to misfortune, I learn to succor the wretched]

And still less will the religious or enthusiastic man understand the unbeliever or the calculator.

## CCXL

(June 7.)—Humanity is a Proteus, so that to penetrate it one must (difficult!) be watching it at once on all its faces.

## CCXLI

(June 9.)—Not merely the dullness, but the wickedness and malice of men stand in the way of the divine laconism.

## CCXLII

(Idem.)*—Society today.... sootiety.

Would you penetrate society?—Go off into solitude.

Solitude is the focus that best permits vision.

## CCXLIII

(June 10.)*—Since comparisons began to be made, never has one been made like this: “The hypocrites are whited sepulchres.” It is nothing less than Jesus Christ’s.

Those of Alessandro Manzoni are in that style.

(1) How well known this aphorism is, and not its merit, moves me not to exclude it from this collection, as Luz himself would no doubt have done had he published his works.—(A. Z.)

[p. 111]

## CCXLIV

(June 13.)*—To write is to choose, and to speak is to let flow.

## CCXLV

(Paris, December 28, 1843.)—The world is governed not by logic but by inconsistency. That is why I call all consistent men exaggerated.

## CCXLVI

(December 30.)—Man is the wiser, the more grounds of induction are presented to him.

By wiser I mean more comprehensive and more practical.

By practical I do not mean what is usually called positive, but one who knows how to avail himself, in order to conduct himself well, of his store of experience.

Because there is nothing more repugnant to my eyes than the skeleton of egoism.

## CCXLVII

(Havre, June 30, 1844.)—My baggage is an image of my illness: a surplus of the superfluous and a lack of the necessary. But when is the life of man not the same?

## CCXLVIII

(Idem.)—“He who does not suffer, what does he know?”—(Solomon.)

Answer: Only he who suffers knows it.

## CCXLIX

(April 12, 1845.)—What great thinker ever failed to write?

Genius, the natural father of thought; writing, its educator.

Writing, too, is the mother of the intermediate thoughts, or links that bind together the great conceptions.

[p. 112]

## CCL

(April 13)—Why is candor usually the companion of perspicacity, when it would seem that they ought to exclude each other? Because the one is the child of the heart and the other of the understanding.

So that there may also be a certain compensation and a wider distribution of gifts; so as likewise to damp down with the veil of candor the fumes (vanity) of perspicacity.

On the contrary, or for that very reason, malice is usually the talent of mediocrity, of nullity, or of weakness.

It is a kind of forbidden weapon carried by those who have no strength of their own.

The investigation of causes, in the physical as in the moral order, and above all in the latter, makes us tolerant and compassionate; in other words, it makes us just and equitable. It is the first of all sedatives.

## CCLI

(April 14.)*—Books. There comes a time when one has more appetite for making them than for reading them.

Another comes before it, in which they are made without wishing to make them; a relief as necessary to the heart as the sigh to the breast.

When thought is very active, even when one is not making them, and without wishing to make them, facts do not distract it, neither one’s own nor other people’s.

## CCLII

—(April 19.)—Man ripens like fruit, by dint of time, of suns and.... of blows.

## CCLIII

—(April 22)—The most difficult thing in the world is to be impartial.

## CCLIV

—(April 26.)—All the phenomena of the universe, in the

[p. 113]

physical and in the moral order, constantly reproduce the image of the necessity of the cooperation of distinct principles for their production: all is marriage and nothing isolation.

## CCLV. (1)

(May 1st.)—Genius the father, and circumstances the mother, of events.

That is why not even the most extraordinary men display certain talents except in particular circumstances.

Hence, too, the necessity of opportunity, and the impossibility of making revolutions unless there exist physical or moral facts, which genius cannot create. Not even Napoleon would revolutionize the Island of Cuba in ordinary circumstances, nor even in extraordinary ones; but let them attack her property, and then the little lamb diventa leone [becomes a lion].

Ingenious men, carried away by their desire, see the opinions of their party as existing things, whereas genius begins by observing what exists outside itself, and that very thing usually inspires in it the action and the mode of action.

Either strokes of the rope, or none at all, other than the “gutta cavat lapidem.” [the drop hollows the stone]

In nothing so much as in politics are effects usually taken for causes.

## CCLVI

(May 3.)—Without interest, men usually do not examine things. With interest, they usually do not know how to examine them.

## CCLVII

(Idem.)—The word is more powerful than the cannon.

(1) Through an oversight this aphorism does not appear among those on Politics.—(A. Z.)

[p. 114]

## CCLVIII

(May 7.)—You who are good, never envy the wicked, for it always goes worse with them than with you.

The anathema is inevitable: “in the sin they carry the penance.”

Always and forever compensation exists.

## CCLIX

(May 11.)—What beauty there is in everything natural, not only in objects, but even in movements! ... The flight of the eagle, the leap of the tiger, the swimming of the duck, the pecking of the chicks.... what grace! what expression!

The habitual contemplation of nature keeps us in a continual hymn to the Creator.

## CCLX

(May 15.)—A sensitive soul is a fount of joys and of sorrows.

The latter are compensated by the former, however much the man who does not study the destiny of man may say no.

For there are sweet pleasures even in melancholy, and very gentle ones in memory, and very lively ones in the imagination.

However positive we may wish to be, we live in the past and in the future more than in the present; above all at a certain age, and most of all when certain guests called helplessness and misfortune come knocking at the door.

## CCLXI

(May 16.)*—Which is the greatest of joys?—The 1st is produced by the sight of the beloved object; the 2nd by the return of health (I speak in the moral and the physical order); the 3rd by the completion of one’s work; the 4th by the return to one’s homeland.

Paternal or maternal love is included in the first; a general’s winning of a battle is included in the third.

[p. 115]

## CCLXII

(May 18.)—To combat prejudices is almost always like kicking against the pricks; but one must keep kicking until the prick is broken.— Otherwise, how would the world move on?

## CCLXIII

(Idem.)—Think in order to act, and in order not to act, think.

## CCLXIV

(October 14.)—The obscurity of the future, the greatest present of Providence to the human soul.

And yet uncertainty is the worst of evils.

One is always pressing on toward the denouement in this drama of life.

Blessed obscurity, spur of the intelligence, and mother of science!

## CCLXV

(December 15.)—Science must promise, even if it does not fulfill.

## CCLXVI

(Idem.)—The promised land: the ideal. While man strives to reach it, he gathers knowledge, and exercises himself; he cannot perfect himself in any other way.

## CCLXVII

(1846.)—How, why, does the image of death so surprise man?— Because, though common, it is never vulgar.

Swept along by the whirlwind of life, men never think of death; such is the empire of the senses!

Nothing gives more heart than to familiarize oneself with the idea of death.

[p. 116]

I recall in this connection the motto of a coat of arms which said: “To dare to die gives life.”

## CCLXVIII

(Idem)—What seems most natural is not always the first thing to occur to the understanding. In the history of discoveries, too, the existence of the chronological order and the logical order is borne out.

Simplicity is usually the sign of progress or of perfection; generally complication comes before simplicity.

I have also observed, more than twenty-five years ago, that the human mind advances along the curve: so that the straight line is not always the shortest way.

The understanding needs to stumble upon some phenomenon which, like a torrent of light, illuminates for it the analogies (of which it had not thought) with other facts that were familiar to it; and that is how laws are discovered.

## CCLXIX

(February 26)—May not the necessity of wearing ourselves out for knowledge and virtue in order to attain them be an indication of their true excellence?

## CCLXX

(Idem)—Everything great has (must have) its spark of the artist.

The great is for fertilizing oneself, not for copying. “Imitatores.... servum pecus”! [imitators.... a servile herd]

Where there is no generation, there is neither art nor science.

## CCLXXI

(Jesús del Monte, March 26)—There is a certain acquired courage as spirited as the natural kind: that of experience; more tenacious than that of youth, as being more deeply rooted.

[p. 117]

## CCLXXII

(Idem)—We must always attempt and undertake as far as nature permits us.

By studying and struggling we shall come to know that limit.

## CCLXXIII

(Idem)—If even philosophers let themselves be carried away by appearances, how are we to reproach the multitude for judging by them?

Judging is as rapid as seeing; and it follows upon seeing as necessarily and immediately as seeing upon the opening of the eyes.

To re-judge, to reflect: there lies the rub.

## CCLXXIV

(Idem)—Feijóo wrote a discourse on the fallibility of some proverbs. I would write a volume on the profound infallibility of the greater number.

Nevertheless, Feijóo did well, above all for that meridian.

“The fool knows more in his own house than the wise man in another’s.” This one is worth an extraordinary deal.

## CCLXXV

(Idem)—However much a man may resolve not to let himself be excited, being composed as he is of so many and such varied elements, in the end some one of them that meets its contrary usually galls him.

## CCLXXVI

(Idem)—It is not only easier to criticize than to edify, but than to edify oneself.

The eagerness always to run others down is so that we ourselves may remain on top.

Swimmers out of the water, who always turn out to be afloat.

[p. 118]

## CCLXXVII

(Idem)—Pride has more disguises than hypocrisy. A volcano that does more havoc in the calm than with the eruption.

## CCLXXVIII

(Madruga, May 23)— * Childhood likes to hear history, youth to make it, old age to tell it.

Here are the three ages linked together and harmonized with one another and with the world.

Everything corresponds: the physical with the physical, the physical with the moral, and the moral with the physical. This trinity spreads itself everywhere like the unity of God.

## CCLXXIX

(Madruga, May 28)—Let persons be silent when peoples speak.

## CCLXXX

(Idem)—Those who do not study the human machine marvel when they should not, and do not marvel when they should, at certain facts in the hygienic order, resembling in this the populace watching the conjurers, which takes for a stupendous trick what is in reality a trifle, and vice-versa, the most trivial one usually seems to it the most portentous.

## CCLXXXI

(May 29)—Better to provide than to prohibit.

## CCLXXXII

(August 14)—Even the image of pain is usually attractive; that of tedium never.

But tedium is perhaps the first of pains.

[p. 119]

It is nevertheless so repellent that it drives off and frightens even the one most possessed by that idea.

It is a true act of virtue to undertake the consolation of the bored, above all when there is neither love nor fear.

## CCLXXXIII

(August 24)—The more ardor we have, the colder are those around us, or do they seem to us?

There is a little of each, for I hold that wherever one force is much displayed, the contrary force necessarily develops, and even more in the moral world than in the material.

## CCLXXXIV

(September 3)—To be complete without prolixity; energetic without affectation.

Nothing worse than triviality, though it be clear and clean as crystal.

## CCLXXXV

(September 4)—When all goes well, everything is superfluous; when badly, everything is lacking. In fair weather there is no need of pilots; in a storm a man is required.

## CCLXXXVI

(Idem)—There are souls that have no soul.

## CCLXXXVII

(Idem)—To confess one’s own fault, the greatest of greatnesses.

## CCLXXXVIII

(September 6)—Feeling, a very fickle principle, a weathervane. Reason, the only compass: good or bad, there is no other.

[p. 120]

## CCLXXXIX

(Idem)—There are creatures who stand outside humanity, who, though they have suffered, do not sympathize with their fellow sufferers.

The human heart is a mine that every day uncovers new veins. Would that the ore were always of good alloy!

## CCXC

(October 15)—The world casts me out of itself more and more.

To enter it one must be an actor, not a spectator; to know it, a spectator after having been an actor.

## CCXCI

(Idem)—There is a great deal of talking, which, faithfully translated, means: there is a great deal being spoiled in this world.

I should like to write, not a book (which is a great deal of talking), but a notebook, on the usefulness of silence; not for the use of diplomats, but for that of families.

## CCXCII

(February 7, 1847)—The power of letters is, and has been, more effective than is commonly believed.

Who made, who formulated the French revolution? The philosophers of the XVIII century.

## CCXCIII

(February 11)—Reason cold is not cool reason.

Reason cold is not reason, for the distinctive mark of reason is to see, and coldness shortens the sight and the horizon.

Cool reason is the tranquillity needed in the mind for it to be fit to fulfill its purpose.

## CCXCIV

(March 2)—Man is so a posteriori that he needs

[p. 121]

the passing of a long time in order to give to events, to men and to things, the place that is their due.

## CCXCV

(March 6)— * Suicide (I said in 1835, and long before, in 1825) proves neither courage nor cowardice: it is a true illness, like fever. Like illnesses, like one and the same illness, it proceeds from a thousand causes. Strong religious or philosophical ideas are what can best oppose this tendency, which, from what has been said, may in many cases disappear on removal of the physical causes.

Some peoples are more prone to suicide than others. The English, because they are gloomy; the Lucumís, because they are brave and lovers of their liberty.

Of course the question of right is already settled: what right can man have to take from himself what does not belong to him, and what may very soon serve other ends? And whence is right derived?

## CCXCVI

(April 3)—Everything in me was, and in my country will be.

## CCXCVII

(April 4)—There are thoughts which, as they spring up, are like taproots that would carry off the whole soil with them.

## CCXCVIII

(June)—To make oneself indifferent: an art that some lack and others have to excess; its acquisition costs noble breasts dear.

## CCXCIX

(Idem)—Time undeceives us more about ourselves than about others.

We come to believe in the maturity of our reason on seeing

[p. 122]

how our former opinions have vanished, and the sphere of the new ones grows wider.

Of necessity one century must pass judgment on the other, just as reason dictates it (or demands it?).

CCC

(June 15)—There exist generous souls who want wings not so much in order to fly themselves as in order to shelter others.

## CCCI

(August 22)—Our pulpits always deserted; our theaters full!

How can there be life without the word?

## CCCII

(Guanabacoa, September 8)—He who is surprised at everything unjust.... so much the better for him.

## CCCIII

(Cerro, November 10)—How easy it is to win the esteem of men! One barely passes across the stage like a comet across the sky, and more memory of one remains than might be believed, in spite of a long disappearance.

## CCCIV

(Idem)—When all is effort there is no life, though there is virtue. There is no life without spontaneity; and without spontaneity there is no enterprise.

## CCCV

(November 17).—He who does all that he wants is the slave, not the master, of his will.

He who does all that he wants does what he will not want.

[p. 123]

## CCCVI

(December 27).—Beneath the statue of Lafayette I would put: “Sans génie.” [without genius]

## CCCVII

(May 27, 1848).—Laugh, but examine.

The mocker always has something of the unbeliever at bottom; the more he protests his sincerity, the less you should trust him.

Yet every firm believer, too, has something of satire at bottom.

Mockery must not be confused with satire; the first is proper to the cold, the second to the warm.

## CCCVIII

(May 28).—Nature destroys in order to produce...... So says man, but she never ceases producing.

## CCCIX

(May 29).—The press is the Pope of the XIX century.

## CCCX

(June 8).—Whether liberty, whether slavery, whether quiet, whether agitation, whether a share in public affairs, whether the retirement of the domestic hearth, whether poverty and persecution, whether opulence and security, whether patronage or neglect,.... Which of these causes contributes most effectively to the development of a literature? All of them help, bringing to light all the states of the human soul. But the greatest things have always been fertilized by misfortune.

## CCCXI

(Cerro, August 15).—Where shall I find my man?—Fool! why do you seek him, if you carry him with you!

[p. 124]

## CCCXII

(August 19).—We are neither so alike that we are confounded with one another, nor so diverse that we clash.

## CCCXIII

(Idem).—What most facilitates everything is the most difficult thing: order.

## CCCXIV

(September 6).—The philosopher has need of every kind of knowledge, as data and as discipline, in order to know the secret and to improve the instrument of the human soul.

Before speaking one must feel, touch, love.

## CCCXV

(September 27).—The sciences are rivers that carry us to the unfathomable sea of the Divinity. (1)

## CCCXVI

(October 25).—What was done out of luxury, vanity, or religious idea came to be the only means of tracing the lineage of an ancient people: the Egyptian mummies.

## CCCXVII

(October 29).—Only talent knows talent.

The occasion of this reflection is laughable, for most comical is the idea that vulgar intelligences form of superior ones. They christen as a simpleton anyone who does not give them a shout or a cudgeling.

(1) In one manuscript it says: “The sciences, mighty rivers that lead to the Ocean of the Divinity.”—(A. Z.)

[p. 125]

## CCCXVIII

(December).*—More thinking is done in one day of solitude than in a hundred of society.

## CCCXIX

(January 23, 1849).—Bitter, most bitter is calumny, but more bitter still is truth, for some.

## CCCXX

(October 11, 1850).—One must be a German even to conceive, and still more to execute, a certain kind of works; and I do not restrict myself to those of pure speculation, nor to those of inexhaustible erudition, which seem to be their two strong points. I allude to those books in which it is necessary to subject an immensity of materials to a philosophical idea—or call it systematizing, which I would call scientificating— knowledge, and to enlarge it, to make men think, which is the fortissimo of the Germans. Examples of this class: the Geography of Ritter, the Cosmos of Humboldt, the occasion of this observation, the History of the Soul of Schubert, the Physiology of Burdach, the Roman History of Niebuhr, and many others.

## CCCXXI

(February 10, 1851).—There is no one who cannot master him who is too impassioned.

There is no one less at ease than he who has always had his own way.

So true is it that, to master others and to master oneself, reason must govern.

## CCCXXII

(February 11).—Yes; primitive forms are lost, and others are gained, and “multa renascentur quae jam cecidere.” [many things shall be born again that have now fallen]

[p. 126]

What! these theologians would thrust upon us a human nature that is neither human nor nature.—Apage. [begone]

## CCCXXIII

—(February 13.)—In youth thought runs in torrents; it is not possible to be so laconic, or at least we do not feel so inclined to condense, as in meditation. So it is in the nature of things: in youth the river runs, and in maturity we stop it to see what it carries.

## CCCXXIV

—(September 18.)—This centripetal force has to struggle with so many centrifugal ones!—An image of my life, which I spend pulling back those who escape along the tangent.

## CCCXXV

—(July 28, 1852.)—Faith in God and in ourselves makes us win; faith in others undoes us. Nevertheless one cannot live without faith, but one must know “où la placer.” [where to place it]

## CCCXXVI

—(Idem.)—That men should have to act under the influence of the Penal Code....!

## CCCXXVII

—(October 19, 1853.)—It grieves me to the soul to reprove, but it fills me with bitterness that those who ought to do it do not help me to fulfill my duty, which is my life.—What would I not give for these words to resound in the heart of the Cubans!

## CCCXXVIII

—(December 14, 1854.)—More strength is required to beget than to conceive.

[p. 127]

## CCCXXIX

—(April 17, 1855.)—On hearing the complaint one believes somewhat in the grievance. One must know self-love better.

## CCCXXX

—(September 18.)—Man is a compound of ⅖ animality, as much again, or more, of passion, and barely the remainder of rationality (a millionth at times.)

## CCCXXXI

—(1856.)—Man does not die when he ceases to exist, but when he ceases to love.

## CCCXXXII

—(December, 1861).*—I would rather see collapse, I do not say the institutions of men, but all the stars of the firmament, than see the sentiment of justice, that sun of the moral world, fall from the human breast.

❦

Note.—The classification of the aphorisms by subject, as well as the chronological arrangement of those belonging to each group, is exclusively my own work, for the manuscripts of Luz Caballero consist for the most part of small scraps of paper (envelopes of letters, margins of newspapers, etc.) without order or arrangement.—To mark those previously published I have taken into account those published by Don Enrique Piñeyro, Don José Ignacio Rodríguez and Don Luis Felipe Mantilla.—(A. Z.)

[p. ii]

and

R eligion .

## D iscourse (1)

D elivered in the S an C arlos S eminary at the

opening of the course in philosophy .

Happy are we, if we succeed in reconciling pro‐

found inquiries with clearness, and truth with

novelty.

David Hume.

Consider, dear friends, what feelings must have taken possession of my spirit from the moment I imposed upon myself the duty of coming to occupy the same post that was filled by my illustrious and ever esteemed master and predecessor, a man whom nature without any doubt destined to be the organ through which she was to communicate to youth her immutable laws and her profound arcana, guiding it along

The Chair of Philosophy which Luz Caballero then began to teach had been occupied by Varela until, in 1822, he departed to discharge his office as Deputy to the Cortes, Don José Antonio Saco remaining in his place as interim professor.—(A. Z.)

(1) The present discourse was printed for the first time (and, I believe, the only time to date) in the fortnightly publication which, under the title of “Brisas de Cuba,” was edited in Havana by Don Néstor Ponce de León, Don Fernando Valdés Aguirre and Don Santiago de la Huerta, in whose Volume I, corresponding to the year 1855, it may be seen (p. 361). Don José Ignacio Rodríguez supposes it delivered on the 4th of September 1824, whereas it was on the 14th: perhaps it is a printer’s error.

[p. 132]

the path of the sciences, and teaching it the maxims of Philosophy, not merely by oral lessons, but by being the first to practice them. Yes, virtuous man, receive kindly this just outpouring of my admiration and gratitude, until I may later pay you another tribute that will be more acceptable to you; deeply sensible of my insufficiency, I shall follow the road you have traced for me, I shall do everything in my power to show myself your worthy disciple, and with this object I shall not part you from my memory for an instant, whether in expounding the doctrines that are to be the target of our labors, or in studying your works, or in inspiring in my pupils that love for science and virtue which you knew how to instill by your mere presence; and here is the homage most acceptable to your modesty that my love and my gratitude and my admiration could render you.

⁂

Gentlemen: The study of philosophy is to be the object of our labors. Let us begin our lessons by lamenting the aberrations and errors of men, who in all ages have aimed their shafts at the most venerable things, without this philosophy, to which the human species owes so much, having been able to escape their biting diatribes, or those who cultivate it to escape their indignation and even their contempt. Indeed, there are many who, on hearing this sweet word philosophy pronounced, break out, as if impelled by a holy zeal, into imprecations against it, persuaded that the words philosophy and immorality are synonymous, and that this science, the foundation of morality, is what has overthrown it; but do not lend an ear to those who reason thus; they deserve rather our compassion, for their faculties are possessed by that spirit of prejudice, the irreconcilable enemy of the spirit of analysis and inquiry.

There is no lack of others, and these are the worst, who, glimpsing

[p. 133]

in spite of their prejudice the fruits that the human race reaps from the study of nature, would like to put out once and for all, leaving us in utter darkness, the brilliant lights that are to illumine our way along the thorny path of life and keep us from falling into the abyss of error as we march toward the temple of knowledge. Their endeavor is to discredit philosophy and those who profess it, never losing an opportunity to inculcate their pernicious maxims.

I ask you, with regard to such as these, to do nothing but suspend your judgment, until you yourselves, by studying the science of sciences, are able to gauge the value their principles deserve; let it never be said that you fall into that same prejudice which animates them.

There are others, finally, who are persuaded that a philosopher must be a strange, extravagant being, who departs in everything from established usages and customs, and is greatly bent on distinguishing himself from the common run of men in his thoughts and actions: but how mistaken is the idea these people form of the character of a philosopher! who should distinguish himself from those who follow error only by not following it, from those who insult him by forgiving them, from those who give themselves up to vice by sacrificing himself for virtue....

Sad, no doubt, my exordium will have seemed to you; but my intention has been to forewarn you, so that you may not let yourselves be taken by surprise by the many who are always, so to speak, hunting after our unwary candor. Take comfort, however, in contemplating the grand picture of celebrated men that I shall present to you, any one of whom suffices to eclipse the whole rabble of detractors; of those indefatigable men who have consecrated their vigils to the study of our science, heaping benefits upon their fellows and receiving as their just reward the blessings of their contemporaries and of a posterity not always ungrateful for their toils. Venerable shade of the immortal Newton, fill me with that holy fervor which animated you in the course of your profound meditations and your fruitful inquiries, impart to me a spark of inextinguishable

[p. 134]

light, so that, guided by it, I may undertake in the company of my disciples the great pilgrimage of Nature!....

Yes, companions, we shall begin by studying man, the masterpiece of his author. We shall consider his intellectual faculties, the way to cultivate and correct them, and likewise the origin of his ideas, interesting subjects which constitute what is called Ideology; we shall then pass on to study the passions and affections of the soul, the fruitful spring of the happiness or unhappiness of mortals, to which end we shall seek the aid of a science called Physiology, which explains the manner in which the important functions of the animal economy are carried out, since the relation between the physical and the moral is more intimate than is believed; passing afterwards, as a consequence of the former, to give rules for moderating these passions, or rather, for directing them well, which is what the science of man’s duties toward his author, toward others and toward himself, that is, Ethics, comes down to.

As soon as we have come to know somewhat the functions that are carried on within ourselves, we shall go out to make our excursions through the whole vast space of nature; yes, for there is nothing in the world that is not subject to the jurisdiction of Philosophy; we shall see matter in its apparent elements and in its infinite modifications; we shall not be content to observe only the things that readily present themselves on the surface of the earth, but shall go down into the deepest caverns to search out its bowels, not led by the spirit of vile greed to seek gold and silver, but to contemplate and analyze all the phenomena it presents in its bosom, it being a thousand times more precious to the philosopher to discover a new combination of the great mass of which the Universe is composed than to find the most prized treasures; we shall rise to the region of the luminaries, and observe with the aid of instruments their motions, their stations, their anomalies, and the laws to

[p. 135]

which those enormous masses are subject that in their varied courses seem to mock all law and constraint; in crossing the atmosphere we shall penetrate the nature, now well known, of that fluid which sustains our life and surrounds the planet we inhabit; light, that body so delicate and so subtle, without which the universe would be a chaos, will not be able to resist our examination: the Newtonian prism will lay bare to us the resplendent colors that compose it, showing us on the very ground we tread that enchanting arc held until the xvi th century to be the harbinger of fair weather, and today, for what it is in itself, the luxury and ornament of our atmosphere; the thunderbolt, the menacing thunder, the lightning, and even the earthquake, far from being objects of terror to us, will be objects of our entertainment, for we shall examine how those phenomena are produced in which nature displays all her forces, and, as it were, makes a show of her superhuman power, by imitating them with our artificial machines; in a word, everything that the wise author of the world has created will be the target of our labors.

From this very rapid and imperfect sketch which I have just traced for you of the science that is to occupy us, you may judge how pleasant and interesting its study will be; do not believe it strewn with thorns, nor that insuperable obstacles present themselves on the road which we are to conquer together; I will not deny that there are still dense clouds which keep us from seeing a great part of this vast field, and that there have even been philosophers, as you will presently see, who by their doctrines have contributed rather to retard the sciences than to advance them; but do not be disheartened on that account, for since the great Descartes the sun of true philosophy has shone, never, never to set: his inestimable method has been the rule of all who pay homage to wisdom, and if it is true, as you will soon find by experience, that a good method is the soul of teaching, I cannot but warmly commend the precepts of this illustrious master;

[p. 136]

he counsels us to begin by doubting everything, taking it into account that we know nothing, and, starting from the knowledge of our own existence, to proceed by degrees further into the investigation of things, always advancing as if by steps, so as to leave no gaps in our knowledge: in this simple precept is contained all that the philosopher ought to do.

Doubt produces a distrust of ourselves which strips us of all presumption, for there is no fiercer enemy of knowledge than that spirit of certainty which brings in its train the ideas of brutishness and ignorance; among us, then, entire frankness shall reign on this point; you will all be authorized to set forth your thoughts with full liberty on any matter we may treat, with the composure and good order befitting a youth eager for knowledge and persuaded that these establishments are the places destined for rehearsing the important parts which those who devote themselves to letters are to play in society.

Here also is the only means of reconciling profound inquiries with clearness, and truth with novelty. Happy I, if in spite of my insufficiency I succeed in inspiring you with an insatiable taste for the study of nature; and a thousand times happy if one day I have the glory of exclaiming, filled with joy and tenderness: “Fortune granted me to expound to the youth of Havana the doctrines of philosophy, and by showing them therein the blunders and aberrations of the human understanding, I have had frequent occasion to preach tolerance to them, and have succeeded by this means in rendering a service, however small, to humanity, by extending the worship of this Goddess of peace, tutelary Deity of mortals.” These are my prayers.

September 14, 1824.

❦

## P olemic , [1]

carried on with D on D omingo del M onte and D on F élix T anco .

I

Half a word to the author of the article

“Religious Morality,” published in the 3rd issue of the “Plantel.” (2)

“Che se la voce sua sarà molesta

Nel primo gusto, vital nutrimento

Lascerà poi quando sarà digesta.”

Dante.

[For if your voice is grievous at first taste, it will leave vital nour‐

ishment afterwards, once it is digested.]

You suppose the doctrine of sensation annihilated by virtue of the attacks made upon it by Royer-Collard, Cousin and Jouffroy, following in the footsteps of the Scottish

(1) In February of the present year (1890) I published in the daily paper of this capital “El País” a piece entitled “An Episode in the Life of Three Famous Men,” which was reprinted by the “Revista Cubana” in its April number following, and in which I recount how the break in the friendly relations between Luz Caballero and Del Monte came about, on account of an article by the latter on “Religious Morality,” published in “El Plantel,” a periodical of Don José Antonio Echeverría and Don Ramón de Palma. I did not then know who it was that, under the pseudonym of “The Subscriber to the Plantel,” contended with Luz Caballero; but the illustrious writer and well-known publicist, my distinguished friend Dr. Don Vidal Morales y Morales, has since obtained reliable information that it was Don Félix Tanco y Bosmeniel, a person of erudition and a man of letters of note, who, born in Bogotá, came to Cuba very young and lived always in Matanzas. (A. Z.)

(2) Diario de la Habana of November 25, 1838.

[p. 138]

Reid and Dugald Stewart, and of the profound German idealists. Well, I hold precisely the contrary, that is to say, that after such attacks the said doctrine is left standing.

Grave indeed is the question, and you have made it graver by seeking to link it so intimately with the all-important one of Religion. But so far neither have you given the reason for your assertion, nor have I: you spoke first, so it is for you to explain yourself at once, in order to proceed in a matter of such moment with the order, frankness and deliberation with which one who is an admirer of yours, but a greater admirer of the truth, wishes to discuss it.

Fair-play.

II.

Answer to the article of the Subscriber to the “Plantel,”

inserted in the “Aurora de Matanzas” of the 30th of November last. (1)

So the author of the article Religious Morality inserted in the 3rd issue of the Plantel does not suppose the doctrine of sensation annihilated! Let us see who is right, Mr. Article-writer, you in saying no, or I in affirming yes. From the very first words of paragraph 4 the author of the article begins to characterize his way of viewing the question, which could not be more categorical. So annihilated does he suppose it, that he “thinks it useless to repeat that such a theory places the sole and exclusive origin of our ideas, even of all the absolute ones, in the senses.” Which is as much as to say: so backward are the people of the opposite camp, that even the absolute ideas they would derive from the senses; that even alone is enough to win the case against you with costs, and you may go on keeping the score, Mr. Article-writer.

Let us go on to see how well the author of the article observes the rules of climax, so as to leave not even a shadow of doubt about his opinion. “From this is deduced (that is, from the doctrine, and

(1) Diario de la Habana of December 5, 1838.

[p. 139]

these are his words) with exact logical sequence the denial of the existence and of the immortality of the soul”...... But a doctrine which with exact logical sequence leads unfailingly to the denial of the existence and the immortality of the soul is untenable, even were it defended by the most skillful champions: therefore he who lays down as a premise that it leads in a straight line to materialism supposes the doctrine of sensation annihilated. Therefore the author of the article Religious Morality presents the doctrine of sensation not merely as annihilated, but as a poison that corrodes society in its first foundations. And let not the article-writer of Matanzas take the word annihilate in so strict a sense as to wish to give us to understand that a doctrine is not annihilated because there is still someone to come forward in its defense, for even the most lost causes find vehement defenders, it being in vain for such champions to present themselves if the cause has been lost before the tribunal of reason. You will perceive the force of these reasons only too well, Mr. Article-writer, for me to stop to detail them.

Not yet content with branding the doctrine of sensation as materialist, the author of the article taxes it with inconsistency as well; here are his words: “wherefore the Thomist theologians, who by an inexplicable contradiction with their religious principles embraced the already bastardized canons of the Peripatos, had to have recourse to the omnipotence of God in order to bring the spiritual part of man off safe.” Therefore this doctrine, if it escapes the reef of materialism, is wrecked on the shoal of inconsistency. Now confess to me in good faith whether he who maintains that a doctrine leads by exact logical sequence to materialism or to inconsistency does not take it to be annihilated.

And mark that this result is reached not just anyhow, but by an exact logical sequence: so that he who expresses himself thus must be more than amply furnished with

[p. 140]

proofs to reduce to ashes all who dare to contradict him. It is true that in the whole article the word annihilate is not to be found; but it does not follow from this that the doctrine is not supposed annihilated, which is the expression I have made use of as a formula. Thus in algebra any letter of the alphabet represents a datum of the question; and he would make himself very ridiculous who sought to deny the value the letter stood for; or, to speak within the reach of all readers, I shall say that this is the same as if one were to affirm that Antonio, for example, had not taken Juan’s life, because the record of the case did not say any such thing, but rather that he had put him to death.

But if you like, we will not fight over the word annihilate, and we will agree that the author of the article has attacked, or has spoken against, or has opposed, as you please, the system of sensation; and there his paper is, alive, and it will not let me lie, and if you press me, I will reprint it. Well, sir, I defend the system: come forth into the arena if you will, and annihilate me if it is in your power, and do not go about skirmishing, but make war plainly and squarely.
