But let us come now to the quotation from Cousin. This gentleman does indeed affirm that he does not wish to destroy sensationalism, but only to reduce it: let us see how he keeps his word. Both the spiritualist system and the sensationalist agree that the soul forms the ideas of bodies through the ministry of the senses: so that the sole point of divergence lies in this, that sensationalism attributes the origin of all ideas to sensation, whereas spiritualism derives some from that same source, and others immediately from the soul. Setting aside for now the question of the truth or falsity of these systems, I ask: can they be reconciled? What! can there be compromises in the sciences, as in the business of life! Things were not made for the sciences, but the sciences for things: nature and man present their phenomena to us, and we try

[p. 141]

to explain them; many explanations may be given, but only one will be the true one. I know well that Leibniz tried to reconcile Plato with Aristotle, but this undertaking is not feasible on the point to which we are confining ourselves. It will be said that Victor Cousin too has undertaken the same task, reconciling Locke with the Scottish and German metaphysicians. Nothing of the sort: he does nothing but combat Locke while affecting impartiality, as the present writer will demonstrate to the point of evidence; and indeed, though at times he attacks him with reason, at others, and not a few, he does not play very fair with him. It is a very old habit of Mr. Cousin—whom I venerate for his eloquence, vast learning, zeal for public instruction and other gifts—to say one thing and do another. Mind, I am not dealing with the conduct of the man but with the tactics of the writer; and, speaking without circumlocution, Mr. Cousin’s Course of philosophy is riddled with glaring contradictions. The present writer, who swears by no one’s words, and for that very reason does not aspire to have others swear by his, has devised the best, the broadest and most impartial means of opening a polemic with the ideas of that illustrious man. And what is that means? To translate his works and annotate them; this is the best way, though not the most brilliant, of weighing the pros and the cons. Months ago this translation was announced, (1) and it has not been possible to put it into execution owing to the translator’s ill health, but the discussion will shortly be opened. Shall I perchance be presumptuous in proposing to combat the doctrines of so privileged a talent? Nothing less than that; nothing is further from my principles; but when a man, however great he may be, is in error, it is given even to the meanest pygmy to convince him of it. Thus my undertaking is easy, most easy; I neither pretend to win honor by it, nor did I put my name to the announcement, nor did I indicate any circumstance by which one might come to know

(1) Luz Caballero never carried out this projected translation of the Works of Cousin, or at least I have no knowledge that he did so (A. Z.)

[p. 142]

the translator: I always leave persons aside and go straight to things; let the truth be told, and let whoever will tell it:

## “Che si la voce sua sará molesta

Nel primo gusto, vital nutrimento

Lascerá poi, quando sará digesta.” [For if your voice is grievous at first taste, it will leave vital nourishment afterwards, once it is digested.]

I said, and I reaffirm it, that the author of the article had sought to link the question of the origin of ideas with the all-important one of religion. You answer me that there is no such pretension of linking what is naturally linked, since the religious question is a consequence of the metaphysical question. I deny it, and I ground myself on this, that one can attack religion following the one system or the other, and without following either of the two, that is, without entering into the metaphysical question. I am not unaware that Cousin and his partisans wish to set the brand of irreligion on those who defend the contrary opinion. But, let us speak plainly, what does the cause of religion gain or risk because it is maintained or denied that the starting point of all our knowledge is experience or reason, or that the latter precedes the former or vice versa? Take, then, whatever course one will, there is no fear of falling into materialism, since in both systems it is held that it is always the soul that knows.

It would seem you have not read the last two articles published in the Noticioso of this city on the 7th of November and the 1st of this month by Cauto, (1) not counting others he has brought out in previous months. In them you will see the doctrine of sensationalism well understood, and I take all the more satisfaction in this citation in that those writings are the work of a student who still attends classes, and to whose arguments the author of the ar-

(1) Under the pseudonym of Cauto was concealed Don José Zacarías González del Valle. (A. Z.)

[p. 143]

ticle Religious Morality ought to have paid some attention. I do not know Mr. Cauto, but his writings have inspired in me the desire to know him and congratulate him, for though I do not believe in innate ideas, the love of truth is innate in me, and more innate still the propensity to pay homage to merit wherever I find it.

You say there is no question, neither grave nor middling.

What! And what if a father of a family, zealous for the education of his children, were to put this argument to you? In Havana there is more than one Chair (1) where that doctrine is professed; but this doctrine sows in the heart the seed of unbelief, or communicates to the understanding the bad habit of inconsistency, which is a very frail plank to save it from other shipwrecks; therefore the chair where such is taught is a chair of pestilence. What would you answer, my friend? Is the said question grave or not? And mind that I am only lifting one corner (to let it fall again) of the veil that covers the matter; and observe that I have not said that such is the author’s intention, but that this is a necessary consequence of his principles.

Finally, as I play so fair, and as the author of the article had done no more than repeat what Cousin, d’Eckstein and the whole brilliant pleiad of editors of the “Revista francesa y extrangera” cackle about so much, I asked him to enter into explanations, to see whether, having studied the matter better than I, he would convince me of the falsity of my principles. To which is added that, the author deserving much consideration from me, I did not wish, as I could have done, to attack him point-blank, marking out for him, as I shall do in due course, the contradictions and inaccuracies that paragraph 4 of his article presents. So that, for going about it so gently, I have earned myself the answer of the article-writer of Matanzas, whom I believe I have satisfied with what has been said, the case remaining open on the main point (for this is an insignificant incident) as well

(1) For barely two months Luz Caballero had been publicly teaching philosophy in the convent of San Francisco.—(A. Z.)

[p. 144]

for him as for all who may deign to attack the reasons of a poor thinker, but one who, poor or rich, always thinks with his own head and always plays fair.

Fair-play.

Havana, December 3, 1838.

III

Fair-play to the author of the article “Religious Morality,”

inserted in the third installment of the Plantel. (1)

What has happened between us is a very simple thing: you, with full knowledge of the case, and being well aware of the opinions which I have maintained on this matter both in public and in private, nevertheless judged it so important, so necessary, or at least so expedient, to introduce the philosophical question into your article, that you did not hesitate to give it a prominent place: and this in a work like the Plantel, intended very especially for the greater number of readers, for whom, in the opinion of Cousin and of every sensible man, the grave questions of Philosophy are not very suitable fare.—That the lights which it offers should be made use of the better to inculcate in the people the truths of religion, nothing more in order; but to lead them into the labyrinth of the psychological doctrines of the various schools, and without so much as entering into the foundations on which they rest, seems to me so inopportune that at the very least Horace’s non erat hic locus [this was not the place for it] fits it.

So much for you.—As for me, what did I do? Nothing more than say: “Give your grounds, sir.”

Who, then, provoked the contest, you or I? The only thing peculiar in this suit, as happens in many others, is that the answer turned into a counterclaim: nor could it be otherwise, since I give the philosophical question all the importance it deserves. In the present

(1) Diario de la Habana of December 7, 1838.

[p. 145]

circumstances above all, it is all the more vital for me in that I see a part of our interesting youth dazzled by the false brilliance of certain doctrines which tend to make human knowledge go backward. —And here is the motive that puts the pen in my hand to annotate Cousin and to answer Del Monte. Did I not impose the first task on myself more than two months ago, exposing myself to making the sacrifice of my frail health? It is plain enough, then, that I have had no design of attacking you or any particular person; I address myself to the matter and to the matter alone, leaving persons aside, as I always do.

—“That we are friends.”—So much the better for the question, for in discussing it courtesy and restraint will reign. And in truth it would be a really beautiful spectacle, and one that may be considered a triumph of civilization, to see two friends, each more zealous than the other for the good of their country (of this country, to which I for my part can never, never pay the immense debt I have contracted with her), stirring the grave questions on whose solution depend the future intellectual advances of her children, and what is more, the future improvement of their morals, without which solid foundation in vanum laboraverunt qui ædificant eam [they labor in vain who build it].

I do not understand, then, either how you fear that the publication of your ideas in El Plantel may be ascribed to ulterior and base intentions; still less that he who attacks them could lack the same loyalty, the same good faith and candor with which you set them down, your thought fixed solely on the good that they might produce for our country. I refrain from entering into commentaries. Do not doubt for an instant that those qualities and this aim are plants rooted in the breast of a man who has always conformed his philosophical conduct to that rule, golden in his humble opinion, which he proclaimed more than three years ago: “the true philosopher is modest and circumspect in deciding, accessible to listening always, and firm only in examining.” And here is the maxim which I have endeavored, with all the earnestness of my heart (which

[p. 146]

is not lukewarm), with all the conquests of my application, I will not say to engrave but to incarnate in the understandings of our dear youth, in order to secure the triumph of true philosophy among us, so as not to let it be carried off by every wind and every color of doctrine, and so that the authority of the school, a veritable Proteus, may not occupy the august throne of reason, before whose tribunal everything must appear, without even reason itself being exempted. I would wish the understanding of our young men to be like crystal, which, giving passage only to the light of truth, should be impervious to the fine dust of error.—I would wish...... victor or vanquished, to reach the haven of disillusionment.

In short, all this means that I give the utmost importance to the question; but not that I compel and press you to enter upon its examination publicly, especially since I am to do so at length in the translation of Cousin. I should be glad, yes, in the innermost depths of my soul, if we were to discuss it privately in your own dwelling, in the presence of two or more intelligent friends, or alone, as you please, accompanied in any case by a stenographer (for we have a very expert one, and he is at my disposal) to take down the reasons for and against that come into play in the course of the discussion, and afterwards offer them to the studious youth of our country as a pledge and fruit of our love for our common mother. And behold here Fair-play clasping in one and the same embrace, with all the effusion of his ardent breast—a most pure and eminently Christian embrace—the mother, the children and the friend, or, to put it better, the brother.

Fair-play.

Havana, December 6, 1.838.

[p. 147]

IV. Fair-play’s Christmas Box for Mr. Subscriber to the Plantel (in Matanzas). (1)

“Tu nihil, invita, dices faciesve,

Minerva.”

Horace.

[You will say or do nothing against Minerva’s will.]

Lord bless me, Mr. Subscriber of my soul, and with what reluctance I take up the quill to rebut your last communication to the Aurora of Matanzas of the 11th of the current month! And truly it grieves me, for in all sincerity we are both—though you more than I, you as the mover and I as the one moved—doing many bad turns at once: let us go on to count them, out of curiosity.

The first, and no slight one, the poor service you do our common friend the author of the article Religious Morality, by dragging him out into the public square very much against his will, and very much against mine, by putting me in the position of having to touch on him in order to defend myself. The second, the no less bad one of your finding yourself under the obligation of using a logic not of the best; not because you are not quite capable of using a better one, but because the cause, however much they squeeze it, gives no more than it has. The third, none too good either, and here my share comes in, that of placing me under the necessity of spending time and paper in refuting questions of whether it was said, or whether it was not said (when so much was said, and so grandly). The fourth, finally, and to pass over many more in silence, the bad, the very worst one, for the poor public, whose patience, without its having eaten or drunk of the matter, as the saying goes, is being worn out with skirmishes and trifles by which it is sought to divert its attention, so that it may not fix it on the main question, the only one that can interest it, and from which you slip away marvelously, like a guerrilla of the first water.

This reckoning done, let us pass on to other accounts; and let us go over them lightly, for there is no need to dig deep, to speak of, in order to settle them squarely.

(1) Diario de la Habana of December 22, 1838.—(A. Z.)

[p. 148]

Agreed, my dear Mr. Subscriber: I will grant you, if you please, that an author writing an article on Religious Morality does not take the doctrine of sensation to be annihilated when he says that “the happy coincidence that there appeared at the same time, in the philosophical arena of France, three minds of such fine temper and quality as those of Royer-Collard, Cousin and Jouffroy, contributed to give greater impetus to the religious movement”— chieftains, no less, of the host of soi-disant [self-styled] eclectic philosophers, that is, spiritualists pure and simple, wrestlers at close quarters, who joined the fray with the upholders of the scholastic theory of sensations, which was the reigning one then (and which now is only a reverend antique), following for this purpose the tracks of the Scots Reid and Dugald Stewart and of the profound German idealists. This, I believe, will be said (con permesso [by your leave], sir subscriber) in order to recommend the grave studies of those illustrious minds and to dispose the hearts of the young in their favor. And this is so surely the author’s meaning, that he afterwards proclaims openly that the philosophical school which they formed, that is to say, Royer-Collard, Cousin and company, “after deepening the study of the intellectual organism, proceeding in its analyses with the most reflective and minute observation of each one of the facts that constitute the act or the operation we call thought, found for it a higher and purer origin than that of mere matter: in consequence spiritualism was re-established in philosophy, and the soul having been rehabilitated by science, with the use of all its rights and exemptions, it was easy for it to claim heaven for itself once more, as its legitimate and inherited patrimony.”—Is this, or is it not, praising spiritualism to the skies and considering it triumphant? Let us now see how sensationalism fares. To hell, and with reason, the writer of the article casts it, by gratuitously confounding it with materialism, which I shall not presume to rescue from that place of pain and gnashing of teeth; but I will bring out safe and sound the doctrine of sensation

[p. 149]

and free of every stain that might contaminate it. Let us not stray, however, from the question of the moment, which comes down to investigating utrum [whether] sensationalism has come off well or badly at the hands of the author of the article. But why stop for such a demonstration, after he has put it in hell, and its antagonist in the empyrean? Let us spare the public and ourselves, then, the tedium of going on heaping up quotations upon quotations, which would begin with the inconsistent Thomist theologians, would in passing get in their dig at the people of the scalpel, would set at war Descartes and Destutt de Tracy, who are not at war, would pass with a little more regard over Locke and his disciple Condillac (always in passing, by way of narrative, not of dissertation), would come down a little harder on that good soul Jeremy Bentham, and would end by nothing less than hurling into the depths of Tartarus sensu-materialism, all of a piece, carried off in triumph by the Teutonic Mephistopheles—that is to say, gentlemen, so that the uninitiated may understand it, by Satan himself in body and soul, with all the Beelzebubian legions into the bargain.

So then, Mr. Subscriber, if he who treats sensationalism thus, after having treated spiritualism as we have seen, is still impartial with respect to the two systems, still supposes that the struggle is not over, still presumes that the doctrine of sensation has not been left on the ground, worse than trampled by its adversary, still faithfully holds the beam of Astraea’s scales level; I frankly confess that I understand neither jot nor tittle, I will not say of the present question, but not even of the meaning of the Castilian language; or else that you and I are differently constituted, since the very thing that seems to you most clearly white seems to me most clearly black. But I am beginning to fear that the public will clamor for an end to the question of annihilation; very well, let it remain buried for ever and ever, proverbially, like that saying “inquisition.... hush!......”, saying each time so ill-omened a word

[p. 150]

is mentioned: annihilation...... mum!........ mum!....

Do not think, however, that I therefore mean to excuse myself from following you step by step, or, as we might say, harrying your rear guard, although in truth it is not very agreeable to keep stumbling upon so continual a want of logic, I having been confirmed this time, as on many others, in how well founded is the common maxim that paper will bear anything; for really I did not expect from your good sense such reasonings as those I now proceed to analyze. For this I have to put heart into myself, for in truth the pen drops from one’s hand.

You say that you do not understand why a doctrine which with exact logical sequence leads unfailingly to the denial of the existence and the immortality of the soul is untenable. Why, yes sir, you know it, but you will not confess it, and so you put me in the position of having to say it. It is untenable because the existence and the immortality of the soul are facts demonstrated by science, and it seems to me that whatever is opposed to the facts cannot be upheld. “On the contrary—you say—I believe, and anyone will believe, that a doctrine which with exact logical sequence, or what is the same, with a perfect analysis, leads straight to its end, which is truth, is not merely quite tenable, but is a true doctrine, the only doctrine, even though it be not defended by able and expert champions.” This reasoning seems to me very fit for showing the pupils in class a good model of sophism, when explaining the subject to them. Such a way of reasoning supposes that one can never start from a mistaken principle, whereas whether the premise be true or not has nothing to do with exact logical sequence: supposing, for example, that I take as demonstrated the truth of this principle: “nothing is willed without first being known,” it is inferred from it with exact logical sequence that the first time a man feels thirst (e. g.) on coming into the world, it is because he had drunk before; a false idea, but a consequence

[p. 151]

that is legitimate. Do you want us to multiply examples? It is a pity to waste the time.

Thus the scholastics taught, with good grounds, in the tactics of the syllogistic form, that one might deny the consequence and grant the consequent, and vice versa; and nothing could be more in order, because the proposition called the consequent may be true and yet not be inferred from the antecedent, which is the same as saying that it is not a true consequent; or else the consequence may be inferred and be legitimate, as in the example proposed, and the consequent nevertheless be false. Therefore, even when there is exact logical sequence, or perfect analysis in the deduction, if the analysis was not exact and rigorous as regards the premises or the foundation, the whole edifice raised upon it comes down most consequentially all of it, or can only be saved through the breach of inconsequence. And then, my dearest Mr. Subscriber, will that doctrine be tenable even when able and expert champions defend it? Would you undertake to stand up for it? Medice, cura te ipsum [physician, heal thyself]. Now anyone will be able to understand (and I say anyone, because you have always understood it) that there is no contradiction in saying that by an exact logical sequence one may arrive at truth or at error, according to the starting point: and that is precisely what the author of the article Religious Morality meant when he affirmed that the doctrine of sensation led unfailingly, or by an exact logical sequence, to use your own words, to the error of materialism, and all the more an error in his view, inasmuch as further on he asserts that the soul has been rehabilitated by science. It is seen, then, luce clarius [clearer than light], that the author of the article is also of those who judge, like me, that by a necessary sequence one may run aground just as well on the reef of error. But let us go on. “No less incomprehensible or more legitimate (you say) is the second consequence you draw, when you say: that he who lays down as a premise that it leads in a straight line to materialism supposes the doctrine of sensation annihilated. The contrary

[p. 152]

is true; that is to say, that he who takes for granted as a premise that the doctrine of sensation leads in a straight line to materialism, defends and upholds the said doctrine instead of annihilating it; he says and maintains that everything is sensation because everything is matter, or material organs, and he consequently destroys or annihilates the religious dogma of the spiritual soul.” Was ever a stranger piece of reasoning seen! Let us take it point by point. The existence and the immateriality of the soul are truths demonstrated by science, as the spiritualists themselves teach, and with them the author of the article; but he who maintains that the doctrine of sensation leads directly to materialism sets this doctrine in conflict with those demonstrated truths: therefore he annihilates the said doctrine instead of upholding it, since he shows its opposition to other truths not put in doubt. If what the Subscriber affirms in the first part of his reasoning is strange, still more incomprehensible is what he asserts next. So he who says that the doctrine of sensation leads to materialism maintains that everything is sensation, because everything is matter or material organs! And you would not have me repeat to you that paper will bear anything? Do such notions deserve refutation? So we have come to make out that the author of the article Religious Morality is a dyed-in-the-wool sensationalist! and not just any sort of sensationalist, but an out-and-out materialist, for it is he who has laid down that the doctrine of sensation deduces with exact logical sequence the denial of the existence and of the immortality of the soul! Who has said that sensationalism maintains that everything is sensation? The only thing this doctrine affirms is that the starting point of all our knowledge lies in experience; without pretending for that reason, for a single instant, to deny the activity of the soul. Everything in its place, my friend: the objects offer the materials, the senses serve as vehicles, the soul is the agent that understands and wills. How can one conceive observation, or experience (which is the

[p. 153]

same thing for the purpose) without the concurrence of all those elements? And what else does sensationalism claim? I am not unaware that among the partisans of this system there are some materialists; but this does not prove that the principle of itself leads necessarily to materialism, for if one had to judge in such a way, far more numerous are the aberrations and contradictions of the various schools into which spiritualism is divided, as it is very easy to demonstrate, without its being possible to say on that account that certain systems are logically the legitimate offspring of others. But even granting you everything for argument’s sake, never, never can the consequence be legitimate that he who maintains that the doctrine of sensation leads in a straight line to materialism, upholds and defends the said doctrine instead of annihilating it: thus, from this day forward, according to this newly minted logic, he who demonstrates that a doctrine is erroneous upholds it; thus I too am going to uphold all the ravings uttered in the Madhouse of Toledo.

With what was said in my exordium, the content of your paragraphs following the one I have just examined is more than answered. So there is no stumbling block until we come to that statement “that the author of the article has not opposed the sensationalist system as such a system, but as an exclusive system;” and by the look of it you too charge it with being so. Agreed: then prove to me that exclusion, or exclusivism, and there goes the first little problem; since you do not combat the system as Cousin and the other French and German idealists expound it, but you combat it as exclusive: that is, that it is not good, for being exclusive: come on, then, let us have the proof: so that, bon gré mal gré [willy-nilly], you find yourself in the field, comrade.

I told you not to go about with skirmishes; because, it being beyond doubt that the writer of the Plantel article had given the system of sensations a thorough dressing-down, and I having come out into the field begging him to give his grounds, there was no other escape left but to give his grounds or to hold his tongue. Whatever amounts to not entering into the principal question must be called

[p. 154]

skirmishes, or, if you like, side thrusts, so that the one attacked runs in to parry them, as I am running in, much against my will, to parry yours.— As to your always playing fair, the public will decide; and as to your always thinking with your own head, the solution of the preceding problem and of some others that will be noted hereafter will offer you ample field to make it manifest. In the meantime, to another matter.

It is the simplest thing in the world to explain the mystery of that paragraph of mine to which you allude, and here is its translation, since you want it: the author of the article Religious Morality expressed himself with some dogmatism on the philosophical question: explanations were asked of him; you came out to give them, being his friend; and see how the nobility of your sentiments carried you away to the point of diverting the general attention by calling it to insignificant points: but it is plain to see that nobility of sentiment is not enough to do a good turn to friendship......... In sum, the blow has turned out for you contra producentem [against the one who struck it]; and no more of it, for heaven’s sake.

Let us go on to business of more substance. You say further on: “that each school has set forth its reasons, its proofs, in so far as proofs are possible in the greater part of metaphysical questions: in a word, each school has believed and firmly believes that it is in exclusive possession of the truth, and judges the contrary one to be in possession of a portion of error.”—Then comes Victor Cousin and says: here I have a system that is going to reconcile them all, taking from each what is true and discarding what is false (what a rational man, how impartial! exclaims unwary youth;) consequently I am in possession of the truth.—A miserable little earthworm comes out to meet him, crying: why, it is not you, but one of those two systems, that is in exclusive possession of the truth. —What resource would be left to Victor Cousin in such a case? To demonstrate the solidity of his foundations.—And to those who repeat Cousin, Cousin and more Cousin (with their customary trisagion)

[p. 155]

what else is left to them? To demonstrate the solidity of their repetition. In vain, then, will you allege “that a system, and a metaphysical system, is nothing other than opinions or hypotheses of men, who live today and die tomorrow, and that those which do not die remain all their life (and alive, too!) in doubt for the most part.” Here there is nothing but voces et preterea nihil [words and nothing more], my dear Mr. Subscriber.—For by system is understood any body of doctrines, true or false; or else a single fundamental idea that constitutes a whole doctrine; or else doctrines already examined and refined, and for that very reason held as truths, or raised to the rank of laws of nature. In this sense it is said that the astronomical system of Newton is the true one, that is, the history of the laws of nature, whereas that of Descartes is the false one, that is, a novel of his own invention. For a long time sensationalism struggled with spiritualism, the palm of victory going in the end to the former, the one—yes, the only one—that explains all the phenomena of the intelligence.—Both have now been put to the test in the crucible of examination, and the first has carried off the sanction of experience. This I say, and this I believe demonstrated to the last degree of evidence, as the public will see in the notes to the philosopher Cousin. (1) You believe the contrary; for you affirm that everything is still in doubt, that there are reasons on one side and the other.

(1) For there are two Messieurs Cousin, gentlemen; or, to put it better, there are as many as three (how we must always come out with a trinity! and it cannot be otherwise, since it is constantly to be found at the bottom of the ancient science as well as of the modern!) But raillerie à part [joking aside]: there is one consistent, circumspect Cousin, who never lets go of the sounding-line of experience in matters of education, nor will he be made to drop it from his hand by the most brilliant theories. This is the Cousin who reports on and zealously promotes the cause of education, and him I adore;—and he is the first. The second is one who promises many great and good things in philosophy, and him too I venerate; but I take him at his word. And the third, finally, is another who, forgetting his pledged word, and abandoning the track of certainty, launches himself into the space of visions—and this one I make bold to call to order, measuring him by his own yardstick, and without letting fall into a torn sack that tremendous “tolluntur in altum ut lapsu graviore ruant.” [they are raised on high only to fall with a heavier crash]

[p. 156]

—Very well, then, let us solve for the unknown, and here is the second little problem that I submit for your solution, so that you may shine in it and give us another pledge that you think with your own head. Fair-play, my friend, that is my motto—I lay my cards open to all, even though they do not do so to me.

It is all the more important to air this matter, inasmuch as from its solution it will appear that there are points in metaphysics as thoroughly demonstrated as in mathematics: a thing that Cousin and Jouffroy likewise maintain, and all who have common sense—and by this you will catch a glimpse of the possibility of coming to teach psychology from a single book as mathematics is taught, as you desire.

Whether or not there are “learned writers at the head of each school with numerous partisans”—does not matter. The truths upheld by Galileo Galilei also had so many and such fierce opponents, that, not content with raising an outcry against the ideas, they persecuted the man himself to the point of wresting from him an abjuration of his opinions; but they also wrested from him that sublime e pur si muove [and yet it moves]. Were the opinions of Galileo a system or not? Have they been buried, or have they come out triumphant from the shock of discussion and the onslaughts of time? Is this the fate of spiritualism? Its last hour came long ago. And have you not heard it strike? For the rest, that the learned follow opinions contrary to ours is a new motive for examining both the one and the other with more deliberation; for when the strong fall,—how shall the weak not fall? Therefore, and in order to adopt the true middle term, the legitimate eclecticism, I never tire of repeating with our Seneca: “non enim me cuiquam mancipavi, nullius nomen fero, multo magnorum virorum judicio credo, aliquid et meo vindico nam; illi quoque non inventa sed quærenda nobis reliquerunt.” [for I have not bound myself over to anyone, I bear no one’s name; I place much trust in the judgment of great men, yet I claim something for my own as well; for they too left us not things found out but things to be sought]

To another matter. Who doubts that by way of metaphysics one passes into the field of religion, and that metaphysics can rationally

[p. 157]

treat some questions (not all) of which theology treats? What I said was that with any one of the systems religion could be attacked or defended, just as without any of them. You do not entirely agree with me in this last part; but you set the question aside, to pass on to the one that occupies us at present. Very well indeed; but since you dissent, there you have another field in which to exercise your head and your pen to convince me of the contrary.—And I tell you from now (and do not take fright) that if the question is pressed, you will see in due time that there are more, and more shocking, heresies in your favorite spiritualism than in that much-calumniated sensationalism, so accused of irreligion; though this tactic is nothing but the bogeyman with which they would frighten us so as not to find themselves obliged to answer our irrefutable arguments: “a fructibus eorum cognoscetis eos.” [by their fruits ye shall know them]

Onward.—You reply, but you do not answer the most natural argument, or interrogatory, that I put in the mouth of the father of a family, and which you find so strange that you cannot understand it.—Really? So well do you understand it, that this very studied strangeness you display is the best proof of it. So inescapable is the consequence drawn from that argument, that I even forgo corroborating it, leaving it, as I presented it, to the judgment of the sensible public, and even of the senseless, whom you will not be able to dazzle with all your most witty sorites, that “you do not know whether in Havana there is a college, and in the college a chair, and in the chair a professor (come now, it is a pleasure to hear) and in the professor a doctrine (there is no doubt the Subscriber has the knack for it) that may plant in the heart the seed of unbelief, etc.”.... What innocence! what a delightful ignorance of what goes on in this world of Havana, so remote from the world of Matanzas! As if no one wrote from here to there, nor were there mails, nor currents, nor have we a press nor printers, nor did steamers cross and recross like our daily bread!—Well, we have been transported to the delights

[p. 158]

of the golden age!—No sir, it is rather that such is the Subscriber’s habit of setting aside (for abstraction is his forte, as befits a spiritualist of the true stamp) that he goes on setting aside everything, even the fullest knowledge he has of who the man is that hides beneath the shadow of Fair-play.—But let him have it or not have it; let him know me or not know me, it is a matter of things, not of persons.—Nor had I the slightest curiosity to know him either; but I have come to know him without wishing to, and even if I wished to set it aside, my abstractive powers do not reach so great a height as those of your worship; though I do answer for my unremembering ones, so as to remember nothing whatever of persons, but only of things, in the examination of the questions we debate.—

I have purposely refrained as much from touching on some notions that figure in your paper as from going deeper into the very points on which I have refuted you; and this because nothing more than what has been said is needed for my purpose, so as not to make myself interminable, so as not to weary the public more than we have wearied it (though I, in my own discharge, might say with the apostle: insipiens factus sum—sed tu me coegisti [I am become a fool—but thou hast compelled me]) and, last of all, so as not to weary myself more than I am already; for since I began to write, never in my life have I taken up the pen with more discouragement, and all who read the present writing will believe me when they notice the haste, and even the leaps, with which I pass from one matter to another, abandoning even what most favors me, and leaving much unsaid that would be very much to the point in some places. On one of the points not touched upon, however, let me at least be permitted, for the sake of its importance, to call attention briefly before closing my discourse. “That all the metaphysicians of the school of Locke, you flatly affirm, ended up, one after another, by burying the soul, or by turning it into tendons and nerves, and Locke himself, by a natural deduction from his system, came to say that God might well have made matter capable of thought.” To which I answer: 1º It is false that all the metaphysicians of the school of Locke,

[p. 159]

consistently or inconsistently with their system, have destroyed the existence of the soul. This is a question of fact, very easy to ascertain, very simple, of the kind you like.—2º Even those among them who have denied the existence of the soul have not done so as a consequence of their system; and here you have another little problem: for it will cost you some labor to deduce so frightful a consequence from Locke’s most innocent foundation: namely, ideas are acquired by reason through the intermediary of the senses, not being engraved beforehand on our understanding: or in other terms, “the faculties are innate, but not the ideas.”—3º Even Locke himself, in suggesting that God in His Omnipotence could make matter cogitative, departs from the spirit and the letter of his system, and he does so when, taking up the question of the nature of the soul, and remembering one of the attributes of the Divinity, he says that it would not be impossible for God to make matter cogitative. This result, then, far from being a consequence, is rather an inconsequence of his system, whose letter we have seen, and whose spirit takes as its pole star not to plunge into the immense but empty space of hypotheses.—4º Into that business of nerves and tendons I will not venture, no sir, for I do not wish to meddle in what is none of my affair, since it is not so pleasant to have one of those giants they call physiologists spring out at me from there (a terrible rabble, being mechanical, material and brawny, no less than disenchanted, unbelieving, and soulless, but not disarmed, for they never let go of the scalpel), and, pulling the reins of my runaway nag, bring down on me in a thundering voice the cudgel blow of “tractent fabrilia, fabri” [let smiths handle the smith’s tools], or follow it up with that other witticism of “hold, man, for you will yet find yourself......” and other flowers of the same tenor.—Abrenuntio! [I renounce it!]

On this occasion let me be permitted to point out that I for my part have so far made no use in these questions, nor will I ever, of a single one of the arguments employed by the physiologists and phrenologists; for they are forbidden weapons,

[p. 160]

all those of such an arsenal. I make war on spiritualism, or rather pseudo-eclecticism, on its own ground and with its own weapons: I accept combat with it wherever and however it chooses to offer it, be it in the vast field of nature, or in the narrow enclosure of the little castle in which it believes itself fortified.—I will demonstrate to it that the history of the sciences is a closed book to it; I will demonstrate to it that the history of humanity is no more open to it: I will demonstrate to it that, far from keeping its word by concluding that promised treaty of peace among the sciences, it is the one that sets them most openly at war with one another: I will demonstrate to it that it is the exclusive one, and the inconsistent one, and the systematic one that would force nature to its whims; I will demonstrate to it that it is the one that tears and mutilates man, and the science of man: I will demonstrate to it that it is the one that tries to lead us away from the sure path, the only one granted to frail mortals for finding the truth—life-giving observation—seeking to make it succumb to undigested erudition.—“Homo naturæ minister ac interpres tantum facit et intelligit, quantum de naturæ ordine re vel mente observaverit; nec amplius scit, aut potest:” [man, the servant and interpreter of nature, does and understands only so much as he has observed of the order of nature, in fact or in thought; beyond this he neither knows anything nor can do anything] man neither knows more, nor can do more.—I will demonstrate to it that without sensation there can be neither responsibility nor conscience.—I will demonstrate to it that sensation is the universal bond that binds all humanity together.—I will demonstrate to it that the holy religion, most pure, divine by dint of being human, and human by dint of being divine, the religion of the son of Mary, the sublime religion of the Gospel (of that book unique of its kind, as fit for the wise and prudent as for little ones and the ignorant;) that religion, yes, is an inaccessible mountain for the so-called spiritualists, if they would be consistent with their principles: I will demonstrate to them that by way of nature we go straight to its creator: I will demonstrate to them that the cultivators of nature must necessarily and eminently be religious.—I will demonstrate to them that philosophy is in the heart more than on the lips.—I will cry out to them with the

[p. 161]

apostle, rebuking the unbelief of the gentiles: “Because that which may be known of God is manifest to them, for God hath manifested it unto them; for the invisible things of him are seen from the creation of the world, being considered through the works that are made (visible), even his eternal power and divinity; so that they are inexcusable” in their unbelief. Let the pseudo-eclectics cease, then, from coming with the bugbear of materialism to frighten us away from investigation.—Let them cease from setting science at war with religion, to the loss and detriment of both.—Let them open once for all to humanity, to all humanity, wide open, the doors of the tabernacle, which they keep shut against it with stone and mortar.—This is what the spirit of the most humane law of the Crucified demands; this is what humanity demands, this is what reason demands, and for this cries out the most humble but strenuous voice of one who wishes in everything and through everything to live up to the shadow under which he hides, for certain considerations, and not for any fear of the light; a feeble voice, but heartened by the breath breathed into it by love of this beloved country, by love of its most tender and engaging offspring, whom he seeks to preserve from the contagion that threatens their freshness and mental vigor; such is the grave mission of one who believes he has found the truth:—the truth, daughter of heaven, whose magic power he cannot resist who feels it and adores it in the sanctuary of his breast, with the vehemence and ardor with which he idolizes it.

Fair-play.

NOTE.—The circumstance that these Works are being published in parts allows me to correct here an error contained in the note on page 142, for in it I said that under the pseudonym of Cauto Don José Zacarías González del Valle was concealed, whereas the one used by the latter was that of Tulio, and the former concealed Don José Francisco Fúnes, a man of clear talent and vast learning, much esteemed by Don José de la Luz, of whom he was a great friend. On the death of Fúnes, Luz wrote his epitaph, which I shall publish further on.—(A. Z.)

[PDF 151]

❖     ❖     ❖     ❖      ❖     ❖     ❖     ❖        ❖        ❖        ❖       ❖        ❖        ❖      ❖

P hilosophy .

Question of Method.

Whether the study of Physics should or should not precede that of

Logic.

P olemic with “R umilio ” and “E l D ómine ” of P uerto -P ríncipe

I.

Answer. (1)

“Itaque huc res redit, ut organum nostrum, eti‐

am si fuerit absolutum, absque historia naturali

non multum; historia naturalis absque organo,

non parum instaurationem scientiarum sit

provectura. Quare omnino et ante omnia in hoc

incumbere satius et consultius visum est.”—

Bacon .

[And so the matter comes to this: that our organon, even if it

were perfected, would without natural history advance the in‐

stauration of the sciences not much; natural history without the

organon, not a little. Wherefore it has seemed better and wiser,

above all and before all, to apply ourselves to this.]

It is my intention to answer the article inserted in the Gaceta of Puerto- Príncipe of the twenty-third of May last, against the opinion that the study of Physics should precede that of Logic. To carry this out as is fitting, it will be well to set forth at some length the foundations on which that precedence (not preference) rests, refuting in passing some of the notions that figure most prominently in the course of the said writing. This exposition becomes all the more necessary, inasmuch as the publication there of a Preliminary Note in which were set forth, albeit succinctly, the motives that prompted

(1) Published in no. 35, Volume VI of the Memorias of the Patriotic Society of Havana, 1838, and reprinted in the Revista de Cuba.—(A. Z.)

[p. 164]

the proposal of such a reform, far from winning it a greater number of partisans, has drawn upon it an antagonist who strives with all his might to oppose it. But let us first relate the history of the case, so that the reader may judge with full knowledge of the matter.

When the chairs of Philosophy were being established in the Havana schools named San Fernando and San Cristóbal, by virtue of a Royal order dated October 1833, by which the Directors of those establishments were empowered to present a set of regulations, setting forth whatever occurred to them on the subject, we seized the happy opportunity—Don Narciso Piñeyro, director of the first, and the present writer, who was director of the second (for it is not possible to keep the anonymity I should wish, for the sake of the question itself)—to propose various improvements and reforms, among them the precedence of the study of Physics over that of Logic, contrary to the practice generally established on our soil. By good fortune it was provided in the same Sovereign disposition that all our observations should be submitted to the examination of the enlightened patrician Don Francisco de Arango y Parreño, as Director General of studies in the Island, so that on his report alone the government of the capital might proceed to the provisional approval of our regulations and proposals. The circumstance of having to be judged by so competent a voice, together with the ease of being able to discuss verbally with the said commissioner whatever objections might occur to him, were motives that weighed effectively—above all as we had so many points to touch upon—in our adopting an exceedingly laconic style, proper to the case, but not at all suited to the reading public, which, being a compound of so many and such varied elements, made it necessary to enter into fuller explanations in order to make it feel the reasons so briefly set out in our report. But as these, brief as they were, were nonetheless perceptible and fairly well linked together, as they had so strongly interested not only Mr. Arango (who in such expressive terms

[p. 165]

expressed himself in his report) but also some individuals who until then had professed themselves partisans of the old plan; and above all, as it was proposed to discuss the psychological matters on which the question turned in no less a setting than the public acts of Philosophy of the college of Carraguao; (1) there was no longer any objection to giving the public, at the head of the Syllabus of 1835, some mere indications that had not at first been destined for it. But far from any voice being raised, either in the press or in the examinations, against the innovation introduced, it was generally received with applause, not merely by ardent and novelty-loving youth, but very particularly by the most mature and sensible men, by the men who consult first the book they carry within them, their own experience, rather than the books and opinions of others. Some of the doctrines set down in my Syllabus must doubtless have found an echo in Puerto-Príncipe as well, after three years, whether from conviction or from a spirit of novelty I do not know (which it is not to the purpose to inquire), a public professor who has just established an Institute of education coming forward on the stage to uphold them (nor do I know whether well or ill). On this account it came to my notice, some two months ago, that communications for and against were crossing on the question in the Gaceta of Puerto-Príncipe; and, curious to see how it was being aired, and not without much pleasure that such points should be aired by the studious men of Camagüey (for, as a Cuban, I am as much a son of Puerto-Príncipe as of Havana), and finally, and perhaps more than all, out of my decided love for science and for the progress of public instruction, I asked a very intimate friend (2) residing in that city to send me all the communications: he answered that he had been unable to obtain some, and that others offered nothing of note, ending by asking me to send him whatever I had

(1) The College of S. Cristóbal was generally known as that of Carraguao, from its being situated in the district so named. (A. Z.)

(2) He refers to Don Gaspar Betancourt y Cisneros, E l L ugareño .—(A. Z.)

[p. 166]

written on the subject, and, should that not suffice to clear up the question, to draw up an article or memoir to fill this gap. My answer was to send him by return of post the Syllabus containing the Preliminary Note published there, as the only thing I had printed on the matter, and as all that my notorious old ailments, kept up if not fostered by the hot season, allowed me to send him for the moment. My friend and correspondent did in fact publish only the Preliminary Note, which was what was most to the point; and behold what has given occasion to the communication in the Gaceta of the 23rd of May which I propose to answer: a communication which perhaps would not have seen the light, at least in the terms in which it is conceived, had its author also read the Syllabus to which the said Preliminary Note served as frontispiece: so I am led to believe by the complete good faith in which I hold the writer of the article, and the sincere desire I seem to discover in him to seek only the truth. But it was not my correspondent’s fault that the Syllabus was not published: the fault was mine, for, bent on narrowing the question, and averse to presenting myself ultra petita [beyond what was asked], I indicated to him that he should do so only with the aforesaid Preliminary Note. I now see that it is necessary, by virtue of the communication of the 23rd of May, to proceed as soon as possible to the publication of that reasoned index, which, offering more material for meditation, will present the question on a broader and more proper ground. (1)

The question may be treated in two words or in two thousand, for it involves one of the fundamental questions of Philosophy: that of method; I shall be neither so short nor so long: not so short, so as not to expose myself to returning to the charge; not so long, because neither does my health permit it, nor does the compass of a communication allow it. And so, without further preamble, let us enter upon the matter.

To begin with Physics, or in general with the natural sciences

(1) The Syllabus having been repeatedly published in this city, the author judges its reprinting here unnecessary, but very advisable (for the question) in Puerto-Príncipe, where it seems not to have circulated.

[p. 167]

is to begin at the beginning: man naturally feels himself carried away to the contemplation of external objects by the countless sensations with which they assail all his senses: thus he must perforce be a naturalist before he is an ideologist: he must begin with what is outside before what is inside; or rather, he cannot know his interior except precisely by virtue of the knowledge of the exterior. The same thing happens to him with respect to the knowledge of his creator: “cæli enarrant gloriam Dei, et opera manuum ejus anuntiat firmamentum.” [the heavens declare the glory of God, and the firmament showeth his handiwork] Hence, because of its importance, it becomes necessary to supply this knowledge from a tender age by means of faith, and this is the sublime work of Religion. To require, then, that the study of Physics precede that of Ideology or Psychology (for without them there is no true Logic) is nothing more than to provide the pupil with the continuation of studies to which he is already well accustomed; it is to proceed in a manner more in keeping with the precepts of analysis, which enjoin us to go from the known to the unknown. But not in this alone does my plan conform to the rules of good analysis: these also recommend that we proceed from the easy to the difficult. And what comparison can there be between the difficulty the natural sciences may present, and the thorns one stumbles upon at every step in the psychological or ideological sciences, (1) and the clouds that so often envelop almost all their questions? What, then, can be the reason why the intellectual sciences are, so to speak, still in swaddling clothes, at the end of so many centuries in which the most illustrious geniuses have undertaken their study with all the vigor of their capacity, from Plato and Aristotle to Kant, Fichte and Cousin; while the natural sciences have made truly astonishing progress, to the point that some of them have been created before our very

(1) I say psychological and ideological indiscriminately, so as to accommodate myself to all systems, and because it is a distinction of no interest whatever to the present question, which is confined solely to discussing the said precedence.

[p. 168]

eyes, witness Chemistry, Crystallography, Comparative Anatomy, Geology, etc.? Nor let it be claimed for an instant that such miracles have been due exclusively to the respective ease of the subjects, without method having had an effective influence. Not to extend ourselves too far in working so rich a vein, I shall give by way of entire answer that the greatest endeavor of the modern ideologists and psychologists has consisted in introducing into the intellectual and moral sciences the same method that has made the natural sciences advance so far: in a word, they have tried to convert the former into sciences of observation and, if possible, of experiment. Even in legislation and in politics we have admirable examples of the application of so productive a method: or rather, of the eminently philosophical method, of the only method that in all rigor can be called scientific. What else is Comte’s famous work on Legislation but the reiterated application to moral, economic and political matters of the method of observation? Who does not discover on every page of his book an understanding steeped in the spirit and procedure of the experimental sciences? (1) As long as the moral sciences, called speculative by antonomasia, did not go beyond mere speculations, they remained without attaining the rank of true sciences: the system of the observation of facts is adopted, and at this fiat the science is forthwith created. So thoroughly are all investigators penetrated with the superiority of this method, that there is no branch of human knowledge to which they do not try to apply it, and always with the best success. Let the works now being undertaken everywhere on the statistics of prisons, of crimes, of insanity, etc., also serve as an example. Formerly one began the examination of

(1) As much may be said of Smith and Say in Political Economy, of Gioia in Statistics, of Quetelet in Social Physics, and above all of the recent work of the physician Parent du Chatelet, which is a model of investigation.

[p. 169]

these matters with some general principles, and at most with the opinions generally established among those who had written about them: today, on the contrary, one begins by studying the facts as they are in themselves—and behold observation; or by placing men or things in the circumstances in which those facts are produced—and behold the experiments of the moral sciences: in short, formerly it was sometimes necessary to suppose the facts; now it is indispensable to study them. “Here as in any branch, as everywhere, as always, I declare myself for that method which places the starting point of all sound Philosophy in the study of nature, and consequently in observation, and which then appeals to induction and to reasoning in order to draw from observation all the consequences it involves.”—Thus speaks no less than the foremost light of Philosophy in France today: there is no need to name Victor Cousin, the present leader of the spiritualist school. If we turn our eyes to the sensationalist school, we shall find the testimony of the celebrated Destutt Tracy, an author well known to Mr. Rumilio: this ideologist, then, treating of the sciences best suited to impart good habits to the understanding, gives the preference to the natural sciences, and among them to Chemistry and Physiology, even over mathematics, contrary to the opinion of those who maintain the latter to be the best Logic imaginable. In effect, the natural sciences offer a multitude of facts, and this exercises the memory; but the facts are similar and varied, and this exercises attention and sagacity; and as these sciences are distinguished by the advantage of being able to resort to experiment in case of doubt, at every step they offer repeated lessons in how easy it is to go astray even when following analogies with the greatest care and performing experiments with the utmost scrupulousness. For this reason Tracy does not hesitate to give them the preference even over mathematics (understood as a method, for the science of quantity is an almost universal instrument, above all for Physics), for the latter, although

[p. 170]

they attain an exactness that the others seldom approach, and that depends on their very nature, present no occasions for stumbling, and so do not inspire that natural mistrust or fear of going astray which characterizes the veteran in the experimental field. Consider the nature of the mathematical sciences, and we shall be convinced that they all come down to a series of abstractions starting from a few data: so that with these alone it is possible for the human understanding, as happened with Blaise Pascal, to create the whole science: an undertaking altogether impracticable with respect to the other sciences, in which one cannot take a step without treading upon facts. Besides this (and it is another characteristic prerogative of the natural sciences) the understanding finds itself as it were forced by the facts to draw consequences and to establish a theory, whose truth it can confirm or refute by experiment, thus opening a field vaster than even the most creative genius could imagine. Who could have told Volta—and Volta is a name that may be placed beside the Galileos and Newtons—of the prodigies of discovery that were so soon to be realized with his admirable apparatus? and even today, with all that we see it accomplishing, who will dare to predict how far it may carry us? What exactness of eye, what depth in investigation, what measure in judging, what sagacity in discovering, what facility in the choice of means and resources must not a mind acquire that has constantly to keep an exact and minute account of the slightest circumstance that may influence the phenomenon, on pain of laboring in vain, or at least of being very far from reality! How can he fail to be thorough and fertile in resources who must be constantly taking note of the temperature, of the humidity, of the height of the air, of its density, of distance, of attraction, of magnetism, of light, and of a million other circumstances in each phenomenon he observes? Not in vain does the understanding of men devoted to this kind of study acquire a certain

[p. 171]

special robustness, a robustness which, other circumstances being equal, is not usually found even in some profound mathematicians. (1) And behold here one of the phenomena that most drew the attention of the ideologist cited, and in trying to account for which he left us a new and precious lesson for Ideology, one most singularly applicable to the question before us.

From what has been said so far the following consequences spring: first: it is more natural to begin with Physics because of the object with which it deals; second: it is so also because of its greater ease; and on this head I would not have my feeble voice alone be heard, but once again the very energetic and authoritative voice of Victor Cousin: (2) a citation all the more welcome to me, inasmuch as the writer of the article awaits the verdict of the great masters before resolving to take a side. By it he will see that we are dealing not merely with difficult and ticklish matters, but precisely with the most arduous and thorny that the field of speculation can offer; in a word, we are dealing with the most backward sciences, with sciences that are yet to be created, or at most are in the making: for it should be noted that I do not call logic, nor does it deserve the name, a collection of rules taken on credit without due analysis, many of which go no further than a do it well, sir, and you have done it well, as our illustrious Varela used to say, as wittily as expressively. The point is that Logic should be, not the beginning of all the sciences, but itself a daughter, an immediate consequence, of two other exceedingly difficult sciences, namely Ideology, or better Psychology, and

(1) I do not mean by this that Mathematics do not also exercise certain mental faculties in another way: but this question would carry us too far at present.

(2) It would be easy for me to heap up in my favor citations from German, English, Scottish and Italian philosophers, and even others from France itself; but I have taken special care to refer to the writers generally known on our soil. I regret, however, not transcribing even one text of Jouffroy, which seems as apt to the question as Cousin himself.

[p. 172]

Physiology (the latter at least as an auxiliary science, even in the opinion of the spiritualists.) But let us hear Cousin out, for I still have many reflections left which will not come amiss afterwards either. In the “Examination of the classification of philosophical questions” he expresses himself in these terms.... “moreover, to begin with the primitive (and note that he is still speaking of what has some appearance of deserving primacy) is to begin with one of the most obscure and embarrassing problems, without light or guide, whereas by beginning with the actual we begin with the least difficult, with what serves as introduction to all the rest. Experience, the experimental method, are applauded everywhere as the triumph of the century and the genius of our age; the experimental method in Psychology will be to begin with the actual, to exhaust it if possible, in order to institute a severe examination of all the principles that now govern the intelligence; only those will be admitted that really present themselves; yet none will be rejected; none will be asked whence it comes or whither it goes; it exists, that is enough; it has a place in nature, therefore it must have one in science.”

And in another place (I shall go on extracting, and in faith I regret it, and even interrupting the text, the passages being too long)—“Now then: in spite of the difficulties this science presents..... (Psychology).... it is not beyond the reach of man..... All the facts of consciousness are evident as soon as consciousness perceives them; but at times they escape its reach by reason of their extreme delicacy, or of the adventitious circumstances that surround them; Psychology gives the most complete certainty, but this certainty we find only in those recesses which not all eyes can penetrate; to reach them it is necessary to abstract ourselves from this visible and extended world in which we have so long dwelt, and with whose colors all our thoughts and words are tinged; we must abstract ourselves from this exterior world which

[p. 173]

is far more difficult to exclude.. Finally, after having gained a secure seat in the world of consciousness, so delicate and so slippery, we must institute a broad and deep review of all the phenomena it comprises, for here the phenomena are the elements of the science.... When this preliminary labor has put us in possession of them all, it remains to construct the science by coordinating them in such a manner that they may be represented in the various classes to which they belong by their characteristic differences, just as the naturalist perceives his plants or his minerals in a certain number of divisions that comprehend them all. This done, much still remains to be done; the greatest difficulties have not yet been overcome.” But enough of extracts for now, and let us continue with our consequences.

Third.—It is likewise to be inferred that the intellectual sciences, and particularly Logic, which, as I said in my Preliminary Note, comes to be the theory of theories, need the data offered by the other sciences, in order to deduce from those very data precepts for the direction of the human mind. How is it possible, without having an idea of the nature and procedures of the science of quantity and of the natural sciences, to give a vote on their course and on the applications of their methods? And what! are not these data, and even the theories derived from them, facts with respect to Ideology, whose object is to give an account of all the phenomena of the understanding? Not without good reason did Aristotle himself say that there are two kinds of knowledge, one mediate, the other immediate, and that the second is necessary for the first to be possible. And so, as long as the facts of the other sciences offered no materials and, so to speak, occasions of investigation for the intellectual phenomena, Logic could not but remain stationary. Did I not fear to write a book, or did I not believe that what has been alleged and what I have still to allege suffices to rectify the ideas of the writer of the article, I would gladly pause to run through

[p. 174]

one by one the chapters of Logic that owe their existence, either directly or occasionally, to the study of the physical and mathematical sciences. (1) No weak proofs of my assertion are those very observations, already cited, which Tracy makes in the work entitled Principios Lógicos, on the comparative study of mathematics and the natural sciences for imparting good habits to human reason. What more! does not Tracy himself treat of Logic after having occupied himself with Ideology and general Grammar, and does he not consider even these last as a consequence of Physiology, as he states in his dedication to the celebrated Cabanis? But who that has read that author will not have noticed at every moment that he addresses his discourse to pupils already initiated in the principles of Physics and Mathematics? and this I do not affirm by inference, for expressly, in repeated places, and from the first lines of the first volume, he alludes to the knowledge they acquired in the previous course of those sciences. Before him Condillac, in his Course of Studies, had explained several fundamental branches of Physics and Mathematics prior to the treatise on Logic; and even had he not done so, one notices at every step in that work examples and even doctrines deduced from Algebra, Geometry, Mechanics and other mathematical and experimental branches. At about the same time the most illustrious Almeyda was publishing in Portugal his Recreaciones Filosóficas, devoting six entire volumes to the exposition of the phenomena of the Universe before undertaking the study of ideas, and adducing for this reasons analogous to those we are setting forth. But who has told the writer of the article that my proposal is an absolute novelty? It is in fact one only in relation to our soil. Nowadays, if one says anywhere in Europe

(1) And the facts offered by the history of mankind? What better commentary on the question of the manner of acquiring ideas than the history of the young German Kaspar Hauser?: of that youth without a childhood of whom the newspapers have spoken so much? But we should have to go very far if this matter were touched upon.

[p. 175]

Philosophy or philosophical studies, no one understands by these denominations anything but the purely intellectual sciences; the natural and mathematical sciences being excluded, not because they fail to form part of Philosophy, but because the former are studied separately, and after the latter, which, rather than the beginning, come to be the crown of all the rest. Thus in all secondary establishments, such as colleges, normal schools and institutes—many of them founded by professors of the intellectual sciences or by their disciples—the branches of the natural and mathematical sciences are taught before Ideology; as may be seen in all the programs of studies, not excluding that of the University of Paris, and those of the universities of Germany and England. There is more: in several preparatory establishments in which the natural sciences are taught, it has been deliberately decided not to establish chairs for Philosophy properly so called, since it is considered too arduous and thorny a subject for young beginners. (1)

And what shall we say if it is a question of teaching Philosophy according to the historical order of the exposition of systems, as has long been the practice throughout Germany, in Scotland, and today even in France itself? Then indeed, to undertake the task, it is required not only to have exercised oneself on the firm ground of nature, but to go provided with all the weapons of Philology and criticism. And is this study suitable for a beginning? Let men of good faith answer. But let us also appeal to the light of our own experience in confirmation of the experience of others. How many times, even before I had meditated so deliberately on these matters, how many times did there come

(1) Let it not be thought that I restrict myself to the preparatory schools of arts and trades, for as regards these the exclusion is understood as a matter of course. I speak of preparatory institutes for literary careers. If the writer of the article still desires weighty authorities in order to make up his mind, there he has Bacon’s table of the sciences, in which (and note that this novelty is three centuries old!) Logic occupies the penultimate place! (in the order of time, be it understood, which is the question.)

[p. 176]

to me the most advanced pupils to declare to me the greater ease with which they understood physical subjects, and this in the case of some who had a predilection for the Ideological ones! How many other times did they come to tell me that they had understood, after other studies and now with maturity, those very points that at the outset had resisted all their efforts! How many and how many times, in short, have I seen myself forced to snatch from the hands of the most diligent pupils those very philosophical works which, once well prepared, they have been able to read with much profit; whereas for the moment all was confusion and muddle to their feeble understanding! Let us always follow the steps of nature if we wish to know her best and soonest. Another very notable fact has been offered me by the establishment of the explanatory method in primary instruction, a method that produces the promptest and ripest fruits, by presenting to children in their reading text passages taken from the natural sciences. This is the true secret for bringing into play all the mental activity of these tender little creatures, not by indiscreetly giving them indigestion, but by offering them the food best adapted to their nascent capacity. And what more and what better Logic than this incessant and animated exercise of all the mental powers, necessarily excited by the influence of objects, and on firm and not slippery ground, as befits those who are beginning to walk; obliged to pass from facts to abstractions, and then from abstractions to facts? What more can be required to strengthen human reason and equip it for new and more arduous conquests? (1) And

(1) Were I not harassed by the incessant fear of becoming interminable, I would copy here part of an article on the explanatory system which I published in the Diario de la Habana at the end of 1832; in it Mr. Rumilio would see how, from a few facts taken from the simple History of the Camel, I go raising children as by steps to the most sublime considerations on the plans of nature and the attributes of its Maker.—This is the chain of induction: here lies the whole secret of Verulam.

[p. 177]

behold how insensibly we have come to end up at the great reform undertaken by the illustrious Bacon of Verulam: “Natura enim (he says in his scientifically metaphorical style) percutit intellectum radio directo; Deus autem propter medium inequale (creaturas scilicet) radio refracto: homo vero sibi ipsi exhibitus radio reflexo.” [For nature strikes the understanding with a direct ray; God, on account of an unequal medium (namely, creatures), with a refracted ray; but man, shown to himself, with a reflected ray.] And will Mr. Rumilio still believe that the sublime principle of the Nosce te ipsum [know thyself] can be put into practice before knowing the rest of the globe? The lights of all the human sciences must rain down in torrents upon the most privileged understanding before a single step is taken in what is the first of studies in the order of importance, but the last in the order of time and difficulty. To mark off the phenomena of instinct from those of intelligence: to examine the causes that may alter those phenomena, or, what is the same, to mark the influence of ages, of climates, of temperaments, of diseases; to know man in health and in sickness (the chapter on mental alienation alone is an episode which, with respect to the auxiliary knowledge it requires, becomes another principal subject); to compare the intelligence of man with that of the animals that most nearly approach him; to collate the instinctive and intellectual phenomena that depend on the action of the nervous system with those of consciousness and reason; to study the history of Philosophy in order to learn the successes and aberrations of the understanding and to glimpse their causes; to draw the dividing line between the sphere of organization and that of education; to mark the precise point, if it be possible, at which the influence of these two causes stops and moral liberty begins.... along all this long and rough road one must perforce pass before reaching the longed-for goal. Physiology—and whoever says this says Physics, Natural History, Comparative Anatomy, Medicine, Mathematics (for it is necessary to note the course of the human mind in all its branches)—Psychology and, of course, Ideology, Grammar, Logic; and whoever speaks thus already includes all the resources of Criticism and Philology, and above all and

[p. 178]

for all, a reason exceedingly fortified and masterly in the exercise of investigation; in a word, for the study of man a whole man is needed, and more than man: the whole of nature.

It will now be understood without effort why I said in my “Preliminary Note” that to begin with Ideology was to begin with abstractions; without its being possible for any sensible person to imagine that one can proceed in any science without abstraction, the first instrument of analysis. To this clarification, obvious enough as it is, I am obliged by the author of the communication himself. When a subject is said to be abstract, what is meant is not precisely that it is so to the exclusion of others, but that it is so in a greater degree: thus we call Mathematics abstract sciences in contrast with the physical sciences; not because there are no abstractions in the latter, but because they are fewer in number than in the former, which all turn upon abstract ideas.

It is also to be inferred from what has been said that, far from lowering the merit of Ideology and the other intellectual sciences, I have on the contrary tried to bring it about that teaching proceed by a more rigorously scientific method, as being a more rigorously natural one: the question is not one of sympathy or antipathy for this or that branch of human knowledge, for if the case were to be decided by predilections, perhaps—or without perhaps—the needle of my balance would incline toward philosophical studies: it is solely a matter of method, of preparing pupils so that they may come to be good logicians, and above all profound thinkers; for, besides a common bond that binds all knowledge together, as the orator of Rome said, each kind has its own place, in the order of theory as in that of application.
