Who can deny the importance of Logic, or rather of philosophical studies? But not a Logic of mere rules taken on credit, or on the word of the master, but a Logic founded on the spirit of observation:

[p. 179]

this is the only means of avoiding those deluding definitions which, together with sound science, the writer of the article condemns, and which produce only null or superficial knowledge.

But what idea has the author of the communication formed of Logic? He believes without doubt, as is seen from his words, that it is a kind of instrument, or universal key, with which all the doors of human knowledge are opened. If that is so, how are we to explain the rapid and sure progress of the physical and mathematical sciences, while the intellectual ones have scarcely begun to grow after so many centuries of cultivation by the rarest minds? Does the writer of the article think that a Newton, or a Galileo, or a Cuvier owed—I will not say their discoveries, but not even their successes, or their non-aberrations—to the previous study of what we call Logic? After all, all these great men were born when knowledge was already somewhat advanced in every branch.—But what shall we say of the discoveries of Kepler and Copernicus? And what of those of Pythagoras and of Thales of Miletus, who flourished even before Plato and Aristotle, the first among the Greeks to undertake the work of founding Psychology and Logic? Everything plainly indicates that the true instrument the human mind employs is abstraction, on the foundation of observation; and in this sense there can be no science whatever without Logic; that is, one cannot take a firm step without deducing, without reasoning in connected sequence, and that is why it has always been said that such an author writes with Logic, or with more Logic than such another: that is, that he knows how to deduce his reasonings from the facts and to link them closely together. Hence, if in all the branches of human knowledge it is necessary for man to abstract—for otherwise he would cease to be man—it follows inevitably that in all branches there is Logic; but this has not been a Logic applied after being learned in general and as a separate preliminary, but a Logic that is the legitimate daughter of nature

[p. 180]

and alternately daughter and necessary mother of all science. (1)

In this sense I will say that man was not first a logician and then a physicist, but that he cannot be a physicist, nor even a man, without reasoning or abstracting. And since he can undertake no study without abstraction, let us choose, to begin with, one of those branches with which he is most familiar, and which may be regarded as the most natural continuation of his primitive observations. Founded on this consideration we should likewise propose for the first steps the study of languages, for it is a matter about which the child has been gathering data since he began to stammer: the language before the particular grammar; and the languages before general grammar: (2) first Physics and Natural History, and afterwards Psychology and Logic. Behold precisely what is practiced in the most practical and the most speculative nation of Europe in didactic matters: there is no need to say that Germany is meant: Languages, Physics, Mathematics first, because that is what is most attractive, and what boys know most about. But it is not in this way that the debate on the precedence of Physics over Logic is to be understood: for if it is a question of natural Logic, or the Logic inherent in every science—or rather, of the use of our mental faculties—there is no question; and the writer of the article will permit me to tell him frankly that the paragraph with which he closes his piece is in open conflict with all the principles laid down in the course of it. The question is whether Logic, as a science whose object is to set forth the phenomena and theory of the intelligence

(1) What a splendid sample of this Logic of the experimental sciences is offered by John Herschel’s famous introduction to the study of natural Philosophy!—If unfortunately my discourse should not succeed in rectifying the ideas of the writer of the article (and even if it should succeed), I shall advise him to read this precious book, that he may admire the point to which induction can attain in the physical sciences.

(2) This does not prevent whoever writes a particular Grammar for beginners from availing himself of the lights of general grammar: or whoever does so on any science whatever from availing himself of the lights of the others; on the contrary, method will gain thereby; but the question concerns the order in which the pupils are to learn, and not what the masters ought to know.

[p. 181]

should or should not be studied before Physics. If the real or actual doubts did not turn on this point so considered, the writer of the article may rest assured that I would not have taken up my pen. I cannot see, then, how, in a tone of triumph, he proclaims at the close that, however much it may grieve the partisans of the new plan, they must confess that man was a logician before he was a physicist. No, I shall always say: first comes observing, then deducing: first receiving impressions, then reflecting them: first being a child, then a man: first growing, then ripening: first walking, then explaining the gait: for the endeavor to study the intellectual sciences first seems to me just like that of one who should insist that the child wait to walk until he had been taught the doctrine of motion. Let him not, from this day forward, unseal his lips either, until he has learned all the parts of speech by heart to perfection.

I have purposely omitted to corroborate another of the reasons alleged in my Preliminary Note in favor of the new plan, both because the writer of the article has not touched upon it and because it is not fundamental, but rather consequential, in the question before us. Nevertheless, its importance for the reform of public studies is so great that I cannot but devote a few brief moments to it.

By requiring of the students of Philosophy the previous study of Physics, those being educated for the law are obliged to follow the course in its entirety; in which two great aims were in view: the one, to instill in aspirants to the study of jurisprudence a taste for branches of knowledge which they commonly lack, and which in practice they very often need. It would be no paradox to affirm that the lawyer needs the science of facts as much as, or more than, that of law: he needs, more than any other cultivator of the sciences, to lay in a store of every kind of knowledge, theoretical as well as practical, for occasion for all of them will arise in the immense field of human transactions. How many points connected with forensic Chemistry, with

[p. 182]

legal Medicine, with Natural History, with Surveying, are not argued daily in the courts, whose solution depends almost exclusively on the knowledge of these subjects! At least let the judge and the lawyer possess some criterion for forming a judgment about them. It is not the science of law that our lawyers most lack, for of that they commonly have more than enough; but I am mistaken: without the other sciences they will know the legal provisions by heart; they will be empirics or pettifoggers, never jurisprudents and jurisconsults. This is a subject that by itself would demand to be treated, not thus incidentally, but quite ex professo. The second transcendental aim was to raise somewhat our prostrate studies. If young men grew accustomed to waiting a little longer before passing on to other branches, there would be calmed to a certain point (not altogether, alas! for other causes operate here) that anxiety to obtain certificates, to see time fly, to gain a sheet of paper, but not a line of knowledge. Let us undeceive ourselves: the true secret for reforming studies is to give them the necessary time and rigor: without time, subjects are neither gone through nor matured: for there is an immense distance between reading and studying, between sticking things in the memory, if I may be permitted the expression, and appropriating or assimilating, as one might say, the knowledge acquired: without rigor in examinations there are no masters, nor pupils—that is to say, there are no studies; and this is a point on which the most experienced practical men of all cultivated nations are agreed. It is truly admirable, in the regulations of studies of Germany, Europe’s teacher in the matter of methods, to see not precisely the requisites and formalities customary among us in these matters (for we turn everything into formulas), but the variety and order of knowledge required of candidates, and the wise measures directed to ensuring success. Such well-judged precepts France, civilized France, could not but copy in their entirety for her own use, erecting them into the law of the matter—“Multi sunt vocati

[p. 183]

pauci vero electi” [many are called, but few are chosen] So it must needs be in justice, and so public morality imperiously demands; for the question of studies is the question of morals: even the most important question of knowledge descends a degree in comparison with that of morals. But fortunately for poor humanity the interests of virtue are intimately bound up with those of science. All are summoned to the feast, but the profaners must be cast out of the sanctuary. An ignorant man in a learned profession either becomes corrupt or perishes! What commentaries does the bar of the Island not offer on this point, which I regret with all my soul to treat thus incidentally! And so: freedom to study as much as one pleases, freedom to enter everywhere and at all hours to search museums and libraries, freedom to communicate with all; freedom in everything, except freedom to be ignorant. But another important point calls us, and after all this is no more than a digression, which, on account of the gravity of the subject and its connection with the main one, I hope my readers will pardon.

It will perhaps be asked how, the reasons that plead in favor of the precedence of Physics over Ideology in the plan of studies being so evident, teaching has nevertheless for the space of so many centuries followed the contrary method: what has been the origin of such a practice? for even the most extravagant prejudice involves a philosophical explanation; nothing is in vain, nothing without its why, and once this is found, the investigator’s understanding remains, as it were, more convinced and satisfied. I shall try to explain the phenomenon with all possible brevity.

One cannot speak of the origin of Philosophy and of philosophical questions without the eyes of the understanding turning toward Greece. The Greeks, those men so apt for the study of the sciences and the arts, nevertheless went astray in their first investigations. These fell, as was natural, first of all upon natural phenomena; and let the writer of the article recall in passing that Thales of Miletus, Pythagoras, and before them their masters the

[p. 184]

Egyptians, and after them the great Aristotle, began their studies with the inquiry into natural phenomena. Precisely what led them astray was their departing from the path of observation and experience on the one hand, and on the other the itch to apply mathematical principles (not mathematics as an instrument) to the nature of things. To the first cause are due the errors of Thales of Miletus in Physics; to the second those of Pythagoras, who, justly dazzled by the prodigious results he himself had attained in the science of quantity, sought to force nature into them. Thus the science of Physics came to end either in a veritable divination or in a mathematical apparatus as ridiculous as it was gratuitous. In this state of things the great Socrates appears, and, seeing the total neglect in which the science of duties lay, and even feeling the contagion of the principles of that erroneous Physics, he drew the choicest youth of his country to the study of morals by counsel and example. The wretched situation in which the physical sciences found themselves, the excellence of Socrates’ new method and the importance of morals were all causes quite effective enough to bring into discredit the study of sciences essentially experimental. It is not strange, then, that the study of these branches began to be looked upon with disdain and as unworthy of men who were agitating the great vital questions of morals and of her daughter, politics: let us not forget the fate of Socrates: Socrates, even more than the sage, was the Apostle and the Martyr of his country. Then came an extraordinary genius, the first disciple of Socrates, Plato, to dig deeper the foundations of morals that his master had laid, having for this purpose to raise the edifice of ideas. Even Aristotle himself, despite having begun to cultivate the physical sciences, and having cultivated them with such success for his time, also contributed effectively to drawing attention to the intellectual sciences, not only by attempting to found them upon Psychology, but by making the first essay of a Logic

[p. 185]

properly so called. Then came Christianity, which had so considerable an influence in spiritualizing the human race, and which, viewed as a philosophical system, may be considered the highest power to which the system of Plato could be raised. Then scholasticism entered to revive, or rather to cause to degenerate, the doctrines of Aristotle: the work of scholasticism was to substitute form for substance, and not to give form to substance, as the Stagirite had proposed: it was to banish things and enthrone words. The philosophers having arrived at this point, how was science to exist? How was it to be believed that it existed in the observation of nature? So it is not strange that they came to the point of considering Theology and Jurisprudence as the faculties par excellence—though Theology is indeed so by the sublimity of its object—giving forthwith a distinguished place, as the most suitable instrument, as the indispensable weapon for literary contests, to Dialectic, not precisely to Logic, and to Metaphysics, not precisely to Psychology; the aim was to vanquish, not to convince. Scarcely was a corner granted to the natural sciences in the artificial scaffolding of scholasticism; traces of what it was are still to be noted; doctors in Philosophy are still called masters of arts. And in truth little was lost by beginning with Logic or with Physics; for all was a thicket of frivolous little words and distinctions, or a confused chaos of metaphysical speculations. (1) Until the great Verulam came into the world to cut down with his mighty axe so tangled a thicket, and with his refulgent torch to light the road that always leads straight to truth. From that time

(1) Nor should it be forgotten, as another cause of discredit for the natural sciences in the Middle Ages, that the little experimental knowledge then possessed had fallen into the hands of people such as the alchemists, magicians, and others of that stamp.— I am left with the regret that, for brevity’s sake, I have treated this subject as if by leaps; but let the reader note that I would rather have walked naturally than leapt: the limits of this paper permit me nothing else.

[p. 186]

to this date the true advances in all branches of human knowledge: without my pretending for an instant that the ancients did not advance in the sciences as in the arts (for in these, who can come near the Greeks?); the only thing deduced from what has been said is that they did not advance as far as with their genius they might have attained, and that there was even a real retrogression, for there is a great distance between the founders of Greek Philosophy and the champions of scholasticism, in whose hands even the good seed that Greek science carried within it degenerated. After Bacon the sciences began to be studied not only by the proper method, but with the proper separation, all the more necessary as the harvest grew more abundant. Thus everywhere new chairs were founded and the old ones reformed, the Descarteses, the Galileos, the Newtons, the Leibnizes appearing, and the branches of knowledge dividing and subdividing more and more in the hands of their various cultivators, to the point we see today throughout Europe. Spain, however, did not feel so soon the salutary effects of so fundamental a reform: the tree of scholasticism was too deeply rooted in her soil, for various special causes that would take long to tell, for it to be an easy undertaking to extirpate it; but at last the overflow of that torrent reached her too; not the weakest proof of this, among others, being—despite what was established in her Universities—the Provision of the regulations issued by the General Directorate of Studies in 1825 for the colleges of Humanities, that their pupils should study the natural and mathematical sciences before the intellectual and moral ones: (1) regulations which we were ordered by the same Royal disposition to keep in view, so as to conform our plan to them; and this was another of the reasons, though philosophically not so strong as

(1) In Spain itself, since the last century, the physical sciences were already being taught in entire independence of the intellectual ones, that is, separately and without Logic preceding them: witness the colleges of the technical corps; another witness, the Instituto asturiano.

[p. 187]

the preceding ones, of which mention was made in my said Preliminary Note. This, then, is a cause that has passed into the authority of a thing adjudged throughout the whole extent of cultivated Europe.

And as the question seems to me, from what has been set forth, not exhausted—for it would not be so in a volume—but sufficiently examined, I shall omit going through one by one other notions that figure in the communication. It has been my intention in my answer, or rather exposition, to make even the shadow of persons disappear, so as to leave the field clear for things: upon one point alone, which is personal to me, I shall venture to call attention, and that for the sake of justice and of the respect I owe the public. It is a matter of making evident that not now, but from long ago, I have been guided in the adoption of my ideas by the spirit of examination, and not by the mere consideration of their being modern inventions. I see clearly that the author of the communication does not say this precisely of me (for, far from it, he lavishes excessive praise upon me) but rather of the other individuals with whom he had previously engaged in combat. I will say more: had I not been persuaded, by the tenor of his own paper, of his complete good faith, of his sincere desire to find the truth, I would already have refrained from taking up the pen. Precisely because I know the propensity of youth to run to extremes, I wished to raise a barrier by calling its attention to the value that many of the ideas of the ancients deserve; repeated proofs of this will be found in the course of my Syllabus, and particularly in proposition 16, where one reads: “The syllogism is no more than a form of discourse, or a means for deduction. Consequently we say of it neither all the good the scholastics attributed to it, nor all the evil the moderns heap upon it. Scholasticism was overthrown; and a true revolution always goes to excess in its first fervor. It is time that does justice to everything.” And the 97th.—“It is likewise a vulgarity to despise what the ancient philosophers said when we cannot understand it (which is the very height of impartiality,

[p. 188]

if it does not err by excess of respect). More than once experience has taught us that their words, under the veil of paradox, enfold great conceptions and profound observations.” In the said Syllabus one will notice an opinion of Descartes now defended, now another impugned; now a siding with the spiritualists, now with the sensationalists; now applauding the ideologists, now modifying, now restricting, now refuting their doctrines: in short, it has been sought—but with heart, soul and life—to inspire youth with the true spirit of philosophical criticism, to the point of preaching to it by counsel and example “that an intelligent and conscientious professor should furnish his pupils with the means of judging him, accustoming them to appeal to their own observations:” (see proposition 95) “that the philosopher must never lavish his admiration, lest he make it degenerate into worship.” (proposition 96) “that, although it may seem superfluous to dictate precautions against authority in the middle of the nineteenth century, it is still repeated and inculcated in them that authority is a Proteus that presents itself under a thousand forms to exert its malign influence: the novel, fashion, the spirit of the age, levity, presumption, self-love are nothing but garments in which authority clothes itself in order to enslave our reason.” (Proposition 94.) And who can doubt that the most relentless enemy of the proposed reform is that same phantom of authority, supported by the right of immemorial prescription for want of other titles of better rank? Why is it that no one, not even the very opponents of the new plan, finds or has found it repugnant that Mathematics should be taught before the moral sciences? because they had been taught so for a long time.

One must already have one’s reason exceedingly fortified to be able to shake off the yoke of authority in whatever form it presents itself; and what form more fearsome for the feeble understanding of pupils than the words of the master? Authority is a plant that has its seed

[p. 189]

in the heart of man: it is as characteristic of him as his very frailty: he who does not know how to walk must lean on him who already walks; he who does not see quite clearly needs a guide to light his way. How many times have we mocked the magister dixit [the master has said it] of the Pythagoreans without noticing that it enfolds a precious lesson in the primitive history of the human mind! To masters is owed respect, but not faith. The first is in the order of nature, the second outside it: the principle is useful, the exaggeration harmful. My intention has been at once to demolish authority and to set bounds to presumption. I should like to contribute my mite toward establishing the empire of reason, and no principle seems to me more suited to the case, nor sums up better the spirit of this discussion, than the very one that dictated my Syllabus; from whose title page to its end nothing else is read, under diverse forms, but “Obest enim plerumque iis qui discere volunt auctoritas eorum qui docent” [for the authority of those who teach is very often an obstacle to those who wish to learn]

Havana, June 8, 1838.

[p. 190]

II

Rejoinder to the Reply of the “Dómine,” inserted in the Gaceta de Puerto Príncipe of November 17 of the present year

(1).

“Pro me labora.”

[Work for me.]

I shall be very brief; as much because the answers to the objections now made to my former paper are contained in it, now at length, now in embryo, as because of the ease of reducing the points touched upon to the clearest and simplest terms.

1st. I agree with the Dómine and with Cousin that “as a philosopher’s method is, so will his doctrine be.” But I can never agree that “if we possessed the inestimable treasure of a perfect dictionary, there would be disputing only in madhouses.” For, although there really arise thousands of questions which come down to disputes about words, and although many of the former are consequently avoided merely by defining the latter, it is nevertheless incontestable that not all, all the questions that stand between men can be resolved into a contest of words. Is the great question now being fought out on the fields of Navarre between Cristinos and Carlists, all of them sons of the same mother, perchance a struggle of words? A very paltry idea must one form of humanity to believe that merely for words it is shedding its blood in torrents: when such a thing happens, and here I have the happiness of walking in agreement with Cousin, it is because great interests intervene on the one side and the other. But let us leave the agitated arena of politics to enter the peaceful sanctuary of the sciences: are they perchance questions of words, the many and so grave ones that arise in them at every step, and the many that are and will long remain sub judice? I should do the sensible an injury, were I to trouble them with the catalogue of these questions, as important

(1) Diario de la Habana of December 17, 1838. (A. Z.)

[p. 191]

as they are undecided, which swarm in the vast space of the sciences. But I cannot refrain from suggesting a brief ideological consideration very fitting to the matter, and it is this, that such a way of seeing makes all science consist, as Condillac claimed, in a well-made language: which is as much as to confuse the effect with the cause; for the reform of the language of science depends not so much on the exactness of the idiom previously employed in the analysis (though this has its good share in it) as on the observation (the source of all knowledge) that we make upon objects. So that observation is the cause, the exactness of language the effect.—An example will make this palpably clear. Let us suppose that a child, or a rustic (who is no more than a grown-up child), in speaking of the heavenly bodies, calls them all indiscriminately stars: in such circumstances, if I make him observe that among those stars there are some that twinkle, and others that never present such an appearance, this simple observation suffices to make him reform his language, and I accordingly charge him to keep the word stars for the heavenly bodies that twinkle, and to apply to those which do not present this phenomenon, e.g., that of planet; his science grows, and his language grows in proportion: that there are planets around which other smaller ones revolve—another observation, another term—let him call them secondaries—that some heavenly bodies appear seldom, and then cease to be seen, displaying tails, beards, etc.: another observation, and another name—comets; and if it is a matter, finally, of gathering them all under one general name or key, they are called orbs. Let us put the case, which must happen and does happen so often in our social state, that a man frequently knows the sign before the object it represents, or else that the sign offers him an inexact idea: in such a hypothesis observation makes him either learn the meaning, or rectify his language, when it does not form it. Is not this neither more nor less than the history of all the sciences? Are not new words invented when there occur

[p. 192]

new observations, or rather discoveries? Yet we do not on that account pretend for an instant to deny the supreme importance of signs, which are the most effective instrument of analysis; being rather of opinion that this very importance had a share in deluding Condillac and other Ideologists to the point of making them believe that a science was no more than a well-made language.

I, on the contrary, will say that a well-made language is the expression of a true science; so that, instead of affirming that every science is a well-made language, it ought to be said that every well-made language is a science: although it is undeniable that the understanding of man cannot progress in its investigations without the help of signs. But, for God’s sake, sir, everything in its place: objects are the materials of ideas; the senses the vehicle of impressions; reason the agent of observation, and signs the instrument for marking the steps and being able to continue the march.—But signs produce in mathematics, therefore they are not mere instruments.—Here is another fact that contributed exceedingly to dazzle Condillac. Truly portentous are the results to which the language of calculation leads us (for, after all, calculation is no more than a language); but let us not forget that this language is exercised only upon the relations of quantity, and cannot but bear upon the data furnished by observation. Thus, after the data comes the effective aid of calculation, which, seizing upon the circumstances of the phenomena, isolates them in order better to understand them and to follow them even into their last details. Never can the maxim “divide, et impera” [divide and rule] be applied with greater exactness, that is to say, “abstract and you will master the object.”—Algebraic signs, then, being the ultimate expression of simplicity and of generalization, take away from our mind the occasions of distraction, freeing it from carrying, as Maine de Biran so energetically expressed it, the double weight of the sign and of the idea, as happens with the signs of common language.— And here

[p. 193]

you have the miracle of mathematics explained in great part; it following likewise that the language of these sciences cannot be applied to all the relations which it falls to us to examine in the others.—So as not to sin by prolixity, I spare the examples, and all the more since I am addressing men of understanding, who will be able to supply what I omit. Only I may be permitted, to close this point, to make a comparison of the principles stated with the supposition on which the Dómine rests in his entry on the “possession of the inestimable treasure of a perfect dictionary,” on which hypothesis he judges that “there would be disputing only in madhouses.” Absolutely speaking, it is impossible to possess a perfect dictionary; for, language being an exact picture of science, only by coming to know everything, and to know it perfectly, would man attain a perfect idiom; that is to say, only by becoming God could he possess a perfect idiom. Therefore a dictionary, strictly speaking, cannot be perfect, but only perfectible, like the science it represents; or, what amounts to the same, it will be relatively and particularly perfect. Now then, can this relative perfection (the only one granted to humanity) prevent diverse opinions from arising in the investigation of phenomena? Can the instrument, however perfected it be, make us dispense in certain cases with observation? Can algebra, enchanting algebra, be applied to every class of relations? Or shall we come to grief on the reef of the Nominalists? Let us not forget that only things are things, and words no more than words, which are worth nothing except insofar as they represent things. Lastly, I do not wish to go further without advising the Dómine of Puerto Príncipe that in this part too it falls to my lot (and it is no small luck) to abound in the ideas of Mr. Cousin, from whom I have the misfortune to differ so much, so very much, on other important particulars, though fortunately not on even one of those touched upon in the course of the present writing. But let us not stray from the principal question.

2nd. Agreed with the Dómine that Logic is the study

[p. 194]

of the mental faculties for directing them in the investigation of truth; or, in other words, the science whose object is “to set forth the phenomena and theory of the intelligence.” A definition which involves a complete refutation of the principles that it is sought to maintain to the contrary. But before entering upon such an analysis, I do not wish to pass over the stumbling-block I have come upon in the Dómine’s 2nd note. He has thought it expedient “to fix the meaning of the word Logic, because the author of the Answer has at times taken it in a broad sense, as when he says: that without Psychology and Ideology there is no true Logic, and at others in a strict one, as when he says: the point is that Logic should be, not the principle of all the sciences, but a daughter, an immediate consequence of two other sciences, difficult enough, to wit, Ideology and Psychology. It is true that I took the word Logic in two acceptations: one for simple coherence of reasoning in any matter whatever, as when one says to write with logic, and the other for the science properly so called. But this is not, strictly, to use the word in a broad sense and in a strict sense, but in two different, though analogous, senses. So then, whenever an expression is employed in broad and strict senses, it has really been employed in two senses; but not whenever it is used in two senses can these be called strict and broad, for such adjectives indicate only the greater or lesser extension given to one and the same idea, but never the diversity that reigns between two or more. So then, first lapse in logic.

But even granting that I had taken the word Logic in a broad and strict sense, it must be confessed that the Dómine has been very unfortunate in his choice of quotations to prove it. In effect, to say that without “Psychology and Ideology there is no true Logic” (as I expressed myself on p. 336) (1) and that this (as I did on p. 339) is not to be

(1) This page and the one cited afterwards are pages of the Vol. of the Memorias de la Sociedad patriótica in which was published the article Answer, which begins on page 163 of the present one.—(A. Z.)

[p. 195]

the principle of all the sciences, but a daughter, an immediate consequence.... of Ideology and Psychology,” is to express the same idea, and to take the word Logic in the same acceptation in both passages, which are precisely the ones chosen by the Dómine to show that in the one case I employ the word Logic in a broad sense, and in the other in a strict sense. And it is very plainly seen that non sequitur: so that, a second lapse in Logic without leaving the narrow bounds of the note. For the rest, it is not contradictory to include Ideology in Logic, taking this word in the broad sense of signifying the whole group of the first intellectual sciences; but strictly, if Logic is what it ought to be, and what the Dómine himself wishes it to be, that is, the science whose object consists in setting forth the phenomena and theory of the intelligence in order to direct us in the investigation of truth, far from including Psychology and Ideology, it must of necessity come after them, for the very reason that on the rectitude of our ideas, that is to say, of our observations, must depend the exactness of reasoning.

Rid now of the note, we ought to pass on to prove, as we promised, that the definition of Logic given by the Dómine involves the most complete refutation of his own doctrines. But as this will appear demonstrated in the whole course of the present writing, and as some proof of it has already been given in the examination of the note, we shall continue following the writer of Puerto Príncipe step by step.

This good gentleman believes, and in very good faith, that because “man knows his sensations before the objects that produce them, he must study the interior first, rather than the exterior.” And he even fancies, in his candor, that he has victoriously turned one of my arguments back upon me, saying: “Man knows his sensations before the objects that produce them, and only comes to the knowledge of these precisely by virtue of the resistance they present to the use of our organs. As a rational being, the Dómine continues, he seeks to inquire into the causes of the effects he feels; but, who

[p. 196]

doubts that without the knowledge of these he would not even suspect the existence of those? Therefore he must begin with what is within, which is the effects, and not with what is without, which is the causes: or, better said, he cannot know what is exterior except by virtue of the interior knowledge of sensations.—“To ascertain each one of the properties of an object, he goes on, we observe the sensations they cause in our senses, and we give to these the names which we afterwards appropriate to those: so that what is called physical analysis might with more exactness be called logical analysis, or analysis of sensations.”—I have purposely chosen to copy in full the favorite argument, the true Achilles of our Dómine; and so that this gentleman may see that I, not yet content with so much, and far from seeking to weaken it, rather propose to strengthen it with its own supports, I shall add the following passage, which goes admirably with the preceding.—“Has not Logic perchance been at all times eminently observant?—Do not all the observations of the other sciences perchance bear precisely upon the sensations that external objects produce in our senses? When man, puffed up, fancies that he ranges through the immensity of space, and reads in the firmament the glory of the Eternal, he is really concentrated within himself, observing the various modifications of his intelligence.”

Let us now see who is the turner and who the turned. Sensations presuppose the existence of objects in nature: therefore man knows objects by virtue of sensations; therefore sensations are a condition for the knowledge of objects, and not the knowledge itself. Everything in its place, and everyone in his part. To acquire knowledge there is needed an object that offers the material, and a subject that feels and perceives what is in it. The most that can happen, and really does happen, is that many of the phenomena that take place in our interior are, like external ones, objective for the soul as a faculty of perceiving; as many as two

[p. 197]

classes of these internal phenomena may be considered, some properly corporeal, such as the sensation of hunger, that of pain, and others really spiritual, such as the operations of the understanding and of the will:—all of which comes to be as objective for the understanding as the exterior world, since of all of it the soul has consciousness, or, what is the same, perceives what goes on within as it perceives any other phenomenon from without.— Moreover, nature does not exist for man except in his own sensations; and the Dómine himself holds this to be so certain, that he believes with me that all the sciences are, strictly speaking, sciences of observation, and is not afraid to say that “when man, puffed up, fancies that he ranges through the immensity of space, and reads in the firmament the glory of the Eternal, he is really concentrated within himself, observing the various modifications of his intelligence.” I cannot understand how these words could have been put into print without perceiving that they involve the most complete refutation of the doctrine it is sought to maintain.—Now then, if nature does not exist for man except in his sensations, if even when he believes himself most exalted in contemplating the glories of the Eternal he is really receiving impressions and taking account of them, it is clear that all his knowledge can rest on nothing but observation, his logical knowledge as well as his physical knowledge: but of these two kinds of observation, or rather, among the objects to which observation is applied, some must offer more facility than others, as much by their different nature as because the understanding is more accustomed to examining them: for it has necessarily fixed its attention on some before others. How has the Dómine not seen that his famous Achilles is one of those arguments of which it used to be said in the schools that they prove nothing, because they proved too much? For in the same way that he has reasoned about sensation to demonstrate that we know the internal world before the external, anyone might argue against him, taking as middle term one of the many mental faculties we have, in order to

[p. 198]

convince him that his vaunted sensations were worth nothing, mimicking him in this manner, e.g.: without attending the soul cannot know; but that which is first is known first [false, utterly false; but it is one of the Dómine’s premises]; therefore it knows the faculty of attending, which is the effect, before the objects attended to, which are the cause. What would the Dómine answer to this and a thousand other paralogisms ejusdem furfuris [of the same bran], if he wishes to be consistent with the principles he has laid down? This is the point I wished to reach, so that the absurdity to which such a doctrine leads might leap to the eyes of all. So the child, because he employs his faculties and feels what passes within him, knows these faculties before external objects! So the man who walks knows the locomotor apparatus, and knows how one walks, and why one walks! Ask a child whether he knows the little wooden horse that amuses him. There is no need to wait for his answer: ask him whether he knows all the faculties he has set in action in order to know it, and he will give no account but of his impressions; from which no doubt we must afterwards set out in order to constitute the science, but which neither constitute it by themselves alone, nor is it possible for the child himself to relate that very thing which has passed within him.

Therefore, to ascertain each one of the properties of an object, we do not observe the sensations, as the Dómine would have it, for they are part of the observation itself; rather, we receive them, and afterwards perceive and know the object: all of which together is what constitutes observation; nor does this prevent the understanding, later on, from reflecting and turning back upon itself, observing all that has passed within it. But does a child, on beginning to acquire his knowledge, already have the faculty of reflection developed? Which, then, is the most natural, and consequently the best, method of teaching: to begin with the study of the faculties, or with that of the properties of bodies?—And to what purpose, either, is it to say that we give to the sensations the

[p. 199]

names which we afterwards appropriate to the properties, when for us the sensations are the representatives of the properties? Or how can it be understood that what is called physical analysis (who is it that calls it so? nobody but the Dómine) might with more exactness be called logical analysis? Unbidden there comes to my pen the celebrated puderet me non intelligere etc [I should be ashamed not to understand], of the most celebrated Melchor Cano.

There is but one mental analysis, Mr. Dómine (pleonasm and all), which may be applied to objects of logic or of physics, or to whatever it may fall to us to analyze; but even if there were many kinds of analysis, you could never obtain from human nature that it should analyze sensations before the properties of matter.—The most peculiar thing in the case is that such opinions should be harbored by one who, on the other hand, shows himself a partisan of the sensationalist system in the course of his writing; for you must know, and all who these presents shall see, that this Question of method (on pain of not having understood it) is the same, the very selfsame, the spit and image, dressed in other clothing, as the one that is debated between the spiritualist and sensationalist systems: a question most important in every respect: a question of life or death for philosophy: a question which suits, more than any other, the to be, or not to be of the illustrious British bard.

But on arriving here, it seems to me time to lay down the pen for the present, for there is no reason to go on occupying with the aridities and thorns of philosophy the place where the public looks for the flowers of variety. So then, let these my firsts of exchange go by tomorrow’s mail to my lord Correspondent, in the assurance that, God willing, the seconds will go by the next, and perhaps the thirds; for your worship must know that, although I promised to be brief, this is one of those words which cannot be kept, if one wishes to keep faith with the subject: besides which, it would be doing neither justice nor even heed to our Dómine, nor keeping the due rule of proportion, to apply the probe of analysis to a minimal part of his

[p. 200]

discourse, leaving almost the whole body of it untouched. Meanwhile, he will already have had, and will yet have, something to amuse himself with in the little draft that has been drawn upon him on his own exchange, dated the 28th of last month, and in those which a true-born little Camagüeyan offers to go on drawing upon him, as modest as he is enlightened, and who, by all accounts, has some funds available and is not among the worst in the matter of marksmanship (1) Come then, for my part, until the next mail, so as not to trouble my Dómine with so many payments at once, for there is neither grace nor justice, neither honor nor profit, in exposing him and myself to the consequences of a protest.—Apage! [Away with it!]

(1) An allusion to the Two Words on the Question of Method, by the estimable young man Don José T. de la Victoria, inserted in the Gaceta de Puerto Príncipe of the 28th of last month, which I have just read with the greatest pleasure, my article being already written.

[p. 201]

## III

Rejoinder to the Reply of the “Dómine,” inserted in the Gaceta de Puerto Príncipe of November 17 of the present year

(1).

“Pro me labora.”

[Work for me.]

(Continued.)

“I cannot comprehend, the Dómine goes on afterwards, why it should be more difficult to know the properties of our own being than those of the other beings of nature, when the inner testimony persuades us that we know nothing with such exactness as what passes within us; an idea happily expressed by Virgil in the mouth of Dido: non ignara mali miseris succurrere disco [not unacquainted with misfortune, I learn to succor the wretched]:” Well, it is I who cannot comprehend, nor surely will the sensible public comprehend either, how such a doubt is possible in my adversary after he has read my paper, and in particular the quotations from Cousin on the difficulties and thorns that encumber the march of the intellectual sciences, and all that is set forth on this same particular from page 340 to page 344

This passage, like many others, will convince the Dómine of Puerto Príncipe that the Memoir on method knows how to defend itself, without need of new props or parapets; a circumstance which ought to exempt its author from further explanations on the matter. But since the Dómine,

(2) exclusive, and above all the considerations that close the last paragraph, all the more conclusive inasmuch as they rest upon a mass of proofs, in my judgment irresistible, and which, as much for the sake of the question as for my own discharge, I cannot but reproduce in the same words. (3)

(1) Diario de la Habana of December 30, 1838—(A. Z.).

(2) He alludes to the pages of the Memorias de la Sociedad Patriótica, where the first of these articles was published—(A. Z.)

(3) Here the author transcribes several paragraphs of his article cited above, and they are those which begin on page 177 with the words “And will Mr. Rumilio still believe....” and end with those of “all nature.”, on page 178. (A. Z.)

[p. 202]

far from considering himself notified and convinced, still does not grasp “why it should be more difficult to know the properties of our own being than those of the other beings of nature,” placing himself under nothing less than the mantle of Virgil’s sorrowing queen, it would be to have not a spark of humanity or sympathy in one’s soul not to come forward with the remedy, once the ailment is known. Come then, to work. It is more difficult to know our own being than the other beings, 1st because in it are united the properties of all bodies and the faculties of all those of its genus, with the addition of rationality, which is no mere grain of aniseed in the question; the microcosm, or world in miniature, no less, and even something more.—2nd. Supposing it to be of the same difficulty with respect to man as with the other animals to comprehend and mark off the phenomena of vitality, the complete knowledge of rationality requires the knowledge of all the mental faculties, which, as internal phenomena, and succeeding one another so rapidly, the soul cannot perceive without being well accustomed to the most intense observation.—3rd. The problem becomes more complicated when we consider that man is the most educable or perfectible animal that nature offers, and, as such, must offer more variety in the development of his mental operations.—4th. There are phenomena, intellectual functions, such as, e.g., reflection, which do not even appear until the understanding is well developed (1) so that, were it for no more than this circumstance, it would be necessary to wait a little longer before undertaking the study of the speculative sciences; since one cannot proceed in any science without the necessary data. But this want of preparation is a sin into which one falls repeatedly by placing the study

(1) And mark you, he who writes thus has given ample proofs, theoretical as well as practical, of how much he believes in the aptitude of the tenderest children, and how much they exercise all the mental faculties (the germ of which all possess) whenever they are provided with nourishment suited to their nascent capacity; and here lies the whole soul of the explanatory method, which is nothing but an application of these same philosophical principles to the system of primary instruction.

[p. 203]

of logic before that of physics, as will be seen in a more obvious manner in what follows.—5th. Another element that comes to increase the difficulties is habit, whose influence on the intellectual faculties reaches so extraordinary a point that one cannot form an idea of its efficacy without setting oneself to examine it very carefully. Let it suffice to note in passing, in a matter as interesting as it is rich, that, since habit produces, together with ease in performing the operations, forgetfulness or lack of consciousness, that is, of inner knowledge, of the very steps we have taken in the discharge of such functions, it is very difficult for us, not to say impossible, to give an exact history of all our ideas at the moment it is asked of us. Ask a child all that his understanding performs when he reads a book rapidly, and he will not be capable of discovering all the functions he is exercising at once, and which he necessarily had to exercise by very slow degrees; here are only the most principal ones: to know each letter—to combine them with one another into syllables—then into words—then to pass on to sentences and periods—afterwards to attend to the sense of each word— then to the understanding of the period—without counting the labor of his ear and of his tongue to overcome the stumbling-blocks of pronunciation, and the no less notable labor of his eyes, in going on seeing the lines before reaching them; in short, the facility of his execution comes to such a point (the same thing happens with the musician) that he is now occupied only with the sense of the author, without being able perhaps to give an account of the size, shape and color of the letters, nor of the width of the lines, nor of the quality of the paper, nor of a million other circumstances which at the beginning unfailingly called and distracted his attention, and which have now remained as it were absorbed and eclipsed in the principal object of reading, which is the understanding of the author being read. This simple example will suffice to convince how necessary it is to study the influence of habit on the mental faculties: a study by itself beyond the reach of those who

[p. 204]

have but a nodding acquaintance with science, and a study so fruitful in results, as much for the intellectual sciences as for Pedagogy, or the science of Education, inasmuch as he will be the best teacher in any branch who can best make his pupils give the history of what has passed within themselves in the acquisition of their knowledge. I am sorry with all my soul not to enter at greater length into a matter which ought to interest our Dómine more keenly than any other, if indeed he wishes to live up to the title under which he appears before the eyes of the public—the title, as holy as it is grave, of director of understandings. But this is no more than one register out of the thousand that will be touched. Let us continue with the enumeration. 6th. It would seem that all the sciences that bear relation to man, even among the natural ones, are precisely the most difficult, since a million causes enter in to modify the phenomena, and it is necessary, in order not to go astray, to take exact account of all of them. Somewhat notable examples are offered, among the natural sciences, by Physiology and Pathology, and among the intellectual by Ethics and Legislation, the last two of which especially are very legitimate daughters of Psychology and of Logic. In all these sciences the causes are likewise hidden, because of the difficulty of subjecting the objects to experiment, a difficulty that does not exist with respect to Physics and Chemistry and the other analogous sciences.—Hence it is that those branches could not thrive until chance, the discoveries in the other sciences, and travels through various countries multiplied the means of observation, and with them the discovery of the facts without which it was not possible to found the edifice upon indestructible bases. I would cite in my support only the famous question of the influence of climates on the human species, in the resolution of which even a Montesquieu and other eminent minds stumbled. How is it possible that in Ethics one can dispense with the previous study of the reciprocal influence of our physical part upon our affections, and of our affections upon our organs?—Or when has legislation advanced, except when it has gathered the facts of the natural

[p. 205]

sciences as guide and rule for its prescriptions? What else does the legislator do but declare in writing the very law already stamped in the nature of things? Can the legislator invent the law? Can he give a law contrary to the nature of things? (1) In such cases, the first may be useless, the second will not be able to live. What a field would criminal legislation not offer me to make manifest its immense debt to all the natural sciences! The barbarous penalties that used to be imposed upon the possessed and soothsayers—when did they cease? When did men begin effectively to correct the criminal codes and the codes of procedure? So that, the investigators not yet being content with the observations that presented themselves spontaneously, moral experiments, if the expression be permitted us, have been instituted by philosophers and legislators: that is to say, until the facts were heaped up from all sides; (and, what so much bears upon our question) until the vivifying and creative method of the natural sciences was applied to the moral sciences, we did not set out on the road to resolving the most important problems of social organization. In my Memoir I cited in passing, believing that I was making myself tiresome by prolixity, among other supporting proofs, the work of Charles Comte, and the Penitentiary system, which has so much improved and will improve the lot of the unfortunate convicts, shedding at the same time the most abundant light on all the branches of criminal legislation. But to whom is owed in great part that spirit of lenity, of tolerance, of gentleness, of circumspection which is taking hold of

(1) It will perhaps be objected that Lycurgus and some founders of religions invented institutions even against the nature of things.—But let it be borne in mind that such men always took their stand on some sentiment of the heart in order to combat others; and let it not be forgotten how long such institutions lasted and what they produced.—The examination of so important a matter, far from harming our way of thinking, would favor it in the highest degree. But it is not possible to enter at length into all the points which it becomes necessary to touch upon by way of proof. If our Dómine, however, wishes us to dissect the present one, or any other of those indicated, we shall gladly oblige him, treating each article in a separate pamphlet.

[p. 206]

all thinking men in matters of legislation and government? To the advances in the natural sciences, without doubt, and above all to the spirit which their method must necessarily infuse into other branches. And I have said only in great part, for who can fail to recognize that the greater part belongs by right, whenever it is a question of tolerance, of sympathy and of humanity, to that divine religion which commands us not merely to forgive, but to love our enemies? But, coming to the example of the Penitentiaries, I will not deprive myself of the pleasure of copying the words of a judge most competent in the matter (Mr. Demetz, councilor of the Royal Court of the Seine), since in them is proclaimed the triumph of the experimental method, the only one that can raise the whole of legislation to the sphere of a true science. “At present I am undeceived by my own experience as to how much on our guard we must be against certain theories, generous in their principle but false in their application, and which lead the understanding astray all the more, the better they know how to take hold of the heart. I left France full of prejudice against the Pennsylvania regime (solitary confinement; for it is well known that there are in the United States two systems at present on trial, or experiment: namely, the aforesaid, and that of communication alternating with isolation.) But on seeing this system at work at close range, my ideas have changed completely, and it is that same system which my conscience today lays upon me the law of upholding and defending. He would err exceedingly who imagined that the science of prisons is wholly a business of pure instinct and sentiment: it happens in these matters as in the exact sciences, in which only, and solely, study and observation give the right to profess an opinion with a certain degree of confidence and authority. Warmth of soul, and talent, cannot here take the place of experience. How is it possible to pronounce by inspiration on this question, when I have seen directors of penitentiaries, veterans in practice, still very perplexed on certain particulars?

[p. 207]

It is therefore a duty (and a sacred duty) in such a matter, more than in any other, not to form an opinion except after the most mature examination.”—7th. It is so much in the nature of things that discoveries in the physical sciences should precede and be the occasion of progress in the intellectual ones, that, far from doing harm, this order reciprocally favors the advancement of both kinds of science. See, if not, e.g., the advantages, not merely material but moral, which progress in the means of communication brings with it, and the origin to which they must be attributed.—In former times all the sea voyages that were undertaken were very paltry and hazardous: the property of direction is discovered in the magnet, and forthwith the seaman takes heart to venture, and now seeks to launch out upon unknown seas: the passage to India by the Cape of Good Hope is accomplished, and at last, two thousand leagues away, the famous world of Columbus is discovered. How many questions are not raised in all the branches of human knowledge on the occasion of so portentous a discovery! Questions not only physical and mathematical, but theological, moral, philological, political, and all of them eminently philosophical! Without the pointing of a wretched little piece of iron toward the poles of the world, would so vast a field have been opened to human speculations? How many material advantages do not the admirable means of communication which iron and steam supply today offer to the peoples! (1) Yet these utilities are still nothing

(1) I do not wish to be confused for an instant with those whom I call the materialists of politics, that is to say, those who more napoleonico [in the Napoleonic manner] maintain that purely material advances suffice for the happiness and well-being of man and of societies. No one has raised his voice more strongly against so scandalous an immorality than he who writes these rough lines, and this is borne out, among other proofs, by the following proposition, which is found in a Syllabus of Philosophy published as early as 1835:—“The morality of interest opens before us an abyss of evils;” and here, among its necessary consequences, is one of those I enumerated.... “2nd. The pretension of contenting man with physical enjoyments alone”. In that same Syllabus the utilitarians are in part combated, though not with the weapons of the spiritualist philosophers, but simply with those of observation, by proving that the doctrine is not complete for want of that indispensable ingredient for compounding science.

[p. 208]

material in comparison with the moral advantages they bring to the human race. What a difference, or, to put it better, what a fountain of differences does the invention of printing alone not establish between ancient and modern peoples! So then, the history of man’s understanding is pointing out to us with its finger that discoveries in the physical order precede and are the occasion of advances in the moral order; or, what is the same, that the former comes first, and is consequently the easier and more perceptible. “For observing comes before deducing; receiving impressions before reflecting them: being a child before being a man: walking before explaining the gait.” I had, then, more than enough reason to affirm that “to begin with physics, or in general with the natural sciences, is to begin at the beginning; and that man naturally feels himself carried away to the contemplation of external objects by the countless sensations with which they assail all his senses.” Since the Dómine shows himself so fond of quotations, I am going to treat him to a few which, in spite of their aptness, I left in the inkwell in my former Memoir for brevity’s sake. Jouffroy speaks, and speaks as he is wont, giving us at once all the elegance of the philosophers of the ancient academy and the admirable talent for exposition that modern science demands.—“Now then, it is an undeniable fact that our attention turns far more spontaneously to exterior objects than to internal phenomena. Is this perchance a new result of habit, or in part the work of nature? Here is a question on which diverse opinions may be formed. For when we consider what a multitude of needs tend to fix the child’s attention, and to hold the man’s, upon external things, what a variety of objects social relations and the inexhaustible field of nature present to his curiosity and to his passions, we shall find ourselves obliged to confess that, even if we had no natural inclination to turn our attention

[p. 209]

toward external objects before internal ones, the circumstances of our condition would be sufficient to impart this bent to our intelligence; and, on the other hand, whether we are deceived by the force of habit, or whether the instinct of our intelligence naturally drags it to look outward before reflecting upon itself, it would be difficult for a sensible and impartial man to reject outright the possibility of a primitive inclination. But be this as it may, the fact that attention is in effect directed toward external objects always remains incontestable; and surely to this propensity, no less than to the paramount necessity of providing for the preservation of our existence and for the innumerable needs of our body, must we attribute the preeminence which the natural sciences have attained over the philosophical in the intellectual development of the human race.”—A truth which I could demonstrate more and more, by showing that all the theological and cosmogonic systems of the Hindus, of the Egyptians, of the Greeks, and in general of all the most notable philosophers and legislators of antiquity, are merely physical systems, founded on the observation of natural phenomena; and this I wished to let be glimpsed in my Memoir when I said that “the first investigations bore, as was natural, before all else upon natural phenomena, reminding the writer (Mr. Rumilio) in passing that Thales of Miletus, Pythagoras, and before them their masters the Egyptians, and after them the great Aristotle, began their studies with the inquiry into natural phenomena.” And see here, no doubt, one of the matters touched upon in my writing which the Dómine passes over, because he believes it, like others, of lesser importance. This gentleman has not seen that the point in question bears so directly on the principal question that it is one of the best data for elucidating it. But further on he will be shown that there is not a single particular in my Memoir that does not matter very principally to the question, since the whole

[p. 210]

of it is made up, rather than of long explanations, of mere indications on the most principal points;—hoping to convince him that even that part which he believes accessory is calculated to carry conviction more intimately to the minds of the readers.—And what more irrefragable proof of the necessity of saying something more still than the very doubts that still assail the mind of Mr. Dómine? On this account I cannot but earnestly beg him (for I am trying in all seriousness to satisfy his understanding), since the present writing too cannot go beyond mere indications in some parts, for just fear of becoming interminable (for this question embraces all the questions of Philosophy), to be so good as to point out to me separately each one of the points that require more elucidation, in order to impress upon him more and more the force of my proofs, which I shall take a very special pleasure in furnishing him as far as lies within my reach, although, to tell the truth, I should take more in receiving light from a master as competent as a whole Dómine must be. Let us not stray, however, from our principal subject.

A proof likewise of the great share which Jouffroy attributes to method in the growth of the natural sciences is found in the following passage from another of his writings: “This new spirit, he says, introduced into the natural sciences, has substituted for the reign of opinions that of observation, and has made them take more steps in half a century than they had taken since the creation of the world.” That is why Jouffroy sighs, and with him sigh all who desire true advances in the intellectual sciences (even those who, like Cousin so often, and not so much Jouffroy himself, are inconsistent with the very principles of Eclecticism), for the application of so vivifying a method to psychological investigations, the only method that can raise them to the rigor and exactness of demonstration, indispensable conditions of all science. As I have been undeceived that it availed me nothing to have quoted, in brief and

[p. 211]

at length, in my former Memoir, in order to stress the difficulties that beset the intellectual sciences and the state of backwardness in which they lay, I wish still to set down two or three more passages of Cousin, and only the epigraph from Verulam chosen by Kant as the frontispiece of no less than his great work on Pure Reason; without prejudice to all that I shall have to say to you on the subject in the analysis of your following paragraph, for the list of reasons I am drawing up to make you understand why it should be “more difficult to know the properties of our own being than those of the other beings of nature” is not even finished yet.

Here are the passages of Cousin; and mark that they are adduced only to demonstrate that all philosophers feel, as by instinct and as the result of their own studies, the necessity, the imperative urgency, of appealing to the only anchor of hope: to the method of observation. I purposely abstain from citing authors of the sensationalist school, since it is plain to see that they must of necessity abound in such ideas. In a Memoir on the classification of “Philosophical Questions and Schools,” after giving a detailed review of the conditions for a classification of the said questions, after having proclaimed, as I showed in my first writing, the superiority of the experimental method as the laurel of our century, after.... (I would warmly recommend to the Dómine the reading of this work of Cousin’s) he asks himself: “And has there been a single philosopher who has fulfilled them up to now (those conditions)? If so, we should have a metaphysical science, as there exist a geometry and a chemistry. Have the philosophers at least distinguished those different pigeonholes or compartments of the great table, since they have not been able to fill them? Have they perchance sketched the outlines and proportions of the edifice, since they have not been able to raise it? If so, there would be a science begun, a road opened, a method already defined (and mark you, I do not go so far, for I neither believe science so

[p. 212]

backward, nor am I here as champion of the opinions of Cousin: it is a question of a fact, the backwardness of the intellectual sciences and the necessity of applying the experimental method to them.) But if philosophers have known neither how to fill those pigeonholes of the philosophical table, nor even how to perceive and distinguish them, what is it that they have done? Here it is in a few words.

“The first philosophers have treated everything and resolved everything, but confusedly; they have treated everything, but their methods are arbitrary and artificial......... Descartes himself, in spite of all the vigor of his understanding, did not penetrate the whole bearing of the distinction between the subject and the object... the abyss that separates the subject from the object did not strike him as it ought; and after having stated the problem well, this great man resolved it with too much lightness.” Here it would be perfectly in place to speak of what the great Leibnitz did; but I am already beginning to tire myself, and no doubt the readers as well, with so many quotations, and there are still many to be made in another part of the present, or rather of the future, writing; for it is now necessary to begin bringing things to a stop for this mail, even before concluding the enumeration of the whys in which we are engaged, since we are already robbing tomorrow’s Diario of more than half a dozen columns; and upon my faith I am sorry to cry halt to my pen here, for at the first stage I came off better than at the present one, in which it has not even been possible for me to close with a full stop, as I managed in the first: but patience, for once all the Diarios containing the parts are gathered together, the whole will needs result, and we shall be on the other side. Meanwhile, and patience once more, here goes the promised text of Verulam, which serves as title-page to the great work of the philosopher of Königsberg, and which I would most willingly print at the head and at the end, as the soul and body of the present and future discussions that may pass with others and among ourselves. “De nobis ipsis silemus: de re autem quæ agitur, petimus: ut homines eam non opinionem, sed opus esse cogitent: ac [Of ourselves we say nothing: but on behalf of the matter in hand we ask that men think it not an opinion but a work; and

[p. 213]

pro certo habeant, non sectae nos alicujus, aut placiti, sed utilitatis et amplitudinis humanæ fundamenta moliri. Deinde ut suis commodis æqui—in commune consulant—et ipsi in partem veniant. Praeterea ut bene sperent neque instaurationem nostram ut quiddam infinitum et ultra mortale fingant, et animo concipiant; quum revera sit infiniti erroris finis et terminus legitimus.” [hold it for certain that we are laying the foundations not of any sect or doctrine, but of human utility and greatness. Next, that, fair to their own interests, they take counsel for the common good, and themselves come in for a share. Further, that they be of good hope, and not imagine and conceive our Instauration as something infinite and beyond what is mortal; since in truth it is the end and lawful limit of infinite error.]—It follows, then, that in the judgment of the greatest metaphysicians the intellectual sciences are, when all is said and done, a mere and simple postulate or desideratum: something more than I needed in order to affirm that they were in swaddling clothes compared with the natural sciences: a truth which will go on appearing more and more resplendent as this discussion proceeds; to the point that its brightness will penetrate even through the very eyes that would close themselves against this river of light concentrated in one focus, and poured out upon one single point.

[p. 214]

IV.

## Rejoinder to the Reply of the “Dómine,”

printed in the Gaceta de Puerto Príncipe of November 17 last (1).

## “Pro me laboras.”

[You labor for me.]

(Continued)

I have already taken up ten columns of the preceding issues of the Diario without yet having got beyond the first column of my antagonist, whose article covers more than three in the Gaceta de Puerto Príncipe; and so, for this reason as well as for having repeatedly indicated to him my readiness to enter into particulars, whenever he pleases, on all those points which, from the brevity with which they are treated, may require elucidation, I shall be permitted to continue on my way at a quicker pace.

8º—Languages, too, must be known by whoever wishes to penetrate to the bottom of the phenomena of the understanding. And I say languages, because the knowledge of a single one is not enough to furnish the data that general grammar requires. Languages are the first monument for the history of the human mind: and so, confining ourselves to the point that occupies us, their study is to be recommended, and must precede that of Logic, not so much on account of the importance of the expression of ideas (which is in itself a great letter of recommendation) as because in languages there is offered the faithful reflection of the operations of the understanding, and the most exact and complete history of its development. Thus the language of a people declares what that people is, what it has been, and how it has been. Languages, in short, present us with the best proofs of cross-examination or confrontation that can be imagined for testing and refining the most important doctrines of the science of ideas.— Here, then, is another element that complicates the problem, convincing us

(1) Diario de la Habana of January 6, 1839. (A. Z.)

[p. 215]

of the necessity of its preceding the study of Logic properly so called, on the supposition, agreed with the Dómine, that by Logic is understood the “science whose object is to set forth the phenomena and theory of the intelligence.” I should have nothing more to do than go on drawing consequences from this definition in order to pulverize all that the Dómine has laid down in his paper. Has this gentleman stopped for an instant to reflect how much that definition embraces?—“All” the phenomena of the intelligence! and then...... the theory of the intelligence! Where are the phenomena of the intelligence? Hidden in consciousness, and they must be disentangled; complicated with those of sensibility, and they must be marked off; modified by age, sex, temperament, climate, education, the state of health and of sickness, and account must be taken of so many modifying causes; confused at times with those of instinct, and they must be distinguished; obscured by habit, and they must be cleared up; reflected in languages, and their light must be received; wrapped up at times in systems, and they must be brought out clear; scattered over the whole compass of the sciences, and they must be gathered up and classified.—Hence 9º— The necessity of becoming familiar with the knowledge of philosophical systems: another obstacle to the solution of the problem, another touchstone for doctrines;—and this is already to enter the vast field of the history of philosophy.—10º Hence, too, as a preliminary step, as a conditio sine qua non, the gathering of the data of the other sciences as facts or examples for the science of ideas, for the theory of theories. This is so obvious that we cannot open any discussion on such matters without immediately confining ourselves to the nature of the physical, mathematical or moral sciences: nor can it be otherwise. How could we, at the beginning of this paper, have aired the question of signs without some algebraic notions? How was it possible to have explained the cause of the rigor and precision of such signs, and their superiority

[p. 216]

over those of the vulgar tongues, without having first informed ourselves of the whole contrivance of the algebraic method? And do we not draw from this analysis a precious document for the science of ideas?—Open the books of all the modern psychologists and metaphysicians, and from the very threshold one will stumble upon notions taken from the other sciences, as a fundamental basis, as knowledge previously acquired; and one will find the natural and mathematical sciences not only furnishing the host for the campaign (the facts) but serving as captain and model for directing it and marching into action (their method)—Would you have me cite in proof all the modern metaphysicians of all the schools, from Descartes down to our own day? So many quotations as I heaped up in my previous work availed me nothing, and what remedy is there?—To quote again:—but I shall do so only with Kant and Cousin—for you already have another quotation from Jouffroy to the same purpose; and if you want them all, you have only to say the word and you shall be served, in spite of the tedium that the thankless task of copying brings with it. The whole preface to the second edition of the “Critique of Pure Reason,” or rather the entire work, might be extracted in proof, for the whole of it is one continual discussion of the nature of the truths of each science and of the methods adopted in it. Before founding anything at all it was indispensable to gather and select the materials and trace the plan of the work, to occupy the ground before raising the edifice, to discuss before teaching. Such is Kant’s procedure. I shall choose, then, only the following words, as the most suited to our purpose: “In this attempt to change the course hitherto followed by metaphysics, taking the geometers and physicists as our model so as to bring about in it a complete revolution, lies, then, the object of this critique of purely speculative reason. It is a treatise on method, not a system of the science itself (the words seem made to order for the question); but for all that it marks out its whole

[p. 217]

sphere, both as regards its limits and as regards its whole internal structure.” There is no need to copy more from Kant.—Let us add the expressions of Cousin in the appendix to his fragments: “Occupied exclusively (he is relating his labors at the Normal School) with introducing into metaphysics the method of the natural sciences, I did not overstep the limits of psychology; and even today I am very far from repenting of this circumspection; for before all else one must fertilize the understanding, and what fertilizes it is method. With method one does not found sects, but one can impart a useful movement.”
