11º I said in my Memoir that the chapter on mental alienation is by itself an episode which, in respect of the auxiliary knowledge it requires, turns into another principal subject in the study of man: holding it needless to point out that we proceed on the understanding that it is a fundamental matter for Psychology, Ethics and Legislation. Without leaving Psychology, how many admirable phenomena, worthy of being observed for the study of the intellectual functions, does not their perversion offer us, through all the degrees and shades in which it presents itself, from the delirium of fever to the despair of mania! How many functions, some altered and others stimulated, alongside some still normal amid the disorder of the rest! And passing from here to morality and legislation, how many precious data for drawing with tact the subtle dividing line between disease and crime! How many delicate indications for determining the degrees of culpability or frailty of our species! What a worthy, what an important study! the most proper of all studies for mankind.—man. And can man be known without going deeply into the examination of these phenomena? And must this study, or must it not, precede that of Logic properly so called? And does not such a study presuppose besides that of other natural sciences, as indispensable preliminaries? Can there be Psychology without Physiology

[p. 218]

, or Physiology without Physics? What am I saying, without Physics? Today less than ever, particularly after the experiments and doctrines of Magendie, which tend to prove that a great part of the backwardness of physiological science was due to the failure to apply Physics to the vital phenomena themselves, many of which are no more than the very phenomena of inert matter observed in living matter: so true is it that the further the sciences advance, the wider grows the horizon of generalization, and the more, by necessary consequence, is the vast field of facts simplified and cleared, those that seemed most remote and unconnected being reduced to a universal bond that joins them all and makes brothers of them! Not without good reason did the illustrious Davy say that complication is the patrimony of the first dawn of science. If I were permitted to dwell on this point, I would gladly point out the errors into which the most enlightened talents in all branches of the science of man have been drawn, solely for lack of observation of their own nature.—Thus, for lack of physiology, countless psychologists have raved; for lack of physiology Montesquieu went astray and Helvétius erred: for lack of physiology Bentham, who is so often right, has strayed from the path; for lack of physiology the best plans of education and the best pupils have come to nothing; for lack of physiology.... But I have pronounced the word education, and under the magic influence it exerts upon my soul, I cannot but recall the clamor that rises on every side in cultivated Europe to make teachers feel not only the necessity they are under of teaching this science in their establishments, but the still more urgent one of putting into practice the precious lessons it offers, so as not to waste the precious fruit of their toils: and this clamor has found an echo on this side of the seas, in that classic land of primary education, the North American republic. Hence, too, another clamor no less universal for combining manual labor with mental, or rather, the

[p. 219]

best system of gymnastics and hygiene in accordance with the data that the science of man yields of itself. A clamor wrung from the innumerable facts presented by the diseases and ailments peculiar to those who devote themselves to study, to the point of undermining the edifice of their health, or leaving it forever frail and tottering.

The more deeply we go into the matter, the more we shall be convinced of the impossibility of taking sure steps in any branch of all those that relate to man and society without the previous study of man himself, which already presupposes the general study of nature together with a countless number of specialties. In psychology, education, morality and legislation we have already heaped up some data for judging the indispensable necessity of the study of man for such branches of human knowledge. As regards history, suffice it to say that it is not possible to form a judgment on innumerable facts without having first formed our criterion for it on the pattern of nature.—How shall our understanding gauge the limits of the possible and the probable, of the credible and the incredible, without having observed the forces of nature, or without being guided by the analogies deduced from these same observations, when it lacks the new experiments indispensable for pronouncing judgment? “For what thing is there that is not a miracle the first time it is observed? How many, many things are held impracticable until they are seen done! At every step we should doubt the power and majesty of the nature of things, if we looked at its parts in isolation and did not embrace its totality with our mind.” So speaks Pliny the naturalist. The first time a common man hears it said that stones rain down, he does not hesitate to banish such a notion to the land of impossibilities and chimeras. And why? Because, not having studied nature, he lacks the means and counterweights for gauging its power. But without going so far as the rustic: even the learned themselves, who are apt (and formerly more than now) to be none too rich in natural knowledge, dared to treat

[p. 220]

as a fable the phenomenon of aerolites recorded in many historians of antiquity, and notably in the Pliny already cited. It would have been another matter with understandings schooled by observation, for even in case such a prodigious fact had not come to their notice, the constant experience of other wonders remarked in the vast field of nature makes them perforce more cautious, circumspect and measured in deciding, and, by an inevitable consequence, solid and profound in whatever matters they undertake to examine, or, what is the same thing and more to our purpose, less subject to errors and backslidings, which do so much harm to science, and less easily contented with a superficial varnish, however dazzling it may be to the raw eyes of the multitude. And let there be no fear that the spirit of rigor and exactness which science demands will sterilize so much as a single vein of the inexhaustible lode of art and poetry, which are rather fostered than diminished by the discoveries of modern science, as it would be easy enough to demonstrate against the opinion of some men of letters of the day. No less unreasonable is the war in which they try to set science against poetry than the divorce that has been sought to establish between that same science and the holy religion of the daughter of Zion. The vivid impression of the marvels of nature must perforce carry us away to the sublime regions of poetry. Its objects and springs may vary with time and circumstances, but its root is too deeply fixed in the heart of man ever to be torn out. Science, far from shaking this root, will offer it new sap and fertilizing water.— It will be the poetry of the 19th century: it will have at once a common bond with the others, and a peculiar character that distinguishes it.

What the study of the sciences will do is put each thing in its place: their progress will prevent adult humanity from being lulled with the same instrument, or in the same tone, as infant humanity: it will happen to the

[p. 221]

species just as it always happens to the individual: the grown man is no longer amused with the same toy he was wild about in the cradle, but there will always be a means of recreating him and tempering his manners.

What new forms has art not taken, when its springs were already thought exhausted, in the hands of the Goethes, the Schillers, the Scotts, the Byrons, the Manzonis, the Lamartines and the Hugos!— But what am I saying, new forms! new subjects never treated before, and which so characterize the age in which we live, have been admirably handled by those illustrious geniuses; and as if to undeceive us that it was not only with the magic of the Middle Ages that the heart could be captivated, the author of Roma subterránea comes forward invoking no other muse than the present, and winning a triumph equal—nay, superior by its noble end—to those obtained by the enchanting prestige of ancient traditions.—Let there be no fear, then, that amid the great discoveries that follow one upon another and the great interests that stir us, the muse of humanity will fall silent for a single instant.

In Public Economy, can a step be taken without running the risk of stumbling, unless one consults the methods and procedures of the sciences and the mechanical arts, which make up a great part of the materials on which its reasonings must bear? See, if not, the lessons which even practical and judicious England, the practical nation par excellence, receives from one of her most distinguished sons, the physicist and mathematician Charles Babbage, worthy successor of Isaac Newton in the chair at Cambridge: lessons set down in the work entitled On the Economy of Machinery and Manufactures. No one would expect at first sight that under so modest a title-page the gravest questions of Public Economy should be included; and yet these matters are examined so masterfully and under such new aspects, showing the far-reaching consequences of the errors that have been and may be committed by legislating about them without previous knowledge of those data, at first sight purely mechanical, that

[p. 222]

no other recourse is left to economists and legislators than to go and learn, physically and mathematically, the sublime science of Public Economy in the severe books of Dynamics and in the humble workshops of the arts. So deeply does the Spanish translator of this precious work appear to have been imbued with these same principles, that he has magnified it with the title of Principios de Economía política, a name with which it never occurred to its modest author to christen it.

But where the last word was said in the way of disenchantments for legislators and rulers, and in general for all who concern themselves with the public cause, is in the famous work of the physician Parent-du- Châtelet, on “Prostitution in the City of Paris,” which I cited by way of a note in my Memoir as a model of investigation. Let the jurists, the moralists, and in general all who cultivate the speculative sciences, come and be initiated into countless data which for them will be so many mysteries: let them come and learn not only things, but method for examining and discovering other things: the method of observation, the rigorously analytic method, the only one that deserves the name of scientific, a spirit that is acquired only in the cultivation of the natural sciences and of their inseparable fellow workers, mathematics. There they will see the best application ever made of statistical, or rigorously numerical, procedures: this is the logic that the century in which we live demands, this is the fare that must be supplied to those who meditate on the lot of humanity, and on the most efficacious means of curing the moral ailments that afflict it. For the book of Mr. Parent is not merely an eminently moral book; from the first page to the last it vouches for its author as the most zealous advocate of good morals, as a most pure and undefiled soul, in spite of the infected breaths that the conscientious discharge of his most grave mission forced him to inhale from time to time over the long period of eight years. Well might this profound philosopher, this preacher of physical and

[p. 223]

moral health, have set as text to his precious doctrinal that famous “et nunc reges intelligite;.. erudímini qui judicatis terram!..” [and now, O kings, understand; be instructed, you that judge the earth!] It is works of this kind that put the Cartesian doubt into practice in investigation, for, calling to examination all the notions that pass current in science, before adopting any conclusion one begins to think anew, proceeding as if nothing were known; it is thus that countless errors are rooted out which had already received the sanction of time and of the most respectable authorities. Not by means other than these was another profound thinker, Niebuhr, cut off untimely no less than Parent, able to shake to its very foundations the venerated monument of Roman History.—Availing himself of every resource that observation can furnish, calling the sciences, the arts and the languages to his aid, placing himself in the very spots where the admirable events of that extraordinary people took place, measuring, counting and weighing: it was thus that the profound Niebuhr equipped himself to undertake the arduous enterprise: until he was thus supplied with data, he did not attempt to weigh and confront with one another the testimonies of the historians, scarcely one of them escaping his severe analytic spirit, at once the destroyer of what was crumbling in the edifice and the rebuilder of the work with those very fragments that proved able to stand every test. But it would carry me very far to try to cite the works that in the present age offer the true model of the spirit of investigation. I would very willingly place beside those I have cited the no less worthy names of a John Frederick Herschel, of a Raspail, of a Tocqueville and of other eminent men in great number, though not so great as is commonly believed, for in the sciences too there is a vulgar, and a large vulgar, and the truly original and searching heads are not counted by the dozen.

I have purposely abstained, in what has been said so far, from including logic among the intellectual sciences that owe most to the natural ones, because it is a point that I reserve

[p. 224]

for a separate chapter in the refutation of one of the subsequent paragraphs of my esteemed correspondent; although further allegations on the matter might already be held superfluous, when one considers that psychology, the mother of true logic in the sense in which the Dómine and I understand the word, is also the mother of morality, legislation and the other speculative sciences to which our observations have been confined. But, having repeatedly promised to air the point separately, the Dómine may now count on another long entry in the record of services rendered by the experimental sciences to logic: rather, he will say, recalling my earlier expressions:—“that not even the science of Logic would exist, had it not been for the natural sciences themselves.” He will already be aware, from what has been said, of how well founded Bacon was when, in drawing up his table of the sciences, he set Logic in the penultimate place, ever consistent with the great principle that I stamped as an epigraph at the head of my Memoir, namely: “that even supposing its organon (that is, its method) to be absolute, it would avail nothing for the reform of the sciences without natural history (that is, without the physical, physiological and psychological facts, for a complete history must include them all); whereas that same history by itself, even without the organon, would contribute amply to the longed-for reform: for which reason it seemed more fitting and natural to occupy oneself wholly and before all else with so important a matter.”

Thus it will cause no little surprise to see how the intellectual sciences have been fertilized, or rather created, by the natural ones, practicing to the letter the profound maxims of Bacon, and all his prophecies being fulfilled, that there should still be in France, in enlightened France, not one but many men of letters who maintain that with the method of Verulam only the physical sciences have been created, but not the intellectual; as though there could be a good

[p. 225]

method capable of creating the one and not the others; as though it were possible for there to be at the same time two equally good methods; as though the sciences were as essentially different as they imagine them, and above all the sciences that relate to man; as though there were, strictly speaking, many sciences, and they did not all come down to a single one, parted and diversified by the urgent necessity arising from the limitation of our faculties and the immense variety of objects and of their very properties. The boldness—I hardly know whether I should rather say the blindness —of these pretended philosophers goes so far that they deny to Bacon of Verulam, yes sir, to Bacon of Verulam, the title of philosopher, granting him only, as a favor, that of first physicist, or founder of modern Physics.—And have those who say so reflected well on what they are saying? Have they taken in what their own words involve? Even granting them that Bacon did not know antiquity (and he wrote the book—and what a book!—de Sapientia veterum), that he did not grasp the state of the question (when the instauratio magna embraces it all, one of the monuments that most honor the human mind); even giving them all they wish for nothing, even accepting from them the most scandalous absurdities, one need only judge them by their own words to convict them either of poor understanding or of ill will. There is no way of escaping the terrible dilemma: if with his method the physical sciences have been raised up, as you confess, his method has necessarily been the father of the intellectual ones; because these either are nothing without the former, as has been demonstrated to the point of tedium, or, what comes to the same, what they are by themselves they owe to the method of observation which they have imitated from the natural ones. The most that could with any ground be affirmed is that Bacon himself gave himself much, and advised investigators to give themselves first, to the study of the nature of external objects rather than to that of the nature of the soul. But in this very advice he left us the most precious and peremptory proof

[p. 226]

of how deeply he had studied the faculties of that same soul, for he was well persuaded that this was the most natural road not only for conquering knowledge and material comforts, but for arriving of necessity, by virtue and on occasion of these very things, as on many points we have arrived, at the resolution of the questions most important for man intellectual and moral. We are told by these same philosophers that Verulam did no more than the materiality (Lord help us with matter!) of bequeathing industry to the peoples.—And what do you think of the legacy, gentlemen? Can many materialities of this sort be bequeathed that would more spiritualize, elevate and civilize the manners of nations? Labor......! the first stimulus to morality, directly and indirectly... the second religion of the people, if I may be allowed the daring expression— the only effective guarantee of its perseverance in the ways of justice and probity. For God’s sake, gentlemen, do not deal morals the fiercest blow with that ill-starred divorce you establish between the physical and the moral. In it the cause of science loses, and the cause of God loses more, which is the first cause for man.—For the rest, one must never have so much as greeted the voluminous works of the illustrious Verulam, or know them only by the two lines of the famous and well-worn saying about the spider’s web, to claim that their author did not concern himself with the faculties of the soul, when there is not, I will not say a page, but not even an aphorism of his works that does not contain either the description of some mental phenomenon, or some precious document for the direction of the human mind. And all of it not by mere indications, but treated very much ex professo, and so amply that in them the whole immense field of the sciences and of letters is traversed. Speaking, then, with all strictness, Verulam ought rather to be styled the prince of the psychologists than the first of the physicists, although he would not disdain this most honorable title either, nor is there any contradiction in decorating him with both laurels

[p. 227]

: or, in a word, that he embraces everything, is the philosopher par excellence, the true restorer and first architect of human knowledge.

The most singular thing in the case is that the leaders of these same philosophers and men of letters are precisely the ones who have most proclaimed the excellence of the Baconian method, and its application to the intellectual sciences as the only plank in the shipwreck; (witness the passages we have cited from Cousin, Jouffroy and Kant, without a thousand others that might be heaped up) and whose conduct would be a real enigma, if in the writings of those same chieftains we did not find the key to explain it. These, in effect, are the ones who at every step suggest those contradictory notions, or at least the germ of them, giving it to be understood that there exist two kinds of observation, the external and the internal, whereas in reality it is nothing but the same function, applied now to the knowledge of external objects, now to that of internal phenomena; for which reason observation may be classified as internal and external only by reason of its object, but not of its principle; a mode of classifying that is not of the clearest nor of the most scientific, and for that very reason all the more open to censure in this kind of investigation, which more than any other ought to join precept with example in the matter of precision.

To two classes belong those who are in reality detractors, and in appearance eulogists, of the merit of Verulam: those who are so because they see the edifice of their science threatened, and many of these belong to the category of ill will; and the rabble of disciples who, sworn and enslaved under the yoke of fashion, which is authority in another dress, do nothing but repeat the fancy their teachers have hit upon, that Bacon was not a philosopher but the prince of the physicists; and these come under the category of poor understanding, of those of whom Tully said that they give up using their own judgment because they hold as firm and valid whatever they see sanctioned by the oracle they adore: an admirable prescription for not suffering from the head,

[p. 228]

but unfortunately not to be administered to those who find themselves obliged to exercise their own.—And I consider that this is enough, and more than enough, by way of digression on Verulam, a sin of which my friend the Dómine will absolve me, since my observations fall not upon his worship but on no less than the editors of an estimable periodical, the “Revue française et étrangère” of Paris.

It is now time to go on with our lengthy enumeration, and I am even going to finish it for today, still leaving in the inkwell the analysis of the functions of instinct (no. 12º) and that of the resources of criticism and philology (no. 13º), besides others of lesser account; so let the 14º and last motive (for brevity’s sake) be the constant phenomenon that the sciences present in the occasion and manner of their formation. This point may be considered as already touched upon in one of those examined earlier, but I wish to present it under another aspect, and my correspondent will feel the advantage of doing so.—It would indeed be an effect without a cause, were the speculative sciences to exist without having been provoked, so to speak, by the urgent needs of man, or, what is the same, by his most vivid sensations. How was geometry born, a science founded wholly on abstractions? On the banks of the Nile, and on account of its floods, which wiped out the boundaries of the estates, it became very important to determine how far that fertilizing mud, so covetously desired by all the farmers, reached; and behold how in this spot, or in another or in others, under analogous circumstances, the science that measures extension must naturally have been born. So long as the need to measure was not felt, it occurred to no one to measure: this need makes itself felt, and man begins to drive in stakes to mark points, and to stretch cords to mark distances. Now he has a space enclosed between his cords, now he possesses the elements, supplied by nature herself, upon which he is going to work out geometry; now he naturally begins to separate the consideration of the

[p. 229]

point and the distance, now he has the line, now he transfers it to paper, divides it, subdivides it, makes it change direction. The line was in nature, but not separately, rather as the contour or boundary of extension, or as the distance between bodies; and upon these bases, and from abstraction to abstraction, he goes on raising with his understanding the sumptuous edifice of the science. Here we have the invariable law of human reason: to begin with the concrete in order to rise to the abstract; practice before theory, so that afterwards, with the progress of the science, practice may be fertilized anew by theory. This is the eternal circle of man’s knowledge; and it may be asserted in more than one sense that advances in the sciences are made in a curved rather than a straight line, and the history of discoveries bears this out, for at times men have been on the very verge of another discovery by virtue of a newly observed fact, and the investigators take another course, passing right by that point which would have led them most directly to the longed-for goal.

From what has been said may be inferred also the error involved in applying the most humane principle of non ignari mali, miseris sucurrere disco [not unacquainted with misfortune, I learn to succor the wretched] to the question of the origin of science, for although it is true that science always begins with sensations, it is not possible to go on with them alone without the ministry of abstraction and the other intellectual faculties. Thus it is certain that much knowledge is born of sensation alone, but it is not possible to raise the edifice of science without abstraction. The celebrated non ignara mali.... applies most perfectly to the sentiments of humanity, and in that sense I have said elsewhere that sensation is the bond that unites the whole human species: that is to say, God has made me feel, and therefore I can succor the miseries my brothers feel.— Here is the foundation of everything, of morality and of science, but here is not everything, as the Dómine claims, of whom I take my leave for today, warning him that rare is the notable proposition among those

[p. 230]

he lays down in his paper that does not conceal some far-reaching error: a farewell not so dry as it seems at first sight, and he will soon see all the juice we squeeze out of the rivederci [till we meet again] with his devoted correspondent.

❦

[p. 231]

V.

## Rejoinder to the Reply of the “Dómine”

inserted in the Gaceta de Puerto Príncipe of November 17 last (1)

“Pro me laboras.”

[You labor on my behalf.]

(Continued.)

Since it seems to many experts in the matter that the question has been more than sufficiently elucidated in my previous articles, to the point of admitting no further discussion, I shall refrain, in the remainder of my analysis, from alleging any reasons beyond those strictly needed to refute my opponent’s.

I attributed the backwardness of the intellectual sciences to various causes, and chiefly to these three: their difficulty in comparison with the natural sciences, the method followed in them, and the lack of data from which they suffered.—The observation I made in my Memoir placed all these causes in evidence. “What, then, can be the reason (I asked) that the intellectual sciences (the greatest favorites among the Greeks, especially after Socrates), after so many centuries in which the most illustrious geniuses have attacked their study with all the vigor of their capacity.... should still find themselves, so to speak, in swaddling clothes?....” That fact alone, then, was enough to infer either the difficulty, or the lack of method, or that of data, or the three causes combined, as contributing to the backwardness of such sciences. I then showed that the seductive picture presented by the natural sciences, together with the conviction that it was due very notably also to their method, made psychologists of all schools eagerly desire the application of so precious an instrument to the study of the intellectual sciences. But even granting “that the agreeableness of the natural sciences may have contributed effectively to their astonishing progress” (a point that will be aired very shortly), it is still not to be maintained that the “aridity of the psychological sciences must have repelled every genius

(1) Diario de la Habana of January 21, 1839.—(A. Z.)

[p. 232]

that was not truly philosophical.” In fact, the facts contradict such an assertion, since such questions, far from repelling the Greek philosophers, were rather the favorite object of their speculations; and so they were likewise of all philosophers in all ages, before as well as after the Greeks; nor could it be otherwise, for the questions of philosophy are as important to man as they are fundamental with respect to religion and morals, not to speak of the spur of curiosity that goads him to pierce the thick veil covering these questions, “where am I? whence did I come? whither am I going?”—so that even from the very threshold of human knowledge, and mingled with the investigations of external phenomena (which in Greece as everywhere else are always the first in time), we stumble upon the most subtle and abstruse speculations of Metaphysics. Consequently, the fact that such questions are agitated must not be regarded so absolutely as the symbol of a very lofty culture in a people (and it is an observation that the profound Englishman Mr. Mill has also made), since they spring naturally from the crudest conceptions of primitive religion. The nature of the thinking principle, its relations with the body, its future existence and responsibility, are questions we can glimpse even in the annals of the most remote periods, and interwoven, more often than not, with the most sacred of all those in the religious beliefs and ceremonies of peoples. In India, as everywhere else—and without the country of physical religion, Egypt, being an exception to the rule, as will presently be seen—we shall find Philosophy from the first profoundly incorporated with the popular religion. Its most ancient development was the elucidation and interpretation of the Vedas as revelations of the Divinity (1) In Egypt, properly speaking, mental philosophy did not develop; or, to avail ourselves of the expressions of Cousin himself: “there thought

(1) See Cousin, and the Edinburgh Review (1833), from which these data are taken.

[p. 233]

remained in its religious wrapping, without having attained its philosophical form.” And here is another precious datum for the question (for they spring up by the hundreds on every side), since this proves that the Egyptians reached a considerable degree of progress without yet going beyond the physical sciences; and for all that, the germs of metaphysics appear jumbled together and, as it were, confounded in their very theological system; for which reason I indicated a little while ago that not even that country of physical religion, as I have called it, was an exception to the principle laid down, namely that such questions are found in a condition of society not so advanced as would appear at first sight.—But why detain ourselves with these antiquarian’s records, when the very words of the Dómine show how convinced he is of the backwardness of the intellectual sciences? Does he not say “that the agreeableness of the natural sciences may have contributed effectively to their astonishing progress, while the aridity of the psychological sciences must have repelled every genius that was not truly philosophical?” But even if he did not confess it, it is an unquestionable fact that the physical and mathematical sciences are further advanced than the psychological: an advance which all acknowledge to have been achieved in great part by virtue of the excellent method adopted in them. If, then, such sciences exist while the others are still in formation, it is clear that they have existed, and have been able to exist, without their help. Therefore Logic is not a sort of universal instrument or key with which all the doors of human knowledge are opened. Therefore the method is exemplified in each science, without any need to fetch it from elsewhere. Therefore every science naturally has its Logic, in the sense that in none can a step be taken without deducing, without reasoning in a connected chain; but this has not been a Logic applied after being learned in general and as a separate preliminary, but rather “a Logic that is the legitimate daughter of nature, of our faculty of abstraction, and for that very reason, alternately the daughter and the necessary mother of every science.” There is thus demonstrated the

[p. 234]

priority and independence of the natural sciences with respect to the intellectual. This argument, which by itself alone would suffice to decide the question, acquires double force when we consider that the excellence of the method followed in the former is no less demonstrated; a method so exceedingly effective that it has not only raised up some of them, but has created others, and has vivified them all. But according to the very words of the Dómine, the natural sciences are agreeable, while the psychological are arid. Let us see how much these expressions contain. It may well be that agreeableness goes hand in hand with difficulties, and aridity with ease; but if the point is examined as confined to the present question, it will be seen that agreeableness and ease are almost synonymous, no less than aridity and difficulty. For in what does the agreeableness of the physical sciences consist? In their object itself, no doubt, but also in the manner of examining it: in that, everything being submitted to the crucible of experience, everything is seen, everything is touched, everything is made easy...... —And what can be the cause of the aridity of the psychological sciences? It does not lie in a lack of interest on the part of their object, so interesting and attractive to man that even in a very backward and primitive condition he dares to attack, and cannot help attacking, as we have seen, the most abstruse questions of metaphysics: therefore the aridity of psychology does not lie in a lack of attraction. Nor can it consist in a lack of applications, for on this side the important science of psychology errs rather on the side of being fruitful than barren; and this in the judgment of none other than the Dómine himself, who follows and cites Cousin, to whom I also adhere on this particular—or, to put it better, it is a doctrine I have always upheld as my own and as that of all who cultivate the science: namely, that Psychology is the root and foundation of all philosophy, (1) or, speaking

(1) In due time I shall set out for the Dómine the sense in which the text of Cousin must be taken, which he alleges against me, triumphantly as he thinks, as well as that of Locke to the same purpose. He will see in its place, and perhaps to his surprise, that we agree on this point.

[p. 235]

more strictly, of all the intellectual sciences, lending its eminent services to Logic, to Ethics, to Jurisprudence, to History, to Political Economy, etc.; therefore, on the side of its consequences, the science of the faculties of the soul is fruitful enough. In what, then, can that aridity to which the Dómine alludes consist? Nothing remains to be said but that it lies in the very nature of its object, which is not so perceptible to the senses, or in the method that is followed or has been followed in it, or in the lack of preliminary data that it may need from other sciences. But whichever of these be the cause—and a fortiori all of them together—they make Psychology a science incomparably more difficult than the physical sciences: therefore.... (have no fear that I am going to draw the consequence that follows from this, as from all the arguments I have adduced, for the principal question: for now I do not wish to leave the point to which I am confining myself) therefore aridity in the present case is almost, and without the almost, synonymous with difficult and ticklish: therefore to the advantage of being first the natural sciences join that of being easier; therefore it is more natural and simpler to begin with them than with the speculative sciences, which is what was to be demonstrated.—The Dómine, then, is left caught in the nets he has woven for himself.—So that I had more than enough reason to tell him, in my leave-taking, that rare is the notable proposition laid down by him that does not at once disclose, or conceal at bottom, some error of consequence. But let the reader note that in my Memoir I was not even content with the powerful motives of the pre-existence and ease of the natural sciences for recommending their precedence in the plan of studies, but made very special and principal mention, besides other reasons, of one most weighty and decisive in the matter: namely, the good habits that their method must necessarily impart to the understanding of the pupils, training it to make new acquisitions with greater ease and security in whatever branches they might afterwards undertake. This last observation

[p. 236]

should convince the Dómine that in attacking my reasons he must keep them all in view at once, because, lending one another mutual support, together they form a body of proofs through which not a single one of the objections he has so far deigned to make to my paper can penetrate. How, if he did not so often disregard my reasons, could he have said at the beginning of the very paragraph I am refuting that “it does not seem to him that the backwardness of the intellectual sciences ought to be attributed to their difficulty,” as though I had affirmed such a notion just like that, flatly, and without having added, as I did in the very next line, in the same period, “how was it that they were almost in swaddling clothes after so many centuries in which the most illustrious geniuses had attacked their study with all the vigor of their capacity, and as an object of predilection for many of them? A proof all too evident that the difficulty in question is no longer conjectural, nor a subject of discussion, but positive, proved as much by the very nature of the thing as by the method followed, since not even the most eminent minds achieved great advantages in the field of their labors.— Attack, then, more by the rules, my esteemed Mr. Correspondent (whom, be that as it may, I always believe to be in the best of faith), so as to weary yourself and weary us less, by not making me go back over old ground.—Henceforth I shall go on answering with a laconism almost like that of a syllabus, as I have adopted it in some parts of the present writing, for I truly long to finish, leaving you always free to ask me for clarifications toties quoties [as often as] you may need them. Meanwhile, I do not wish to take leave of the paragraph I am picking to pieces without pointing out to you that the agreeableness of the natural sciences could not have had a great share in their astonishing progress, since, with all the pleasure they must have inspired, it is in the last half-century that they have progressed most: before that time they were in many and good hands, all of them were extremely attractive, some of them very necessary to man, and yet they made no advance: can a more complete proof be given that

[p. 237]

their cultivators were not following the proper path? But there is more: the experimental method is adopted, the way of observation and induction, and under its effective influence not only do some sciences advance, but others and still others are created, more and better being achieved in half a century than in the fifty-five preceding. My adversary is no less mistaken in attributing the progress of mathematics, too, to the agreeableness of its study; though somewhat hesitant this time, as is seen from the note he adds, in which, although he hits upon the true cause, he still does not explain the how to us entirely; which is precisely the key to the problem. Of course, in the progress of all branches of knowledge, physical as well as mathematical, the attraction they have in themselves must have some influence up to a certain point; but as regards physics, it is already demonstrated that this is not the principal cause; and as regards mathematics, we shall presently see that its advances are not to be attributed to it either.—They depend, immediately and principally, no less on the nature of the instrument that the very character of the object allows to be applied to it, than on the course or method that this same object permits, and even obliges us to adopt. In this sense I agree with you that mediately the nature of the object has a great share in the perfection of mathematics: a proposition which is in open conflict with the one you lay down in the text, where you attribute their progress principally to their agreeableness, for you say no less than that in your view it is no other cause. This glaring contradiction shows all too clearly that the Dómine’s ideas are not fixed on the subject, and how right I was to tell him that, although in the note he lighted on the true cause, he had not yet entirely hit upon what he was looking for. He glimpsed, roughly and in the gross, that the very abstractions of mathematics, by reason of their extreme simplicity, no doubt (though he does not even express this), must contribute to simplify the science and make it progress; but he did not see, or at least did not specify— and it was this that really illuminated the question—that the immediate causes, though produced by the first, could be

[p. 238]

none other than the signs and the method of mathematics. In fact, as these sciences deal only with the relations of quantity, with very few facts for observation to suggest to the understanding, the understanding can then, by dint of linked abstractions, raise the whole desired edifice, as I affirmed in my Memoir had happened with the celebrated Pascal, who, from the scantiest and most indispensable data on geometry that his father had furnished him, deduced without further help all the fundamental theorems of that science: an undertaking utterly impracticable with respect to the other sciences, in which it is not possible to take a single step without treading upon facts; and here is another proof that the Memoir on method knows how to defend itself; and you can convince yourself more and more, by going over its whole context, of the grounds on which I asserted at the beginning of this paper that there is not one reason of those that come into play in the matter that is not found either developed or sketched in that work, because in reality I have added nothing new since; I have done no more than add some shading to the outline, so that it may be seen in a better light. But returning from this very small digression to what matters most, we shall observe that in mathematics man has, up to a certain point, the power of creating the facts on which he is to found the science, since the slightest circumstance, without need of apparatus or of reagents, produces a countless number of facts which bring with them infinite considerations and give rise to many more, as well as to a multitude of applications. The examples could be multiplied to infinity, taken as well from geometry as from arithmetic, or from any other branch of pure mathematics. It is, then, in the nature of its object, rather than in its agreeableness, that the progress of the science of quantity consists; and in the simplicity and plainness of the signs it employs lies the secret of that concentration of attention, or absorption of all the mental faculties, which the Dómine admires in the mathematician of Syracuse, absorbed to the point of not

[p. 239]

perceiving the storming of a city: and in proof that such concentration is due in great part to the nature of the means employed, and not precisely to the enthusiasm those sciences inspire, we shall observe that there is no science nor art, nor human project, that is not vivified and carried forward by enthusiasm, the sole father of great things, for mediocrity was never enthusiastic; and without making mention of the daring navigators and travelers—among them the prototype of enthusiasm, the illustrious Christopher Columbus—and without leaving the natural sciences and our own day, let us remember that merely to determine the differing respirability of aeriform fluids the chemist-poet H. Davy inhaled even the most mephitic gases; the indefatigable Baron von Humboldt applied two caustics to his back, merely in order to experience the galvanic excitations more keenly and sharply; and the intrepid Eusebio Valli, after inoculating himself with the plague in Europe and in Asia, came all the way to America in pursuit of yellow fever, to defy it on its own stage, falling a victim to his inextinguishable love of humanity, to that enthusiasm which death alone could extinguish, and yet not conquer.

I could make palpable, with a thousand examples, the influence of common signs in the other sciences; but besides this point being very perceptible, there is no room for more this time, as I find myself forced by the fourth page to take my leave until the next mail.

Appendix.

Here is a new fact for the question, one that unites every merit and needs no commentary, since it comes from no less a place than Puerto Príncipe, and from the savory pen of the Lugareño, as patriotic as he is enlightened, who, giving an account of the examinations of the Colegio Calasancio (1) in the Gaceta of the 2nd of this month,

(1) This school was precisely under the direction of Don Miguel Storch, who was none other than “The Dómine” who was contending with Luz Caballero. (A. Z.)

[p. 240]

says, among other things also worth reading, the following:—

“On the 22nd the classes of Logic and Physics presented themselves for examination. It is not our intention to enter into debate about the works that serve as texts for the analysis and demarcation of our mental faculties.... We refrain from entering into the question, because we do not consider ourselves entitled, nor do we find ourselves with sufficient capacity, to give our opinion on the ancient and modern schools, which rest on the one side on columns as massive as a Locke and a Destutt de Tracy, and on the other on a Cousin and a Jouffroy. We should only wish that the Professors of the Logic class would meditate well on the works of all these great minds before sowing the seeds of the one school or the other. This class was followed by that of Physics or Natural Philosophy, much more entertaining for the public, and in which we observed more progress, more quickness in the boys, the result of confidence in their knowledge. Thus they explained with ease and exactness the principles and the nature of the science, the general properties of matter, the various physical phenomena, light, rain, sound, etc., and ended with a brief exposition of the elementary principles of Mechanics, the Theory of motion, etc. It is a pity that the College does not possess the necessary instruments, indispensable for the study of Physics, for the examples in books can never be either so pleasing or so suitable as the material fact that places a physical cause within the reach of all.”

Here goes another parallel fact:—I have heard several students, among the oldest and most learned of a philosophy class in Havana, assert that if arguments were lacking to establish the priority of the study of Physics over that of Logic, the experience of what passes within themselves would convince them to the last degree of evidence, as they feel at every step not only the greater ease, but also the necessity, of appealing to the preliminary knowledge of the physical and mathematical sciences in order to expound and understand psychological doctrines.

[p. 241]

VI.

## Rejoinder to the Reply of the “Dómine”

inserted in the Gaceta de Puerto Príncipe of November 17 last (1).

“Pro me laboras.”

[You labor on my behalf.]

(Continued.)

We come now to the 8th paragraph of the Dómine, in which he explains himself in these terms: “.... the object of Physics is the universe, that of Logic the intelligence: the first offers an immense space to the observer, the second a mathematical point...” Even granting that it is so (a thing no one will admit), I shall answer him that more toil and skill are needed to reach the summit of Mont Blanc, which counts barely three miserable leagues of perpendicular height, than to devour the miles across the delightful plains of Lombardy: here even the most sluggish wayfarers let themselves glide along; there only the most seasoned travelers climb. Difficulties in the sciences, which is the whole question, are graded more by weight than by number, more by quality than by quantity. Nothing is more extensive than natural history, inasmuch as it comprises the description of all the works of God that are within man’s reach, and yet nothing is better suited to the comprehension of the tenderest creatures. Nor is it a question of a child, or even a youth, beginning his studies by traversing the whole vast field of nature: what is done first, for the child, is to choose some chapters of the science, and then, for the youth, some branch of it, such as Physics; and the Dómine will excuse me from stopping to compare the difficulties of a chapter of Physics with those of one of Logic, since he may take it as understood that while a pupil is mastering a single chapter of the latter, he can acquire four of the former; and I appeal to all who teach and learn those sciences, who will not let me lie.

(1) Diario de la Habana of February 10, 1839. (A. Z.)

[p. 242]

But apparently this gentleman has brought up the dimensions of Physics and Logic in order to explain why there have not been so many discoveries in the latter as in the former, and so has made his not very good cause, if that be possible, still worse. In the first place, from the greater or lesser number of discoveries the state of advancement of a science cannot precisely be deduced; inasmuch as in the sciences, as I have already indicated, measure is taken not by quantity but by quality: thus Geometry, for example, such as it already was in the time of Euclid, is a science more advanced, more formed, more rigorously a science, than many of the natural sciences, not to speak of the psychological (which, properly considered, are natural), on which discoveries have rained down; and even if not a single theorem had been added to those the Greek mathematician bequeathed to us, and discoveries in the natural sciences had followed one another without interruption, none of them could compete in exactness with the science of extension. And why? Because res ipsa vetat [the thing itself forbids it].

In the second place, even comparing the natural sciences among themselves, we shall see that progress cannot always be inferred from the number of discoveries, but from their importance. Sometimes countless facts of the same kind are discovered which advance the science in that branch little or not at all, while a single fact of a different character effectively stirs the spirit of speculation and contributes more to the real progress of the science. At other times it happens that, even when the discoveries are important and numerous, they still do not suffice to clear up the question, if it is obscure in itself. In Chemistry more discoveries have been made in these last years than in Physics, and yet no one doubts that the latter science is more advanced than the former. Note, finally, that science is constituted by generalization and classification: so long as facts are not reduced to a key, and wander about independent and scattered, there is no theory, that is, science properly so called; one will not have got beyond the most superficial empiricism.

[p. 243]

“Compare the ancient Logic with that of today,” the Dómine continues, “and one will be forced to confess either that the intellectual sciences are susceptible of a perfection we cannot even conceive, or that at present they are not in swaddling clothes.” Let us take the first part of the dilemma.

If you confess that present-day Logic is more advanced than the ancient, you must necessarily agree, by virtue of the demonstrations set forth in my previous articles, that this progress has been owed to the lights it has received from the natural and exact sciences, and to the method it has imitated from them; so that Logic turns out to be their debtor, in substance as much as in form. And this would be the place to draw up the list of loans those sciences have made to the intellectual ones, but I shall content myself with indicating some of the principal items of that account. Before proceeding to it, what would the advancement of modern Logic over the ancient prove, even if it were independent of the progress of the other sciences? Nothing, except that Logic, being a science, is perfectible too, like any other. And how has my adversary not perceived that his words do nothing in his favor and may do much against him? For the principal question turns on the precedence that ought to be given in study to Physics over Logic, a precedence that, among countless other reasons, was founded on the state of advancement in which the natural sciences stood with respect to the intellectual: that is to say, the backwardness of the latter was not absolute, but relative to the state of the former; and so they were compared with each other in their present situation, so that the comparison might be exact and the argument have force. Of what use is it to you, then, to allege that modern Logic is more advanced than the ancient, when my reasoning left no entry for such an observation, since it was so rigorous that, granting the progress of Logic, the intellectual sciences still turned out today to be, as it were, in swaddling clothes with respect to the natural? —Read the Memoir again with care, and you will see removed all the

[p. 244]

difficulties that have occurred to you up to now, and some more, if not all that can be raised. But let us now come to the few items of the little outstanding account.

Let us open any treatise on Logic whatever (even confining ourselves to the plan and text adopted in our country), and we shall be convinced that there is scarcely a subject, and notably those of most weight, that does not presuppose knowledge derived from the other sciences. It is not possible to take a single step without continually having recourse to them. I will say more (and it is an observation I have made even with respect to young men already very advanced and of more than middling understanding): if theories, or generalizations, are learned without applying them to the data on which they rest, it is absolutely impossible to grasp not only their scope, but even their tendency: thus I have often seen many young men discussing speculative questions of logic or metaphysics without being able to take one true step in the matter—in a word, without understanding them—after repeating generalities taken on credit but not comprehended; inasmuch as they lack the data both for founding and for assaying the doctrines. It is necessary to study particulars more—which, in the judgment of Cousin (and in this I go with him amplectis ulnis [with open arms]), are what form and nourish the understanding—before showing off those generalities, precious, very precious in themselves many of them, but not a few of them like glass beads: they glitter exceedingly, but like those beads they are reduced to impalpable dust at the slightest breath of analysis.

It is plain to see that at every step we take the promised list becomes more superfluous; but it was promised, and here it goes.

1st. To demonstrate rigorously that experience is the starting point of our knowledge, which is the entrance of our course, it becomes necessary to appeal to Physiology in order to dispel all doubts. (1) —In fact,

(1) I leave aside for now, of course, the particular point Cousin raises in his attack on Locke, as to whether or not it is advisable to begin with the

[p. 245]

do we not observe in the infancy of man certain operations which are not really learned, and which for that very reason it is necessary to show do not depend on innate ideas? Thus we touch primo limine [at the very threshold] the importance, and even the necessity, of the study of Physiology for forming a complete history of the inner man. And so as not to cite Destutt de Tracy once more, who maintains that Physiology should be preliminary to the study of the intellectual sciences, I shall cite the Stagirite himself. Does he not speak of a vegetative soul, of an animal one and of an intellective one? Well, then, this is an abridged history of man, for those words are not exactly what they sound like to our modern ears, but in reality present the three stages through which man passes, and which in the end are found united in him: thus he is first a plant in his mother’s womb, then an animal in early infancy, and finally properly a man on reaching the age of discretion. Nor must it be forgotten, in this and other questions, in order to avoid grave difficulties and apparent contradictions, that Aristotle does not take the word soul in the sense we give it, but in its own primitive sense of breath or life in the first two cases, and only in the third in the present-day acceptation, as is evident from various passages of his works, for it is beyond doubt that he establishes the spirituality of the thinking principle. That is why it was said in the Syllabus cited several times (in which the endeavor has been to instill in youth true philosophical freedom, that is, no authority of school, but no presumption either) “that even in the case of not being able to understand what the ancient philosophers said, it would be a vulgarity to despise them, considering that experience has more than once taught us that their words, under the veil of paradox, enfold weighty conceptions and profound observations.”

question of the origin of ideas, a question that you will see treated at length by me before long, for at the moment my intention is only to make a list.

[p. 246]

2nd. The very act of treating first of all of the intellectual operations as an indispensable preliminary indicates that Psychology must also precede Logic. And I wish to take this opportunity to elucidate the question further, or press it harder, on this point.

One of three things: either Logic comes down to saying “draw good consequences from every premise” (which is really and truly its object, or, better said, that of method), and then there is nothing more to add, and it is enough to say with our illustrious Varela, “do it well, and you have done it well”; or Logic is the science that treats of the formal laws of reasoning, and then there is no doubt that little or nothing has been advanced in it since Aristotle, but it is also incontestable that even so reduced it still requires a great deal of knowing, and even presupposes other knowledge; or, finally, we understand by Logic, with the Dómine and the greater part of the moderns, “the study of the mental faculties in order to direct them in the investigation of truth, that is, the science whose object is to set forth the phenomena and theory of the intelligence,” and then all the arguments I have presented in the course of this discussion have irresistible force, for Logic presupposes much and varied knowledge; which is why I told the Dómine that I could not make out how he had printed such a definition, which contained the most victorious refutation of his principles. Even in the second acceptation, that is, the restricted one of the word Logic, many of the arguments presented hold good.

But let us suppose that all I have said to demonstrate the relative backwardness of the speculative sciences is worth nothing; let us suppose that Logic owes nothing to the other branches, and that they are its debtors; let us grant for argument’s sake that it is the most advanced of the sciences, and even that it cannot possibly advance further, owing to the perfection it has reached. Let us concede that the moral sciences do not need as many experiments as the physical, though those they do need are not few; that many of them were cultivated with success from the most remote

[p. 247]

antiquity; let us hold as null and void and of no effect whatever we have written on the subject; and with all that, and in spite of all that, our point of view on the principal question, that of method, remains standing. Nothing can be clearer, and it comes down to this simple syllogism: “Method requires that one begin with what is easiest and most intelligible; but Physics, and the natural sciences in general, are easier to acquire than the speculative sciences, and more within the reach of youth; therefore, if both things are to be taught, and one wishes to proceed with method, one must begin with the former.”

Granted, by all means, that from the acquisition of Logic, and of the intellectual sciences in general, light results for the other sciences, for, as Tully said, all human knowledge has a common bond; but it is not good logic to infer from this that one must begin with it.

I shall recapitulate, with the brevity with which I have just done so for the principal argument, the other cardinal reasons on which the question turns. In the study of the natural and mathematical sciences one gains not only in method but also in one’s stock of data, for there can be no exercise more suited to all the intellectual faculties in their first steps: therefore this consideration too pleads for their precedence (not preference). Who does not conceive—or, better said, who does not touch at every step—the perfection with which those sciences can be acquired without the previous study of Logic? And I say the perfection on purpose, that is, that they need nothing but themselves in order to reason soundly in them, which is the ultimate end of Logic. As for mathematics, it was never doubted that they could be acquired with perfection before or after, that is, in entire independence of every other branch; and rather they themselves, as an instrument, are necessary preliminaries in almost all. But all the opposition is directed against Physics—and why? Let it be frankly confessed: because it had not been done so, not because it ought not to be done. Still,

[p. 248]

not content with alleging so many reasons, I sought to explain historically the why of such an anomaly, so as to leave the minds of my readers better satisfied. But are authorities perchance awaited before deciding, as Mr. Rumilio expressed the wish from the very beginning?— Well, there they are by the hundreds, as I showed in my Memoir, saying that in cultivated Europe this business had already passed into the authority of a thing adjudged; that everywhere the physical sciences are taught in entire independence of the intellectual, etc., etc. If Mr. Dómine still wants texts, here is Article 41 of the Regulations of the First Normal School of France, drawn up, or at least sanctioned, by its director, Mr. Cousin, from whom my adversary cites a lengthy passage in a tone of triumph. The said article, then, says: “All candidates for the degree of bachelor of sciences, without distinction, follow in the first year the course of general and experimental Physics.” In Holland, a country as classic as Germany in matters of public instruction, according to Article 6 of the Regulations for the teaching of mathematics, in the lyceums as well as in the universities, it is provided, not merely that the examination in mathematics shall precede the study of the physical sciences, but that “it shall precede even that which is undergone in order to qualify for the various degrees of candidates (1) in letters.” It would be easy for me to go on heaping up texts upon texts, as well with respect to the educational regulations of the countries indicated as of Germany, England, and even Spain itself, as I have already done in citing the Article of the Regulations of 1825 for the colleges of humanities of the Peninsula, where it is ordained that physics, chemistry and natural history, and of course mathematics, be studied before Philosophy strictly so called, into which Logic enters as one of its principal branches.

Proceeding in good faith, it is not possible to cast any further doubt

(1) In Holland there are only two university degrees, that of candidate and that of doctor.

[p. 249]

on this matter, and it might well be considered closed here; but since I have not finished the list I began, and parts of the Dómine’s communication still remain to be analyzed, I shall be obliged to leave the conclusion for another mail, noting for today that the spirit of those provisions is the same that animates, and has always animated, all my efforts in the matter of method; namely, “that there is but one science,” and that it is necessary, as Cousin also cries out, to fertilize the field of some sciences with the seeds of others. It is thus that we shall put an end to superficiality, and thus only that we shall be something in the world of intelligences.

NOTE.—Although the preceding article ends with the offer of another to put an end to this polemic, the truth is that the latter was never published, nor does it exist among the manuscripts of Luz Caballero; and his not having written it is explained by the fact that the intervention of a third writer of articles, who signed himself “Adicto,” made him abandon the almost finished series and begin a new one.

To the respected Lic. Don José Tomás de la Victoria, resident in Puerto Príncipe, who took part in the question as a note of Luz Caballero’s reveals (p. 200), I owe the knowledge of who the persons were that were concealed under the pseudonyms of Rumilio and El Dómine. Mr. de la Victoria says: “The pseudonym ‘Rumilio’ was that of Lic. Don Manuel Castellanos Mojarrieta, the same who, having settled in that capital, became Secretary of its Most Excellent City Council. ‘El Dómine’ was the other Licentiate, Don Miguel Storch, a Catalan, formerly director of the school then called ‘El Liceo Calasancio.’”—(A. Z.)

❦

[PDF 238]

❖     ❖     ❖      ❖     ❖     ❖     ❖      ❖       ❖       ❖       ❖       ❖        ❖       ❖   ❖

P hilosophy .

## QUESTION OF METHOD

## ( polemic with “ adicto .”) (1)

I. (2)

“Et pulsanti aperietur.”

[And to him that knocketh it shall be opened.]

Here I am, more than justified in my resolve to answer the Dómine of Puerto Príncipe so at length on the question of method, even against the opinion of many and reputable experts who judged it more than moderately elucidated, and even beyond discussion, after my first three articles of reply. Now a new champion presents himself, but with the modest shield of a doubter, offering new objections, (3) confined to the doctrines set forth by me in the Memoir that stands printed in those of the Patriotic Society for the month of September last. It is somewhat singular

(1) In the course of the preceding polemic the present one arose, which is in reality a mere continuation of the former, and was provoked by an article inserted in the Diario de la Habana of February 12, 1839, signed by Adicto, which article was followed by others in the Diarios of the 14th, 15th and 18th of the same month. (A. Z.).

(2) Diario de la Habana of February 16, 1839.—(A. Z.).

(3) We shall see whether they all are, or whether perhaps they turn out to be the same ones dressed in other garments.

[p. 252]

indeed that, the doubts being confined to the Memoir, they should not have drawn breath until five months after its publication, the objector living in Havana; but after all, such conduct may admit of an easy way out, by alleging illness, absence, occupations, time to ruminate the matter, or above all the powerful reason, for the case, that it was not the will of the writer of the article to publish his ideas until the present. But what does not allow of so simple an explanation is the confining of the doubts to the Memoir, when for months I have been publishing, and even at this very moment am publishing, glosses and more glosses upon the doctrines maintained there; my astonishment rising higher still when I consider that he has not even cared to finish hearing my reasons.

So then, either the new impugner has not read a single one of my latest articles, or the new reasons I have set forth in them do not weigh so much as a dram in the balance of his criticism, or I do not deserve to be allowed to finish speaking without interruption. Who does not know that when one presses on in a question one always starts from the point at which it was left? Either the impugner has proposed to himself (for he does not say so) to fight me so step by step that he will not leave a sound bone in a single one of all the articles on the matter published and to be published by me, and so has chosen to start from the root; or perhaps he was impatient to take the floor, bored by now with hearing the previous speaker hold forth, with no hope of the weather clearing (thus I am coming round to the opinion of my friends the experts, who consider the matter already beyond doubt; and then the doubts proposed will not be doubts, but rather an itch to doubt) though it will be said that I am treating my part as good as concluded—a suspicion that in no way whatever touches my new antagonist, who I judge has taken up the pen with the most perfect candor, and whom for that very reason I proceed to answer so minutely. But let us have done with further charges or preambles of this kind, in order to occupy ourselves exclusively with the principal business: now for Logic and method, and even for tactics,

[p. 253]

so as not to have to write eternally, and, last and worst—which is the most stubborn of all—with the grief of having preached in the wilderness. Taking, then, as pleaded all that appears in the record, and above all since the new impugner reproduces many of the objections presented by the other party, let us reduce the question to the briefest terms possible, stating each of the principal arguments, so that this gentleman may point out to me the points on which he agrees and those on which he dissents, in order to bring the discussion to a happy conclusion, and to examine one by one the disputed points in a separate file. In this way there will be no confusion of any kind, nor will it be possible to reproduce an argument already answered, unless it be by reinforcing it with new and effective reasons: thus we shall write, it is true, perhaps a dozen articles on the one side and the other; but each of them being necessarily short, together they will scarcely make up one very long one, with the inestimable advantage of proceeding with a logical rigor that will not allow the principal reasons at play in the question to be smothered under heterogeneous matters. In a word, the task is to solve for the unknown, and there is no more suitable means than to adopt, if possible, formulas as simple as algebraic ones.—So let us come to the reckoning.

1st—The question is purely one of method, and, being so, the consideration alone of the greater ease of the physical sciences over the intellectual ones, in case both have to be taught, is decisive for precedence. Tell me, then, categorically and ante omnia [before all else], in your answer, whether you do or do not hold Physics to be more within the reach of beginners than Logic. Yes or no—nothing more.

2nd—Say likewise whether the first is not more agreeable than the second, and for that very reason better suited to inspire love of study in tender beginners.

3rd. Say also whether that procedure is not more natural in which one starts from the known to the unknown, or, better said, in which one does no more than continue in that branch of which the young

[p. 254]

beginner has most knowledge; since observing the phenomena of the universe comes before observing the phenomena of the understanding, which, according to Locke’s most happy simile, fares at first like the eyes, which see everything and do not see themselves; for until much time has passed, and consequently many experiences, the child’s understanding does not begin to reflect upon itself—and here comes in that long and precious quotation from Jouffroy which I printed in my third article, if I remember rightly.

4th Item, say whether you believe that in order to profit in algebra, in geometry or in any branch of mathematics, a previous course of the science of Logic is needed?

5th Item, say in the same way whether you conceive that there is any matter in which a step can be taken without the exercise of reasoning;— and, in case you answer in the affirmative, as is to be expected, how you believe it necessary to make the theoretical study of the instrument precede that of the very matter which of itself obliges us to employ it in the best way possible.

6th Say likewise how you conceive the formation of any theory whatever, and what a theory is.

7th Item, say plainly in how many senses you take the word Logic— and which of them is applicable to the courses followed among us.

8th Say also whether Logic as we learn it here needs or does not need the preliminaries of other physical, mathematical, and even philosophical branches, and whether many of these cannot be acquired to perfection in entire independence of it (Logic, as a separate science.)

9th Say whether the acquisition of language (and there is nothing more logical or more philosophical) presupposes that logic previously learned, or only the natural, most natural and necessary use of our innate faculties of perceiving, abstracting, remembering, deducing, etc., etc.

10th Say whether, when one is going to teach grammar, for example, one begins with the doctrines of syntax, or with the individual knowledge of the parts of speech.

[p. 255]

Answer, then, this simple interrogatory, while I meanwhile proceed to answer your paper point by point.—It will be a prolix, tedious analysis; but you and fate have willed it so; and I have resolved to make myself understood, and I am going to see whether I succeed.

From the beginning you give it to be understood that you take the question in all the breadth the Dómine gave it, since you undertake to compare the physical sciences with the moral sciences. It would be easier for you gentlemen to defend yourselves if you presented the question in a more restricted form, that is, if, taking the word Logic, as Kant does, for example, in the proper and restricted sense of the science of the formal laws of reasoning, to the exclusion even of Psychology, you maintained that it was a necessary, or at least a suitable, introduction to the study of many sciences; and even with this reservation you still could not hold your ground, as I have demonstrated in one of my articles, and shall demonstrate again later for the forgetful. Or else, proceeding with more consistency, with more logic, you maintained that Logic so considered—and this is what the philosopher of Königsberg intends—is independent of all the other sciences: which does not mean either, in sound logic, that all these latter are dependent on it; and then you would be truly impregnable. But to take the question in one sentence in all its breadth, and in the very next line to restrict in the reasoning the meaning of the word Logic, straining to demonstrate what no one ever attempted to deny, is the height of inconsistency; and the sure means of never getting out of an eternal vicious circle, of one of those arguments that, as they used to say in the schools, prove nothing, by proving too much; for in this way we should come to rest, beyond remedy, in this: that reasoning on any matter whatever, even the most trivial, would not be possible without the previous study of the science of analysis; or in other terms, that Logic would be the mother of reasoning, when it is incontestably its legitimate daughter, or a necessary consequence of its existence. And mind, I do not attribute to you the express contention that a previous logic is indispensable

[p. 256]

for the use (good or bad, which is not the question of the moment) of reasoning; but only that such is the consequence into which your own principles must of necessity drag you.—How is it possible that any knowledge can be acquired—except that which, following the Peripatetics in this, Descartes called first principles, which depend solely on the impression just experienced, with no other aid than perception, as, for example, the idea of sound, of color, and even many of those that bear upon internal phenomena— without all the mental faculties coming into exercise, among which so principal a part is played by that of deducing in forming the greater part of our notions? If, then, this exercise is called Logic, there is no dispute, nor can there be.—But let the writer of the article tell me, where did man learn such a logic? Nowhere: it is necessary, it is instinctive, and in this sense the mother of all science; or, to speak with more rigor, this method, this procedure which we necessarily and naturally follow in the investigation of things, we call logical if it offers the due concatenation; so that logical comes to be a synonym of connected, or rigorously deduced. Thus we know what is or is not well deduced by virtue of the reason which the Creator granted us for that office, just as we distinguish whether an object is crooked or straight by virtue of our having been endowed with eyes to see: without its being necessary that we should previously have been given a lesson in seeing. How could those rules for avoiding errors and drawing good consequences have been given, except as themselves deduced from the observations which we necessarily had to make in the exercise of our faculties? Which, then, comes first, giving rules or experiencing? How can one conceive even the very formation of Logic, even in its restricted sense, without starting from the basis of observation? Here is the order and the place of each thing: first, there must be external objects; then the mental faculties are exercised upon them, acquiring notions of the external

[p. 257]

world, and in this exercise the mind has afterwards observed what happened to itself while it was observing bodies; afterwards it has generalized and classified these observations by means of its instrument, of its reason, not of the rules, which are not yet formed (their time and place will come to them); then it will discover certain invariable laws, and by virtue of them we shall come in the end to this, that it will finally dictate rules or precepts for proceeding thereafter. To sum it all up, and to reduce everything to one formula: first observation, second science, third art; which is what properly executes what science has legislated by virtue of its supreme power of observing, owed to the faculties with which our munificent Maker endowed the understanding.
