Therefore even that restricted logic, confined only to the theory and rules of analysis, presupposes observation, and observation the exercise of our faculties. And do things go otherwise in any other science in the world? Let us cast our eyes over mathematics, over physics; let us see how they were formed, and we shall be convinced that they do not owe their existence to that of logic as a science. But these demonstrations I have already made, and at great length, in my previous articles, and you may, if they do not suit you, impugn them. Let us come to the doubt that might perhaps be raised: it will be said that no one has claimed that logic is the creator of the sciences as regards the materials peculiar to each one, but that these are the offspring of observation applied specially to each branch. Very well: then, if this is granted me, the sciences were already created with the materials and the instrument of observation: in that case, what is left for logic to do in the making of science, since it invents neither the sciences nor their axioms? “To pass over them with a cuique in sua arte credendum [each must be believed in his own art],” as the great Verulam profoundly said. How is it that people will not understand so clear a question? Whatever the doctrines maintained in these matters, or the sense in which the word logic is taken, the question is asked, on the

[p. 258]

supposition that our courses of philosophy consist on the one hand of physical sciences, and on the other of intellectual and moral sciences, at the head of which goes Logic: with which of these groups is it more suitable, or, in other words, more methodical, to begin?

But not only is it more suitable, because easier, to begin with the natural sciences; it is also the case that by the mere fact of studying them we are exercising our faculties in an admirable manner, so as to leave them well trained in investigation: so that, without our being told in them to follow good method, we are following the best and the most adaptable to our condition, remaining in consequence trained for the future when we devote ourselves to other, different branches. The situation of one who learns in this order may very well be compared to that of a raw traveler whom, without confusing him with a minute account of the road he must follow so as not to go astray, one provides beforehand with signposts at all the doubtful points, against the time when he reaches each of them: to which of the two means of marking the way will the inexperienced wayfarer give the preference?; or, if this simile does not suit, here you have another, perhaps still more exact: to place the young man in the field of nature, obliging him in case of doubt to appeal to the revelations of experience, is as if one were put into a machine in which one is necessarily compelled to walk straight, and, in case of a fall, the cause of the fall is pointed out, and for that very reason one is taught to distrust one’s own strength, and hence to avoid new mishaps. How is it possible that anyone who has called himself to account for what has passed in him and in others in the acquisition of any science or art, or language, can harbor the least doubt on this point? In fact, I close my eyes and my books, and I ask myself: how did you acquire your physical knowledge? Did what logic taught you have any part in it? None, of no kind whatever. For logic, or rather the special philosophy

[p. 259]

or the why of each science comes after its acquisition, as we shall see presently. I needed, for example, to study the motion of bodies, and I set about making or reading various observations and experiments concerning bodies in motion, deducing then, necessarily, by virtue of my faculty of deducing—which nobody taught me (a thing it is even ridiculous to utter), but which God granted me— certain special laws that govern these phenomena: I can, then, become completely versed in mechanics, and shall be able to resolve whatever doubts may be put to me concerning it, without the previous study of logic. Now, to be sure, in this same mechanics and the other physical sciences I may have noted their special features, what characterizes them, their critical part, their philosophy, the why and the course of their procedures: all of them things that are so many precious data for the intellectual sciences and consequently for the theory of analysis; but data which presuppose the study, or exercised observation, of the aforesaid natural sciences: therefore these rather fertilize the intellectual sciences than are fertilized by them, although all of them communicate their respective lights to one another. But could I have acquired even the foundations of mechanics without arithmetical and geometrical notions at least? Impossible, I answer at once; for I remember that in motion I had to measure time and distance, observe direction, estimate forces: the straight line and the oblique had to come forward, and the parallelogram and the diagonal, and the angles and the circle, and numbers, and even the whole retinue of algebraic formulas with their various combinations; here I see, at least, mathematics as an instrument and material with which I must be familiar beforehand. From all which I deduce that mechanics is a physico-mathematical science; a philosophical, theoretical, special conclusion, which I have reached nevertheless—what am I saying, nevertheless?—precisely by virtue of the likewise special study I have pursued of mechanics itself. So, then, I do not claim to isolate some sciences

[p. 260]

from others; on the contrary, I have always maintained that they are all diverse branches of the same trunk, that is, that there is but one science, or that all are more or less sciences of observation. Mathematics, being among those most dependent on reason, cannot fail likewise to start from observation, although it is among those that can advance furthest with comparatively fewest observations, its object, which rests on the suppositions we make, allowing it to do so; so that in this respect there is no science more rigorously logical than mathematics, in which, through the simplicity of the relations examined, as well as through the signs employed, it is possible for the science to constitute itself by itself with the greatest rigor and exactness, in entire independence of any other —an independence which no one who understands the nature of that science will be able to deny it with respect to Logic, understood in whatever sense you please. On this subject I will recall the amusing case of an eminent mathematician who, having spent a great part of his life investigating and meditating exclusively upon his mathematics, without having devoted himself to any other branch, chanced to open a book entitled “The Art of Thinking,” and exclaimed at once: “What! So now I have to learn to think! And what have I been doing all my life?” Rare will be the mathematician who would not at once say as much; though I do not claim that by that circumstance alone, however much to the point, he will think rightly; for neither mathematics, nor logic, nor any method whatever is of any avail for certain understandings that never reason with exactness, a thing that depends on the very mental faculties of the individual. Wherefore it will be observed in general that rules serve rather to avoid errors than to produce truths, or, what is the same, are more negative than positive, if not always in the letter, at least in the effect of their prescriptions. This does not prevent them from leading indirectly to positive results, there being verified in this respect that celebrated algebraic principle that “minus times minus gives

[p. 261]

plus.” Lastly, my impugner, who before examining the question has turned his eyes upon antiquity, must have stumbled in the first pages of the history of Philosophy upon the name of Pythagoras of Samos, who by the force of his genius discovered the elements of the mathematical sciences, especially of Arithmetic, Geometry, Music and Astronomy, centuries before his compatriots busied themselves with creating Logic properly so called, as may also be seen from the words of Aristotle which I quoted in my last article in answer to the Dómine of Puerto Príncipe. For although it is true that the Stagirite is charged with giving obscure accounts of the systems of his predecessors, so that his own may appear more precious and original, such is not the case with regard to the expressions to which we allude, which have nothing mysterious or veiled about them, but are far too categorical and circumstantial for him to have dared, had things not been as he says, to publish them so openly in the presence of his learned and intelligent compatriots: besides which, all the historians agree on it, in company with the most eminent metaphysicians, among whom, as we also saw, are counted a Kant and a St. Hilaire. Let it be noted likewise—what matters for our purpose—that Aristotle did not come into the world until more than 200 years after Pythagoras. Moreover, when we see in the history and the nature of the science itself the evident causes of its progress, we can ill attribute that progress to other, extraneous causes. If I conceive how much light the demonstration of the equilateral triangle, for example, must have afforded to the progress of Geometry, how am I to attribute such advances to the state of the science of logic, or to other remote and extraneous causes? In every science nothing else will be discovered but the application of reasoning to the observations made on purpose, or presented by chance. And since we are on the history of Philosophy, let the impugner note likewise that the starting point of Philosophy

[p. 262]

was the question of the origin and of the elementary principle of the world: Philosophy at once tried to resolve it, applying experience and reflection, now to the matter of sensation, as the Ionian school did; now to its form, as the Pythagorean practised; next it tried to resolve it by the opposition of experience and reason, as was seen in the school of Elea; and finally, as came about in the atomistic school, by the union of the one and the other. A physical question eminently; for with Physics, with the examination of external objects, all Philosophy must necessarily begin; and see here how history agrees with the other demonstrations I have presented on the subject in my previous articles, to which I have to refer my adversary at every step (although he has not deigned to confine himself to them), so as not to weary the public with repetitions, of which it has tolerated too great a number from us, and still has more to tolerate, since we are compelled to it; but let it always remember, in my exoneration, the apostle’s insipiens factus sum, sed tu me coegiste [I am become a fool, but thou hast compelled me]. But even though one science may have come before or after another, or may have taken more or less time to form, this circumstance by itself ought not to influence the decision of the question of method. One must not confuse the issues. In that of method, what must decide is the circumstance of greater ease and that of better preparation; and these might very well concur, as in fact happens, in some branches of later and even very modern formation. Thus my eagerness to refute my antagonist on this point of the priority of the sciences is not so much to defend my plan as to make him see the inexactness of his ideas; for such is the nature of the question that, even were the prior existence of all the intellectual sciences demonstrated, the physical sciences being today easier to understand, and it being clear that they can be acquired to perfection without the aid of Logic, that suffices, and more than suffices, to decide the contest over precedence.

[p. 263]

But let us not carry these preliminary considerations further, sufficient by themselves to move in favor even the mind most prejudiced against, especially if it has steeped itself in those set forth in the last articles in answer to the Dómine; although perhaps they will not produce the longed-for effect of convincing the new doubter, whom, I repeat, I believe to be in perfectly good faith in his opinion; and for that very reason I shall be excused for refuting one by one all the ideas in his paper I believe inexact, and for marking the contradictions into which I judge he has fallen, all of it with the greatest possible brevity; for even keeping to this plan, I fear with only too much reason that my scrutiny will turn out too long. So extensive is my adversary’s work, and unfortunately so much is lacking in it, and so much is superfluous!

It is true that “to the moral sciences we owe our most finished institutions in the moral order;” but that does not prove that the moral sciences owe nothing to the physical sciences or to observation, which is the point in controversy: on the contrary, those words indicate that institutions are the result of science, that is, of the previous observation of phenomena and of their classification; there being a palpable contradiction—and let it be the first noted —between maintaining on the one hand that morality is a science of observation, and then in the very next line “that it only calls for the aid of reason to put itself on the level of the most limited capacity.” In that case there are no experiments, no observations to wait for in the moral sciences, for the understanding of a number of questions already resolved, and the resolution of many others still to be settled. It is true that you are dazzled by the following reasoning, which is the soul of the greater part of your doubts: the moral sciences treat of the feelings and ideas of men, so that man carries his object and his instrument with him, and has not therefore to wait for great study or chance to reveal their secrets to him, as happens in the science of nature, in which centuries pass without the needed

[p. 264]

discoveries being made, inasmuch as the object is outside us; so that when the physical sciences were in swaddling clothes, the moral sciences were already very far advanced. Here, in summary —and rather strengthened than weakened—is my antagonist’s Achilles. Let us now see whether it can withstand the slightest breath of analysis. In the first place, under the generic name of moral sciences, or by another name intellectual or speculative sciences, properly or rather improperly so called (which is not to the point now, though it will be later), are comprised Psychology, Logic, Metaphysics, Ethics, Legislation, Politics and Public Economy. As to the first, no one doubts that it needs to receive light from Physiology, a natural science, if only to draw rightly the dividing line between the phenomena of sensibility and those proper to the understanding, without counting the countless questions that either can be decided only with its aid, or can be admirably illuminated by it. Thus far, then, a debt of the moral science Psychology to the natural science Physiology; and I believe we shall be agreed; but if my adversary is not, he may let me know, so that I may proceed to a longer demonstration of that single particular, although many are the matters that call us. Logic. This, even taken in the strict sense of the formal laws of reasoning, has to be indebted to other branches; and if it is understood in the broad sense, which is how it is taught both in our courses and in those of Europe, and which for that very reason constitutes our principal question, there is scarcely an important chapter of it that does not derive light from, or is not wholly the offspring of, other sciences, and among them very directly of Psychology, Mathematics and Physics.—Both points have been demonstrated in my answer to the Dómine, although the second is still pending, because, not contenting myself with some examples (which is enough for my proof), I offered him a long list of debts, which I left interrupted. Now comes Metaphysics; and as regards it I have demonstrated, not only in my own opinion, but

[p. 265]

in that of the foremost experts, that it is today scarcely more than a desideratum: so many are the abstruse questions with which it has to occupy itself, many of which will probably remain forever insoluble, that some even deny it the name of science, though I am not of that number. Thus, as regards metaphysics properly so called, you will relieve me of further proof of its relatively backward state. We come now to Morality; and here is what has most dazzled you in the question, for you have said to yourself: How could Morality fail to be more advanced than the physical sciences, when without Morality societies cannot exist; when we find Morality even in the most barbarous hordes; when we find Morality cultivated with such brilliant success by the foremost minds of antiquity and from the remotest times; when without Morality responsibility cannot exist, and consequently it is eternal and invariable, deeply engraved in the heart of man, and independent of every system: see, in confirmation, to what high degree of science the Morality of the Crucified attained; whereas the conquests in the physical sciences have been the slow and protracted work of time, men having formed the most mistaken ideas of the causes of phenomena through a long course of centuries: witness, among innumerable others, the hypotheses on the system of the Universe until the coming of Copernicus into the world.—Nor will you throw it in my face that I weaken your reasons; for, without even finishing the dissection of the various members of your Achilles, I have strengthened him with the most powerful musculature that can be attached to him.—But in the first place it is necessary to distinguish between practical Morality and moral science, for plainly in some parts of the objection the word is taken in the first sense, and in others in the second. In this question, as in others of its kind, the unknown would always be much cleared up by fixing correctly the meaning in which the terms are taken: if the infant state of knowledge had been distinguished from its state of greater progress, and

[p. 266]

especially if due attention had been paid to the essential difference between communicating doctrines by mere authority (as happens in the cradle of society by means of religion) and investigating the principles, relations and causes of things by diligent study (which is what constitutes science), then the whole controversy would have come down to no more than a miserable play on words.— Indeed, no great progress in moral science is needed to know, for example, that death is an evil and life a good, illness an evil and health a good; for it is enough merely to have been born to feel it, and consequently nothing is more natural than that men, as a necessary consequence, should hold immoral whatever tends to destroy those goods or to foster those evils.—Here, then, we have Morality, which necessarily starts from our first impressions, without such a thing, nor even the code that might be founded on such bases, yet deserving the name of moral science. Unjust beyond measure would the Supreme Maker have been had he left the foundation of responsibility to the long lapse of time, or to the investigations of science, while making us responsible. Precisely the same, neither more nor less (though, strictly, rather more than less)—as we shall see presently—happens with the physical part. How much knowledge even the most savage man possesses of the nature that surrounds him, and how much and how many items of it he applies to his needs, getting the best out of them, without attaining to the true causes, without yet having the science, which consists in the doctrine, in the why of things and their relations— and here is how sometimes, not always, as I showed elsewhere, art naturally precedes science, the observation of facts always precedes reasoning. If we undertook even to make a brief survey of this physical knowledge of the savage, we should see how far it exceeds the intellectual and moral knowledge he possesses. He has a notion of foods and their action, he knows poisons and antidotes, he knows the seasons and their influence on his

[p. 267]

body and on vegetation, or on hunting and fishing; he builds himself a hut, although he is not yet an architect: he fills the need, but he has not yet arrived at art, nor at science. He knows the action of fire on bodies, he knows the qualities of certain animals, he measures time by means of the stars, and for that very reason studies their phases and respective positions, and yet he is still no astronomer: he knows a number of minerals, plants and animals, and yet he is no naturalist: he cures the ailments of his suffering brother by virtue of the secrets he possesses, and yet he is no physician; in short, if the most savage man were to set forth all he knows in regard to physical nature, and in great part even in regard to its causes and relations, it would be a matter for filling many volumes.—But why do I linger over this idle demonstration? Are the facts that serve as the basis of Morality, perchance, of an essentially different character? Is it not the impressions man receives that afford it its foundations? Is not reason the agent in both cases? We have, then, that it is very inexact to affirm that in the moral sciences we must know more because we carry within ourselves the instrument and the object of the science. It will be said that in respect of Morality and Legislation antiquity has left us monuments more finished, more scientific, than in the field of the natural sciences, as is proved by the political and religious systems of the Moseses, Lycurguses, Solons, Numas, the Roman legislation, and above all the very religion of the Crucified, without counting the respectable phalanx of celebrated and truly scientific works on Ethics, Politics and Legislation left us by the most eminent philosophers, Greek as well as Roman. Before answering this objection, allow me to recall that even if the case were such as it is represented, still all the monuments in moral and legislative science put together could not for an instant sustain the comparison, as regards scientific rigor, with Euclid’s elements of Geometry alone. And never forget, sir, this datum so precious for the

[p. 268]

question. Coming now to answer directly, I will say: that worthy to be set in comparison with those cited are the works of Aristotle and Theophrastus, of Epicurus, of Pythagoras, of Hippocrates and of Pliny on natural phenomena, and the discoveries made by the Egyptians and Greeks in Astronomy, in Geometry, in Music, and in general in the physico-mathematical sciences. Aristotle’s famous natural history alone is a treasure, inestimable not merely for its precious facts but for its admirable classification; a monument to which there is nothing to oppose, either ancient or modern, in the judgment of the first authority on the subject, Georges Cuvier: to Hippocrates, the first observer of antiquity, what do we oppose for his work de aëre, locis et aquis, considering the age of the world in the epoch he reached? And what shall we say of the immense knowledge in Mechanics, in Hydraulics and in all the auxiliary physico-mathematical sciences presupposed by those sumptuous monuments of Egyptian, Persian, Greek and Roman architecture? Let their temples and their palaces speak for me, their pyramids and sarcophagi, their roads and their canals, their aqueducts and their fountains, their ports and their ships. Only open the works of Vitruvius, and anyone will be astounded not only at the profound knowledge in the physical and mathematical sciences displayed by the eminent architect, but at the eminently scientific method with which they are set forth. Let their statues speak for me.... ah! their statues..... they speak well enough by themselves: has there ever been seen a delicacy, a finish greater than that offered by the sculpture of the Greeks? Who does not stand enraptured, pained, involuntarily mimicking the contortions of the priestly father, on contemplating the group of the Laocoön! Who had a more intimate feeling for nature, or how could it have been reached except by the most minute observation of external forms, of physical facts?— Canova, the first artist of the age, would fling away his chisel in despair each time he looked at one of those fragments

[p. 269]

rescued by England from the ruins of the Parthenon: and with reason, for I, merely from seeing those majestic figures from behind, would stand astounded at the expression of their air and attitudes, and absorbed in contemplating the superhuman power by which it had been possible to animate those colossal stones in that manner. “Potens est homo de lapidibus istis suscitare (non filios Abrahæ— apage!) sed filios animæ suæ.” [Man is able of these stones to raise up (not children unto Abraham—away with that!) but children of his own soul.]— Let even the relics unearthed from Pompeii and Herculaneum speak for me with regard to their artifacts, and there will be observed, in works of necessity as in those of mere ornament, for public and for domestic use, in metals as in stones, an exquisite taste and finish, which will always be the delight no less than the despair of modern craftsmen.—What physical knowledge of bodies and their laws do these arts not presuppose? The very arts of war, all founded on physical knowledge—to what state of advancement did they not attain among those notable peoples of antiquity? Gymnastics, to which the ancients gave so much importance, not only as a powerful weapon of war, but very especially on account of the influence of the physical on the moral, which they perceived from the remotest times (“mens sana in corpore sano” [a sound mind in a sound body]), offers another precious argument of the height their knowledge of nature had reached. But... nature.... who knew her better, or who was more worthy to be her interpreter, than the most sweet Virgil? The Georgics are the most precious treatise on agriculture that antiquity has transmitted to us, a treatise of which the whole work de Re rustica of the celebrated Columella is a no less important commentary. It would be difficult to present a book of any epoch more finished than the Georgics; and here we leave aside the poetic merit, on which head it has no rival, and consider it merely as a most vast treasury of observations as well gathered as they are arranged.—And since it is a matter of displaying works finished in their kind, and in the matter of sciences of observation

[p. 270]

(let me be allowed a certain disorder in my ideas, caused by the haste with which I write, since it in no way harms clarity, the only target that must not be lost sight of in writings of this kind), let the competent declare whether there are many to set beside the Geography of Strabo: full knowledge of the subject, wealth of observations, solid judgment, measure, discernment, are gifts that vie with one another throughout the work, and make us feel at every step, as happens to us with regard to so many other branches, the infinite important works of the ancients that we have lost forever; for it must not be forgotten that we still cannot but judge them in a very incomplete manner, very often almost groping, at other times entirely in the dark, and in no case with that full knowledge of the matter that right and just criticism demands. These considerations lead me as if by the hand to touch briefly on the physical knowledge presupposed in the ancients by the practice of the conjuring arts—of magic, I mean—which the priests employed to support their oracles, and to maintain and give credit to their empire over souls in order to govern bodies. There will scarcely be anyone who has not glanced at something about the miracles of Apollonius of Tyana and other impostors, miracles very particularly spread abroad by Philostratus, and of which much of the same style may be seen in Lucian, Flavius Josephus and other writers of antiquity. These soothsayers or priests had of necessity to appeal to astronomy and to knowledge of the atmosphere in order to foretell celestial and meteorological phenomena with such exactness as might give them a divine investiture. “The power of bringing down fire from the heavens, even in circumstances in which the electrical influence was at rest,” says Sir David Brewster, “could not but be regarded solely as a heavenly gift. Another irresistible instrument of imposture must have been offered them by the power of rendering the human body insensible to fire; and in the combinations of chemistry and the influence of soporific drugs and embrocations the

[p. 271]

ancient magicians no doubt found their most profitable means of deluding.”

The secret use they made of scientific discoveries has in great part prevented their procedures from coming down to us; but although we are very ill informed regarding the progress of the ancients in the various departments of the physical sciences, we nevertheless have sufficient proofs to affirm “that almost all the branches of knowledge contribute their marvels to make up the stock of the magician, so that some idea may even be obtained of the scientific acquisitions of primitive times by means of a diligent study of their fables and miracles.” There is not a single branch that did not pay them its tribute; acoustics, hydrostatics, mechanics, and especially optics were in their hands an inexhaustible mine of the deceptions they sought.—Were I not afraid of extending myself too far, I would gladly extract a thousand passages, from Pliny especially, in confirmation of all that has been said.

II. (1)

I will say more: until the moderns themselves had advanced considerably in the study of the phenomena of nature, they went on denying various facts related in the most authentic manner by the ancient historians, among others the existence of aerolites, or stones fallen from the atmosphere, to which the aforesaid Pliny alludes, and which no one doubts nowadays, any more than the famous burning mirrors with which Archimedes set fire to the fleet of the enemies of his country, stationed at a great distance from the shore. The same has happened with countless passages of Herodotus, whose history may be considered in great part as a precious narrative of his own travels rather than as an account of things past, and is for that very reason extremely

(1) Diario de la Habana of February 23, 1839.—(A. Z.)

[p. 272]

important as a collection of observations on all matters. The ancient philosophers’ manner of instructing themselves was the surest means of their occupying themselves with purely physical questions as much as with moral ones; for none of them considered his education finished if it had not received the complement or finishing touch of travel; most of them, not content with a simple tour of the countries they visited, removed to them with the intention of studying thoroughly their physical and moral nature, fixing by preference on those most celebrated for their advances in the sciences, as the Greeks did with respect to Egypt, whence Thales of Miletus, Pythagoras and others of less renown drew a rich harvest for the natural sciences. Of this last it is well known that he even glimpsed the true system of the world, afterwards revived by Copernicus; (1) and of Epicurus it may be said that he offers the outlines of the Newtonian theory as regards the system of universal attraction, and of modern chemistry as regards the atomic theory, to judge only by the exposition of his doctrines given by his disciple Lucretius in the poem “De rerum natura.” The passages in which these coincidences are noticed are truly admirable, without our claiming on that account that Newton and the modern chemists went to learn chemistry from Epicurus or from Lucretius: it is only a question of showing the height to which the ancient philosophers rose in the physical sciences.

But what more or better proof that all peoples began with the physical sciences than their very primitive religious systems, all founded on the cosmogonic systems which, in their Vedas and myths, or mysteries and alle-

(1) On this subject I might also cite the passage of Seneca the tragedian, as famous as it is widely known, which begins “venient annis sæcula seris,” [there will come, in late years, an age] in which the existence of the new world is foretold in the most decisive and circumstantial manner. If I wished to avail myself of the resources that modern erudition offers, there is the German Heeren, who would furnish me astonishing data on the navigations of the Phoenicians, and in general on the commercial relations of the peoples of antiquity, and the consequent progress in the arts and branches which those relations presupposed.

[p. 273]

gories, the most ancient of them offer us? There are Egypt and India, notably, to convince us that from the cradle of society, from fabulous times, metaphysical and religious questions appear mingled with the exposition of the phenomena of the physical world, or, better said, the metaphysical and moral part appears as an immediate consequence of the knowledge of the material universe; nature thus warning us, in each and every thing, that we cannot rise to the spiritual except by the ladder of the corporeal. How easy it would be for me to let myself run on, discoursing over this most rich field, and availing myself of the lights shed upon it by the ablest Orientalists as well as by the foremost philosophers of cultivated Europe! The history of Astronomy alone would suffice to convince anyone to what astonishing point of advancement the ancients attained in the physico-mathematical sciences. But these indications are sufficient for my purpose; which has certainly not been to air the question of superiority between ancients and moderns, but only to demonstrate that the former were more advanced in the physical sciences than the learned writer of the article believes, and at the same time that all peoples necessarily follow the law of nature, of beginning at the beginning, that is, of receiving impressions before reflecting upon them, of observing and knowing external nature before knowing themselves. Were it not so, the famous nosce te ipsum [know thyself] of the Greeks would have no meaning, for the preaching to them that they should study themselves proves that, engulfed in the investigation of the external world, they had forgotten the most important investigation of their inner one. Nor would the memorable revolution caused by Socrates be any better explained, for it would in that case be an effect without a cause, since all he did was to draw minds, absorbed in the inquiry into external nature, to the study of morals, by counsel and example.

But can morality be isolated from the physical, and isolated with advantage for its study? By no means: for just as in the study of our mental faculties we have

[p. 274]

had to invoke the light of Physiology, if only to mark off the effects that belong to instinct from those that pertain to consciousness; in the same way, in Ethics, where our passions must first be described, together with the causes that extinguish and foster them, it is necessary to appeal to that same precious science to determine the influence of the bodily organs and functions upon our moral affections, the study of which presupposes that of Physics properly so called, no less than that of Pathology, or the knowledge of the disorders of the functions, since it is not possible to know the healthy man well without knowing the sick man well, and vice versa; here we touch, as happens at every step the moment one goes deep into any branch of human knowledge, upon the close connection that exists among them all, there being, strictly speaking, but one science, divided and diversified by reason of the limitation of our faculties, so out of proportion with the immensity of nature. I said and proved that the chapter on mental alienation alone would give important lessons to the psychologist as well as to the moralist and the jurist, upon the most delicate points of their respective specialties. All of which proves that the perfection of Ethics will in great part keep pace with the progress made in this branch of the natural sciences. From what, if not from the want of Physiology, in Psychology as well as in Ethics, does it come that great problems of these two sciences have been handed down from generation to generation, receiving diverse solutions, as the various systems that have reigned in them show, without hitting upon the true one?

On the other hand, no one who reflects upon the history of human revolutions can fail to recognize that Ethics, as a science, is perfected in the same manner as the others, being as much the daughter of observation and experience as any of the most experimental; with only this peculiarity, that often, besides the centuries it is wont to wait for its discoveries (in which it also bears a good resemblance to the physical sciences), the establishment of any new principle, however profitable it may be to humanity, still costs torrents of

[p. 275]

blood. And once it is established nothing seems more natural or more in keeping with reason, the following generation very often marveling that the adoption of a great truth in the moral order should have cost so dear. I do not see how, granting that Ethics is a science of observation, and for that very reason susceptible of progress, that it is linked with others and above all dependent on Ideology, I do not see, I say, how it can be denied that morality will vary or be corrected according to the progress of the physical sciences, all the more since, in order to grade certain actions, it is necessary to know their causes. Let no one believe, not for an instant, that we are trying to inculcate that the eternal and unalterable principles of the moral law, engraved upon the heart of man, are variable. An example will make our idea understood: by reason of the idea that peoples had formed of sorcery, for instance, those guilty of this enormous crime were condemned to death, even among Christian nations, (1) according to their legislation, which is always the expression of the dominant opinions, the indispensable basis of their morality; the men of that time being in this very consistent, for, starting from the principle that sorcery was a pact with the devil, and hence the gravest offense against the Divinity, they found no proportionate penalty for it, and everything seemed to them too little for the magnitude of the crime. Then comes the torch of the physical sciences to enlighten minds as to its true causes, human and very human, nothing divine or diabolical about them, and the pretended crime is reduced to a little sin of charlatanism. Need one do more than cast one’s eye over the long history of human superstitions to multiply examples of this kind?

Thus, far from being strange, it is in the nature of things, not merely that morality, or rather the principles of action of diverse peoples, constituted under different cir-

(1) Barely a century ago the last was condemned, and not in Spain but in Scotland.

[p. 276]

cumstances, should vary, but even those of one and the same people in the various centuries of its existence, History offering no phenomenon more constant than that continual changing of the springs that move humanity.—Compare the Middle Ages with the eighteenth century, and this with the present one, among the Christian nations, and, if you will, in a single one of those nations. All of them, and France most notably, were wild in the twelfth century to cross to the Holy Land for the conquest of the sepulcher of Christ; and that same nation in the eighteenth no longer feels the sacred fire of the religion that made it work prodigies on the fields of Palestine, but in its stead, carried along by another stimulus, performs deeds no less portentous, presenting a wall of breasts to all Europe in arms against it. And would those same springs move, and in case they moved, would they move in the same way as before, the France of the nineteenth century? No one dissents from the negative answer: allowing me, however, to observe that in the midst of so much variation and instability the heart of man is one and the same, unalterable; ideas change in it, but the faculties remain, which are congenital to it, inherent in its nature; at the bottom of the crusades as well as of the campaigns of the revolution we see the same enthusiasm, the same susceptibility applied to diverse objects and fed with diverse fuel. If nothing happens in vain in the nature of things, and if history is the theater where humanity plays its part, nothing could be more fitting than those variations, which in the last analysis are another name for progress, since nature herself seems to point out to us with her finger that each idea or spring, once its destiny is fulfilled, has no reason to reappear upon the scene, or at least not playing the principal part it played before. Only in this manner can history be explained, for otherwise the facts would be effects without a cause: after the license of the eighteenth century there necessarily came the moderation of the nineteenth. (1)

(1) A singular thing. Cousin, who explains history in the same way,

[p. 277]

But what need was there to resort to history in the present question, when the simplest reasoning by itself suffices to leave it settled forever? Ideas influence actions effectively, since man acts according as he believes; but ideas change infinitely; therefore actions must follow the same fate. The science of morals will vary precisely by virtue of the advance of physical knowledge, and of necessity the practice, that is, manners and customs, will vary.

I wish to give another luminous example before passing on to develop new ideas. It will already have been noticed that I choose them from among those that show a change not only in legislation but also in morality, as the inevitable consequence of a change of opinion, both so as to give no entrance to new doubts and for the inestimable advantage of making plain the close relations between the one science and the other, bringing us in the end to be convinced that the civil code of a people is likewise its moral code, which happens only because the state of opinion is copied in it exactly and faithfully. From the very hour and moment that opinion varies, the letter of the law is dead; it still exists in writing, but with the same kind of existence, for the people in question, as any law whatever of Lycurgus or of Solon; it remains on paper but does not govern, it is not taken as the pattern for the actions of individuals, nor as the rule for the determinations of authority.—But let us come to the example promised, which is furnished us by usury. Not only as illegal, but as sinful, was the slightest increase in the interest of money above what the law had fixed held in all Christian nations, in spite of the vicissitudes that commerce naturally presents, a veritable ocean that now withdraws its waves from the shore, leaving it dry, now drives them back to does not admit sensationalism: the phenomena in every branch are the cause and occasion of the notions.—I do not wish to hold forth now, but only to point out a contradiction, lest it be thought that it lay in me, who have combated other doctrines of the same author.

[p. 278]

flood it. As this civic-moral principle depended on the conception the Christian peoples held, it was necessary that this conception should vary in order for that standard to cease to govern; the very increase of commerce among men presenting new combinations, new mercantile phenomena, the general fact was made evident that the same thing happened to money as to any other commodity in the market, “that it is worth much if it is scarce, and worth little if it runs abundant,” and from that moment the moral law was abrogated and the civil law undermined. With what right, with what justice could a lender be required to give his coin cheap, while he was asked a higher price for any other article? This simple reflection is enough to make plain the force of the injustice that such legislation inflicted upon him. And such is the empire of demonstration that the most respected tribunals of the civilized world have long since, in the matter of usury, without those laws being repealed, declared a higher interest than the legal one quite lawful, according to the circumstances of the market at the time of the contract. Are these, or are they not, conquests, and great conquests, of science? But what triumphs, so well and so laboriously won! Were they perchance obtained thus simply, merely by the appearance of the idea that is mother of them all, or have they been the result of long discussions, prolonged even for centuries and handled by the most eloquent pens, by the pens of the benefactors, of the moralizers of the human race? And see here what distinguishes the progress of the moral sciences, as I have already indicated: it must of necessity be slower, given that their experiments cannot be so perceptible nor so prompt as those we perform in the laboratories. Even in the physico-mathematical sciences it is noticeable that those points which cannot be submitted so soon or so easily to experiment, because they run counter to the primitive or grosser testimony of the senses, are more difficult to establish beyond doubt, as happened with the Copernican system. Here too one observes the

[p. 279]

greater ease with which the precious instrument of mathematics adapts itself to the natural sciences than to the moral ones, without meaning by this that it is not applicable to the latter also, for it is so in a considerable degree, and that will even be one of the most effective means for their progress. (1)

Let us reflect further upon the example presented, and we shall see whether it is fertile in consequences applicable to the main subject. It proves that the whole force of human genius is not enough to discover certain truths, however obvious they may seem, once found, to those who, profiting by them, had not the labor of seeking them; but that it is necessary for circumstances to have unfolded the facts, so that, these becoming more perceptible, observations may bear upon them, and the science may afterwards be formed. The reader will remember how I told the Dómine that the existence of the speculative sciences could not even be conceived, or that they would have to be considered as an effect without a cause, unless they were produced on occasion of man’s own needs; and thus I explained the birth of Geometry.

In the same way, Political Economy was born precisely in Great Britain, where there was bound to be more occasion for speculating on these matters, by virtue of the greater expansion and development that the industrial arts and mercantile dealings had in that field. In no other way has the Jurisprudence of modern nations been formed in great part. The same happens in Politics; if revolutions do not unfold certain facts, in vain would human reason strive to foresee them and prevent them.

(1) The experience I have in polemics of this kind obliges me at times to enter into explanations so obvious that to many they will seem needless, and that to me, besides being needless, seem tiresome: for in truth it would be delightful to speak not merely as little as possible, but even by formulas, if it can be done, in scientific matters; but however precious such a method of exposition may be, everything is relative in this world, and everything must be subordinated to the indispensable law of saying as much as is necessary, above all when unfortunately it is still necessary after more than the usual has been said.

[p. 280]

Civil and political institutions themselves can and must be considered as veritable trials or experiments, which in the course of time reveal to us results that we could not expect, and that at times we could not even foretell. The framers of the Anglo-American constitution felt the force of these truths well, for they have always characterized it by the expressive epithet of our great experiment. Indeed, even though the political government of the United States is a republic, what points of resemblance are there between it and the republics of antiquity? What a diversity of physical, moral and political elements come into play in the one case and in the other! One need only sketch the parallel to draw the consequences. Were not all the theories of antiquity in matters of government thrown into disarray by this single example? Not because they deserve our contempt, but because, their authors not having had these new facts before their eyes, the consequent ideas could not be awakened in them. Were it otherwise, what business would be simpler than that of governing peoples? But unfortunately it is the most difficult of all, inasmuch as more than any other it demands the costly and slow fruits of experience in the moral order. Why do we hear it repeated at every step that the French revolution is the most instructive book for those who direct nations? Because it threw into disarray innumerable received doctrines, and was the fertile mother of countless others that all the perspicacity of human genius, devoted exclusively for centuries to speculating on these matters, could never have reached. We have, then, that in Ethics, in Jurisprudence, in Public Economy, in Politics, just as in any other experimental branch, facts precede enactments, experiments precede doctrines, causes precede effects: it being precisely worthy of note that when this natural method is set aside, and one tries to divine instead of hitting the mark or deducing, what is achieved is that the speculative sciences stand still and even go backward instead of ad-

[p. 281]

vancing. And see here precisely the fault commonly found with the ancient philosophers, who in their bold syntheses soared through imaginary fields, forgetting the firm ground of facts. How much further would the Greeks, with their brilliant gifts, not have progressed even in the experimental sciences, had they always followed this path! But once the moderns had seen the efficacy of such a method in those sciences, they could not but long—above all on perceiving the identity of origin in all of them—for its application to the sciences improperly called moral or speculative, so as to impress upon them a firmer and more rigorous course, the sure pledge of their further advancement.

And here I am naturally led to resolve another of Mr. Adicto’s doubts, concerning the backwardness in which I affirmed the moral sciences found themselves in comparison with the physical and mathematical; for as to their being sciences of observation and susceptible of progress, notwithstanding that they rest on some principles as unalterable as they are universal, not even a shadow of doubt can now remain even to the most prejudiced understanding.

Before airing this point, let Mr. Adversary permit me to remind him that under the name of moral sciences are comprised in general all those branches of human knowledge in which experiments and observations are not materially made with instruments and reagents, as is practiced in those called natural. This reminder is all the more necessary as Adicto has almost confined his argument, in order to demonstrate the perfection that in his view the moral sciences have reached, to Ethics itself and to Jurisprudence; whereas he should bear in mind that even if those two sciences were really in the state of advancement he claims, this demonstration would still not weaken in the least the general assertion that the sciences called moral are backward with respect to the natural ones, since under that generic name are

[p. 282]

comprised Psychology, Logic, Metaphysics, the Philosophy of history, Pedagogy, Politics, Public Economy, etc., and, as I said before, all those branches which, though they are of experience and observation, do not observe and experiment with apparatus and instruments. For this reason I was always inclined to call those sciences intellectual: a term doubtless less exposed to equivocations and interpretations. Thus we shall distinguish: sciences speculative par excellence, Mathematics, which constitute a separate branch; physical or natural sciences; and lastly sciences speculative, or intellectual or moral, which we have already enumerated, and which partake strictly of the nature of the first and of the second, though still more of the latter than of the former, being eminently sciences of observation. So that this epithet may be applied to all of them, for even Mathematics themselves have their starting point in observation; but, being the only ones that in their progress can dispense with observation, let this distinction remain as the most characteristic for the others, while to those of quantity belongs by right that of eminently speculative, since in another sense they are all intellectual, for in all of them the understanding enters as a cause.

Had this nomenclature that we propose been kept in view, perhaps many questions such as the present one about the sciences called moral would have been spared. For in recalling that they were sciences of observation, it was already given to understand that they were susceptible of progress, and that in their inquiries we were not to rely exclusively on the deductions of human reason. However, if we reflect a moment, we shall recognize that, in the state in which men’s understandings then were, it was not so simple as it seems to hit upon a denomination that should characterize the true nature of those sciences; since that very thing was the question, inasmuch as men were under the notion that in them one could proceed merely with the instrument of reason, or, in other words, that the sciences called moral were purely speculative.

[p. 283]

How true it is that precision of language comes in the wake of ideas! Until the endless stumbles made in the moral sciences—all the more notable as they contrasted with the firm course that the natural sciences, thanks to method, were taking—undeceived their cultivators as to the inefficacy of their procedures, they did not begin to distrust their isolated reason, and to understand the indispensable alliance of reason with observation for reaching, and reaching well, the longed-for goal. It was still not enough that a voice as energetic as it was authoritative, contemplating the errors of old, should cry out: “the understanding of man must not be given feathers to fly with, but lead to serve it as ballast.” It was necessary that men should see the miracles worked by these principles in the physical sciences before they would wish to apply so well-proven an instrument to the other branches of their investigations.

It is true that there is no branch in which men do not start from certain observations, the ancients as well as the moderns, for even the boldest hypotheses bear upon facts or observations; fictions themselves were invented not for the sake of inventing, but to explain realities. The difference, then, between the purely rational method and the experimental one comes down to this, that the first, believing a few scant data sufficient to raise the edifice of science, dispenses with further observations, whereas the second, judging these indispensable, checks the flight of genius as soon as it lacks these precious steps and materials for raising the structure. From no other source springs the discord between theory and practice in the intellectual sciences; for when a doctrine is ventured without being supported by all the necessary data, as has all too often happened in Legislation and in Politics, it is wont to be dashed against unforeseen facts, that is, against the laws of the moral world, which properly constitute practice. Who does not know that a countless number of civil laws of different times cannot and must not be considered otherwise than as

[p. 284]

veritable trials or experiments which, applied to society, were to reveal the action of its springs and point out the way to correct the theory? And if we go into the wide field of Politics, who is unaware of the more or less disastrous trials made by nations in their system of government within a very brief period?

Wherever we turn our eyes in the vast theater of human knowledge, we shall find as an invariable law the necessity under which man lies of not leaving the eternal circle from experience to analogies, and from analogies to experience, from facts to reasoning, and vice versa. From which it is seen—and be it said in passing— that it is not our intention to lower in the slightest the high rank that human reason occupies, this spark of the Divinity; on the contrary, we wish to make it more worthy of its origin, by reminding it of the law to which the Supreme Maker subjected it, in order to ensure that sureness of aim which is the result that can bring it nearest to Him.

Strictly speaking, we ought not to say that theory is at odds with practice, but rather that it is incomplete or false, if it does not embrace the facts it ought to comprehend, or if it distorts them to suit itself; for theory must be no more than the general expression, or key, that links all the facts together. But it is not possible for human reason to discover from the outset, not merely all the facts (for these reveal themselves, or circumstances reveal them, or genius wrests them from nature herself), but not even the relations of the facts: hence even in its very theories it must necessarily be progressive. With ample grounds, then, did he speak who said that facts were more precious when they contradicted received doctrines than when they confirmed them, our theories being, strictly, no more than approximations to the knowledge of things.

Before entering upon the comparison of some sciences with others, allow me, for the sake of greater clarity, to point out:

[p. 285]

1st. There are certain principles in the moral sciences as evident and certain as those that hold in mathematics; for instance, the existence of thought is as demonstrated as any axiom of geometry, and it is as patent that health is a good and sickness an evil as that two and two make four.—2nd. It does not follow from this that in the rest these sciences are of the same rigor as mathematics.—3rd. Let it be remembered that in mathematics one can proceed with reasoning alone, whereas in the other sciences this is not possible without observation.—4th. Observation is usually more easily and promptly carried out in the experimental sciences than in the moral.—5th. The instrument of calculation, though applicable to all up to a certain point, is more so to the former than to the latter; and thus is explained the admirable state of advancement that many branches of Physics have reached.

All this presupposed, let us enter more fully into the question. And beginning the comparison with Psychology, the mere fact of the diverse solutions that have been given to the question of the origin of ideas—a question we find proposed from the very first steps of the science—would suffice to decide the one that occupies us so much. Let us take it only from ancient Greece, and we shall at once notice how differently Plato and Aristotle resolved it. For centuries upon centuries the schools formed by these illustrious leaders remained divided upon a question so fundamental in the science, that by itself alone it is enough to characterize, and even to produce, the diversity among philosophical systems. Thereafter, almost exclusively, the solution of the Stagirite reigned during the long period of scholasticism, until the great Descartes came into the world and, overthrowing the Peripatetic school, revived and modified the ideas of Plato. Then Locke appears in England, reproducing with new data the sensationalist system, and shortly afterwards there appears in Germany a star of the first magnitude, the universal Leibniz, who tries to illuminate, and does illuminate, all the horizons of science, and, claiming to reconcile sensationalism with spiritualism (which is in itself already another solution),

[p. 286]

frankly confesses the leaning of his opinions toward those of the Greek idealist rather than toward those of his illustrious disciple. But in spite of this impulse given by Leibniz to Philosophy, sensationalism nevertheless makes its fortune in France in the hands of Condillac and of the famous school that followed him, at the very time when in the North of Germany there appeared another great original thinker, Kant, summoning the dominant opinions to examination and producing a revolution in men’s minds, perhaps the most notable since Descartes, though at that time still confined within the limits of the wide Teutonic family: a revolution that called them back to spiritualism, but to a much modified spiritualism, for Kant is not strictly Platonic nor Cartesian.—About the same time there began to flourish in Scotland the famous school of Edinburgh, which, though inclining to spiritualism, nevertheless placed its chief endeavor in making a complete history of the phenomena of the soul, in order to prepare a theory of its faculties, which is still wanting in the science. I shall not speak of the systems and varieties of systems into which Germany divided and subdivided itself after Kant: it will suffice me to cite the most notable names of Fichte, Jacobi, Herder, Hegel, Schelling and Gruppe, with which we come down to our own days; and turning our eyes toward modern France, where sensationalism had struck such deep roots, thanks to the philosophy of the eighteenth century no less than to the progress of the physiological school, we shall find the famous Cousin, (1) importing and seeking to acclimatize in his country the new German idealism, for which the celebrated Royer-Collard had prepared the way by means of the Scottish doctrines, more closely related to that system. But scarcely do these champions present themselves in the arena when other captains, no less valiant, come out to dispute the ground with them and to deal them blows from which it is not easy to rise.

(1) Cousin is a disciple of Hegel and Schelling, but as regards his plan of conciliation, or his eclecticism as he understands it, he is properly a pupil of Leibniz.

[p. 287]

This most imperfect historical review is more than sufficient for my purpose, which is limited to demonstrating the relative backwardness of Psychology through the vicissitudes of opinion on the fundamental question of the origin of ideas, as old as the science itself.—I will say more, without leaving mere history: even the present-day metaphysicians themselves, whom we might comprise under the generic name of rationalists, are still really subdivided into speculative, synthetic and positive; or, if you will, with Damiron (though perhaps not in so comprehensive a way), into spiritualists, eclectics and theologians; Bonald and Lamennais may be numbered among the positive, Hegel and Cousin among the speculative, and Eckstein and Schelling among the synthetic.—So then, after so many turns and returns, through the space of so many centuries, and after passing through the crucible of the most privileged minds on earth, the famous and fundamental question is still sub judice! And can Psychology bear the parallel with Physics, or with some other natural sciences? And mark you, it is not a matter of depressing some sciences in order to exalt others, for this is no question of antipathies or sympathies.—In this order I could go through, one by one, the principal chapters of Psychology, and, comparing them with others of the physical sciences, carry more and more conviction of the grounds of my assertion. But, besides that such a demonstration would be an endless proceeding, I purposely chose as an example a vital question in the matter, a mother-question, so to speak, so that I might be excused from entering into further details, for Mr. Adicto’s doubts compel me quite enough to enlarge more than is usual: for which reason I shall be permitted to go on sketching the state of the other sciences called moral or speculative, so as afterwards, at the end, to confront them all at once with the state presented by the greater part of the natural sciences.

The lot of morals, or E thics , considered as a science, has not been much better than that which fell to Psychology, the former being unable to avoid, up to a certain point, a similar

[p. 288]

fate, being a necessary sequel of the latter. And this is the proper place to note that the article-writer has confused at every step practical morality, or moral principles, with the theory or science of morals. I do not say that Mr. Adicto fails to recognize that there are moral principles or facts and at the same time a theory concerning these facts; and so far am I from being able to think so, that the same gentleman claims that the ancient philosophers, and after them the Fathers of the church, following the light of the Gospel, reached the non plus not merely of preceptive morality but precisely of moral science. But this does not prevent him from having fallen, in other passages, into the confusion I have mentioned.

There are certain fundamental principles or facts in morals common to all countries and to all religious systems; and although moral science is founded upon them, it is not they that constitute such a science. Science consists in the why of things, in the investigation of the relations and causes of facts, and, by a necessary consequence, in their arrangement and classification, so as to furnish formulas that may guide and serve for further progress and applications. So it is that in Ethics, considered as a science, its cultivators present the same divergence of opinions that exists among the metaphysicians with respect to Psychology. To this day the two systems concerning the principle of our actions, and the standard for gauging their justice or injustice, still dispute the ground, namely: on the one side the disinterested, and on the other the utilitarians, who are subdivided into various shades according as they understand those same principles more or less broadly; while there are even those who do not agree absolutely with either the one or the other, and charge both with a want of due observation. —It is true that a man educated according to the maxims of Christianity will not only know what he must practice in such and such particular cases, but will even give an account of many of the great questions relating to the soul and the world, such as, for instance, the spiritual nature of the former and the creation of the latter.

[p. 289]

But does he know scientifically what he must do and believe, or does he know it by virtue of its having been communicated to him by mere tradition? And see here the role of religion and that of science perfectly marked off. By both roads man arrives at the same result, but the paths are different, and different the manner of walking.

Let us go on describing the jurisdiction of preceptive morality and that of moral science, and let us observe: 1st, that even many of those moral principles or facts that are held to be universal are not so in reality, since in different nations actions contrary to one another are practiced, those being held good in some countries that are held bad in others; 2nd, even in the nations that ought to be most uniform, being subject to the influence of one and the same religion, the most opposite practices are found; and 3rd, even one and the same nation offers them according to the various epochs of its history. Whence it follows that even in practical morality, leaving aside the theoretical, men are subject to the law of progress. It is likewise deduced that, although through Christianity man may have reached the non plus ultra of Morality, this does not mean that he has arrived at the non plus ultra of science, in which there is so much to discover, as well as to arrange and classify in what has been discovered.

Before going further, it is necessary to make a distinction with regard to Christianity: either it is considered as a divine institution, and then there is no question, or what there is to say is favorable to my way of seeing, since the necessity of its introduction proves that not merely human reason by itself alone, but not even that same reason enlightened by the experience of the centuries, was enough to reform the morals of the human race; or else, even considering it humanly, we see in it the consequence and the result of progress, for history shows us man in the first steps of society with a gross, material religion, then worshiping idols, then passing from idolatry to polytheism, next

[p. 290]

this system destroyed by theism, whose first universal representative was offered us in the Jewish people, this last coming to be absorbed, amplified and further spiritualized in the sublime religion of the son of Mary. And see there the march of humanity in every branch. Thus painting came before hieroglyphics, and these before letters, as parables came before arguments.

## III. (1)

It is then more than manifest how inexact is Mr. Adicto’s notion, for he claims nothing less than to set an impassable bound to human nature, which is so eminently perfectible. Far from considering Christian Morality as the end, it ought rather to be regarded as the fertile germ of endless fruits for the improvement of humanity. To what causes other than the spirit of Christianity, and the concomitant investigations in the physico-mathematical sciences, which it permits and even encourages, is the present superiority of the Western world over the Eastern due? Those inquiries and conquests of modern civilization offer the understanding of man new facts, in the physical world as well as in the moral, which effectively assist the advancement of the science of duties. Without leaving Christianity, was it not formerly believed to be an action acceptable in the eyes of the divinity to use compulsory means to inculcate our belief in the recalcitrant? And today, the spirit of Christianity being better understood by the generality, are not leniency and tolerance toward every kind of dissenter held to be an evangelical virtue? And yet had not the gentle religion of the Lamb preached forgiveness even to enemies? It was necessary, then, that time, experience and greater enlightenment should go on exerting their influence on the masses, before the divine maxims of the Crucified should in practice become gen-

(1) Diario de la Habana of March 3, 1839.—(A. Z.)

[p. 291]

eral. No people had the miracles of Jesus nearer to them than the Israelite people, since in their very bosom all those prodigies were wrought, and above all the peerless one of the redemption.—And yet none was more obstinate in its principles in refusing due faith to so many marvels. Not in vain did Christ himself say that “no one could be a prophet in his own country.” I said, before the article-writer my adversary, that there would be no better course of Ethics than one in which there should be adopted as text, not merely the catechism of our holy religion, but the Gospel itself as it is written; and this because it seems to me the only book that was made for little children and for adults, for the ignorant and for the learned. But it is evident that in expounding the Gospel the professor would have to lay hold of the resources offered him by the other branches of knowledge, in order to make the truths taught in it better felt. Yet even here the whole of Ethics considered as a science is not contained. Since it treats of the inclinations of man, of his passions, it becomes necessary to study the influence of all agents, physical as well as moral, upon our affections, so as to draw as a consequence the means of correcting them. Thus, without leaving its application to practice, Ethics finds it necessary to implore the aid of the other sciences of observation, in order to take sure steps toward bettering our morals, which is the final end for which it was created.—And how is it possible to effect this improvement unless science rises to the causes of things? And can that height be reached except very slowly, and by means of the facts presented by the history of the decline and progress of humanity? And this being so, that is to say, Ethics being a science of observation like any other, and a science in particular much like medicine, in whose facts a million causes come in to alter and diversify the phenomena, scientific exactness or rigor can hardly be attained in it until great progress has been made in observation. The

[p. 292]

same thing has happened to Ethics as to Medicine: though both are so ancient, if not as sciences, at least as practice, or empirically, they belong to the least advanced branches of human knowledge; and this does not lie in the men, for there have been and are many great physicians and moralists, besides the fact that both sciences possess some truths as evident as the principles of Euclid; but their advancement is hindered by the very object and nature of things; res ipsa vetat [the thing itself forbids it].—The comparison between Ethics and the art of healing is all the more exact as the former may in all rigor be called the medicine of the soul, just as the latter is that of the body. And will a heap of mere prescriptions, or even the detailed description of the symptoms of its moral ailments, be sufficient to cure our soul, if one does not go up to the source of the causes in order to combat them? “But we are in possession of the book that comprises all our remedies, the Gospel (it will be said on the other side); therefore there is nothing more to seek: there are life, and the medicine and the science of the soul; one neither can nor should take a step further.”—Not so: because the law of grace, far from abrogating the law of progress, came to confirm and foster it; because the road of science is one and the road of the Gospel another, though they are not for that reason at odds: not so; because the Gospel is the code, and science its exposition: not so; because the Gospel could not include all the data of the human sciences: not so; because man has been placed in the world under the condition of knowing things by experience: “ante hominem vita et mors, quod placuerit, dabitur illi,” [before man are life and death; whichever he pleases shall be given him] God himself has said by the mouth of the prophet; for He did not, by having made him the peerless gift of the Gospel, strip him of free will, nor exempt him from the law of studying in order to know. And see here, imperceptibly, the place that each thing must occupy marked off, even confining ourselves to practical morality: science, to know the motives and causes; the Gospel, to determine responsibility, once

[p. 293]

the motive of the action is known; for invincible ignorance, as the theologians teach with good grounds, exempting from the obligation, consequently excuses from the fault, or, to put it better, there is no fault on the part of the agent who acts under it; but from the instant we come out of ignorance nothing excuses us any longer, and here conscience and the Gospel—and, up to a certain point, conscience formed by the Gospel—come in to cry out to us aloud about the fulfillment of our duty, without its being lawful any longer to allege an excuse of any kind; for just as we are endowed with understanding in order to know, so free will has been given us in order to act.

But even supposing that all humanity were perfectly uniform in the practice of all its actions, whether as a consequence of its own nature or through being all equally flooded by the light of the Gospel, this moral phenomenon can still be conceived as existing in entire abstraction from what constitutes moral science. Indeed, since this science is occupied with the investigation of causes and the classification of facts, it is clear that understandings may differ either as to the motives of actions, that is, the facts of the science, or as to the manner of presenting and ordering them, or as to both heads together. And it is properly here that the various systems come into play, which differ all the more as their authors do not see the same facts in some branches; and precisely because systems are expositions of facts, they must also vary according to the facts. This is exactly what has happened in Ethics, as much as or more than in any other of the intellectual or natural sciences. In the first place, on the supposition that they turn upon the same facts, I wish only to mention the various principles to which moralists have attributed our actions, as the motive or root of them all, and we shall find at least six, namely: physical feeling, according to Epicurus and those of his school; the moral sense of Hutcheson; perfection, according to the Stoics and Wolff; the

[p. 294]

will of God, according to Crusius and other theological moralists; education, according to Montaigne and Helvétius; and finally the political constitution, according to Mandeville. Nor does it avail to say that systems are many and that truth is but one, and that unity is indivisible, and other notions of this tenor, which, though they be true and demonstrated, do not alter in the slightest the state of the question. Doubtless truth is one; but as the difficulty lies in discovering it, the understanding of men cannot help inventing systems and more systems so long as objects are not seen under all their relations; so that the very examination of the systems helps it to come to discover the truth. It seems to me needless, after what has been written, to develop my thought further. And can this doctrine perchance endanger the cause of good morals? That indeed would be a very grave question. But far from its being possible to harbor such a fear, we are offered here the very occasion to commend divine justice, which would not have proceeded in due order if, while on the one hand requiring responsibility of man, it had at the same time made this depend on his later knowledge, that is, on the slow and difficult conquests of science: such a demand would have been the height of injustice toward man—toward man so ignorant in his first steps, toward man condemned to earn knowledge, like his bread, by the sweat of his brow. So that it was necessary and consistent that Providence should found morality upon bases as universal as they are indispensable, necessary consequences of our own organization: thus it is as necessary, as indispensable, that all men should consider death an evil and consequently the slayer a criminal, as that the sun gives light and is round. And see there formed the individual conscience and the universal conscience, with the element of free will, which must always be entered into the account, independent of and prior to all education; and behold, with this simple reasoning (for there are many others with

[p. 295]

which to do it), the dazzling system of Helvétius refuted without our meaning to: a system which claims that even the faculties or principles are acquired, when in reality they are innate, not contenting itself with limiting such a doctrine to ideas alone. But let us not enter—for it is not now to our purpose, though we would do so most willingly—into the examination of the doctrines of this celebrated philosopher.
