I wished, however, to arrive at the consequence I drew, in order to bring out two other points very important for our question, which for that very reason I shall do no more than indicate, namely: 1st. How, even though morality be founded on universal bases, the opinions of philosophers can influence even practice, as those of the aforesaid Helvétius, set forth with the eloquence that distinguishes him, have influenced it all too much. 2nd. The intimate connection that reigns between Ethics and Psychology, to such a degree that moral systems will be such as the psychological ones are, from which they necessarily derive. Now then: consequences for our question, confined to the backwardness of the moral sciences with respect to the natural. Therefore a science so questionable in its fundamental points, or at least a science in which one can still be deluded to that degree, is not so advanced as others in which it is impossible even to attempt it.—Therefore, having demonstrated before that Psychology has not yet resolved some of the fundamental problems it set itself from the remotest antiquity, and its kinship with moral science being so close—of which it is nothing less than the legitimate mother —there follows by the most irresistible inference, not merely the backwardness of the daughter, but the necessary inevitability of that backwardness.—But how many other most effective causes contribute to foster it! Let us observe, first, the ease with which men, by the very nature of moral objects, are led to judge them in different ways, even supposing them free from all prejudice: so that even in the science of analysis itself rational evidence has been distinguished from moral; and it is said that a thing is

[p. 296]

morally demonstrated in the historical order, or another analogous one, which is as much as to say that it is not a demonstration like the purely rational, or absolutely mathematical one, but only a relative one, or, to speak with rigor, a veritable probability raised to a high power. Neither let us forget that, as new truths in morals have to struggle against rooted habits and interests, they meet with still more resistance to their being admitted and digested than those of the other sciences, which combat only prejudices, or mistaken judgments.—So that these latter have to contend only with the understanding or with the senses (no small contest, to be sure), whereas the former have to deal with the understanding and the will, which, even leaving aside all depraved intention, contributes effectively to darken the understanding further, or to make it less permeable to the refulgent light of truth.—Nothing, then, more in order than to try to inculcate morality from the tenderest years, and, if possible, from the cradle, by precept and example for the time being, adding the no less important means of conviction when it can be effective, as soon as the precious dawn of reason begins to break; nor can we be charged with contradiction, as Mr. Adicto charges us, for not beginning by teaching physics to children, moral principles, or the rule of their actions, coming first, as is practiced and must be practiced in every system of education, whatever the ideas or doctrines professed.—But this point, in spite of its simplicity, calls for some explanation, owing to the way in which the article-writer has presented it.

I said elsewhere that he had confused, very especially in the present case (for he also does so in other passages of his article), preceptive morality, or moral principles, with morality considered as a science, that is, the why of our actions and their classification. —Indeed, there are inculcated in children, with more than enough reason, even though they do not yet perceive the motives, the principles or rule of our

[p. 297]

actions, but not the why of them; in a word, they are taught the art, as we have already established, not properly or even improperly the science of acting: they are instructed in an empirical manner; they are told: this you must do and this avoid, because God commands it and it is good for you, without entering into the motives of action, nor into the origin of our inclinations, nor into the causes that influence them, nor into the classification of the one and the other, or of our duties and rights; for the very simple reason that they are not yet fit by nature, nor furnished with the previous knowledge, that such delicate matters demand. Thus it would be downright to make a mockery of a professor to charge him with going to give a Course of Ethics to early infancy; and yet reason dictates, and experience convinces us of, the efficacy with which morality is inculcated in the first years. However, and without departing from this very example of the education of infancy chosen by Mr. Adicto, I wish to make him see how much the method and the doctrines we recommend are worth for fertilizing human reason from its first steps, by making the procedure under which morality itself is taught to tender little creatures as little empirical, that is, as scientific, as possible.—Experience has convinced us of the influence of habit in material things as in moral ones; for which reason we do not hesitate to mold our children early, by words as well as by example, to those actions which we hold to be good, even when they cannot yet grasp the motives. So do all parents and teachers act at the beginning, and so ought they to act.—But then the light of reason dawns; and here methods begin to divide, according to the ideas of those in charge, some continuing, more or less, exclusively under the same order of counsel and example (means always effective, most effective), and others adding, not an untimely scientific classification, but reasons or motives of action within the reach of those being educated; a means which, by strengthening their nascent intelligence, contributes to increasing the efficacy of the former ones, and consequently to the

[p. 298]

more direct attainment of the object.—To achieve it, the lights of the physical sciences, among others, are invoked, to lead them by the simplest chain of inductions to the demonstrations of many of the most sublime truths, and those most precious for conduct, which they could perceive little or nothing of at the beginning, and which they surely would not have perceived by any other road. I should only like to call Mr. Adicto’s attention to a few chapters of the little books I composed to establish the explanatory system in one of the schools of this Capital, and from them he would see, even without having personal experience of its efficacy, or without news of its good results having reached his ears, by their simple reading, how from the simple history of the camel, of the wretched oyster, or of any other natural object, the tender understanding of children goes on rising by the easiest steps to the contemplation of the most sublime designs of the Supreme Maker, of his providence, goodness, and other attributes, and becoming consequently imbued with the deepest gratitude, flooded in a torrent of love and religion, which is all of it love and love alone. Thus by this system, that is, by placing man in his various stages on a ground and with a nourishment suited to his capacity, he is made more moral, more logical, and more of a thinker; he is spiritualized more and more by being made to think about sensible objects. Not for nothing has the system we call explanatory been called, and with ample exactness, logical or analytic. And behold how with Physics, that is, with a few features of Natural History and other branches, not with the science strictly so called, I make man as logical as he can be, from the moment the light of reason begins to illuminate him.—In the same order demonstrations within his reach are made to the child simultaneously of the goods and evils that result from this or that action, and as they come to perceive them, they come to grow fond of Morality. Another principle no less rational had to guide me in the composition of the little books, and that is the need to avoid tedium, a reef on which it is so easy to run aground

[p. 299]

when it is a question of early childhood; and to this end it is necessary not merely to dress Morality in pleasing attire, but to take particular care that many articles which do not treat of morality at all be interspersed with those that deal with it: and this is the most fitting stratagem, so that those very ones devoted to forming the heart may take better effect. Needless to point out that by so wholesome a system, a system of manliness and robustness for the nascent understanding, the instruction of infancy is not confined to the sterile and exclusive exercise of the memory, but all the mental faculties come into play in greater or lesser degree, according to their state of development. But let us not be carried away by our predilection for the most important branch of education, for even wishing to be brief, we should never finish; it is enough for our purpose merely to point out that in the application of the explanatory method to early infancy, as in the means adopted to make it bear fruit, one has only to see another application of the same principles that have guided us in recommending the precedence of the physical sciences over the moral ones with respect to early youth. And on coming to this comparison the far-reaching import of this question of method will leap more to the eye, and all the importance I attribute to it will be perceived. Indeed, on the conviction held by those who direct education will depend the whole difference in the system of teaching; it being beyond doubt that those who are imbued with the principles set forth will fertilize the understanding of man and lead it by the proper degrees, to prepare it for greater and more difficult conquests, and to ensure the betterment of the heart; the one holy end to which all knowledge must aspire, and before which even this very knowledge loses its importance, becoming a means and only a means toward that other great end for which we were all created. Now indeed it falls to me to apply the “nosce te ipsum,” [know thyself] and I believe I shall be understood and we shall understand each other.

Now too the reader will become aware of the levity with

[p. 300]

which Mr. Adicto has called routine-bound the system that has most combated routine: had he called it new to him, venturesome, bold, of doubtful success, and above all repugnant to established usage, well and good, for to that end there might have been grounds, or at least very plausible appearances; but to characterize precisely as routine-bound that which most fights against routine, which destroys it, and gives the reasons for its destruction, is to commit the most gratuitous and unwarranted antiphrasis that can be committed. But as it seems, Mr. Adicto (for everything has its why) saw that it was a matter of practicing before giving rules, and, this smelling to him of empiricism, he straightway christened us blind empirical dabblers with no more thread of theory to guide us in our impracticable labyrinth; and so far are we from exaggerating in this extract we give of his words, that he goes so far as to impute to us the consequence that we should be capable of claiming even that Law be practiced before it is studied theoretically in the classroom, and other niceties in the foregoing style, about which the reader has data to spare for judging us. But this is not the only time Mr. Adicto has fallen into mistakes of this kind, which, together with others of a different nature, will be noted in the brief survey we shall make of his writing once the exposition of doctrines now occupying us is concluded. He will be convinced more and more that if we want physics first it is so that those being educated may afterwards be the sooner and the better logicians. In short, the whole spirit of my plan comes down to strengthening and invigorating the intelligence gradually, the better to ensure the betterment of the heart: to spiritualize man more, because the more I spiritualize him, the more I raise him up, the more I bring him near to the Divinity; and in this sense I am a disciple of the Divine Plato.—Amplectis ulnis. [with open arms] (1)

(1) I say in this sense, because I am no Platonist in attributing to reason more than is its due, nor, consequently, in venturing into the world of hypotheses, “non vires humanæ mentis extollamus” [let us not exalt the powers of the human mind], for this produces not only errors in Philosophy but even evils in the moral order.

[p. 301]

IV. (1)

Believing that by this time Mr. Adicto’s doubts about the state of the moral sciences will have been dispelled, doubts that arose from the idea he had formed of Ethics, I may be permitted not only to omit the much that can still be alleged concerning the relative backwardness of the latter, but to adopt for the demonstration, as regards the other branches of the intellectual sciences, the greatest possible laconism, reducing the proofs to a simple list of bare facts.

Next comes Jurisprudence, and here, in my judgment, are the arguments that prove its respective backwardness.—1st. Division of opinions on the fundamental point of the manner of constituting the science, and this most stubbornly precisely in the country where Law is most cultivated scientifically. I allude to the two famous schools now reigning in Germany, the historical and the philosophical, the first claiming the complete restoration of national legislation, and for that very reason declaring itself against the project of a general code; whereas the second believes the time has rather come to frame one; and this was the opinion that prevailed in the civilized world, and above all in France, until there appeared Savigny’s famous writing “Of the Vocation of Our Age for Legislation and Jurisprudence.” No question seemed more decided than that of the necessity of codification, above all after the framing of the new French code, which the learned of all nations never tired of admiring, since nothing could seem more natural and more in order, and more proper for constituting the true science, than the plan, harmony and classification that reign in a work of this kind: the outward forms of a symmetrically constructed edifice could not fail to dazzle. But to this the anti-codifiers cried out: well and good, let the work be raised, but first it is necessary

(1) Diario de la Habana of March 19, 1839.—(A. Z.)

[p. 302]

to gather and examine the materials, and we are still far from that point; otherwise, by dint of wishing to be philosophical you will turn into system-mongers, and what is worse, into false and retrograde system-mongers, for if facts are lacking to science it will be the expression not of the nature of things, but of our fancy: and behold how we plead for the true experimental method in Jurisprudence.—Now it would be in order to set forth the foundations of the philosophical school, to compare them and weigh them against those of the historical. But since our purpose is no more than to allege the fact of the division of opinions in the science, on so momentous a matter, we shall dispense with entering into particulars, without its being understood, of course, that we have taken sides with one party or the other. Let us go on, then, with the list we began:—2nd. Another argument for the respective backwardness of the science is offered us by the state in which criminal legislation still finds itself, in spite of the progress it has made, and the very different course it took from the middle of the last century. Who does not know that quite other, and very other, are the principles that have governed from then until now, thanks to the lights shed by philosophy and the experience of nations? And not merely as regards the doctrines on the grading of crimes, but even those that relate to procedure. Here Mr. Adicto has, among others, a very notable proof of the insufficiency or impossibility of applying Roman Law to the needs of modern society, whose springs, in so many branches, are so different from those that moved that famous people. I recognize as much as anyone the wisdom of that great monument of antiquity, Roman legislation; I am the first to confess not only that the science made great progress in the hands of its profound jurisconsults, but also that its codes constitute one of the most notable repositories of human knowledge even in other branches, as cannot fail to happen, for civil laws must in great part necessarily copy the physical and

[p. 303]

present the picture of the knowledge of the age to which they belong. But does it follow from this, perchance, that the Romans reached the non plus [utmost limit] of legislation? and supposing this were granted with respect to their civil state and their customs, can it be claimed with respect to modern societies? All that can be deduced is that the ancients knew a great deal of Legislation, if you like, but not that the state of this science can be compared with that which most of the natural sciences present; for even granting all that knowledge of Legislation to the ancients and the moderns alike, the very nature of the science prevents its progress from being as rapid and sure as in the natural ones; to which is added that the fundamental questions yet to be resolved are many more, and of longer standing, in the moral sciences than in the physical. Nothing more natural than that the peoples of modern Europe should generally adopt Roman legislation, since in it manifold needs common to all peoples were satisfied and provided for: they found themselves with an edifice already built, and, being obliged to lodge somewhere, they had to make use of the opportunity, so as afterwards, in the longer run, to discard what was useless and seek what was necessary; for there is both excess and want in the codes of Rome for modern society.—3rd. Rare is the interesting question in these matters that does not present diverse aspects, apparently equally tenable: if not, see what happens in the debates on bills of law, it sometimes turning out that afterwards, in practice, the opinion of the minority triumphs. That is why I affirmed that many laws are nothing more than trials that receive their sanction or their revocation from practice itself. To the examples I have already alleged we shall add others no less convincing of the respective backwardness of these branches, and let the questions of the penitentiary system, prostitution, foundlings and divorce be those chosen as proofs.

The first question, after the infinite observations

[p. 304]

or rather, the innumerable experiments that have been carried out (for that is the name deserved by the diverse material, practical tests to which prisoners have been subjected, in order to observe the manner of operating of each system) in cultivated Europe as well as, most notably, in the United States of America, is still not entirely decided, and opinions remain divided even after men of the caliber of a Tocqueville and a Beaumont have reported on the matter, and after its having been the subject of commissions composed of the very pick of the legislative bodies of France and Great Britain, not to bring up the admirable labors, theoretical and practical, that have been carried out in Germany and Switzerland. I do not mean by this that we are still as backward, in the matter of Penitentiaries, as before these investigations were undertaken. My only purpose for the moment, and I shall not tire of repeating it, is to show the backwardness in which these fundamental points of legislation stand with respect to those corresponding to the majority of the natural sciences. And I am so far from alleging that backwardness to the discredit of the moral sciences, that I find myself more convinced every day that these tests or experiments in the moral order are the only means of coming to decide such questions, being precisely the best argument in favor of the doctrines I uphold.—What was known about prisons when the most humane Englishman Howard began to write? How much is not known from then till now! To what is the progress owed if not to the different mode of investigation? The learned deluded themselves in moral matters, believing that by their very nature they could proceed a priori, with only the lights of their reason. That is why certain questions, difficult in themselves, have passed from generation to generation as virgin and unresolved as they were at the beginning.

As for prostitution, let Mr. Adicto read what I set forth to the Dómine, confining myself to the famous work of Parent-du- Châtelet;

[p. 305]

or better still, let him read the whole work, which, offering a vast field to his meditations, will yield him as its result this grave lesson for legislators: “that there are evils of such a nature in the social body that they are sooner avoided than cured.” In this book he will see the best proofs of the vicissitudes and the fate that befall human regulations when the only road that can lead to safety is not followed, demonstrating not merely the inefficacy of the means, but most often their increase of action, or true efficacy in working contra producentem [against their own author], in multiplying the evils they set out to check, and even in producing others of a new form, totally unexpected and unforeseen.—But the method, the scientific rigor, the knowledge of cause, the profundity with which the great lesson we allude to is set forth in that book, are gifts which, making it unique in its line, have given the true pattern of how such questions ought to be treated in the 19th century, which, in spite of its vaunted positivism, is still not as exacting as I could wish in this matter of scientific rigor, the only resource for having science. It is not the spirit of positivism, as some believe or affect to believe, to exclude or to make little of the moral springs that influence the human heart, drying up the sources of all feeling and poetry. Quite the contrary; positivism is synonymous with reality, and the real and effective history of man could ill be written if Psychology omitted to give an account of the causes that so influence him; and the moralist and the legislator would proceed far from well who did not foster the action of certain motives that were placed in us by wise nature precisely to polish and temper us. —Positivism, then, means nothing more than rigor in demonstration; leaving in the sphere of conjecture whatever has not been duly made manifest, however plausible it may be to our reason and flattering to our passions. And if not, there is no science, but delirium, and even fatal error. Let us not divorce poetry from science; both rest on the

[p. 306]

same foundation, truth; and we should act all the less truly and wisely in setting them at enmity, inasmuch as both can work together toward the same holy end of improvement, poetry serving as interpreter to the multitude of those very hard-won acquisitions of science, which, left in their natural difficult form, would be like a treasure still hidden in the bowels of the earth, visible only to the eyes of the geognost, but not usable for the purposes of life.

The example offered us by the question of foundlings is no less notable for our subject than that of prostitution, confirming likewise in the most patent manner that abandonment, like that other moral gangrene, belongs to that class of evils which are sooner avoided than cured. An example all the more worthy of attention, since the learned and the legislators had already believed they had reached the final term for attaining the end by the closing of the wheels, or cradle-houses. The result of such a measure, as of other analogous and even contrary ones adopted previously, has been to increase the evil instead of checking or diminishing it, and in many cases to produce drawbacks of another kind. Thus it is that in France, where the measure of closing was most tried, persons versed in this matter are crying out for the reopening of the wheels. Indeed, the statistical data gathered in the various Departments prove on the one hand the prodigious increase of foundlings as a result of that provision, and reveal on the other evils as grave as they were unexpected. One of the most profound investigators of the matter says: “If the advantages of having closed the wheels appear scarcely visible, their drawbacks are at times most grave...... it being possible to say in general that the Departments where there is only one foundling hospital always show a greater mortality than those where there are several.”— Then let the fruits of illegitimacy die, the partisans of the system of suppression will still cry, for they do not even deserve to live

[p. 307]

nor their mothers to obtain a reward, or at least a consolation in their frailty.—But what will be objected when we see that the facts tell us not only that the mortality grows, but also the number of those unfortunates, and reasoning declares to us that it cannot fail to increase? The suppression of the wheels led to recourse, among other measures, to the expedient of leaving natural children with their families, an expedient which for the moment brought about the premature death of a great portion of the foundlings, its other consequences not making themselves felt so soon; but as such a system continued, the number of illegitimate children was seen to grow rapidly, and that of prostitutes, and that of needy and beggar families. The cause subsisting, the effect cannot cease: but if the foundlings or orphans already in existence are abandoned, they will become a plague that will infest society in a thousand ways, and most especially by multiplying the number of the unfortunates of their class. Therefore, if one truly wishes to diminish the number of foundlings, it is necessary to improve public morals, to turn aside the causes that corrupt them, to oppose to them at least the spread of religious instruction and of industrious habits. To attain such holy ends, the first indispensable step is to take them in, and take them in in foundling hospitals, and by an irresistible consequence we come to end in a result contrary to what was held to be wisest and most effective, it being possible today to affirm without misgiving, with the support of the data: “That the more illegitimate children are taken in today, the fewer foundling children there will be one day.” So it had always seemed to me, as soon as I set myself to consider this matter, and I remember that in past years, when the majority of my young contemporaries were decidedly of the contrary opinion, inspired most especially by the reading of the doctrines of Comte and other celebrated publicists and jurisconsults, my judgment was never satisfied, foreseeing the need to institute new observations and experiments in the matter. I bring up what I myself

[p. 308]

have gone through, to make youth feel more and more the fruits of the spirit that the natural sciences necessarily instill, for I attribute to no other cause my exactingness and rigor in demonstration, as well as my reserve and distrust in judging, consequences of the habits communicated to the understanding by the experimental method; the reason why, were it only in this respect, and setting aside the great advantages they offer in themselves, and those they bring to knowledge of another kind, the physical and mathematical sciences ought to be cultivated with great care.

The most grave question of divorce was the last example chosen for my purpose.—None was better debated when it was proposed to establish it in France, the reasons alleged in its favor no doubt triumphing, since it was enacted. Nevertheless, when the French returned to the natural course of things, when the waters of morality returned to run where they were wont to go, the reasons that had been adduced against it began to prevail, coming in the end to the abolition of divorce, by the most weighty consideration that marriage is regarded as the primordial basis of all moralization; it being well worth noting that in spite of the march of time, which wears everything away, and in spite of the extraordinary upheaval of 1830 and its consequences, nothing has been altered or attempted to be altered in the matter. But whether or not the re-establishment of such a remedy be advisable for the present state of French society, what is to my purpose is that very divergence of opinions that has reigned in the matter.—Observe any discussion on any of these matters, and it will be seen that there is scarcely one of importance in which the minds of all remain convinced and, consequently, brought into agreement. Will there be a law on affairs of moment which, even after the most profound examination, is yet more than a mere trial? And can it be otherwise, considering the nature of things? What proofs, as opportune as they are luminous, would not

[p. 309]

the laws on education offer me, in every country of the world? But by what right shall I dwell on such demonstrations, when there is no longer anyone who doubts that education is an eminently experimental branch, more experimental than any other, as those who see already saw it from the remotest antiquity, and more than anyone the never sufficiently celebrated Quintilian, whose Institutes are the first book in its line ever composed on teaching?

Appealing to the testimony of those versed in the matter, let them answer whether even in the most trodden questions of Jurisprudence there does not still remain much to be desired, not merely for the better exposition in theory (for that is another story), but even for the daily decision of practical cases, and this even in those nations that have the most perfect codes or can count on the greatest cultivation of the science, and for that very reason possess the more methodical books and guides. Among the many objections Savigny has made to the modern codes, and especially to the French, those that come to be recast in the points we allude to are admitted as just and wise by the very jurisconsults of France. (1) Among those objections or criticisms is counted the conformity of the plan of the Code with Roman jurisprudence, with the Institutes of Justinian; and as this principle is at odds with those upheld by Mr. Adicto, who considers the Law of the Romans as the non plus [utmost limit], or final term of the science, it is well for us to dwell on the particular, in spite of having set forth elsewhere the place that great structure of human knowledge ought to occupy, indicating what it has in excess and what it lacks to fit itself to our present requirements.

It is enough to have had a nodding acquaintance with Roman legislation to be convinced that, amid the prerogatives that distinguish it, it suffers from vices no less inherent in it, and especially as a body of science: innumerable repetitions,

(1) See Lerminier, “Introducción á la historia del Derecho.”

[p. 310]

an infinity of useless provisions which time and history have swallowed up in their course, a quantity of fragments that are not true laws and contain definitions, etymologies, digressions, historical and critical observations, interminable discussions to find out whether in this or that case an action or an interdict ought to be employed, and this to arrive at one and the same result; finally, subtlety without end and want of method. I am sure there will not be one man of understanding who will tax with exaggeration the judgment just set down; which is so far from being dictated by passion that it is almost verbatim the very criticism of a more than competent authority, and one, rather, so partial toward the law of the Romans that he even finds in it very often the rigor of geometrical demonstration, and who, while confessing the superiority of the Greeks in all branches of human knowledge, assigns the palm of Jurisprudence to the sons of Romulus, their disciples. This so competent authority, the best ideal of impartiality that modern philosophy can offer, as being the most complete and by title of the greatest genius, is none other than the great Leibnitz. Well, the same man who expresses himself with full knowledge of the case in those terms afterwards sums up his judgment in this formula, reducing to four the defects from which such a system of jurisprudence suffers: “superfluitas, defectus, obscuritas, confusio.” [superfluity, deficiency, obscurity, confusion]

And since we are on Leibnitz and on Jurisprudence, I recommend that Mr. Adicto run through the extensive list of works that that eminent genius still desired for the reform of the science; a list that exceeds thirty works, each more important than the last, and which the curious may see in the appendix to his Nova methodus, or in the extracts made by Lerminier in his already cited Introduction, which is a better-known and more popular book.

But even supposing all those preliminary works were finished, there still remain the method and the classification for constituting the science, and still more for constituting it

[p. 311]

on the footing of rigor on which the physical and mathematical sciences stand. For it is not claimed that the ancients had made no advances in the moral sciences, since they attained much and good in them, and still less is it claimed that there has been no progress since; all we maintain is that the said sciences, notwithstanding that many of them have been cultivated since the remotest antiquity, are not and cannot be as advanced as the majority of the natural ones, including some born only lately; and that, being all of them sciences of observation, that non plus ultra which is suggested could not have been reached, considering that by that mere fact they are essentially perfectible.

V. (1)

It still remains for me to adduce Public Economy as proof of so solid a doctrine: a proof almost superfluous after what has been said, and which, only because I have offered it, I shall confine with the utmost rapidity to a few points that establish the said doctrine incontrovertibly. In this field, as being less explored, the divergence of opinions on fundamental questions holds the field all the more. It is enough for me to point out, on the question of population, the famous contest between Malthusians and Anti- Malthusians, and the no less celebrated one over free trade in England, sustained by the eminent and ill-fated Huskisson: I say, and in England, where all the circumstances favorable for deciding this kind of controversy better and sooner concur. I wish no more than to point to the grave matter of the influence of taxes and legal restrictions on manufactures; the infinite questions that spring from the subject of manufactures themselves, and above all the most important one of their influence on the morality of peoples: the indispensable necessity for the legislator and the economist of consulting the tendency and the results of the systems in the workshops themselves, and, as a necessary consequence,

(1) Diario de la Habana of March 24, 1839.—(A. Z.)

[p. 312]

the no less indispensable necessity of taking light from all the auxiliary sciences and arts before deciding these vital questions. It is impossible to take a single step in so precious a science, which embraces all the grave matters of social interest, without finding oneself in the lists of opinion; the experimental method being precisely the surest and most effective means of emerging victorious as soon as possible from the midst of the labyrinth in which those champions entangle themselves who wish to rely exclusively on the weapons of their reason, without carrying as their standard the facts of the past, observing those of the present and waiting for those of the future. No better proof on this score than the famous work of the Englishman Charles Babbage on the Economy of Manufactures, and the collection of reports given by the commissions in the British Parliament. Wherever we open these collections of interesting facts and reasonings on matters of the greatest social moment, we shall be convinced that works such as those of Adam Smith and Jean-Baptiste Say are not enough for going deeply into Public Economy; errors of grave consequence being committed if the light of the physical and mathematical sciences on the one hand, and that of experience and observation of moral facts on the other, are not carried before us.—I do not wish to weary the reader with extracts, and above all when in my opinion I have made the principal points of this discussion so clear that I believe that by now I am preaching to the converted, the new doubter himself included. I shall nevertheless cite a few facts, opening Babbage’s book at random, so that it may be seen how even the most insignificant economic question touches upon the experimental sciences, and to a certain point its resolution depends on them.—The tax on windows in England made manufacturers resort to interior lights and reduce the size and number of those conduits necessary for ventilation; and the fact is that everyone perceives their necessity, but the importance of light for health is not so generally known, an importance that rises

[p. 313]

to a high degree with respect to cold and variable climates, and notably in England, which, besides being cold and variable, has a proverbially dark atmosphere.

Another example of this kind is furnished us by the great drawbacks produced by the regulations of the excise on national manufactures, considerably impeding the natural progress of improvements, and deterring manufacturers from making experiments.

Patents will be my third example.—No doubt it is important to preserve to each inventor the exclusive use of his invention until he has been amply indemnified for the risks and costs he has incurred, no less than for the skill he has displayed in carrying it out.—But the degrees of merit are so various, and the difficulties of legislating on the matter so grave, that the near impossibility has been reached of framing a law that is not subject in practice to the most serious objections.

As much may be said of the law on literary property, and it is very singular that precisely those kinds of property that require the most elevated talent and the greatest culture, which more than any others are pure creations of the understanding, should have been the last to be recognized by the State. Fortunately the means of deciding on infringement of literary property are not very difficult; but the laws in force in many cases bring great drawbacks and obstacles to the advancement of knowledge.

4th example, and I conclude, this last having the merit of bearing on the rectification of very widely received ideas. The principle that the government ought to intervene as little as possible between workmen and their masters is so generally and so firmly established that it is of the utmost importance to guard against its misapplication. What might not be said of the same principle applied to education and to the great question of freedom of teaching! Had it been carried out, we should have neither (nor would those have them who do have them, I mean)

[p. 314]

normal schools, nor a law of public instruction, nor.... but this matter would carry us too far, and is not one to be treated by way of digression.

The whole work of that illustrious physico-mathematician is a continuous demonstration of how indispensable a profound knowledge of these matters is for economists and for governments, if they do not wish at every step in their decisions to compromise the interests of science and, what is more, the well-being and advancement of the governed. The reading of such a book will be all the more profitable to youth, inasmuch as in it they will feel with their own hands that one cannot attain a true criterion on economic points without the data offered by the natural sciences; becoming convinced for once of the exactness of that principle of the Latin satirist: “Nunquam aliud natura aliud sapientia dicit.” [Never does nature say one thing and wisdom another]

Well then: in view of this state of things, can there still be anyone who claims that the so-called moral sciences are on a level with the so-called physical ones? How is it possible that the former can bear comparison with the latter? Let us now set aside the causes of which we have spoken so much in the course of these discussions, and confine ourselves solely to the effects. Cast but a glance over Physics, Astronomy, Mineralogy, Crystallology, Botany, and even Chemistry, and it will be seen that most of the treatises of these sciences, as much by their classification as by their doctrines having been reduced to true theorems, offer the scientific rigor of Mathematics. I do not mean by this that many points do not still remain to be discovered, and others to be elucidated, in such sciences; but that they are more and better constituted than the others in the points already ascertained. Yesterday, so to speak, Crystallography was born at the hands of the celebrated Haüy, and at the same hands, and with barely the first quarter of the same century gone by, it achieved an exactness, a rigor and a fruitfulness in its deductions that are truly geometrical, as indeed it is to advantage by the subject it treats of.

[p. 315]

Can this happen to such a degree in any of the moral sciences, or even in all the natural ones, as in fact it has not happened in medicine, because the nature of its object resists it? For this reason the outcry of the reformers, of this natural science as well as of the moral sciences, rises the louder in favor of the method of observation as the only plank in the shipwreck; for if the subject itself hinders the ease of progress, how much will the force of this drag not increase if one does not keep to the experimental method!—Let us compare what has happened with respect to some of the great fundamental questions in the one branch and the other of human knowledge, and we shall see that those relating to the physical world, once resolved, have remained so forever—a notable example, the Copernican system, and all the more notable since it is a doctrine at odds with the first gross testimony of the senses: (1) whereas the moral ones have been resolved several times with every appearance of remaining so eternally, and afterwards the solutions have proved failures, being handed down from generation to generation and reaching us unresolved. Such has been most especially the fate of Psychology, the foundation and root of all the moral sciences: it being well worth noting that all its cultivators, whatever their opinions may otherwise be, sensationalists or spiritualists, are unanimous in regarding them “as sciences that are not yet organized, or are badly organized, and for that very reason inspire much less the need to pursue them than that of constituting or reforming them.” (2). It is no wonder, then, that Charles Comte, so passionately bent on resting on nothing but facts, should uphold with all his strength a doctrine maintained even by those who do not always walk on such safe ground. But

(1) I say first gross testimony on purpose, because it would be easy for me to demonstrate that with the same testimony of the senses, more extended and refined, we have arrived at the truth. This demonstration would be no very sweet draught for the pseudo-eclectics; but it would take us too far from our purpose.

(2) Words of Jouffroy.

[p. 316]

far from its following from this, as the writer of the article gives to understand, that I have formed my opinion on that of this celebrated moralist, it follows, on the contrary, that it is not possible to have had a nodding acquaintance with the physical sciences and then enter upon the moral ones without the contrast formed by their respective method and procedures leaping to the eye; so that this is a necessary consequence of the very knowledge of things: for which reason all who cultivate both the one and the other are perfectly agreed on this point, an indispensable result of their own studies. And see what happened precisely in my case: proof of it, that as soon as a favorable opportunity presented itself to me in the career of teaching for applying these doctrines, I hastened to take advantage of it, as I did in the project submitted to the government for the foundation of the Chairs of Philosophy in the Colleges of San Fernando and San Cristóbal, proposing the precedence of Physics over Logic in the course, as a vital point for the method: a project in which I believed I was doing a service to the cause of studies in general, and notably to the cause of my country, and which was presented as early as 1833, when I knew no more than the name of Charles Comte, for it is scarcely two years since the works of this illustrious writer came into my hands. Not that I hold it a disgrace to follow in the footsteps of so eminent a man, but because I wish to demonstrate to Mr. Adicto that I am not a man of fashion in the matter of opinions, nor one to let himself go with the current; but that, good or bad, my own are the daughters of examination. So true is this, that both before and after I have combated one or another doctrine of Comte himself, at times without knowing, from what has been said, that they belonged to him; having borne in mind, in proposing him as a model of method in the moral sciences, the circumstance that his treatise on Legislation is a book in everyone’s hands.

I believe I have finished the task of answering Mr. Adicto, it seeming to me that I have not left unanswered a single one of his principal arguments.—But since there are to be found

[p. 317]

many inaccuracies in the course of his paper, inaccuracies which I have offered to rectify in order to satisfy his doubts completely, it becomes necessary to make this survey. Before that, however, it will be well to sum up briefly the points already demonstrated.

1st. I have demonstrated that, the physical sciences being easier than the moral, the former are more suitable for beginners than the latter.

2nd. Likewise, that the ancients were more advanced in the physical sciences than is commonly believed.

3rd. That it was more natural for man to begin his studies with nature, and that so it came about.

4th. That there are certain principles in morality that are and must be universal.

5th. That these principles are not so many in number as some claim.

6th. That the moral sciences, being sciences of observation, are necessarily perfectible.

7th. Consequently, that however much the ancients advanced in these branches, they could not for that reason reach the non plus ultra; above all, considered scientifically.

8th. That a great part of the progress of the moral sciences depends on the advances in the physical.

9th. That the latter are notoriously more advanced than the former.

10th. Finally, that the effective means of promoting the progress of the moral sciences is the rigorous application of the method that has made the natural ones flourish: in sum, that the experimental method is the truly analytic one, and for that very reason the only instrument that can aspire to universality.

Let us now proceed to our critical notes, putting down only the most necessary ones, and without confining ourselves to the points already answered; for were everything to be noted down that deserves notes in the article, it would be necessary to fill with them another

[p. 318]

three dozen columns. So, without further preamble, let us come to what is necessary.

1st. Mr. Adicto speaks: “to whom (to the moral sciences) we owe our most finished institutions in the moral order, and consequently in the logical order.” This consequence is what I do not perceive, even granting the antecedent, which cannot be granted so easily.

Logical order means concatenation, connection, and that is offered by all subjects, the physical as well as the moral. What does it mean that to the moral sciences we owe our most finished institutions in the logical order? Let the word logical be understood in whatever sense you please, it cannot be brought in here.

And if logic, according to the writer of the article, is the “indispensable prelude without whose introduction the study of the sciences will fall out of tune to the point of discord,” the other moral sciences can ill precede it either in the historical order or in the logical; for in the first case logic would in reality come first, and in the second it ought to.—I forbear to discourse further on this point, and let us go on to the second contradiction, for the first was examined at the very beginning of the present paper.

2nd. “Logic,” continues Adicto, “has positively existed before all the sciences, for they owe it this character.... This is very obvious: exact analysis, logic itself, is exercised upon objects, or whatever exists that can be analyzed”.... I want no more: never has a pro me laboras [you are laboring for me] come more opportunely than in the present case.... Who

does not see that here logic is taken for the very faculty of reasoning, which creates all science, and Logic as one among so many?

This could never have been the question: the only question was whether or not what we call the course of Logic ought to precede the teaching of Physics. Does Mr. Adicto not notice that the word logic, taken, as indeed it is also taken and ought to be taken, in the sense of connection or concatenation in ideas, is applied to every kind of subject? Thus are called, and

[p. 319]

very rightly called, logical exercises—that is, exercises that teach one to reason, or more properly that promote reasoning—the questions and explanations on the reading text in the explanatory system: with the same propriety grammatical exercises are likewise called logical; without the pupils, in either case, having taken a course of Logic, for they still belong to primary education.

3rd. “And if it is true,” continues Adicto, “that analysis is in the last result the father of all artistic and scientific knowledge, why postpone its theory to isolated practice, always clumsy and routine-bound?” Great, and very great, is the aberration in these ideas! Why, if you have a son, Mr. Adicto, who, out of the natural curiosity of childhood, plagues you with questions, as usually happens, about every object that presents itself, do not answer them until you have taught him the theory of analysis: let him wait, let the little creature have patience, since it is only for his own good that he is put to that torment; for it would be a most grave charge upon one’s conscience to let him practice analysis, exercising his reasoning so clumsily and by rote.

Away, then, and good riddance, with the great Montaigne and all the abettors of explanation, who would have it “that the child be accustomed to reason about whatever offers itself to his eyes: a stone, a tree, a river, a house, a passage of Caesar, or about Charlemagne—everything serves as a book in this apprenticeship.” Will you need more arguments to be convinced that analysis is exercised, and ought to be exercised, while still ignorant of its theory, without such practice involving contradiction or bringing drawbacks? Drawbacks! Advantages, and great advantages, on the contrary, will be gained by those who have been accustomed to use the instrument without knowing how or why they use it, nor of what elements it is composed: not because such notions are useless or harmful, but because the propitious season for sowing them in the understanding has not yet arrived. Is it necessary to explain to an apprentice of the violin the doctrines of sound on which the construction

[p. 320]

the instrument is built, and even of the very sounds he draws from it? On the contrary, at the outset such an exposition would be untimely, pedantic, premature, unnecessary, and I would even say harmful, because it would increase his confusion instead of easing his practice. By all means let him be given the rules at the moment of execution, so that he may do it better; but many of them (for this cannot be said of all), though derived from the theory, must not be confused for a moment with it, as our doubter unhappily does. The precepts, on the contrary, are an integral part of the practice itself: it all comes down to this, that he who has first walked the road warns the novice traveler to pass by such a spot, or to avoid such a quagmire, so as to reach the desired end better and sooner: precisely neither more nor less than the master carpenter telling his apprentice how he must hold the plane so as to cut better and not cut himself: here are the rules without the theory.—Let us now see the same rules derived from the theory. Suppose it is a matter of teaching the pupil his sums: clearly not a step can be taken without inculcating in him certain precepts, many of which are founded on the theory, that is, on the science properly so called; but in order that he may calculate with exactness and speed, if this is the only end we set ourselves, as happens in the primary schools, it is not necessary to instruct him in such foundations. It will be convenient, it will be useful, it will be necessary, when our purpose is another, that is, when we try to initiate him into the science of numbers, for so far we have not gone beyond the art, which has always been defined as “a collection of precepts for doing something with ease and perfection.”—A pupil may very well be skilled, most highly skilled, in solving whatever problems are set him on fractions and the rule of three, for example, and yet be ignorant of the doctrine of fractions; in a word, he may already be an excellent reckoner and not yet an arithmetician. —I will say more. But even if we proposed to raise him to

[p. 321]

the principles of the science, it would still be advisable—what am I saying!—it would be necessary to instruct him in certain facts or data which are the true foundations of the science, since the theory rests upon them. And these facts will be the more numerous, and will offer the more points of comparison to the pupil’s understanding, the greater his previous practice has been.—Thus this method, the only natural one, far from being open to the charge of routine, is eminently intellectual; it is the most logical imaginable, not only for the good habits it imparts to the understanding, but very especially because it obliges it to climb by the proper steps. And so, my dearest Mr. Adicto, it is not “better nor more obvious to inculcate the doctrine, even though it be obscure and unintelligible (is it possible that this heresy has been set down on paper?), passing immediately to give it reality”...... for without going any further, my friend, you have already given yourself up; and chalk up the 3rd contradiction. Indeed, why do you add “passing immediately to give it reality,” etc., unless because you perceive that it would be impossible to make the theory intelligible without descending to the facts or particular cases of which it is nothing more than the general exposition? Man’s understanding naturally begins with the concrete, and when we wish to raise it to the abstract, if it does not grasp things quickly, the only means of making them penetrate is to return to the concrete, that is, to the examples which have necessarily preceded the theory in the understanding, the theory being the cipher or key that embraces them all. Can one explain what the declensions are like, for example, and what is more, can one expound the syntax or theory of a language, without the pupil’s having known the elements of that language and having exercised himself somewhat in its workings?

On what and on whom, otherwise, was the doctrine to rest? On data that do not exist, on an understanding unprepared, completely new to the subject: and is this logical, or analytical, or scientific, I do not say in the last analysis, but at the slightest breath of examination? Each time you will be more and more convinced,

[p. 322]

Mr. Adicto, that my method is the eminently logical one, which I have not dared to propose without having meditated very carefully on the march of human reason, and all the more so since it was at odds with what had been established by the authority of the school under whose influence you took up the pen. As the whole continuation of the 1st column and the greater part of the 2nd of Adicto’s article turn on the same idea and present the same contradiction over and over, and even in a more palpable way, if that be possible, I shall not trouble the reader by stopping to note them further, especially since what has been set forth is more than enough for the gentleman author of the article to resolve his doubts on the point; and to resolve his doubts, as he has repeatedly declared to us, was the object he set himself in writing: so I have believed, and therefore I am bent on responding to his praiseworthy purpose.—But in this general answer the very special and peregrine notion that “the practice of Logic is the theory of the other sciences” must not be understood as answered.

Well, now I do declare that I do not know what science is, nor what Logic is, nor a jot of theory, still less of practice.—Logic, considered with respect to the other sciences, can be nothing more than the method, or procedure at most, that there must be in every science to make it one; but it can never constitute its theory, which consists in the exposition of the special ideas of each chapter of a science; so that in one and the same science there are as many theories as there are points generalized or systematized.

What an antilogical way of reasoning! In the same way one might also maintain that signs were the practice of analysis, because with signs analysis is practiced; and even that would be more plausible.

But that “Logic is the theory of the other sciences”.... why, even this is not yet what the author says, but “the practice of Logic.”— Had you followed my method, Mr. Adicto, you would no doubt have struck out this proposition

[p. 323]

as soon as you had set it down. And what is that effective prescription? To keep to particulars, my friend, not to be carried away by generalities, to have recourse to little examples; and a single one would have sufficed you to kill that singular idea from the first instant of its natural being.

If we were to mark every one of the propositions that deserve it, especially in Mr. Adicto’s paper, it would be necessary to write a long volume: we shall do so with some of those that stand out most, or that do not seem to us to have been expressly refuted in the general rebuttal or answer we have given them.

VI. (1)

...........................................

...........................................

In every science there is necessarily a concatenation in the reasonings, independent of the acquisition of Logic as a separate science, and with all the more reason independent of Logic as it is professed in our lecture halls, in the teaching of which Psychology and Ideology are included.

Therefore the author of the article comes round to the very thing that I have been inculcating with all my strength from the beginning.

We have, then, one of three things: either contradiction, or reductio ad absurdum, or agreement with me; and I do not doubt that Mr. Adicto will adhere to this last course, not by any virtue of my own, nor by grace, gratis data [freely given], but by force of my demonstrations and of his own words.

6º The difference between practical Logic and natural Logic that Adicto wishes to establish then entangles him in a labyrinth from which he absolutely cannot get out; and among other creepers in which he has become enmeshed is this which follows,

(1) The present article is unpublished; for although the censorship authorized its publication, as appears in the manuscript I possess, it never came to be published. The first leaves of the said manuscript are missing.—(A. Z.)

[p. 324]

which I cannot manage to qualify, nor even to christen.—“The practice of this science lies precisely, as I have already said” (so he goes on repeating, as an unquestionable foundation in his view, that error manifest by every light) “in the theory of the others, and so I judge it more proper to call it practical Logic than natural Logic, giving it a character different from that which it has through the study we make of its rules and principles, or the exercise of these; and unlike the other sciences, which we qualify by the mere speculation of their doctrines or the execution of them.”—All that I, sinner that I am! can make out of so much muddle is that Adicto takes the word theory as synonymous with exercise of the faculties in the sciences; and as he finds this exercise in all of them, he thence deduces that such exercise or practice is the theory of every science.

7º Nothing is gained by laying down what no one will dare to deny: “That morality is universal, and superior and prior to every system”: for that neither prevents there being systems in morality as a science, nor the systems from influencing the practice of morality. The question is not, nor can it have been, anything more than to show the relative backwardness of the intellectual sciences, the divergence of opinions that has reigned and reigns in them among the various peoples, among the various ages, and even among the learned of the same age, serving very especially for the purpose.

8º Very worthy of note is the way in which, without perceiving it, our author openly confesses the necessity man is under of beginning with what is outside, and this precisely in the paragraph that follows the one in which he tries to prove the contrary.—And chalk up contradictions, for I am losing count.—“Man, contemplating round about him, in the first days of his social existence, the multitude of objects which prodigal nature vied to present to him, as so many stimuli directed through his senses to awaken his reason, until then lulled to sleep by ignorance....” and in this style he continues

[p. 325]

to express himself; all of which may be regarded as a commentary on, or amplification of, my own doctrines, albeit overloaded with some irrelevant matters and commonplaces.

9º The most singular thing in the world is the language the author adopts next, contrasted with that which he uses afterwards in other places. Nothing could be more contrary to his purpose than what he himself lays down: for it all comes down to persuading us that man began with the observation of nature, and that later social needs obliged him to study Morality as well. And the man who toils for this purpose is the same who declares afterwards that he has not been convinced by this proposition of mine: “Man must begin with what is outside rather than with what is inside: better said, he cannot know his interior except precisely by virtue of the knowledge of the exterior.” Is the reader surprised at this continual contradiction, to the point of doubting whether the author had the target of his purpose well in view? Well, his wonder must rise still higher when he notices the leap with which he makes the transition to Charles Comte, not to mention the glaring contradiction (that, in his pen, is by now a conditio sine qua non) into which he falls with another very notable passage of his writing: and no ordinary contradiction, either, of the sort that often consist more in the words than in the ideas, through the bad application or the want of understanding of the former, or of those other very cloaked ones that the writer does not usually perceive in the heat of composition, nor at times any but very few of those who read: it is a contradiction in the spirit or soul of the matter, to the extent that almost at the end of his writing he holds to be an error of Comte’s the very thing he is now about to attribute to him as his greatest laurel. The contradiction falls on nothing less than the fundamental or master idea of the famous work of the publicist cited; namely, on the demonstration that the moral sciences are sciences of observation just as much as Physics or Chemistry. The only way out that

[p. 326]

I find for our Adicto is to blame the slip on a lapse of memory; for, his paper being somewhat long, he may at the end have forgotten what he had set down at the beginning; but if he suffered a lapse in a matter so essential, it is proof that his ideas are not clear or settled on the point, and here, precisely, is the ailment from which his writing suffers most and the tone that really characterizes it.— But let us come to the leap or strange manner of making the transition; and the truth is that at the moment of extracting his words my surprise grows, if that be possible. Indeed, after concluding the idea he gives of the origin of society, of law and of civilization, he puts to himself the following objection—and it is necessary that I say it is one, for it to be recognized as such—bearing on the system he expounds; for besides being a great truth, it furnishes him with the firmest support, and may be regarded as the spirit or quintessence of the doctrine he maintains. “It might perhaps be said,” he says, “that man abandoned the study and examination of nature too early, to launch his reason into the field of metaphysical speculations...”; and so it is in truth, as I have already demonstrated to the Dómine first and to you afterwards; though, to speak more exactly, we ought to say that we find those metaphysical questions in the cradle of society always mingled and confused with physical questions, not, however, the latter abandoned for the former, nor independent of one another: “I shall answer this,” he continues (who would have expected Adicto to set about answering what most favors him?), “that the three sciences mentioned” (he alludes to Morality, Legislation and Politics) “are not based, as was mistakenly believed until now, on theories and systematic hypotheses, but on the nature of things and the exact observation of facts, as an illustrious French writer of our time proves to the point of evidence” (an allusion to Charles Comte); “so that today we must regard them as they have always in fact been, daughters of observation, in the same way as Chemistry, Physics, and the rest that bear this name

[p. 327]

par excellence”.... But here there is much to disentangle and to cut away. In the first place, no one has believed, or could believe, that the moral sciences are based on theories and systematic hypotheses, for in reality they treat of the nature of things, that is, of the phenomena they offer to observation, to explain which the systems were devised. It is very true that these are found in the science and have harmed its progress, by very often turning its cultivators aside from continuing along the safe path, the only safe one, of observation, persuaded as they were that the powers of their understanding sufficed them, that is, that they could proceed a priori, authorized in their mistaken conception by the very nature they attributed to those sciences; but from this to saying that these sciences treat of the systems there is an immense distance.—The sciences treat of objects, of realities, while the methods or procedures employed to study them may be more or less effective, or the explanations invented to give us an account of the phenomena, that is, of the realities, more or less well founded.—No one doubts that methods as well as systems will have considerable influence on the advancement of science, and for that very reason it is very conducive and very proper to apply the experimental method in order to put an end to hypotheses and to arrive more quickly and more surely at the truth. Thus true science is summed up in observation, and for that very reason it could never be said that it lay in systems exclusively. Nor can it be affirmed thus absolutely that the moral sciences have always in fact been sciences of observation: indeed, to be such sciences they must have been so in such a manner; but they have neither always been regarded as sciences of observation, nor is everything in them that has aspired to this distinction science.—In short, one notices in all these ideas that the author confuses what has been with what ought to be. And this is how Comte is to be understood, and how he is found very consistent in his doctrine,

[p. 328]

which comes down to demonstrating that the moral sciences cannot progress except by way of observation, and, as a necessary consequence, that their scant progress or their backward steps are to be imputed principally to the failure to apply so precious a method. And there you have, simply unfolded, the strange way in which our Adicto made the transition to Charles Comte.

10º I already noted in my general refutation that whoever read some loose passages, and even entire columns, of my antagonist’s paper would take him for the foremost upholder of my doctrines; it being, for that very reason, very singular that he should tell me and preach to me as things of his own many of those which I had more than once published in my answers to the Dómine and in some other writings. But he resolved to confine himself solely to my Memoir published among those of the Patriotic Society, and there was no way of making him change his purpose, even though my writings subsequent to it, and all prior to his, were nothing but glosses and more glosses on the Memoir.

Here is another proof of the contradictions; Adicto says: “Nor could man, in so backward an age, give himself up to studies that demand extensive and infinite knowledge, with no other guide than his feeble powers, nor other protection than the narrow circle of his intellectual faculties.”—Let us first straighten out the structure, so as afterwards to unravel the sense the better, a preliminary labor to which almost everything that leaves the author’s hand condemns us.—Studies that demand knowledge...... what is this? I thought that it was man who demanded knowledge, and that he acquired knowledge by means of study. Now, it may be said that certain studies demand certain previous knowledge, or something of the sort; but it makes no sense, or rather it is a nonsense, to affirm in general that studies demand knowledge. This being settled as it ought to be said, I now say that if man in so backward an age could not hit upon the discoveries

[p. 329]

in the physical sciences, very particularly for lack of certain instruments which, once discovered, are themselves the seeds of infinite other discoveries, the same thing happened, and was bound to happen, and to a certain point in a greater degree, with respect to some of the moral sciences: it happened because men were really as backward in them as in the physical sciences, or even more so; it was bound to happen, because the one kind are as much sciences of observation as the other; and it was bound to happen even in a greater degree, inasmuch as certain facts that are the object of the moral sciences could only present themselves with the development of society, and many of them after a great lapse of time.— Thus we see man in the infancy of society drawing from facts inductions as gross in the moral order as in the physical order: thus, in the same way that he attributed the most ordinary phenomenon to a supernatural cause, he fashioned for himself a material and vengeful God in his own likeness and that of sensible objects, and in no other way did he infer the penalty of death for all who committed faults that today scarcely count, when they count at all, as mere frailties.—All that the author says, then, about the poor and misguided inductions, or none at all, that peoples formed in their beginnings, and that savage nations still form today, concerning the very phenomena that served as the occasion and fuel for immortalizing the great Newton and other eminent physicists, the same, and even more, much more beyond comparison, applies to inductions in the moral order.—Backwardness was the common lot of almost all branches in the nascent state of society, and notably of some of the moral ones, which had to wait upon the progress of the physical sciences.

Science is one, Mr. Adicto, as you also say elsewhere; the most singular thing being that you say it but do not believe it, or at least do not feel it; for I cannot conceive how the same man who maintains such doctrines respecting the progress of the moral sciences can inculcate that maxim, which is as it were the cipher of the relations and dependence

[p. 330]

in which the different branches of the same trunk stand.—But this is by now an ailment that we have repeatedly noticed and noted, namely, that you lay down general propositions without having verified their reach by particular cases, a thing which amounts to taking them on credit.—Meanwhile, keep the account of charges, and chalk up now the seventh contradiction, or rather the same contradiction multiplied by seven.—And before going further I advise you not to take the trouble another time of expatiating on more ideas than are necessary for your purpose: (1) I allude to the exposition you give us of the Newtonian system, which is neither to the point nor in the least needed by the public: it was enough for you to cite, for your purpose, the fact of the well-known theory, as a splendid example of the induction to which that favorite son of nature had risen. This advice fits a hundred other places in your writing to a nicety, which, as I characterized it from the beginning, has much that is superfluous and much that is wanting. I wish, moreover, before passing on to something else, to make you an observation important for understanding the history of the progress of the sciences, and it is the influence exerted on them by the introduction of a new instrument; more being owed at times to these means (which serve as the occasion for other unexpected advances) than to the greatest efforts of human reason. It will be said that human reason is the inventor, and that everything is owed to its endeavors in the last analysis; but that is not the import of the observation we have made, which

Let the present remark serve for all analogous occasions that may arise. Though if Mr. Adicto knows me, as a pupil of mine ought to know me, he will be well aware that this pen is moved by no other spring than the love of truth, and the consequent eagerness to propagate it.

(1) It is with great reluctance that I take the liberty of giving this advice and of adopting a frank tone in my criticism (for such must in part be the character of my answer), yielding to the desire to resolve my pupil’s doubts; for Mr. Adicto honors me with the title of his master, and in that capacity I am all the more obliged to enter into these corrections. I do it also from another motive no less compelling for my heart: the advancement of our youth.

[p. 331]

in no way opposes so patent a truth: what we mean to say is that the introduction of a new instrument is usually the occasion not only for the principal question, whose solution was being sought, to be resolved, but for opening a vast field in which shrewd investigators wrest from nature other and yet other secrets which centuries and more centuries might have passed without anyone stumbling upon, since it was not possible for them to be divined by the understanding of man, who can know nature only a posteriori; nor is this answered by the generality, however exact it may be in another sense, that men of genius are born when and as they are needed; for if this were always precisely and rigorously so, science would not present certain anomalies that it does present: we should not observe that very often the understanding of man prefers, or rather is led to prefer, the curved line to the straight in the path of discovery. But let us return to our principal idea.

Great, no doubt, was the secret that Volta wrested from this fruitful mother, set down in his famous electromotive apparatus; but who could have foretold to him (and mark that he was one of the great men of science), who could have foretold to him, I say, that his machine, his most simple machine, would by a most rich series of linked discoveries decide the great question of the identity between electricity and magnetism, and, what is more important still, turn the minds of the great speculators to the dynamic and plastic study, so to speak, of the great phenomena of life and of the organization of matter? However much the most enthusiastic imagination might have feigned at the time of the discovery of the illustrious physicist of Pavia, however much it might have launched itself unbridled through the spaces of fiction, it could not possibly have stumbled upon these portentous realities.

The invention of the telescope by Galileo, did it not reveal to men another heaven, and make them know the old one better? But, since I have cited Galileo just when we arrive precisely

[p. 332]

at the place where the author does so, it will be only right that we correct some inaccuracies he has committed on this particular.

“Astronomical knowledge,” he says, “was in no better state until the celebrated Galileo, for no one dared to oppose or to submit to investigation purely orthodox doctrines, eliminated from science in order to raise them to dogma, as is set down in our Vulgate;”.... I have stood perplexed a long while over the meaning of the present passage, and almost decided to eliminate that participle eliminated, taking it for a printer’s error, and to substitute another word for it so as to get on with my task: but on noticing that the said participle matches the obscurity that generally envelops the whole period, I return to my pristine perplexity and indecision, and am left almost in the dark. Let us, then, examine it under the two hypotheses of eliminated and not eliminated, to see what we can make out.—But before the eliminated comes the purely orthodox, which also asks, and asks first, for its corresponding correction. If he had said orthodox plain and simple, without the addition of purely, he still would not say it well, but he would no doubt say it less badly: he would not say it well, because the epithet orthodox should be applied in the question only to propositions or doctrines decided by the Church; and although the motion of the sun is spoken of in the sacred writings, the Church, until Galileo, had never decided nor concerned itself with such a matter: but he would say it less badly than with the restrictive purely, because the word might then have been taken in the sense of doctrines universally admitted in the science of that age, and with all the more reason throughout Christendom, the ecclesiastical powers included, yet not by any decision, but as universal science. But purely orthodox is equivalent to merely, nothing but orthodox, a qualifier that cannot be applied to doctrines that were also principally astronomical; which might or might not be orthodox, but never purely or exclusively so.

[p. 333]

Let us come now to eliminated, and let us suppose at once that there is no misprint.
