In that case let us translate the proposition: to eliminate is as much as to banish or to erase, and in this last sense it is applied in algebra to a procedure by virtue of which fractions are made to disappear: we have, then, “doctrines banished or erased from science in order to raise them to dogma”: which cannot be the author’s meaning, for he knows that those doctrines, far from being banished, were so rooted in the science of the time that they were one of the causes that contributed to the persecution of the very sage who strove to refute them. So this hypothesis cannot pass.—Let us see whether the one of substituting another word for the term eliminated is more fortunate.—And what word shall we substitute for it? It is plain that here the choice belongs by right to the author; but as I do not have him at hand to ask him, I am obliged to choose for him, his right remaining reserved to put whichever he pleases in place of mine; though it will be hard for him to come out with good sense from that period, whichever word he might adopt.—Let us suppose, then, that he meant to say emanating or derived, which is the least unfavorable thing that can be supposed of him, since after all it is a truth that they were doctrines deduced from the science of that age: but even in that case, what sense can there be in saying that “no one until Galileo, that is, Galileo himself, dared to submit to investigation purely orthodox doctrines, emanating from science, in order to raise them to dogma, as is set down in our Vulgate?” If they were orthodox, there was no longer any need to raise them to the sphere of dogmas, because by that mere fact they were so already.—But let us suppose that they were raised before or afterwards: in that case it would be, or might be, the Church, or the partisans of the contrary opinion, who raised or sought to raise such theories to the rank of dogma; but never Galileo, who was precisely the one striving to bring them down from that rank and even to eliminate them in perpetuum from the fold of true science;—and even so, with our interpretation, a favor is done

[p. 334]

to Mr. Adicto, for we have given its full latitude to the word dogma, extending it to any doctrine generally consecrated by the science of the age, whereas he restricts it to religious science, as his expressions “as is in our Vulgate” show only too well: a purpose still more contradictory on the part of the celebrated Galileo. But there is nothing that is not open to censure in that ill-starred period of the author’s. “Dogma as it is in the Vulgate,” he says:—In the Vulgate there is no such dogma: dogma means a prescriptive maxim in which this or that point is taught and commanded to be believed; but there is no such thing in the passages of the Bible that treat of the motion of the sun and the stillness of the earth: nothing is prescribed or taught in them, but the portentous events that befell are merely related, accommodating themselves to the opinions and language universally adopted. But let us go on to another point, for later, and in due season, we shall return to this last one.—How does Mr. Adicto dare to assert that Galileo was the first (without my seeking in the least to lessen his eminent merit) who dared to oppose these doctrines and call them to examination, when it was nearly a century since Copernicus had published the famous work on the system of the world that bears his name, dedicated to no less a person than the then reigning pope, Paul III; a system which, as is well known, revived and enlarged the ideas of Pythagoras on the matter? But who does not know that Galileo himself always designates it by the name of the Copernican system? This slip on the author’s part is all the more remarkable as he generally shows himself very addicted and given to rummaging through erudition. —But I wish to show him more and more that he has not paid attention to the matter on which he writes. Has not Mr. Adicto been struck by the extraordinary phenomenon in the history of the human understanding, that the very doctrines which had been published with impunity by Copernicus, and not just anyhow, but in a book dedicated to the Roman Pontiff, seeking perhaps an aegis in the very

[p. 335]

dedication, should have drawn down upon Galileo, nearly a hundred years later, when civilization had made some progress, all the terrors of the Holy Office? Various were the causes that concurred to produce this result, and which, so as not to stray too far from my purpose, I shall do no more than indicate rapidly.

In the time of Copernicus the ecclesiastical authority could not yet be so much on tenterhooks, so to speak, as in that of Galileo, when it had already suffered the attacks of the various innovators in philosophical and religious matters; when Protestantism, which had already raised its standard in Germany, had stripped the Church there of at least a part of its power; while under the immediate shadow of the Holy See philosophers were bound to be less bold in their speculations and less frank in the promulgation of their doctrines; to which causes is joined another very effective one, if it is not the principal, and which we find in Galileo’s own temper and in his relations with a political party which was, like him, hostile to the Aristotelian opinions that were then dominant: motives all of which contributed more than enough to embitter tempers and to commit both sides to the fray. So true is this that there is no lack of data to prove that the Florentine astronomer, far from being persecuted, was very signally favored in the question by no fewer than two Pontiffs, Paul V and Urban VIII, with whom he had lengthy interviews, and from whom he received salutary counsels and admonitions, the more dictated by sincerity in that they did not content themselves with words, but displayed the greatest zeal on his behalf, as is all gathered from his correspondence with his friends and with various personages of that age. The war was properly waged on him by the followers of the Aristotelian doctrines, whose upholders, these being dominant at the time, were able to turn the Authority into the instrument of their machinations.—And here is a point of view from which it does not appear that Mr. Adicto had

[p. 336]

looked at the question; and he must remain convinced this time that there is scarcely a matter, even among those seemingly most thoroughly established, in which it is not necessary to renew the examination, if we wish to give ourselves an exact account of things, leaving the sad category of mere repeaters.

Returning now to the passages of Scripture to which I offered to confine myself, I shall repeat with the great Father Saint Augustine, referring to another analogous question, that, since the end of the sacred writings is not to form physicists or mathematicians but religious men, it is not strange that, being concerned principally with what pertains to opening to us the ways of justification, they should accommodate themselves to the common language of people. Even learned astronomers themselves would become insufferable pedants if in such cases, setting aside common language, they used the rigorous and exact language demanded by the demonstrations of science. Such is the empire of usage in matters of language, as the profane writer said long ago, that among us now, for example, and in spite of protests having been raised against the use of the word gas applied to a combustible liquid intended for lighting, even the knowledgeable see themselves a thousand times obliged to use it in the sense in which the torrent of the vulgar applies it and sweeps them along. But let us not insist on matters which, being only too well known, need no further elucidation; our Adicto already has more than enough material to feel for himself that he must not confuse dogma with the simple narration of facts, or the mere description of appearances, nor yet the different character of the decisions of one and the same Authority, according to the points on which they bear. I am left only with regret, since he is so given to the flowers of erudition, at not copying out for him to the letter the delightful epistles of Galileo himself, where the matter is examined so profoundly and originally, and clothed with such charm of style, as was to be expected from the hands of that universal genius, as subtle and shrewd in inventing as he was easy and flexible in expounding. It is a delightful and edifying

[p. 337]

fragrance that breathes from all his pages, full at once of enchantment and dignity.

Let us repeat, then, in conclusion, with the illustrious Galileo, and in the sense of Charles Comte, “épur sí muove;” [and yet it moves] not to thunder at our Adicto, as in his own expression the proselytes of fanaticism did, but to remind him of the lesson that results from all these discussions; é pur sí muove means that it is necessary to keep abreast of the progress of science so as not to be swallowed up by the torrent of time, of the time which, “erasing the lies of opinions, confirms the judgments of nature:” (1) é pur sí muove, for movement is as much the soul and condition of the phenomena of the moral world as of the effects of the physical world.

## VII. (2)

11º—The author now goes on to demonstrate what no one has denied him, and what is in the nature of things: the backwardness of the ancients with respect to the moderns in the natural sciences, and notably in Astronomy; and although it would be easy for me to show that he has not been of the happiest in his choice of example, I do not wish to dwell on the particular, both because it is unnecessary to the principal question and because I have already shown him that antiquity was more advanced in physico-mathematical knowledge than is commonly believed. In fact, many pages of the history of Astronomy (for example, the labors of the great Hipparchus) would suffice to belie many of the propositions that Adicto lays down in his account. Thus even the points on which this gentleman is best grounded are subject to their ifs and buts, and cannot be admitted without distinctions and alterations. Some observation, however, I must make to our author on this score. I tell him, then, that in

(1) Cicero.

(2) Unpublished.—(A. Z.)

[p. 338]

regard to the history of astronomical science that writer is not the best guide whom I have imagined him to designate under the allusion of the colossus of the XVIIIth century, for he was not an astronomer, nor did he carry out profound investigations on the subject. Item, moreover, in order for readers to judge in the matter it is necessary, if not to be an astronomer by profession, at least to possess certain knowledge on which the judgment may be founded. On this occasion I shall make an observation that seems to me applicable to a great part of Adicto’s paper, or rather to Adicto’s conduct in writing, and it is that he reads only one single book on the matter he has in hand, a thing that does not suffice in points of erudition. Were it not so, how could certain thoughts seem new and extraordinary to him which, besides being in no way surprising or original, are found repeated infinite times in the ancient writers themselves? To the proof. “The first rudiments in every kind,” he says, “are slower than the great advances.” “This thought,” he adds, “full of truth and wisdom, is the product of the colossal genius who astounded Europe with his learning in the century just past.” In the first place, not the lights of erudition but the simple light of reason is needed to judge that such a thought, though true, is not so original as it seems to Mr. Adicto. Colossus, and very much a colossus, the astounder of Europe in the XVIIIth century may be: but the appraisal of his strength cannot be made from that sample of it you give, and much less when it is not his own property. An ancient, and not one of those who sit in the choir of the Homers and Platos, but of those who stand in the second rank, had already said, and better (for not even propriety is in excess in the expression of the colossal thought), “omnium rerum principia parva sunt sed suis progressionibus usa augentur,” which, turned into the vernacular, amounts to “all things are small in their beginnings, but afterwards grow with their progress,” an observation so inspired in man by everything that surrounds him that even to a savage it can and must

[p. 339]

occur, as soon as he has the poorest stock of experience. So we find that the vaunted gigantic thought has come down to a trivial one: without this meaning that I deny that great and ingenious ideas are to be found (and so many!) in the writer to whom we allude; but neither does he himself hold that one to be such, nor is it possible to infer the lion from such a claw. All this on the supposition of granting Adicto that the colossus to whom he refers is as much a giant as he believes, who might well turn out a pygmy in the scientific matter in question, inasmuch as universality joined to profundity is almost impossible to find; for not every day, nor even every century, are Aristotles and Leibnizes born.

12º Vain is the pains our author takes in devoting two entire columns to making manifest the connection and common origin of the moral sciences, no less than that of the physical sciences, for these are principles that no one has denied him; and whoever had not read my earlier writings would judge, on seeing the tenor of his, and the eagerness he puts into setting forth certain matters, that these were precisely the ones to which I had shown most opposition. To no one could that long sermon be less justly or less opportunely addressed than to me, especially on the part of one who calls himself my pupil, and who, by his reckoning, must have been so from the year 1824, and already then heard repeatedly from my lips, and if his memory fails him can see it printed in my syllabuses, “that the science of nature is no more than one, and that the divisions men have made, being caused by the limitation of their powers and the immense variety of objects, are no more than branches of one and the same trunk:” besides which, this is the ABC or obligatory introduction to every course in any of the natural sciences, as may be seen in all the didactic works that exist on the subject. So that more originality and merit than results from setting forth the connection of the moral sciences among themselves, similar to that of the physical sciences, which is all that Adicto does in this place, could be claimed for making it manifest that, the former being likewise sciences of observation

[p. 340]

too, and procedures and methods analogous to those of the latter having to be followed in them, and bearing likewise on the nature of things, they turn out to be in every respect as much branches of the same trunk as the others.—Adicto touches somewhat on this point of view in another part of his writing, but, besides not doing so with the specification indicated, he seems there to be unfolding my opinions rather than his own.

13º So my Mr. Adicto has also fallen in with the fashion of putting about that the sensationalist philosophers attribute ideas to the external senses exclusively (almost, he writes) without the participation of the internal sense?—When, where, how, or by whom was such a thing ever claimed? How can experience, the source to which the sensationalists refer our notions, be conceived without the participation of the active internal principle that judges and passes sentence on the very impressions that external objects cause upon the sensible organs?

So you too have taken up the craze of exclusivism? Mind you, that big word is nothing but one of the bogeymen with which the soi-disant eclectics frighten unwary youth—eclectics they truly are in this business of choosing the means to suit everyone’s taste and to excite each one’s fears. So they cry materialism! to frighten the timorous; exclusivism! to attract the rest, those who pride themselves on being generous and impartial; eclecticism! so that many, dazzled by a banner so proper to the century, may hasten to swell the ranks; and all may carry away the great lesson of intolerantism, under the hypocritical appearance of magnanimity and magni-capacity.

Exclusivism!—yes, it is necessary to be exclusive, and all those who believe their opinion well founded necessarily are so, including those very people, or rather, in the front rank, those very people who make a show of the contrary. Let us come to accounts: the whole question is this: it is a matter of explaining a phenomenon of nature, for example how ideas are acquired; one of the

[p. 341]

systems says it is this way, the other in a contrary way.—Now a third party steps into the quarrel, saying: I will reconcile you, gentlemen; we shall explain one part of the phenomena by the one doctrine and the remainder by the other, or of the two we shall make one.—If the first proposal seems irrational, what shall we say of the second? Well, this is the fundamental idea of so-called eclecticism.

With the verbiage of exclusivism and of intolerance fright is instilled, the seed takes root, and it beguiles more than a few who consider themselves thinkers of weight—it is a marvel to behold. Thus some admit what is most contradictory and conflicting, just so as not to be exclusivists.—By all means let science busy itself with explaining the systems, and with giving an account of errors, as it does of successes, for these are so many facts within the jurisdiction of science; but this does not mean that doctrines most opposed to one another in their principles are to be upheld. It may also, and must, happen that if a system is made up of several ideas or elements, conciliation is possible with respect to some of them; but when a system is reduced to its simplest expression, or when it consists of a single fundamental idea, how is such a compromise possible? What! is there room for compromise in the phenomena of nature, as in human affairs, as in matters of Government, and at times even of literature? Here is another of the causes of the delusion: the parallel with political matters and matters of taste. True eclecticism must consist not in reconciling everything but in explaining everything; and to explain everything it is necessary to study everything.

Analyze, my friend, and you will see that in this there is nothing on the part of our opponents but voces et preterea nihil [words and nothing more]. But this is not a matter to be treated thus in passing; and having been touched on only to correct an idea of yours, the slight reflections we have just made suffice for the purpose. I wish only to advise you not to allege monomanias and idiosyncrasies in support of the so-called spiritualists

[p. 342]

and as an objection to the sensationalists, because in so doing you only give arms to the latter, with the inordinate advantage of calling them to fight on their own ground.

You give us to understand, with the case of the brave soldier flying into a fury at the mere sight of a thimble, that the sensationalists maintain the necessity of the actual presence (pardon us the redundancy) of sensible objects in order to produce ideas, as if they denied the power of the imagination, the reproductive faculty of memory, and even the influence of association, both in that kind of phenomena and in the most ordinary effects offered by the exercise of the intellectual operations. Do you not observe, on the contrary, that the phenomena presented by mental derangement, cured on countless occasions through the very state of the various internal organs, making these so many true senses, corroborate more and more the doctrine of the so-called sensationalists?—Better not stir it.... and hold one’s tongue! and let us hold our tongues, gentlemen of pseudo-eclecticism!

❦

[PDF 330]

❖      ❖     ❖     ❖     ❖      ❖     ❖      ❖     ❖      ❖     ❖     ❖      ❖     ❖      ❖

## P hilosophy . (1)

Correction.

To the Citizen of the world, residing in Trinidad, greetings!

Great is the honor you do Mr. Varela and me in pointing out that, notwithstanding our intimate friendship, we differ in philosophical opinions. But this proposition, presented thus in isolation, and above all conceived in the terms in which you have put it, deserves more than one correction. —Here are the words of the “Citizen of the world:” “Even Don José de la Luz y Caballero, who, according to what I hear, is perhaps a greater friend of Mr. Varela than you (the Lugareño) are, does not follow his philosophy.” “Evident proof,

Don José Ignacio Rodríguez reproduced this piece by Luz in Appendix E of his “Vida del Presbítero D. Félix Varela.—Nueva-York—1878.”—(A. Z.)

(a) See Appendix no. 17.—(A. Z.)

(1) I insert this article immediately after the polemics on Question of Method, because of its reference to them. It was published in the “Gaceta de Puerto Príncipe” of May 2, 1840, preceded by the following lines from Don Anastasio de Orozco.—“Mr. Editor of the ‘Gaceta de Puerto del Príncipe.’ Dear Sir: I send you an article which my very dear friend and kinsman Don José de la Luz y Caballero has sent me from Havana; he forgoes the pleasure of publishing it in that city for the friendly sentiment of defending our friend Lugareño on his own ground, Camagüey, where the question with the Citizen of the world has been raised.” (a)

[p. 344]

you add, carrying the consequence of your own premise too far, that he is in accord neither with his method nor with his philosophical doctrines.” I said that your assertions stood in need of more than one correction, and I shall prove it at once.

To begin with, it cannot be asserted with any foundation, merely because I disagree with some few of Mr. Varela’s opinions, that I do not follow his philosophy, a word under which a whole system of doctrines is comprised. It is well known that the work of my illustrious countryman serves as the text for my lessons on every day of the week except Saturday, and sometimes one day more, which I devote exclusively to refuting the doctrines of the French eclectic school, making the very writings of its famous coryphaeus the target of our polemic.

And see now, briefly, Mr. Citizen of the world, how I am a disciple of Varela in many respects. Varela overthrew scholasticism on our soil, and I applaud and shall applaud its ruin. Varela was our legitimate Descartes, in more than one sense: both for having destroyed the principle of authority by counsel and example, in word and deed, and for having in consequence introduced philosophical freedom of thought, the true eclecticism; so that, not content with destroying, he set himself to build, and to build with materials of the most exquisite quality he had at his disposal; precisely with those which serve today as the basis even of those of my doctrines in which we differ, or may differ. Varela took the great, the bold step of introducing Physiology, an indispensable element for the complete study of man, into the field of science. I do not merely follow in his footsteps in this, but see in that study the future not only of Philosophy strictly so called, but even of Education, Ethics, and Legislation.

Varela was always the declared enemy of all Ontology, and I never cease to wage war on that Proteus, whatever

[p. 345]

the specious, and even venerable, forms in which it seeks to present itself.

Varela was the first, and more than anyone in this country, to discern the importance of the physical sciences, not only for the material advancement of society, but for directing and strengthening the understanding in every kind of investigation, and very particularly for the progress of rational Philosophy, or Philosophy properly so called. I hold it superfluous to demonstrate that I abound in the same principles, my endeavors for the diffusion of this kind of knowledge being notorious—knowledge that is wanted in all careers and circumstances, and very especially by a great part of our youth, who, devoted exclusively to Jurisprudence, lack a criterion for judging the countless cases that later present themselves to them in the vast field of human transactions; this want is also a very principal cause of the spread among us of many of the errors with which the works of the metaphysicians are infested, and notably those of the new pseudo-eclectics, not excepting even those who are physiologists of the school.

Lastly, and not to prolong this review further, Varela, not satisfied with emancipating our understanding, not satisfied with what seemed the substance, and wishing to make the reform effective, attended also to the instrument, and in the manner of Julius Caesar, nihil actum reputans, si quid superesset agendum [counting nothing done while anything remained to be done], he cut as well the shackles that chained the exposition of the sciences to the signs of a dead language, though one idolized and possessed by him (the greater merit!) to a degree of which those who judge only by his writings in that divine idiom, despite the elegance and Atticism that distinguish them, can as yet form no idea: for to gauge his mastery one had to have experienced, in sweet intercourse with this sweetest Varela, the ease and facility with which he handled the tongue of Marcus Tullius and of Terence in familiar conversation. I, though as fond as he, and as fond as any man, of the robust tongue

[p. 346]

of Latium, that tongue of men who were men, need not say (and who can any longer call such a principle into question in the middle of the XIXth century?) that I would sacrifice all my predilections for the sake of providing the easiest and most expeditious vehicle for the diffusion of knowledge.

And with this the first assertion of Mr. Citizen of the world seems to me more than sufficiently corrected. Let us pass now to the other correction promised:

“Evident proof, you add, that he (the present writer) is in accord neither with his method nor with his doctrines.” In the first place, I object to the inference; for from the fact that two individuals differ in some, and even in many, opinions in the vast field of a science, and above all in the science of sciences, it does not follow in good logic that they disagree even in method; and so far is there from being any contradiction in this, that even in the sciences of peculiar experimentation, for example in Chemistry, though all its cultivators follow the same method of appealing to the laboratory, and even at times obtain the same facts, the most heated controversies arise over the inductions that are sought to be drawn from the said data, since that operation is the work of each man’s head, and there is therefore room for the tot capita tot sententiæ [so many heads, so many opinions]: witness the hard-fought contests going on at this very day between the famous Swedish chemist Berzelius and one of the foremost chemists of France, namely Dumas.

In the second place, it is very probable that Mr. Citizen of the world has imagined that, because Mr. Varela places his Logic and Ethics before Physics in his “Lecciones de Filosofía,” and because I have maintained, and in my view proved, that the physical sciences ought to be studied before, or independently of, the so-called moral sciences, we differ completely on the question of method, or order, in teaching. And in good faith Mr. Citizen has had grounds for judging thus on this point, for he will find in me nothing but justice and impartiality. But I shall set forth briefly how the matter stands, and I hope to leave completely satisfied

[p. 347]

my purpose of correcting the said second notion.

It may well be that Mr. Varela did not at that period (more than twenty years ago) share my ideas on the question of method; but even had he shared them, it was not exclusively in his power to alter the general order of studies at that time, either in the San Carlos Seminary itself or in the Royal and Pontifical University, on which that establishment depended and still depends. (1) It is true that the Statutes of the College allowed the professor much more latitude than those of the University for making alterations and improvements, and our patriotic and zealous master took advantage of so precious a circumstance to carry out as many reforms as he could for the benefit of the studious youth of the country. What is more! He overstepped the limits of the statute in more than one most important and far-reaching reform, as has been proved, struggling and ever struggling—for nothing great is achieved without struggle— and supported at times by the suggestions and counsels of the great Espada (unicuique suum [to each his own]), supreme head of the Seminary and a head born for everything.

Different, however, have been the circumstances in which I have found myself with respect to the question of order in studies. It may truly be said that for me it has been only to wish and to obtain. It was in fact a matter of establishing chairs of Philosophy in the colleges of San Fernando and San Cristóbal, about the year 1833, and as the Royal order on the matter entrusted to the ever-lamented Don Francisco Arango y Parreño, in the capacity of special commissioner for the plan of studies, the inspection and interim sanction of whatever the Directors of the said establishments might propose to that end—these being at the time Don Narciso Piñeyro of the first and the present speaker of the second—we seized

(1) At this very day Mr. Ruiz, who teaches in the Seminary and at Carraguao, begins the course in the first establishment with Logic and in the second with Physics.

[p. 348]

the opportunity to promote all the improvements and reforms we judged useful, and among them the precedence, not preference, of the study of Physics in the course of Philosophy, according to the branches of which it consists on our soil; and we seized the opportunity with all the more spirit since we were sure of the approval of the enlightened Mr. Arango for whatever redounded to the benefit of the country, the unceasing object of his vigilance throughout his long public career. The said improvement had the good fortune to obtain, not merely his approval, but his most decided applause, as no one is unaware at present, least of all in Puerto Príncipe, after the polemic raised there at the beginning of 1838 by Mr. Rumilio, and continued by the Dómine in the same Gaceta of Camagüey, with him who traces these rough lines.

But be that as it may, let Mr. Citizen of the world know that I have the honor of counting Mr. Varela among the decided partisans of the method of teaching Physics before Psychology, as he has expressly declared to me in a letter written months ago from New York, (1) which I do not have before me now; and, to be more precise, he adds in it, if my memory is not unfaithful, that he was in everything of my opinion, and perhaps also for other reasons which I had not adduced, or something to that effect, for I remember the spirit more than the letter.

So then, believing that I have fulfilled my purpose of correcting the mistaken notion under which Mr. Citizen of the World labors regarding my philosophical opinions as compared with those of my illustrious master, I conclude by hoping from his sense of justice that he will be pleased to apply to the case the precious maxim so opportunely invoked by El Lugareño, by that patriot proof against every test, who is all chivalry and good intention, sapientis est mutare consilium [it is the part of a wise man to change his counsel]; and let the Citizen know, and let the world know, that the modest Lugareño, in rendering that

(1) See appendix no. 18.—(A. Z.)

[p. 349]

testimony of his zeal to the illustrious Varela, did nothing but yield to a profound feeling of gratitude, of justice, of love for his native soil; for so long as the Island of Cuba is thought of, he will be thought of who first taught us to think.

Havana, April 20, 1840.

❦

[PDF 337]

❖     ❖      ❖     ❖      ❖      ❖     ❖      ❖     ❖      ❖     ❖      ❖     ❖      ❖      ❖

## P hilosophy . (1)

On reading the discussion that has arisen over an article written by M. Costales on the necessity of the study of Ideology for that of Literature, and criticized with excessive levity and injustice by an Eclectic, who suddenly and without previous recommendations has appeared for the first time before the public with the vain and pompous title of Savior of Philosophy, we have taken up the pen to make various observations, which have no other object than to prove the falsity of some doctrines which Mr. Eclectic blindly adopts and follows, and the great errors he has committed through having disregarded the rules of ideology in setting up a purely literary discussion. We shall say nothing of the spirit of opposition observable in his writings, because in taking that trouble we should be false to our character, and it would appear clearly

(1) This article was inserted in the Diario de la Habana of September 21, 1838, signed “A Lover of Truth,” and it was occasioned by a polemic being carried on in that newspaper by Messrs. Don Manuel Costales and “The Eclectic.” The former had published in La Siempreviva an article entitled “Ideology,” and the latter attacked its doctrines in the Diario of August 11 of the said year. The discussion once begun, Costales maintained his opinions in the Diarios of August 16 and September 13, and “The Eclectic” his in those of August 24 and September 27.— (A. Z.)

[p. 352]

proved before the public that Mr. Savior of Philosophy has not been able to be the savior of himself, because, giving entry to the passions, he has directed his ill-advised pen along a path contrary to the one he ought to have followed as a teacher of youth and as Savior of Philosophy.

If Mr. Eclectic, guided by the light of reason, had kept solely to the facts, he would have opened a discussion of the highest interest for the sciences; but, incautious, or perhaps ill-advised, he preferred to hurl himself into the abyss of conjectures; and as Mr. Eclectic, as may be deduced from his writings, is not much accustomed to exactness in his judgments, nor dwells much on drawing consequences, nor observes, in short, the best-demonstrated philosophical rules on entering upon a question, and, declaring a systematic opposition to the school of Condillac, scorns even the word ideology as belonging to the XVIIIth century, he has inevitably and necessarily fallen into error, thereby giving proof that he is very little instructed in philosophical matters, and that he has not known how to sustain the pompous title of Savior of Philosophy with which he has presented himself.

When a man filled with an investigating and philosophical spirit seeks the truth, animated solely by the desire to find it; when a well-understood wish to diffuse enlightenment brings his writings before a public in which he may perhaps meet learned and profound philosophers who will judge them severely, he ought to proceed with great moderation, arguing only with the weapons of reason and of conviction, without ungratefully insulting the memory of celebrated men who have rendered great services to the sciences, without despising their opinions merely because they belonged to past centuries. Let these be examined with deliberation and impartiality, let them be combated with reasons, not with sophisms, and if they were generally adopted, and it is proved that they are erroneous and that by their interest and high importance they compromise society and stand opposed to the progress of enlightenment, then, and only then, could

[p. 353]

we say to him who should render so great a service: You have been useful to the youth of our country and to the whole world; you deserve our gratitude and our admiration for having been our Savior.

To say that Ideology is the badge of the school of sensationalism is not to understand the true meaning of this word; it is to make arbitrary interpretations incompatible with the rectitude with which a good philosopher ought always to proceed. Know, Mr. Eclectic, that the word ideology means: that part of philosophy which treats of the origin of our ideas. It does not specify the means by which they are acquired, for that knowledge is the result of its study. Mr. Eclectic says with great levity that Mr. Costales ought not to have used the word ideology because it is the ensign of the XVIIIth century; this proves to the point of evidence that Mr. Eclectic does not study the origin of our ideas, or, what is the same, does not study ideology; and as this study constitutes the object of the school of Condillac, it follows necessarily that he is the mortal enemy of that school; therefore he who signs himself Eclectic is no eclectic, but a systematist, a most stubborn defender of spiritualism; therefore it is not the desire to propagate enlightenment that moves his pen.

Mr. Systematist, for so we shall call him henceforth, blindly admits that abstractions are ideas; he has not fixed the true sense of this word, and we regret to tell him that in this he has committed a grave error, for abstraction, being the consideration of a property as existing by itself alone, is an operation of the soul; unless Mr. Systematist has altered the meaning of this word, accommodating it to his system, or wishes, as a necessary consequence of his principles, to confound the means of acquiring ideas with the ideas themselves.

Mr. Systematist blindly affirms that the history of the sciences in no way differs from that of the human race; we regret also to tell him that in this he commits another grave error,

[p. 354]

for, the sciences being a body of exact principles, errors belong only to their history; and as that of the human race comprises the vices, crimes, offenses, etc. of men, it is clear and evident that it does not enter into the domain of the sciences. Therefore the history of the sciences differs much from that of the human race; therefore Mr. Systematist has not understood what Mr. M. Costales has told him on this point, or else he is stubborn and rebellious against the force of reason and the convictions of reasoning.

The exactness of ideas: here is the principal merit of the historian and of the philosopher. Without this essential requisite their productions would have no value, and far from interesting us in their favor, we should regard them with indifference and displeasure, lamenting their blunders and errors. The same we say of the poet, for, words being the expression of our ideas, if the former are not exact, neither will the latter be. When poets do not express their ideas with exactness, we consider them deluded, and their productions the offspring of a delirious imagination, and we lament their aberrations. The true philosopher always seeks exactness and truth, combating error wherever it may be found, wherever self-love may protect it; and as Mr. Systematist blindly maintains that to be a poet it is necessary to be inexact, or, what is the same, to commit errors, we conclude that Mr. Systematist is anti-philosophical, and, he being so, we do not regard him as a man who seeks the truth impartially. Thus, then, he ought not to have disputed on philosophical matters, and it is he who attacks and threatens the present state of philosophy, proclaiming errors and upholding false doctrines.

Mr. Systematist affirms that Ideology “is the ensign of the XVIIIth century;” this is another error, for in the century we have reached the study of our primitive ideas has been and is recommended, a study which must necessarily precede that of later ideas, of those abstract ideas which man does not form until he is accustomed

[p. 355]

to think. The writer of the article says in the 7th paragraph of his public debut that “Psychology is the study of ideas,” and, expressing himself thus in so general and vague a manner, he must necessarily agree that for this study it is proper to begin with the origin of our ideas, of those that are acquired first, and to follow the gradual development of the other ideas, so as to have a complete and exact knowledge of them. These and no others are the rules prescribed by a true and luminous philosophy, and which he who devotes himself to the study of the sciences ought to follow. He who dispenses with this fundamental basis of later knowledge will stumble upon error at every step, will not know how to uphold the truth, and his writings will bear a stamp of clumsiness and inexactitude. Therefore the study of Ideology is necessary for that of Literature; therefore Mr. Systematist has proved nothing with his articles.

We shall conclude by observing that in the article “Ideology” nothing is said of sensationalism, nothing of spiritualism; that there is not in it so much as one idea, one opinion, from which the school to which its author belongs might be suspected. In writing it he sought to encourage the youth of our country so that, proceeding always in accordance with ideological principles, they might think with exactness and, making their productions worthy of the esteem and consideration of sensible men, might thus contribute to the advancement of the sciences and to the progress of enlightenment. We are persuaded that if this same article, which Mr. Systematist has criticized so bitterly, had borne the title of Psychology instead of that of Ideology, he would not have made himself known to the public. Finally, we advise him, with the frankness that is characteristic of us, to recover his calm, to read with interest and deliberation the article he has tried to destroy, and, if he means to set up as a critic, to profit by the sound doctrines and luminous principles it contains; for in this way he will always proceed with exactness and method, and will learn to propose a literary

[p. 356]

question, to sustain his opinion duly, and he will be spared having someone come forward to prove his errors and aberrations to him.

A Lover of Truth.

*

**

Mr. Eclectic (1).

Moved by the interest which the question raised by you against Mr. M. Costales has aroused in us, and desiring to illustrate the matter with some observations, we earnestly beg you to answer the following questions, for we wish to know your way of thinking before giving our own opinion:

1st What you understand by idea and by abstraction, and whether you admit a difference between these two words.

2nd Whether the true philosopher ought or ought not to study the origin of our ideas.

3rd In the event of answering the preceding question in the affirmative, whether or not you admit that these are acquired through the senses.

4th If you deny that our first ideas have sensations for their cause, explain, in accordance with the philosophical doctrines you profess, why deaf-mutes from birth are so limited in their intellectual faculties.

5th Whether the ideas of justice, of goodness, etc. are innate, or the result of comparisons after man has exercised his senses; and whether these ideas can be had without some object being represented to our soul.

6th Ideas being a result of the exercise of the intellectual faculties, whether this exercise can take place without the intermediary of the brain and of the nerves, and whether our soul proceeds in isolation in the operations of thought.

7th If you answer the preceding question in the negative, explain, so far as it bears

(1) Diario de la Habana of September 27, 1838 (A. Z.).

[p. 357]

on philosophy, and in conformity with the doctrines you follow, the causes of madness and of idiocy, telling us at the same time whether idiots have an idea of God, of justice, of goodness, of cause, etc., and the other abstract ideas whose origin you seem to wish to deny absolutely to the senses.

We eagerly await the answer to these questions, and we believe at the same time that you, Mr. Eclectic, will not slight us, (1) for so we hope from the good wishes you show for the progress of enlightenment. In this confidence I remain your faithful servant, who kisses your hand.

The Friend of Youth.

❦

(1) The Eclectic gave no public answer to these questions, and the polemic was abandoned (A. Z.).
