A ppendices .

I.

D. O. M.

[Deo Optimo Maximo: to God, Best and Greatest]

## WHO GRACIOUSLY ATTENDS THE STUDIES OF YOUTH

## THE WHOLE MATTER OF EXTINGUISHING

## OBLIGATIONS, IN ROMAN

AS WELL AS IN SPANISH LAW,

IS DEVOTED AND CONSECRATED BY

D. JOSÉ DE LA LUZ Y CABALLERO,

## STUDENT SCHOLAR OF THIS SEMINARY COLLEGE

The place of the disputation shall be the Great Hall of the same College, on the 15th day of July of the year 1817, at the customary hour, there presiding Fr. Lic. D. Justo Vélez, Moderator of National Law.

BY PERMISSION OF THE SUPERIORS.

HAVANA: PRINTED BY DON PEDRO NOLASCO PALMER.

[p. 360]

II I certify that by decree of this date, issued by the Provisor and Vicar General Governor of this Bishopric, by appointment of His Excellency the diocesan Bishop, Don José de la Luz y Caballero is appointed provisional Professor of Philosophy in the Royal and Conciliar Seminary College of this city, with assignment of the income and emoluments of the chair. Havana, the ninth of September of one thousand eight hundred and twenty-four.—Francisco Mª Castañeda .—Vice-Secretary.

III. On the thirty-first day of October of the year one thousand eight hundred and thirty-three: the three canonical banns having been dispensed by His Excellency Dr. Don Juan Bernardo O-Gavan, Vicar Capitular etc. I, Don Agustín Díaz Ramírez, Lieutenant Curate of this Auxiliary Church of the Santo Cristo del Buenviaje of Havana, with license of His Excellency and Most Illustrious Lordship the Archbishop Administrator, signed the day before yesterday before the secretary Don Ignacio María Olea, joined in marriage, in the dwelling house of the bride, Don José de la Luz with Doña Mariana Romay, unmarried, natives and residents of this city, the former of the parish of the Espíritu Santo, legitimate son of Lieutenant Colonel Don Antonio de la Luz and of Doña Manuela Teresa Caballero, and the latter of this parish, legitimate daughter of Dr. Don Tomás Romay and of Doña Mariana González; to whom, having put the question, I had for answer their mutual consent, of which the witnesses were Don Manuel Iturrondo, ordinand, and Don Agustín de Palma; I instructed them to receive the nuptial blessing at the first opportunity; the sponsors were Don Francisco de la Luz and Doña Rita de Zayas Bazán: and I signed it.—Agustín Díaz.

IV. Royal Economic Society,

I have the satisfaction of communicating to Your Lordship the appointment which the Royal Economic Society, at its first general meeting, was pleased to make of the worthy person of Your Lordship to fill the Vice- Directorship

[p. 361]

for the coming biennium, Your Lordship being able to attend the last meeting to take possession of your post.—God keep Your Lordship many years. Havana, December 17, 1834.—Antonio Zambrana.

V.

Political and Military Government OF HAVANA.

On this date I write to the Rev. Father Rector of the Royal and Pontifical University of this City as follows:—The file drawn up in consequence of the representation made by Don José de la Luz, that he be granted permission to conduct a class of Philosophy in this city, having been passed to the First Lieutenant Assessor General, the latter has advised me, under date of the 4th instant, as follows.—Most Excellent Sir.—In view of the favorable report of the Most Rev. Rector and of the circumstances that concur in Don José de la Luz, an accredited professor of Philosophy, Your Excellency may be pleased to grant him your Superior permission to establish in this City a Class in that science, in strict dependence on the University, as the said Rector indicates, and with the obligation of presenting within one year the approval of Her Majesty, saving etc.—And having concurred in this opinion, I transmit it to Your Reverend Lordship for your information and whatever else may be fitting.—Which I transcribe to you for the purpose that concerns you on your part.—God keep you many years.—Havana, 7 September 1838.—Joaquín de Ezpeleta.—Don José de la Luz.

VI.

Ministry of the Navy, COMMERCE AND GOVERNMENT OF OVERSEAS.

Section of Commerce and Overseas.

Most Excellent Sir.—Her Majesty the Queen Governor has been informed of the documented petition of the lawyer Don José de la Luz which Your Excellency

[p. 362]

forwarded in a letter of the 31st of October last, requesting that approval be given to the grant of permission which Your Excellency has made him to establish in that city a private chair of Philosophy, dependent on the University, asking at the same time that it be extended so that he may found a general College of Studies which, comprising within its institution a normal school on the bases and principles its nature demands, shall be subject to the rules and formalities prescribed for the Peninsula in the Royal Order of the 12th of August of last year; and Her Majesty, in being pleased to approve the permission which Your Excellency granted the said party to establish his private Chair of Philosophy, dependent on that University, has seen fit to order that Your Excellency report on the new grant he seeks relating to the foundation of a general College of studies.— By Royal Order I communicate it to Your Excellency for your information and the corresponding effects.—God keep Your Excellency many years. Madrid, 31 January 1839.—Chacón.—The Governor Captain General of the Island of Cuba.”

*

**

Ministry of the Navy &c.

Most Excellent Sir.—I have reported to Her Majesty the Queen Governor the letter of Your Excellency of the 31st of July last, number 235, in which, complying with the last part of the Royal Order of the 31st of January of the present year, Your Excellency reports on the claim of Don José de la Luz that the permission granted him to establish in that city a Chair of Philosophy be extended so that he may found a general College of Studies; and Her Majesty, being informed, at the same time that she has been pleased to grant Luz the favor of being able to set up the aforesaid

[p. 363]

College, on condition of not teaching the higher faculties in it, and of remaining subject to whatever may be enacted on public instruction in the special laws that are to govern in the Island, has been pleased to resolve, in conformity with the opinion of Your Excellency, that the said College be under the immediate inspection of that Government, Your Excellency being able, whenever you think fit, to name visitors to inform themselves of its state and of the teaching given in it, who shall give account to Your Excellency of all they observe, for your information and guidance; on the understanding that if, as a result of the information Your Excellency acquires, you should think it advisable that the said establishment should not continue, you may, in use of the powers which the laws grant you, order it closed at once, without need of further proceedings, formalities or consultations. By Royal Order I communicate it to Your Excellency for your information and other effects.—God keep Your Excellency many years.—Madrid, 26 September 1839.— The Governor Captain General of the Island of Cuba.

## VII

Political and Military Government OF HAVANA.

For one of the vacant places of substitute members of the Board of Population, I have seen fit to appoint you, trusting that you will accept a charge which the government confers on persons who merit its confidence and are capable of discharging the functions of the Corporation.—God keep you many years.—Havana, 4 March 1841.—The Prince of Anglona, Marquis of Javalquinto.—Don José de la Luz Caballero.

[p. 364]

## VIII

## ROYAL ACADEMY OF BELLES-LETTRES OF BARCELONA

Et Rege et Lege. [By King and by Law]

I, the undersigned, Secretary of the R oyal A cademy of B elles -L ettres of B arcelona , certify that at the Meeting held on the twenty-ninth day of January of this year, Don José de la Luz Caballero was admitted as Corresponding Member, and in witness whereof, and that it may serve him as Title, I issue the present, which I sign in Barcelona on the tenth of March of one thousand eight hundred and forty-one.—Seen and approved.—Joaquín Rey.—President.—Ramón Muns.—Member Secretary.

IX.

—Mr. Turnbull held in Havana the offices of Consul of H. B. M. and Superintendent of liberated Africans, the latter created by virtue of the treaties concluded by the governments of Spain and England in the years 1817 and 1835, with the aim of effectively and really abolishing the traffic in African slaves. The English agent carried out his charge with tenacity, was always on the alert, and besieged with his remonstrances the Captain General, who at that time was Don Jerónimo Valdés.

The English Consul was far too great an obstacle for so many men interested in the continuance of the slave trade and in the treaties with England remaining a dead letter; and so it is understandable that General Valdés should have concluded the dispatch in which he reported to the government of the Metropolis the insubordination...... of a handful of Black laborers......, and however persuaded he was “that this incident was entirely isolated,” by stating the “urgent necessity” of driving the British Consul from the island. Turnbull was, then, the object of hatred in that unhappy society, and

[p. 365]

the nightmare of General Valdés, to the point that one day in 1842 the first Authority of the Island exclaimed, in the presence of some persons of his court: “Who will rid me of this man!”—The wish to please the satrap was the origin of the attempt to exclude him, immediately after he was relieved, from the Economic Society, which had counted him since 1838 among its corresponding members.—(José de la Luz Caballero. Estudio crítico, by Manuel Sanguily.—Havana 1890.)

X.

Preliminary statement of the lawyer Don José de la Luz Caballero.— (September 26, 1844.)—Asked whether he knows or presumes what is the cause of his being at present held under arrest.—He answered that it is because the order of imprisonment of His Excellency the Captain General was served on him, through the Town Major of the Fortress, for complicity resulting against him in the case of the conspiracy of Blacks in this Island.

P., whether in this capital or in some other place he had known and had dealings with Mr. David Turnbull, formerly Consul of H. B. M. in this Island, stating in that case what relations he had with him and when was the last occasion he saw him.—C., that some five years ago he was introduced to him in the character of a learned traveler, during a short stay he made in this Island, before he was Consul and even before he had published his work on this country; and that he showed him all the attentions that are shown to a foreigner in such cases. Turnbull afterwards returned to the Island in the character of Consul, and then he came to visit the declarant, who returned the visit, and there it all ended: for his occupations on the one hand and his ailments on the other (for nearly four years now) have made him disappear completely from the stage of the world.

P., whether he could state on how many occasions and on what date he visited Turnbull after the latter took possession of his consulate.—C., that the one time referred to in his previous answer was the only occasion on which he was in his house, not having the date in mind, though it is easy to ascertain, because that functionary had only just arrived in this capital in the character of consul.

[p. 366]

P., in what company and by whom he was introduced to Turnbull the first time.—C., that at the house of Dr. Madden, a physician by profession and a member of the Mixed Commission, a friend of his.

P., whether he remembered the subjects on which the conversation turned.—C., that the conversation was miscellaneous, as it usually is at any table, and he remembers that there was much talk, among other scientific matters, of Meteorology, the differences between tropical phenomena and those of European climates being pointed out, all present having spoken indiscriminately on the subject.

P., who the other persons present were.—C., that the company was made up of the family of the said Madden and other foreigners engaged in commerce, whose names he does not remember, there being present also several foreign ladies, among them the wife of the aforesaid Madden.

P., where Mr. Turnbull then lived.—C., that on the Calzada de San Luis Gonzaga, where it is public and notorious that the said person lived.

P., whether he went alone on that visit.—C., that he went alone and found him accompanied by his wife, who did him the honors, he being at the time in the inner rooms, and without any other person appearing during that call.

P., as to the particulars of the conversation.—C., that the visit was brief and the conversation turned on indifferent matters, usual in such cases.

P., whether he had relations of friendship with Turnbull’s Secretary.—C., that never.

P., whether by chance he spoke at any time with Mr. Turnbull concerning the project for the absolute emancipation of the slaves of this Island.— C., that never.

P., whether he knows or has come to presume that Turnbull had attempted to spread notions intended to stir up the slaves, so that they might obtain their absolute emancipation.—C., that he has heard said what has been generally related on the matter.

P., whether he remembers having visited or frequented some Society near the Convent of Paula, which Mr. Turnbull attended now and then.— C., that this is the first news he has of any such Society.

P., whether he does not remember having gone again to Mr. Turnbull’s house in the period of the government of His Excellency Don Gerónimo Valdés, and in that case whether he

[p. 367]

made it accompanied by a young Englishman, stating likewise the object. —C., that never, neither alone nor accompanied, during the time to which the question is confined.

P., whether he likewise does not remember having made some delivery of money to Mr. Turnbull, and for what object.—C., that never in his life has he handed over sums of money, either to Mr. Turnbull or to anyone else in this world.

P., whether he has conferred with any person concerning the advantageous position in which the castle Number 4 stands.—C., that he has never spoken of castles.

P., whether he has known in this capital the foreign mulatto named Luis Gigaut.—C., that these days, on his return from France, are the first time he has heard him named.

P., whether during the government of His Excellency Don Gerónimo Valdés he has maintained intimate relations of friendship with Dr. Don Santiago Bombalier, Lic. Don Manuel Martínez Serrano, Don Domingo del Monte, Don Juan de Dios Corona and the other individuals named in these proceedings, who are pointed out to him by the Prosecutor for the declarant’s proper understanding.—C., that he knows Dr. Bombalier; that for many years he has had relations of friendship with the Licentiates Manuel Martínez Serrano and Don Domingo del Monte, and that as regards the other persons indicated, he knows some and not others, without restricting this to particular periods; but that he has certainly not been in contact with them during the government of Mr. Valdés.

P., whether at an earlier time the declarant spoke with Lic. Don Domingo del Monte and the other individuals who have been pointed out to him about particulars relating to the project for the absolute emancipation of the slaves in this Island.—C., that never.

P., whether it has come to his knowledge that some notable persons of the country have worked, in concert with Mr. David Turnbull, to have the said plan of emancipation carried into effect, and on what terms.—C., that never.

P., whether it has come to his notice that certain persons mixed up in the said project have worked from outside the country for its realization.— C. that never.

P., whether he has likewise been unable to understand the object and purpose of certain movements that were observed on the country estates toward the end of the year just past and the beginning of the present one. —C., that, being in Europe at the time, he cannot determine the object and purpose of such movements: there the first news of them arrived as partial uprisings on some estates, and consequently of all

[p. 368]

of them, they were classed like the other Black uprisings that have broken out from time to time in the Island; it was afterwards that the newspapers began to speak of a more general conspiracy, with ramifications in various parts of the Island, which was said to have been discovered by the Government.

P., whether he has no evidence for presuming that those movements were powerfully influenced by certain persons, interested on account of their aims and principles, eager perhaps to obtain by such means innovations in the system of government that rules in this Island.—C., that he neither has, nor believes there can exist, any such evidence.

P., on what date the declarant absented himself from this capital and to what place he went, that is, the last time he did so.—C., that on the 29th of May of 1843 he left Havana for New York, where at the end of fifteen days he embarked for Havre de Grâce, at which place he arrived on the 20th of July, and finally at Paris on the 24th of the same month, having resided constantly in that Capital until his return on the 15th of August last.

P., whether he can state the object of that journey.—C., that its object is a matter of public notoriety, and was besides advised by physicians of note: the restoration of his health, as is known to the very government of the Island.

P., with what person of this Capital or of other parts of the Island he has kept up a correspondence during his absence.—C., that with none, owing to his near impossibility of writing without great detriment to his health, as the whole Island knows; having written nothing but a few notes to his wife, which may rather be called bulletins of his health, as can likewise be certified by all who saw him in Paris, and above all by the first physicians of that Capital.

P., whether during his stay in Paris he had any interview with Lic. Don Domingo del Monte.—C., that he saw him familiarly and frequently (for he has had no reasons for doing otherwise), as happens between friends and countrymen.

P., whether in any of their conversations Lic. Don Domingo del Monte spoke to him about Mr. David Turnbull and the projects he had in hand for carrying into effect the emancipation of the slaves in this Island.—C., that never.

P., whether he remembers at all having treated of those particulars with any person whatever.—C., that with no one.

[p. 369]

P., whether he does not remember having heard it said that Don Domingo del Monte as well as other persons have worked effectively in concert with Mr. Turnbull to bring about the said emancipation.—C., that he has heard it spoken of generally, and even with indignation, as an imputation that was being made against him in this case; everyone holding it to be a solemn imposture.

P., whether the declarant judges Mr. del Monte free of responsibility in the present case, and on what grounds.—C., that the question is not well put together, and he therefore abstains from answering it.

The Prosecutor explained to the declarant that his object in putting the foregoing question to him was confined to having him record his opinion as to the sentiments of Mr. del Monte on the question of emancipation, and at the same time state whether he considered him capable of having had an influence on the events that were observed toward the end of last year in the jurisdiction of Matanzas and Cárdenas; for, having kept up close relations with that person, he could very well give his opinion on the matter.—C., that in the first place the proof that the previous question was not well conceived, and did not even express the Prosecutor’s idea, is the explanation he has just given, as it will be easy to demonstrate on another occasion, which he does not do now to avoid prolixity; and confining himself to the question, he answers that he has always heard him speak in a sense contrary to emancipation, and considers him incapable not merely of exerting an influence on such events, but even of conceiving the idea of them; for in all his relations with Lic. Don Domingo del Monte he has always found him a decided lover of the country, and as such the deponent is honored by his friendship.

P., whether he has come to conceive the idea that the former Consul of H. B. M. in this Island, Mr. Turnbull, plotted to disturb the order in it, working with enthusiasm toward a rising of the slaves; stating at the same time the grounds he may have had for it.—C., that he heard it said so when they arrested him, the declarant being at the time ill outside the city.

P., whether it came to his knowledge that during the time of His Excellency Don Jerónimo Valdés, several members of the Society of Friends of the Country, scandalized by the conduct observed by Mr. Turnbull, had made a motion asking that his name be struck from the rolls of that respectable

[p. 370]

Corporation, considering it an anomaly that among the Friends of the Country there should be counted as a corresponding member one who in every respect was trying to destroy it.—C., that it is necessary to distinguish periods, for the motion was made a long time before Turnbull was arrested, and when shortly before he had been in the full exercise of his consular functions, and as such in correspondence and other relations with the Government of the Island; and confining himself to the question itself, within the Society, the declarant refers in all its parts to the official letter which, as Director of that Corporation, he sent in those circumstances.

P., whether the said motion was made before or after Mr. Turnbull was arrested.—C., that this has been answered in his previous reply.

P., whether he was Director of the Economic Society when the arrest of Mr. Turnbull took place, and what course that respectable body took in view of that circumstance.—C., that he believes he was Director at that time, for he was so from the end of 1842; and that he does not know of the Society’s having taken any measure on account of the said circumstance.

P., of what mind the declarant was with respect to the motion to which the foregoing answers allude.—C., that his said official letter leaves nothing to be desired on the matter.

P., whether the declarant really and truly did not know, at the time of the motion, that Mr. Turnbull was making himself conspicuous by his zeal in the matter of the question of emancipation.—C., that he had heard much talk of his repeated remonstrances, as the organ of the British Government, for the cessation of the slave trade, and of some steps he was taking in the discharge of his ministry in the character of Superintendent of the emancipated (for he held both offices), admitted as such by the Supreme Government and by that of this Island.

P., whether he came to understand the object Mr. David Turnbull had in landing furtively on the shores of this Island, for which reason he was placed under arrest.—C., that he does not know the reason why he should have done so furtively.

P., whether he likewise could not understand the political end Mr. Turnbull may have had in view in introducing himself in that manner into this Island.—C., that he knows nothing as to the political end; and that the only thing he heard generally said at that time was that he had come to claim a certain number of Blacks, from he knows not what foreign colony, who

[p. 371]

were held as slaves on one or several sugar estates of the Vuelta-Arriba, in the discharge of his functions as Superintendent of emancipated Africans.

P., whether he knows or has heard it said that during the time Mr. Turnbull held the post of Consul, arms and other munitions of war were brought into this Island by his arrangement, and to what end.—C., that this is the first news he has on the matter.

P., whether he has heard it said that some persons contributed money or otherwise toward procuring the said arms.—C., that he knows nothing of that either, nor would he even believe it, if he were told it, of sons of the country.

P., whether he does not infer that there exists in this country a party which in every respect has tried, in concert with Mr. David Turnbull, to raise the slaves, in order to obtain, under cover of the difficulties the Government would necessarily encounter, innovations in politics.—C., that he believes it neither exists nor can exist, and pledges himself to carry evidence on the matter even to the most prejudiced minds.

P., whether the declarant has kept up relations of intimate friendship, or of any other kind, with the present Consul of H. B. M.—C., that he does not even know him by sight.

P., whether he has heard it said, or knows, that this functionary is identified with the ideas of Mr. Turnbull touching the project of emancipation. —C., that he knows nothing on the matter.

P., whether he has known in this capital Don Francisco Senmanat, and what kind of relations he has kept up with him.—C., that he has known him by sight, and so many years ago that he does not even remember exactly the last time he saw him.

P., whether he has heard what object this individual had in coming to this country at the end of last year.—C., that he neither knows it nor can gauge it, since he was at that time two thousand leagues away.

P., whether the declarant has had any part in some project of conspiracy that had as its object the absolute emancipation of the slaves in this Island, stating in that case the political end that moved him to it.—C., that what the declarant has taken and will always take part in is, and will be, staunching and healing the wounds that other hands have inflicted on his country, for whose welfare he will shed his blood to the last drop.

P., who in his view are those who have inflicted those wounds on his country, and what means they have used.—C., that

[p. 372]

this is not the occasion to state it, for it would be necessary to enter upon a detailed analysis which belongs elsewhere.

P., whether he suspects that some persons, moved by a spirit of animosity toward the declarant, have injured him in the present case, directly or indirectly.—C., that he must believe so, on seeing how innocence is dealt with.

P., whether the declarant has kept up relations of friendship with any chiefs of the Republic of Mexico and of the Spanish Main who have been in this capital.—C., that never.

P., whether he has kept up relations of friendship with Don Félix María Tanco, and in that case whether he has met with him, or with another person of the same surname, in any Society in this Capital or outside it.—C., that his relations with Don Félix Tanco have been few, for this gentleman has almost always resided in Matanzas, where he was Postmaster, the two knowing each other by name, and he having paid the declarant a visit in this city when he was ill on his return from the United States.

P., whether the declarant knew the mulatto Gabriel de la Concepción Valdés, alias Plácido, and likewise, for any reason, the Black Miguel Flores. —C., that he has never known either of the two.

P., whether he has heard it said that the aforesaid Plácido and Flores were emissaries of Mr. Turnbull and of certain Societies established in this Capital for carrying the plan of emancipation into effect.—C., that the first he does not know, and the second he does not know and very much doubts.

P., whether he has likewise not heard it said that one of those Societies had its seat in the house of Don José Ayala, and was composed of white persons and persons of color.—C., that he who does not know of its existence can hardly know its circumstances.

At this point the Prosecutor ordered the proceeding suspended, to be continued when necessary in the course of the trial; it was read to the deponent lawyer, who declared himself in agreement, observing only that, in view of the tenor of certain questions put to him in the present interrogatory, he finds himself obliged to enter the most solemn protest, that it may have all the effects allowed by law when the time comes to set forth the irregularities to which he refers......—Pedro Salazar.—José de la Luz.—José Fernández Cotá.

*

**

[p. 373]

C onfession of D on J osé de la L uz C aballero :

J une 10 of 1845.

P., the preliminary statement he has given in this case, which stands at folio 1320, having been read to him, whether it is the same that he made; whether he affirms or ratifies its contents, whether he has anything to add or remove, whether the signature and flourish with which it is authenticated are in his own hand, and whether he wishes it to be taken as part of this his confession.—He said that all that has been read to him is the same as he has declared, that he affirms and ratifies its contents anew, having nothing to add or remove, acknowledging as his own the signature that validates it, and therefore he wishes it to be taken as part of this his confession.

He is charged with the complicity that appears from the record against the confessant in the project contrived by Mr. David Turnbull, ex-Consul of H. B. M. in this Island, by which he tried to bring about the emancipation of the slaves.—He said that to all that hubbub of suggestions, impostures and contradictions observable in the declaration of the Black Miguel Flores (which has just been read to the confessant) he gives as his sole discharge that he be answered: when?

P., whether with the discharge he has given he considers that he denies the charge that has been made against him.—He said that he does.

Pressed as to how he denies the charge made against him, when he has seen the accusations against him, through the reading that has been given him of them, from the declaration of Miguel Flores at folio 278, and what was set forth by the mulatto Gabriel de la Concepción Valdés at folio 814; facts which, viewed with his preliminary statement before one, show some agreement, for he has said that in Paris he visited Don Domingo del Monte, whose opinions are manifest, since for them he was exiled or expelled from the Island; and in this Capital Mr. Turnbull; and although this may not be sufficient, it so happens that this gentleman, both in the period of his Consulate and before and after it, has clearly and in the face of all shown his ideas of emancipation; attention being drawn, in these circumstances, to the official letter which stands certified at folio 667, which is the production of the confessant, reproving or rebutting the idea of some members of the Society of Friends of the Country who, seeing and considering that it was not fitting for Turnbull to be associated with them when he was plotting against it, asked for his expulsion: by virtue of which he is admonished to be frank and explicit, stating

[p. 374]

all he knows on the matter.—He said that his discharge comes almost wrapped up in the charge itself; and confining himself to the particulars it embraces, he answers: 1st, that it is one thing to visit a friend and fellow countryman, and another to conspire against the country: 2nd, that where the opinions of Don Domingo del Monte have been manifest to the confessant is in his opposition to the slave trade, but never to the emancipation of the slaves: 3rd, as regards the confessant’s visits to Mr. Turnbull, he refers to what he has declared: 4th, the official letter dealt with having the regulations of the Society observed, and in any case the admission or expulsion of Mr. Turnbull was a matter of opinion, and, decided for or against, can by no means constitute a charge; 5th and last, as regards the mention (an honorable one, to be sure) that Plácido makes of the confessant in the statement which has been read to him, he has nothing to say except that he has never been on any such estate; not answering the description given of his poor learning, since that is neither his business nor a charge.

P., whether he is convinced that to conspire is a crime, and that the law punishes this class of criminals with all rigor..—He said that he is........ Antonio Lara.—Antonio Llorens.—José de la Luz.—José Fernández Cotá.

*

**

## DEFENSE OF LUZ CABALLERO

M r . P resident and M embers of the C ourt :

Don Andrés María de Foxá, Lieutenant of the 2nd company of Volunteers of Merit, and defender appointed by Lic. Don José de la Luz Caballero, in fulfillment of his charge has the honor to state to your Lordships that:

Don José de la Luz Caballero rests his defense on the merits of the record and on the justice of the Tribunal.—Havana, 15 October 1845.— Andrés María de Foxá.

XI. ISLAND OF CUBA Superior Civil Government.

In conformity with the opinion of the Inspectorate of Studies, and having heard such further reports as I thought fit concerning the particulars to which

[p. 375]

you refer in your petition of the tenth instant, I have resolved to grant you the permission you request to establish under your direction a College of primary and secondary instruction; dependent, in the respective branches of teaching, on the provincial Commission of public instruction and on the University, in accordance with the spirit and letter of the Royal Orders of 31 January and 26 September 1839, and of the General Plan of Studies in force in the Island.—Which I say to you for your information and the consequent effects.—God keep you many years.—Havana, 15 February 1848. —Don José de la Luz Caballero.

*

**

Institute of Secondary Education and of Applied Studies OF HAVANA.

The period of fifteen days granted you by His Excellency and Most Illustrious Lordship the Director of Administration on the 8th of April last, and communicated to you by me on the 12th, for sending in the list of the professors of that College, none to appear but those authorized by the Government to exercise the office of teacher in secondary education, having been more than exceeded, and the extension I granted you on the 28th of the same month having also expired, in use of the powers with which I am invested I have resolved that from today the classes of secondary education in that College be suspended, a measure that is exceedingly painful to me, but which you have brought upon yourself.

You will be pleased to acknowledge receipt of this communication to me within the term of three days, stating that you have notified the order to the parents, guardians or persons in charge of the pupils, so that they may transfer the enrollments of those concerned,

[p. 376]

and being warned, should you fail to do so, of whatever may be proper.— God keep you many years.—Havana, 5 May 1869.—José Dº Mantilla.— Don José Mª Zayas.—Director of the College El Salvador.

## XII

Secretariat of Government.

Section of Government.

Taking into consideration the qualifications of Your Lordship, and very particularly the assiduity and good results with which you have always devoted yourself to the instruction of youth, I have thought fit to appoint you a member, on an interim basis, of the Most Excellent Inspectorate of Studies, until Her Majesty, to whom the corresponding consultation is being submitted, resolves what she deems fitting concerning the definitive appointment.—God keep Your Lordship many years.—Havana, 30 December 1858.—José de la Concha.—Don José de la Luz Caballero.

*

**

Most Excellent Sir.—Honored so spontaneously by Your Excellency with the appointment of interim member of the Most Excellent Inspectorate of Studies, it is exceedingly painful to me (because I love my country and work) to be unable to respond to such confidence as it deserves and as I should wish; my notorious ailments depriving me of the possibility of attendance, and thereby of being an active and worthy member of that respectable body. This circumstance in no way diminishes my readiness to contribute the humble share of my experience and my zeal; nor does it lessen in the slightest the high esteem in which I hold the distinction conferred upon me.—God keep Your Excellency many years.—Havana, 2 January 1859.—Most Excellent Sir.—José de la Luz.—His Excellency the Superior Civil Governor.

[p. 377]

## XIII. PRESIDENCY of the Most Excellent City Council.

His Excellency the Governor and Captain General, in view of the record of the elections of the 1st instant, has been pleased to appoint the first twenty-three on the list of those elected who obtained the most votes; and Your Lordship being one of those included in that number, I have the pleasure of informing you of it for your information and satisfaction.—God keep Your Lordship many years.—Havana, January 26, 1860.—Antonio L. de Letona.—Don José de la Luz Caballero.

*

**

Exceedingly obliged by the vote of my fellow citizens, no less than by the approval of His Excellency the Governor and Captain General, for the office of member of the Council, I have the sorrow of stating to Your Excellency the absolute impossibility of any attendance in which my peculiar as well as notorious ailments place me. This same impediment I set forth in January last to His Excellency’s predecessor, when he communicated to me my appointment as member of this Inspectorate of Studies; regretting then, as now, my inability to contribute in this new capacity to the service of a country so adored.—God keep Your Lordship many years.— Cerro, 27 January 1860.—José de la Luz.—His Excellency the Superior Civil Governor.

*

**

PRESIDENCY of the Most Excellent City Council.

His Excellency the Governor and Captain General, having been informed of the resignation Your Lordship has made of the office of Alderman to which you were appointed on the 19th of January, has been pleased

[p. 378]

to declare it inadmissible, by virtue of the express terms of article 6 of the Law of the 27th of July last: which I communicate to Your Lordship for your information and the consequent purposes.—God keep Your Lordship many years.—Havana, February 16, 1860.—Antonio L. de Letona.—Don José de la Luz Caballero.

*

**

It is nature, not will, that bars my entrance into the City Council of Havana. That is why I judged it idle to apply article 8 of the Law to myself, and inapplicable article 6, on which the refusal of His Excellency the Governor Captain General rests. The case is, then, one of mere observation, which the Government may verify how and when it pleases, in the understanding that I was never a man to muffle myself in vain pretexts, nor to cover myself with any other cloak than that of truth.—God keep Your Lordship many years.—Cerro, February 19, 1860.—José de la Luz.— His Excellency the Superior Civil Governor.

XIV

I keep a sheet of paper on which my father, Don José María Zayas, from the room in which Don José de la Luz lay dying, informed my mother of the approach of the fatal end in these words:—“June 22 1862—L. it may be that when you receive this, Don Pepe will no longer be alive.—J. Mª—” and afterwards, as though the messenger had been called back when already on his way so that something might be added, one reads a line written with a blotted pen and an agitated hand, which says: “at 7¼ he died.”—(A. Z.)

XV.

Wishing to give a solemn testimony of the regard in which the Superior Government of this Island always held the literary merits and the public and private virtues that distinguished during his life Don José de la Luz y Caballero, formerly a Member of the Inspectorate of Studies and Director of the College of El Salvador of the Cerro, I have thought fit to order as follows:

[p. 379]

1st. At the conveying of his body to the General Cemetery, which is to take place, according to the arrangement of the executors, today at five in the afternoon, one of my aides-de-camp shall attend in the state carriage of this Government and Captaincy General.

2nd. The Royal Literary University, the Royal Academy of Medical Sciences, the Body of professors of the General Preparatory School, and the Royal Economic Society shall be invited to attend in a body: all these Corporations shall attend with their insignia, if they are entitled to use them, or in strict mourning dress.

3rd. The Heads or Directors of the establishments of public Instruction dependent on the Government shall be instructed to suspend teaching for three days, as a sign of mourning.

4th. These orders shall be inserted in the official Gazette of this Capital. —Havana, 23 June 1862.—Francisco Serrano.’

## XVI

When Don José de la Luz died, the Curate of the parish of the Cerro was the Presbyter Don Cristóbal Suárez Caballero, his friend and frequent visitor. As soon as Luz expired, Father Suárez ordered the bells of the parish to toll every half hour, and I have many times heard it related, though always as a rumor that I have not verified, that someone pointed out to him that such a demonstration was improper in the case of a Mason, and it was even added that the curate, a man of violent character, answered whoever gave him the warning that he was master in his own parish, or something to that effect.

Whether or not the said rumor was true, what I have indeed heard from the lips of my father, Don José María Zayas, on many occasions, is that Father Suárez, fearing some outrage of intolerance at the moment of the burial, issued the parish certificate required for the interment of the body, stating that Luz had received “the Holy Sacrament of Penance,” and so had it recorded in the corresponding entry of the Register of burials.— My father would add that this ought not to be made public, on account of the harm it might do to the Presbyter Suárez.

According to my information this priest died a short while ago in the Canary Islands, where he was born, and this circumstance moves me to relate what I have said. For the rest, I have never thought that the point has any other interest than that of re-establishing the truth of an event.—(A. Z.)

[p. 380]

## XVII

(Letter of Don Gaspar Betancourt (El Lugareño) to Don José de la Luz.— February 29, 1840.)

Off goes a packet in the Camagüey tongue. Next week the second article of the journey, “Trinidad,” will go, in which I give our dear countrymen a few nips, but very gentle ones. I only take one caiman’s snap, in passing, at a Friar I came across on board, a Franciscan, a Spaniard, who told me he was going to open a class of philosophy in Trinidad. We got into the subject, and the man is a poor friar, and nothing more; just imagine, when he told me that he was not in agreement with some of Varela’s philosophical doctrines, and that he considered Latin indispensable for the study of philosophy....

*

Well, to come back to the Franciscan, you will see, Pepe, what a discourteous and shameless friar he is. I said nothing in my article “Trinidad” that could offend a living creature: he has seized the radish by the leaves, or the monkey by the tail. In short, read his attack and my answer, and pronounce sentence. I could answer him in another style, but I want no quarrels with friars...... From his article one can judge what milk that friarly cow will give.—Say something to him, man!....

XVIII. (Letter of Don Félix Varela to Don Manuel González del Valle.—October 22, 1840.)

In many places they are taught simultaneously (Physics and Logic), and if I am not mistaken, even our Don José de la Luz did so, and perhaps does so still. I remember that when he wrote to me that he was teaching Physics before Logic, I answered him that I found one advantage in it, namely that the students prefer the study of Physics as being more agreeable, and in this way their taste is formed, while they are taught Logic at the same time without perceiving it.

## END OF THE FIRST VOLUME

** (Letter of the same to the same.—March 29, 1840.)
